# Chapter Summaries

Per-chapter detail for the rolling summary. Two to five sentences each. Newest batch last.

## The Index: All Fifty Chapters Of Volume 01

**This is the file a later batch reads when it needs to know what a chapter was about without loading it.** Every row below was checked against the chapter it names. Where a row and a per-chapter entry below it disagree, **the row is right and the prose is the authority**; two such disagreements were found at the close of the volume and the per-chapter entries were corrected in place — Chapter 47's date span, which the page carries to the ninth of the seventh rather than the fourth, and Chapter 48's counting house, which is not one of the twenty-nine holders but a building that takes every packet.

Months are numbered, as they are on the page throughout the closing batch. "Date span" is the chapter's own span, taken from the calendars in `state/continuity.md`, and no weekday name appears anywhere because none appears in the manuscript.

## The Index: All Fifty Chapters Of Volume 03 — CLOSED

**Volume 01, Chapters 1–50, is closed** and its fifty-row index is the record of it. **Volume 02, Chapters 51–100, is closed** and its fifty-row index is above. **This is the Volume 03 index and it is closed at Chapter 150 and Volume 03 is closed with it.** Where a row and a per-chapter entry below it disagree, **the prose is the authority**. **One row was corrected in place by the Volume 03 audit, and the prose is what it was corrected to: the date span of Chapter 150 reads *1st–2nd, 5th month, next year* and not *2nd*, because the chapter opens with Marek Kest arriving on the first and the second is the day of the review and the season's beginning. The Volume 03 index is otherwise closed and is a record and was not touched.**

Months are numbered, as they are on the page throughout. "Date span" is the chapter's own span, taken from the calendars in `state/continuity.md`, and **no weekday name and no calendar month name appears anywhere in Volume 03**, because none appears in the manuscript. Twelve calendar month names and six weekday names were written into the ten closing chapters and every one of them was found and corrected before the batch closed; the record is `The Corrections Made On The Page Before Volume 03 Batch 0005 Closed, And Checked Clean` in `state/continuity.md`.

## Volume 01, Batch 0001 (Chapters 1–10) — The First Correction

### Chapter 0001 — The Summary

The seventh of the month, late-afternoon inheritance docket in the Auremar Roll Hall. Marek finds a third witness notch on the Venn clerk's summary that does not exist on the witness copy, and a filed correction with no authority attached that substitutes "the present holder" for Aldren Venn's name. A witness line hung off the oldest docket entry names Mara Venn as the household witness, which is the entry nobody reads. He asks Tamsin Rook to make a private comparison copy, then carries the slip withholding the summary to the Table: Pell refuses to countersign it on an evening docket, on the grounds that the next clerk to open the file will find his name on it. Marek keeps the summary out of the packet himself, sends three things instead of four to the courier, and stands in the cold yard with an unsigned slip in his coat and a night to decide whether to be on the river at all. Stage 0 only; he gains a preserved copy and a question, not authority.

### Chapter 0002 — The Work That Is Named

The eighth, at the Reed Cut channel and the Venn farm. Tavi Venn works the gate linkage and refuses to discuss inheritance in the lane. Mara Venn, from the step and not the kitchen, dates the original to the second month of the year four years back, gives her account — she witnessed it, she never signed a continuation, and a young clerk brought a paper to the field in the sixth month that she refused — and keeps four years of household figures in a wax tablet. Sivra Oris states publicly at the gate that the field-side upkeep was Aldren's from the day he signed until the day he died and the gate and navigation duty is hers, refusing both readings. Tamsin goes ahead to the court; the deadline is fixed: gates inspected on the eleventh, winter-seed notices issued on the fourteenth.

### Chapter 0003 — The Clerk's Window

The eighth, Tamsin's POV at the Mosswake Roll Court. Ivet Sarn refuses to reopen settled precedent and gives her real reason, recorded by Tamsin with date, time, witnesses, and the two inches of hand, pinned up publicly. Told to write the reason down, Sarn adds one thing and says it once: it took her four days to open a file in the fifth year, and on the fourth day the relief rolls could not be certified. Tamsin writes *four days* under the date and does not ask again. Tamsin then indexes the received tray and finds an unfiled fourth-month Dunn summary carrying the same four lines, with Nessa Dunn, fifteen, in the surety line. Nessa has signed nothing and has been told at the gate that she is going to owe it. The Venn packet goes back down the river that evening with the summary left out of it, by the morning courier, because a packet left in a district tray over a flood weekend is a packet the Table certifies on Monday out of habit; the unsigned slip is in Marek's coat.

### Chapter 0004 — The Cabinet

The ninth, at the Notaries' Table in Auremar. Waiting to be seen, Marek turns over the back of the correction and finds a flat, even certificate impression and a broker's fee entry naming Candlewright Lane, authorised by a district office with no business doing so; he writes the address down twice and understands he has a name and no use for it. Pell concedes the correction is probably bad and argues that reopening a flood-season file costs the district eleven hundred households a late winter; he offers to certify this week and compare later, and explains that a comparison cannot be had by a man who is not in the room when the sleeve comes out. Tamsin puts the Dunn copy on the table openly rather than in a drawer, and Pell reads it three times. Marek leaves a written comparison request on the desk; a board notice then records a sealed administrative hold on the Venn file on a reference that carries no office name, not to be enforced, removed, or assigned.

### Chapter 0005 — The Next Household Worker

The ninth, evening, Nell's POV in the Greyfen dye-house. Ostyn Vare reads the continuation clause aloud, honestly, and the clause makes a departing worker's tool debt answerable by "the next household worker." Marek arrives intending to remove the document and is refused. Nell finds the four-line Venn summary in his satchel — the third line of it, the one carrying the substituted guarantor language, being the sentence she recognizes from the wage schedule six inches from her elbow — and forces him to state what he knows, then demands the draft he has been hiding: an invalid proposed exception that recites a comparison he never made. Tamsin admits she found it in the wastebasket and refuses to work beside him until he discloses everything. Nell's terms: he shows her anything he writes before he writes it, and she establishes that the concealment is three days old, not three weeks.

### Chapter 0006 — The Emergency Stair

The tenth, with the closing beat on the eleventh. Marek learns what the hold costs: it saves the field from seizure and forbids the Venns from assigning the crop, so the family cannot feed itself, and the field-side duty is being done unpaid by Tavi because no one else will touch it. Magistrate Leda Rell offers a closed order that would preserve the grain and release seed tonight, founded on the corrected file and without the family in the room. Marek asks for a public rehearing on the twelfth instead and accepts the cost. He hands Tamsin the whole file and asks Mara and Tavi to decide whether to testify; Tavi testifies about the linkage and Mara about the paper offered on the field, and Tavi then names the thing she actually wants — the gate reset and the west bank work put on the water office in writing, with the household expressly not required to do the field-side, because she has cut that ditch unpaid since the fourth month and knows the lane will read the work as acceptance. At the clerk's window after hours, Sarn sends her own rider to Auremar on the court's custody slip and says she will hold whatever comes back until the twelfth and not before the hour. On the eleventh the witness order goes up on the board, and Tamsin finds it dated the ninth and drawn at eleven.

### Chapter 0007 — The Order on the Board

The twelfth, Tamsin's POV on the morning of the hearing. She stops the court before the applicant opens by showing that the posted witness order is a third issue the register does not have, with the widow's name struck through in raking lamplight and the broker's certificate put in front of her. Pell objects that a copyist's memory is not evidence, and withdraws his objection about the provincial hold while keeping the rest. Rell reads the face of the hold aloud and the First Seat's fire-and-water device is named in open court for the first time. Marek asks the court rather than the Table to direct that the leaf be produced; Sarn produces it at ten out of her own strong box, in a Table sleeve with the Table's seal on the wax, enters it in the minute book as received into the custody of the court, and demands the applicant's written application as protection for herself. Garren Pyle's certificate is shown to be a cold press mark, not a true seal, and to carry the copied third notch again. Rell issues a limited audit of the Venn file, named and witnessed, and warns that whatever she says this afternoon will be copied by clerks who do not know Marek's name.

### Chapter 0008 — The Sentence the Court Wrote

The twelfth, afternoon. Marek argues from the leaf that the instrument names a person and requires a signature from anyone assuming a continuing duty. Rell refuses to narrow the public-necessity sentence, because a signature asked for in February is not a signature. At the recess she reads the sheet Tavi asked for on the step of the Venn house on the evening of the tenth and says *that is a small thing and it is the whole case*, and tells Marek not to be thanked for it in front of the lane. She rules at twenty past three in five terms: Tavi's personal debt void; the field preserved until the second month, when seven years from the signing are up; Sivra Oris's gate and navigation duty unaffected; a new signature required of any successor; and the bank works and gate reset on the district water office, with the household expressly not required to perform the field-side upkeep. Exercising the audit, Marek receives the first Roll-Answer, which is accurate about the record the court has just made and names the present holder of the field rather than the girl whose debt was voided; he pays in ash taste, counterpressure, a headache that persists, and a temporary inability to lie about one named obligation. The public-necessity sentence is read at two o'clock and in the same minute the registrar files the long-unfiled Dunn summary on the authority of it, which is before the terms are ruled; and Nessa Dunn asks the bench whether she has just been made a debtor by another name.

### Chapter 0009 — What the Lane Made of It

Tamsin's POV over the four days after the twelfth, at a trestle table. The ruling mutates in the lane into "the holder pays," which is the opposite of the fourth term, and Tamsin corrects it in public and tells the lane to keep the copies in drawers rather than wait for her. Mara asks about the spring and gets an honest answer: until the second month, not security; and when she then asks whether anything left in the file can come back for them, Marek cannot give her the reassuring sentence, Tamsin answers in his place, and she writes the unanswered question in her own book. Orra Dunn is afraid a hearing will expose the whole household, and receives the date of the twenty-third and a separate sitting requested in the register. Nell reads her own clause aloud, signs a new wage schedule with a visible exit clause that the guild has not yet accepted, and sits on the third stool. Odile Fenn refuses to examine Tavi while a guarantor claim is open. Tamsin collates the twenty-six copies — nine her own, six from a Greyfen copyist, four from the hooded woman, three from Ivo Harker's tin, and four from two unnamed men off the bank who argued their way to the right reading — and finds a faded red running stitch with a sequence of small non-letter marks in the margin of one of them, on a leaf that was otherwise blank, and states plainly that she does not know what it is. A court runner summons them: the exhibit's chain is being opened, and a sealed First Seat page with a thread in its fold has been slipped in behind the memorandum.

### Chapter 0010 — The Form

The seventeenth and eighteenth, in Auremar. Marek arrives on the seventeenth with the headache still in his skull and the ash taste still in his mouth, and finds that he can no longer lie about the guarantor, which he proves twice on a quay. He stopped at Mosswake on the way down and Perrin Ashe, the penny doctor at the river stair, put two fingers on his eyes and told him the reading had not let go of him, and to come to a person before a book. Under a joint order, Ruhl Dunnet states that he may take a person but not a shop, and only once the paper is safe, so Tamsin inventories Corvin Dray's office over two days with the eleven waiting clients sent out to the yard; on the second morning a man who gives his name as a firm offers to have three documents of the sealed exhibit released before it is opened, free and unsigned, and says a fourth can be taken from the woman holding a copy in the Venn lane; Marek and Dunnet refuse together and have the offer, including the attempt to reach the woman named in the twelfth, written into the book with the hour. The ledger is a price list: sixty-one copied certificates and ninety-eight sold witness sequences at four shillings, across Venn, Dunn, and two provincial files at Glasswater and Emberwatch. Corvin admits selling a mark and a date and refuses to name the four clients who write the sentences. The chain reveals a four-paragraph printed form with a series number and a print run requiring flood-season continuity filings to be entered in the routine column and not itemised. Dunnet arrests Corvin only after the ledger is copied, at the sixth hour. Marek writes the nine-paragraph audit request by hand, argues with Tamsin over whether to name Glasswater and Emberwatch, and gives in to her; Examinations takes custody of the request before the lamps go out, without deciding scope, and the intake clerk writes the file number on Tamsin's copy in ink and initials it.

## Volume 01, Batch 0002 (Chapters 11–20) — What a Ruling Costs

### Chapter 0011 — The Schedule

The nineteenth and twentieth, at Reed Cut. Hallis Dren needs the fifth term's works actually done under the water office's own flood authority, and he has written the sheet himself and will not have it posted from an applicant's word, because his certification and his mother's canal house are both on it and because the reserve is nineteen sacks short and the rate falls in the fourth month hardest on the smallest holdings. Marek gets it nailed to the gate rail with the memorandum number, the date, and Dren's name, and holds one end of the cord for twenty minutes in silence. The crew arrives on the twentieth under a foreman who reads the sheet three times and calls it a good sheet; Tavi Venn has been in the ditch since first light, is made to stand clear, watches her own ditch empty, and hands her bar back at noon. The fifth term works. The lane decides she took the ditch, out loud, kindly, from two women bringing soup, and Marek cannot say the one sentence that would correct it because the sentence about the guarantor does not come, and thirty people count the silence. He tells Tavi the true reason — that he has no standing to advise her either — and she says it is the worst sentence anyone has said to her in a year and the first one that was true. He writes to Tamsin and adds that the lane's version is going to spread and that he has not stopped it, because stopping it is how it gets into a record as his opinion.

### Chapter 0012 — Until Full Review

The twenty-third, the separate sitting in the Dunn matter, in the river courtroom with the fixed benches roped out. Orra Dunn says in open court that she does not want her household named anywhere and then asks for the opposite, because a clerk in the second row has been writing since they came in and she wants one piece of paper in the matter written by somebody who is not the applicant. Nessa, fifteen, says she signed a school paper, a hoe, and a seed-share paper, wants to be examined for the seed trade, and will not sign anything. The registrar admits the summary was his own hand and that he entered it at two o'clock under a sentence read at two o'clock. Marek states in open court that he has no offer and asks for three things, expecting one. Rell postpones the guarantor duty with a date and refuses the bar on enforcement, and says out loud that a postponement is worse for that household than a refusal would have been and that she cannot give them a refusal because the sentence is hers; she then directs, on the record and dated the twenty-third, that this court will not again apply that sentence on its own motion without a separate hearing and the signature of a named person who has been heard. In the corridor, Hallow Parrow of the Seeders' Aid Fund asks whether it is the end of it, and Marek cannot say the comfortable sentence and instead tells him the true one, which Parrow values. Orra tells Rell she was right about the cost and came anyway, and says she did it because a thing in her own mouth can be looked at and a thing only in a creditor's book cannot. The date is now a number travelling to a factor at the speed of a river boat.

### Chapter 0013 — The Register

Orra Dunn's POV, the twenty-fourth onward. She does the sum on a board that can be wiped: four creditors, a floor worth four hundred and eighty clean and two hundred and ten with a pending duty on it, and two hundred and seventy that is not a market but a court saying a number out loud on a Friday. She goes to all four. Grice will hold to the first week of the fourth month if Nessa is taken off the paper in front of him; Kell's mother will sign that the floor is not security; and Emrys Tale has already sold his paper on the floor to Grice for a hundred and six and says so in his own door, having decided the certainty was worth more than the ninety-two, and tells her Grice will serve it on the forty quarters of rye in the second month. A neighbour of forty-one years' standing walks half a mile in the rain to give her four pounds of tilling seed she does not need and cannot refuse, and that, not the court, is the day. On the third day Orra asks the court window for a public register of pending duties, against everything she said on the twenty-third, because a duty that exists only in one creditor's hand cannot be contested by anybody but that creditor. Sarn starts it, charges a shilling, refuses to waive it, and writes Nessa's refusal into it. Nessa asks for the entry to go on the wall, not in the board, because the seed office sends a boy round every Monday. A woman of about thirty-five in a river court's grey coat gets off the packet boat on the fourth day with a bundle of papers that are not hers and will not give her name.

### Chapter 0014 — Four Days

Ivet Sarn's POV, the thirtieth of the ninth month into the fourth of the tenth. The unnamed woman in the bundle coat comes to the window again and Sarn refuses to say whose file it is at a window, promises to look it up, writes *offered and declined* with the hour, and finds a Mosswake file from the fourth month of the fifth year carrying the same four lines. She asks for twenty minutes with the magistrate for something four years old that is not her business, and asks Marek to sit down and not speak, which is a request he recognises as her asking him to watch her not protect herself. She produces a fourth-month relief tally from the fifth year with her own nineteen-year-old hand on it: *Seed allocation, upper district, held on the fourth day, docket open. File: Sallow, R., barn term, upper. Reopened by me. Held.* Four days, the same four lines, a minor in the surety line, and a docket three weeks from a relief certification, so that going in meant the whole upper district ate in the wrong weeks. She would do it again and says so. She dictates that the record carry the reason and the consequence together, that the Sallows be told first and by her in her own words, and that Marek be sent to the bottom of the lane, where he stands in the rain for five hours and is not allowed up. Rennie Sallow's answer is that he had thought about nothing else for a fortnight and can now stop, and that he wants the eleven pounds recorded as his and not the court's, and that he wants a place where it says a clerk put a thing in a drawer, and that he wants that place to be the window he has been coming to for forty years. On the fourth of the month, with the magistrate in the chair, the record takes both together, and Marek says out loud the thing nobody else will: the wording in the Sallow file is the wording in the Venn correction, four *years* older, made in this building, so this is a practice and not a fraud, and there is no wrong party in the room for a minute book to rule on.

### Chapter 0015 — The Second Box

Nell's POV, the ninth of the tenth month onward, in the Greyfen dye-house workroom and Marl Crenn's assessor room. Crenn is frightened of the exemption form's second box: a form has been read with another form, the box is full, and a dyers' house where people can be named loses its certification — thirty-one years, four houses, about forty-one apprentices — and the thing on the bench in that building is a court ruling in twenty-six copies, which is not a ruling about dye-houses. She will not tell Nell that taking it off the bench empties the box, because the bench is swept every fourth day and a ruling comes back. Vare says out loud that if Nell signs nothing he is the man who had a document in his hand for three weeks and used it against her, and would rather lose the house's exemption trying. The amendment names the departing worker, sets out the sum, gives eight weeks, and carries the balance and the cost of recovery by a **Greyfen Dyers' Tool Fund** and by no household and no worker; it has to be a body with a name, a keeper, a book, and accounts that survive the four of them. Crenn signs, dates, and says plainly that she is not signing because she believes it but because a form that has been read will be read again and she would rather it be read the second time with her name on it. She then warns Nell that the clause is good and is a *form*, will be printed with a number, and travels. The four houses on the lane raise nine pounds a quarter between them; the man on the rack goes round asking foreman's signatures; two sign because they are frightened and the Caudle house has said nothing, and the levy comes out hardest of a woman on four and six with four children and no other trade. Nell takes the number on the card and goes herself, because a foreman walking round a lane is the guild speaking.

### Chapter 0016 — The Permission

Tamsin's POV, Auremar, into the eleventh of the tenth month. The four paragraphs of the First Seat form, recopied large with the paragraphs numbered, undo her: paragraph four says it is not a direction as to validity and gives no authority and takes none away. It is not a crime, it is a permission, and the correct thing to do with it is administrative. Its four paragraphs are each ordinary in a flood season with four hundred and sixty rolls to certify, and what they do is remove the last person in the chain who might have looked: the answer to *I have heard something about my own barn* stops being an argument and becomes *the court is not obliged to examine this individually*. Nine days of two wrong options. Publishing it is an attack on a specific court, and the court is the one that gave a widow her field; not publishing it means families in three districts find out from a district office on a quarterly return. Pell Vey asks, in the Chancery yard, how many of thirteen named people he has, and is refused; he then says one true thing, that he did not know about the form, found out three weeks ago from a boy with a tray, and would not have acted on it in the eighth month, and explains his own worry — a form with a run on it is a form about a business, and somebody in it will eventually need to know which thirteen names are with a copyist. Tamsin writes it down in full and underlines that he says he did not know and did not act, and does not tell Marek. On the eighth day she opens the chain in front of Dunnet and the boy, sees the second sealed page, does not open it, and gets the fact of it entered in the inventory book with the hour and three sets of initials. On the ninth she sends copies of the form to all thirteen named people with a plain warning and a date, and does not publish, and does not ask Marek, and tells him afterwards in one sentence. He opens his mouth to argue, nothing comes, he says "No," and asks for the whole of it including the parts where he would have been right. In the last section, on the eleventh of the tenth month, Hallis Dren receives an ordinary quarterly return, answers the fourth and fifth questions honestly — one, and yes — and sends it four hundred miles without ever learning what it was.

### Chapter 0017 — The Mark

The eighteenth and nineteenth of the tenth month, Candlewright Lane and the district house. A month after the arrest the room is empty, the chandler wants his stair back and asks for his trade loss to be written down, and four of the eleven clients have shut: one shuttered a counting office against investigators who were not constables, one sold his plates, one left the district, one stopped answering letters. Three cannot work, and the order that protects paper, attendance, and hearing does not touch a person's competence, so Dunnet minutes that it does not. Bryn Mawer, seventeen, Dray's copying girl and not among the thirteen, asks to be examined in the copying; Marek puts her in a supplementary sheet, read by her before he sealed it, on the ground that a name and a date on her side of the paper is worth a fortnight of a tired man's patience, and she asks him not to record that she was frightened. Brammel gets the green box back in a leather case with a receipt, his own half of the copy, and the district's inventory stays in the district store; he cannot get his name out of item nine, and reads the return before signing it. Dunnet carries the district's offer: Dray's plea on the local counts, the Venn correction struck and the file restored complete, the request narrowed to Auremar and the form series dropped. Marek refuses, gives three reasons on the record, and separately has the plea refused and entered with a trial set; Dunnet tells him he must tell Mara Venn himself. Dray's statement, taken in a cell with a magistrate, a clerk and Tamsin Rook, is eleven pages with no person's name in it: scale matrices are bought from a furnace-side counting house on a row by the Kiln sheds, the owner appears in the ledger as a mark — a filled square, a bar, a nick — against thirty-eight lines in the *carry* column in three districts, and Dray never went up the stairs because looking would have cost him four years of trade. He makes his own not-looking into evidence, in his own hand, and Tamsin writes it down unkindly. A statement that accuses no one cannot be received against a distant district, so Marek signs a narrowing confining the request to Auremar with the two provincial entries reserved and not abandoned, tells Tamsin in the same hour, and has the time written beside his signature. He ends on the fact that the arrest changed nothing about the form and that Emberwatch is four hundred and forty miles away and unwritten.

### Chapter 0018 — The Rota

Nell's POV, the twenty-third to the thirtieth of the tenth month, between the Greyfen vats and the Venn lane. Three sums and a fourth on the flat of a dye vat: nine pounds a quarter across four houses, and where it lands, and the Caudle share, and the winter — the reserve is spent, the provincial hold is still on, and a household that is fighting has no hours, so Orra Dunn's forty quarters went into the upper barn two days late and eleven days late in the fourth month is the difference between seed in the ground and seed in a sack. On the twenty-third Marek stands in the workroom doorway and is made to take the broken stool at the end and copy, on the condition that he says the same eleven sentences to anybody who comes in; he copies four hours and is for the first time in eleven days not interrupted. Tamsin's four-line letter gives the table to the lane; Nell refuses it twice in public and then, on the twenty-seventh, nails a rota to the table leg naming Ivo Harker, the seed-barn copyist entered as a role because a rota can hold a person who has no name in a book, Coll Fenner, who has finally given his name, and Wenna Sallow, Rennie's daughter, who kept the copy of the tally. On the twenty-eighth a child of seven is sent by Bettany Skail to ask that a paper be read out loud before anybody signs, and the fourth term is used in the lane by nobody anybody trained, and Nell has it entered without the child's name so that she can point at the list in four years' time. Mara Venn brings out the wax tablet and makes it the household accounts. On the twenty-ninth Nery Caudle signs the amendment in her own yard with two pounds and five a quarter of her own wage in it, having had the terms written on the back in her own words. On the thirtieth Wenna Sallow asks, at the table, whether a duty stands against the Sallows, and Marek says the true thing — that he does not know and will not tell her mother there is not — and the sentence comes out of him without difficulty for the first time in nine weeks. In the workroom that evening he tells Nell about the reading: it stopped, it is not a cure, he will not pay a penny to Perrin Ashe to be told the word *well*, the headache went on the twenty-sixth, and he minds losing the shield more than losing the headache. He tells her about their mother — the Chancery roll with a blank where the name should be and a word meaning *struck*, a copy in his father's tin since he was seventeen, a disputed file with their names on it that is neither enforceable nor free, and a sealed page he will not open in a workroom on a Thursday. Nell refuses the ninth month without forgiving it, tells him what it cost her to lie to a vicar about the levy, and gives him the sentence: someday he will be standing in a room and she will be behind him and will need to have been told first. The two letters arrive at the table at the fourth hour, addressed to nobody in particular, and the second is not signed.

### Chapter 0019 — Itemised

Tamsin's POV, the third of the eleventh, in the copying alcove. The left letter came off the Mosswake landing on the thirtieth of the tenth on river-court copying paper with the counterfoil edge still on it, signed *P. Ostrow*; the right one is a single sheet of good laid stock, a true copy of an endorsement, addressed to *whoever is doing this*, with no letter and no signature. Both carry the same four lines, filled in, and beneath them the public-necessity sentence in the exact words of the twelfth of the ninth — seven weeks and three days — and neither is written the way a court writes a thing: no semicolons, no *save as aforesaid*, the words in the order a person sets down what is supposed to go in a box. Both filings were **itemised** — in the individual column, with a number of their own — so the First Seat form's rule failed in at least one, and the reason is either that the form never reached that court or that somebody read it and refused it, and nobody can tell which from the paper. The Emberwatch instrument is a **dyers' house**: the four lines are on a craft term, a second instrument in the house names a person, and the exemption rule means the first must be read with the second, four hundred miles away, and Tamsin can only see it because a woman in Greyfen read the same form to her in a workroom. Perry Ostrow, thirty-five, Glasswater copying room, is two days in the alcove upstairs: she entered the endorsement into the filing book on the third of the tenth, told nobody at home, posted the sheets to the lane on the twenty-ninth, came up the river herself on the first, and gave her name in four minutes on the strength of a promise that Mosswake would enter her letter in the record with her name on it and write back; her husband is blind since the spring, when he read the summary at the top of the delta landing, and she said the same sentence to Sarn at a Mosswake window a month earlier. A Glasswater miller has asked whether the cut is security and has written to Osric Lave in Greyfen, who wrote to the Mosswake court about the Dunn barn; Tamsin puts that in ink in a minute book rather than in a lane. Then the trick: a clerk of a district court may admit an exhibit on her own authority, exhibits are copied to the parties on the day of admission, and a copy that has gone to a party cannot be un-copied. Tamsin wrote to Sarn on the second, told Marek on the third, let him strike a sentence out of the application and sign it, sent it, and told him she had enclosed the Glasswater file without asking — wrongly, on purpose, in one thing, and asked for the time on it. Then she said out loud that she is frightened, because the thing is bigger than a widow and she is a copyist with a stool.

### Chapter 0020 — Nine Hundred and Forty

Marek's POV, the eighth and ninth of the eleventh, in an eleven-foot passage behind the third gallery and the second-floor Received Room. The Forms Received rack is not locked, because a locked rack says *this is worth stealing*, and it is indexed by the office that sent each thing rather than by what the things are, which is why the answer is in the building and was never found. Tamsin has been on the floor of the passage for three days with thirty-one sheets fanned out. Sarn's note reports the two provincial exhibits admitted on the fourth on her own authority, copies issued the same day, and underneath the stamp a note that she put them in before she told the magistrate and has left a paper on the bench about it. The sheet is **Model Endorsements for Continuity Filings (Flood Seasons), series MC/FS**, sealed by the **Standing Office of Provincial Continuities**, distributed to all district courts and landing offices holding a flood docket and to be filed with the standing instructions and not with the papers; base issue of the second month of this year, run five hundred and sixty; amendment sheet dated the twenty-second of the ninth month, run **nine hundred and forty**, carrying a form of words in which the third line is the Mosswake sentence verbatim under a note reading *Adopted from a provincial court, the ninth month. A court may adopt this wording or any other.* Ten days after a ruling, from a public copy. The First Seat form fits nowhere: it is a quarterly *return*, it does not say what the answers are, and it works by making the honest answer small, not by making it a lie — which is why Dren could answer one and yes and tell nobody. Two documents, two offices, four hundred miles of river, and no document anywhere saying the seat issued the form because the office printed the circular. The four lines predate the Sallow file by four years and the first issue of the series is not in this building. Marek writes to Rell and does not send her, and Tamsin says the real reason out loud for him; then, at the eighth hour, he files a two-page supplementary request naming the series, the base issue, the amendment sheet, both runs, the distribution line, and asking who signed the sheet. Nevin Trost of Examinations, tired and not an enemy, wants the provincial exhibits back because the request was narrowed to Auremar and two provinces are stapled into a file that does not mention them; Marek makes him take a thirty-day extension and write the reason in the box. The seventeen-year-old's supplementary line is left in on purpose. The unproduced second sealed page is asked for and refused, and the refusal goes in the form with his name against it. The last thing is an unsigned offer from a man who is not the head of the office: certify the Mosswake practice, empire-wide, for six months with a review in the fourth month, and the request, its preservation demand, and the pursuit of the form series and the circular all close. Marek does not answer, and understands that the thing he wanted at twenty-four and the thing he wants now are the same offer in two coats.

## Volume 01, Batch 0003 (Chapters 21–30) — The Process and the Copies

### Chapter 0021 — Forty Lines

Hallis Dren's POV, the eleventh to the fifteenth of the eleventh month, in the Reed Cut water office and at the canal. The board on the office wall has forty lines and no dates and four hundred and sixty parcels, and Dren compiles the district's own arithmetic in four columns and sends it to the Mosswake Roll Court, to Upper Cut, and to Millrace, and to Examinations, on his own letterhead, with his name and his mother's canal house on it. Sayer, the district works foreman, prices a hearing at three hours and a shilling and sixpence for a witness, which makes fifty-two hearings a hundred and fifty-six hours, or twenty-six days of a forty-two-day window, which leaves sixteen certifying days and therefore two hundred and eighty-four relief rolls uncertified before the rate is struck on the sixth of the fourth month. Dren writes in the second column that a single answer gets the rolls out on time, and that one is not an argument about the sentence, and that it is not a large sum and it is not his. He does not say what should happen. Dren's name is on it and his mother's house is the security, and she makes him read the columns aloud by candle and says she will not be the only person in the district holding a copy of them.

### Chapter 0022 — Nobody Under It

Marek's POV, the fifteenth to the twenty-first of the eleventh, Auremar. Dren's letter arrives on the fifteenth and the intake clerk puts it on the same table as the unsigned certification offer by accident. Marek works out over three days that what is wrong with the offer is not the sentence but the *review*: a review date with no named person is not a review. He and Tamsin go through the register of the Mosswake window and establish, out loud, that the process he is about to accept will not cover the Venns, the Dunns, the Sallows or the Greyfen houses, and he accepts it anyway. On the twentieth he refuses the certification in the Received Room with Nevin Trost and the intake clerk in it, gives two reasons — no standing to certify for forty-six districts, and a review with nobody in charge of it — and has the refusal entered, read back, and signed on the back of the offer. He does not write the process. He writes a list of what it must contain and hands it to the court, the water office and a copyist. Trost then tells him that the office is under an obligation to state the refused offer's terms to every district on the distribution, which he had not thought of.

### Chapter 0023 — Four Boxes

Tamsin's POV, the twenty-sixth to the thirtieth of the eleventh and the first of the twelfth, at the Mosswake Roll Court. Rell agrees in nine minutes to let the court try the thing and refuses to put the court's name on it until the fourth month's docket is done. Sarn refuses to start a second register and Tamsin gets past her by counting the cost out loud: twenty-six days of window is two hundred and eighty-four relief rolls uncertified. The form is made in four boxes — the hearing, the witness, the cost, the next review — with the words *I do not understand this* written down as a complete and acceptable answer, a witness who is not the applicant and not a creditor, a cost ceiling, and the fourth of the fourth month as the review. The two registers go on the same board because a hearing list shows who came. Sarn keeps a list of people who have agreed to witness and has Pella Osk write out two hundred and forty forms by hand, and asks Pella at last what the copying room was like on the ninth of the ninth. The first person to use the form is a woman who has been told by a broker that she need not come at all.

### Chapter 0024 — Four Questions

Nell Kest's POV, the second to the fifteenth of the twelfth, the Venn lane and the Greyfen workroom. The reading table fails in the middle of the month because a reader who is asked to say it again says it again, and a woman with a good memory for voices and no reading at all understands every word and can give back none of them. A woman of about sixty-five at the seed barns produces the four questions, and the fourth — *who read this to you, and do they get paid if you sign* — is the one that decides whether the other three can be trusted. They go on a chalk board on the sixth, the rota sheet comes off the table leg, and the table belongs to the lane with a list of names people add themselves to. Vare says out loud that a foreman who has been audited stops being the person anybody brings a paper to, and walks the clause into four workrooms himself. Nell is then four days in bed in the second week and comes back to find the table running without her, and writes in her own book that the only thing that would have helped is a person at the end of the table with a book who writes down who came, and that it is not the table's job. She is paid to copy the amended clause into the schedules of the houses that have not signed, collects the nine pounds a quarter, and records Nery Caudle's two pounds and five and a quarter. On the fifteenth a letter comes from a cooper at Millbrook Reach: the shop's gauge went on a bond on the fourth and a daughter of sixteen is in the surety line.

### Chapter 0025 — The Reply of the Thirteenth

Marek's POV, the sixth to the twentieth of the twelfth, the Received Room and the copying alcove. Marek's first draft of the reply reads as a sulk and goes in the stove; the second states the offer, the refusal, the process and the cost as four reported facts, and asks to be answered by a person with a name rather than a date. In the corridor behind the third gallery he finds the standing instructions for the ninth year and the line at the head of the flood leaf: *Printed forms of this series are issued under the same authority and no further authority is required.* The Standing Office of Provincial Continuities has no person at the top of it, and Pell Vey says so, and says he has signed for it nine times without asking. Pell signs the reply on the thirteenth, under his own hand, the first time his name has been on anything in the matter. Marek posts the letter to Magistrate Rell that has been in his coat since the eighth of the eleventh. Pell then asks him for the ninety-word corridor conversation of the ninth, and Marek decides not to tell Tamsin, and is about to act on that when Tamsin tells him first about the yard, and about the line she wrote under it and never asked. He discloses his own nine days, they agree a rule about the unsigned instruction, and she forbids him the First Seat quarterly return in the reply and puts her objection in his coat pocket. The reply goes out on the ordinary distribution to forty-six districts, one of them Millbrook Reach.

### Chapter 0026 — The List

Tamsin's POV, the twenty-second to the twenty-eighth of the twelfth and the first of the first month, the Greyfen lane and Mosswake. A set of three sheets costs three and a half hours, and the paper costs two shillings and ninepence, and both go in the same column so they cannot be taken apart. Forty-one people end up holding a copy: the twenty-six from September, two at the Mosswake window, and thirteen new ones, most of them asking for the *reply* rather than the ruling. It fails on money at the Greyfen press, where the copyist charges four pence a sheet and explains that the six free copies of September were anger and not charity. The woman in the hood refuses to be a keeper and gives the reason Tamsin writes down. Vare does not answer four letters and is not chased. Thirty-eight of the forty-one are written to, thirty-one answer, ten do not, and four of those ten are in Emberwatch. The young intake clerk offers a quire out of the Chancery stores and is refused, because an office's paper in a district is a list with an office's name on it. Tamsin writes a four-page list of who holds a copy, in her own book, with no heading and no promise, and answers Iselle Oster's letter from Millbrook Reach, which contains a gauge, a bond, a sixteen-year-old in a surety line, and the sentence that a district which obeys a form perfectly has been made quiet rather than safe.

### Chapter 0027 — What the Form Does to a Stranger

Nell Kest's POV, the twenty-ninth of the twelfth into the thirteenth of the first month, the dyers' lane and the assessor's room. The Nine Elms signs with its four weeks used, so four of the eight houses on the lane have signed and four have not. The Pry, which has had nothing wrong in forty years, asks what it is paying a levy for, and Nell writes down the answer she cannot give: the levy is the price of having an amendment, not of the amendment, and the only way to stop paying is to sign. Marl Crenn reads out the fifth line on the back of the exemption form herself and makes Nell read it, in front of a foreman and an assessor from a second association, and a woman from the next province works out in eleven seconds that a house of hers with a boy on a term has lost its certification in a document she came sixty miles to be told was good news. The levy is recalculated onto the four signing houses on paper, and Crenn says it will take a year and a fight and that Nell should expect to lose it. A sheet comes back from the next province with a question about a girl of nineteen and a wage already paid, and Ostyn Vare recognises the girl as Lisse of the Marrowgate house, who has been paid every Friday since Michaelmas.

### Chapter 0028 — Early

Orra Dunn's POV, the second to the twenty-ninth of the first month, the upper seed barn and the Mosswake window. Orra asks to be entered in the register of hearings and is refused in eleven seconds: a hearing list is a record that you were heard, and she has not been. She then asks for the fourth month's review to be called in the second month, and Sarn enters the request on the slate with *asked, not granted* under it and gives her a copy for a shilling. The slate does the work the hearing cannot: Grice's boy buys two copies, Grice writes an enquiry, and Sarn answers all three of his questions in writing, including that the court will not promise nothing will be done before the fourth month. Grice serves on the twenty-ninth of the first month, a month early; Orra moves forty quarters of rye out of the barn in a night with two carts and Bettany Skail, meets Emrys Tale on the road, and is told he came. Ilsabet Kell, unasked, offers to stand in the second month with her own note and her own words, and Orra has to write the sentence that says *I know what this costs and I am doing it anyway* in somebody else's words and then live with the answer. Nessa comes down off the loft ladder and says she is coming to the fourth month.

### Chapter 0029 — A Mile Away

Nell Kest's POV, the eighth of the first month into the nineteenth of the second, the Venn lane, the Sallows' gate, and the Caudle house in Greyfen. The Venn term ends on the nineteenth and nothing after it is decided; Mara Venn does the sum on her husband's board and finds that what the household has afterwards is a house, four quarters, two rooms over a shop, and a gate. Nell promises the evening of the eighteenth and asks no questions. She is then four days in Greyfen on the copying rounds, on purpose, and comes back to find the table running without her with eleven names in chalk and somebody having washed the top of the trestle, which is the first evidence she has that the table has an opinion about itself. The part that fails is the part nobody notices: a woman came on the Thursday and there was somebody at the table and nobody wrote down that she had ever come. At the Caudle house the fourth question uncovers a second paper under a cloth, brought by a man on a rack, and Nery Caudle's answer is that the reader was paid either way. Wenna Sallow has taken her own name off the board and says why: a thing which only works when you are not the one in trouble is not a thing for people in trouble. Nell does not write to Marek, and understands exactly why, and is not going to tell him.

### Chapter 0030 — Nobody Was in the Room

Tamsin's POV, Auremar and Millbrook Reach, the second to the twenty-second of the second month. Perry Ostrow will not come, and gives Tamsin the four instructions that make the fortnight possible: say you are nobody four times, and do not take a paper into the district, because a paper means a decision has already been taken. At Millbrook Reach, Sabra Ollett, a landing clerk of nine years' standing who has never once examined an individual filing, shows that the instrument that took the gauge is a creditor's filled-in copy of the printed model, stamped by her office in the routine column in the tenth month with **no number against it**, so that the document that took a cooper's gauge out of a shop is a line in a book that says guarantee, shop goods, bond, and the name of a district office, and nothing else. In the shop Tamsin asks the four questions, says she is not a court four times, writes down that a sixteen-year-old in the surety line gave the fourth answer twice and differently, and has Iselle Oster and Tace Oster sign what was written. She opens a register of her own with nothing behind it, stating on its face that it has no seal, no office and no authority and that nothing in it is evidence. She then watches a wheelwright in the same town using four questions copied onto the back of a bond receipt, and understands that the only difference between the two documents is that hers can be asked about.

### Chapter 0031 — The Column That Does Not Tidy

Tamsin's POV, the second to the twenty-sixth of the second month, Auremar. The copy list has forty-one lines, six things per line, and she has only ever filled five, because the sixth would be a guess about why a holder did not answer. She accounts for **five of the ten** non-responders in a sentence each — a woman in Greyfen with four children and no candle, a household in Reed Cut that stopped answering after the seventh of the twelfth, a man who had moved forty miles and whose answer came back on the fourth of the third, a man who had been in a bed in the ninth month whose wife promised he would answer when he was up, and a house in a row off a Kiln road where a copy was put in a drawer by a man who was not living in it — plus a **tenth she had never asked at all**, because she never had an address for him, which is also the copy-list canon of three unasked holders. One man in Greyfen comes back on the fourth of the third, taking the count from ten to nine. She addresses four letters to a lane that has no numbers, decides that confirming an address is a promise, and does not send them; she pays four shillings and a halfpence for a rider post to ask the assessor from the next province one question. Putting the Emberwatch exhibit beside the four non-answers gives the chapter's finding: the only paper in that town is an endorsement a clerk **itemised** and underlined for the next reviewer, and he did it well, and the instruction against itemising was never issued to a dyers' trade because a trade does not hold a flood docket. The answer arrives on the twenty-fourth of the second month from **Bettin Rahl**, the standing assessor of the Emberwatch Dyers' Combination: the four are **one house**, the fourth past the weigh-house on Sillrow, a house of the name of **Ferren**; a house in that trade may not write to a court, a guild, a factor, a district office, or a stranger about an instrument without the assessor's leave in writing first, a rule thirty-one years old at the second leaf of the Combination's book and the reason the trade has had no dispute in forty years. It is not fear. She puts the name on the list untidied, and tells Marek about the letter and not about the four unsent envelopes in her drawer, and says she will tell him in about three months and not a word sooner.

### Chapter 0032 — What the Court Can Do

Marek's POV, the second week of the second month to the second of the third, Auremar. Nevin Trost returns nine lines from the Mosswake court scoping what a **limited sitting** may do: hear a sworn statement as to a party's own duty under the instrument, record it, and give copies — and it may not order disclosure, strike the correction, touch MC/FS or any standing instruction or any return under a fire-and-water device, be described as an audit, or be cited elsewhere. Trost adds a seventh line on the applicant's own application: it may not support any application not made in that court on that file. Marek works out that his best available outcome is now one sentence in a form that cannot be reissued. He is barred from calling the result an audit for ever, and writes on the stair that a finding which cannot be named in the record is worth less in the room and the same in the street. Pell certifies the parties, warns that listing the Standing Office for notice will make the file a series file in two years, and is right; Marek takes the office off the list. He also declines to ask Pell about the yard of the fourth of the tenth, because doing so would disclose that Tamsin has the record of it. Marek narrows his own request in writing on the twenty-first, with nine things he is not asking for, and tells Tamsin at the fourth hour — which she names as the third and smoothest version of his concealment, and makes him write the rule down with a date on it. The court grants the sitting for the first week of the fourth month. The only person who can say whose duty the instrument created is a counterparty, and a counterparty is notified, not served, by the district water office that keeps the book.

### Chapter 0033 — Four Names in Chalk

Nell's POV, the sixth to the seventh of the third month, the dyers' lane in Greyfen and the assessor's room. She walks an hour to the Caudle house first and gets Nery Caudle's permission to publish her, and the Caudle's share of the recalculated levy is **two pounds and five and a quarter** because she signed on the twenty-ninth of the tenth and asked for it in the coin she paid in, which leaves a farthing over and *balance to the first-named*. On the seventh she does the arithmetic aloud at the trestle table with nine people, puts the four signing houses in chalk, and writes under them which one asked and which three did not. The Pry, which has paid nothing, tells her it will matter in a year that the Pry is not one of the four. Roke explains what a farthing does in a trade: it is not money, it is a thing a house says about itself. The household sums are three and a half shillings a week each; and the levy raises nine pounds a quarter into a **Tool Fund that already holds four pounds eleven and fourpence and has never been empty**, which is the same sentence twice in two trades in two districts in two hands, and only Nell has both. The assessor from the next province has decided in her own association's favour — a second instrument since the sixth year ends the exemption, eleven houses exposed — and has sat on it for nine days; Crenn wants it read aloud in the room on the assessment day and not sooner. A returned envelope arrives at the table from Emberwatch with **B. Rahl's name on the front and nothing inside**, which is not a thing anybody writes on an envelope.

### Chapter 0034 — In the First Person

Sarn's POV, the twenty-first of the third to the second of the fourth, the Mosswake Roll Court. She puts the trial's accounting on the public wall between the two registers, in her own hand and in the first person, having burned two earlier attempts. The trial ran the first of the twelfth to the twelfth of the first with her window shut **on purpose**, she asked the roll-keeper the question so the answer would be in somebody else's hand, and she never told the magistrate; all of it is now on a wall with the day on it, including the line that no household in the district waited for a seed parcel because of the six weeks. Thirty-four hearings, a hundred and two hours, nine shillings and fourpence of the eleven; **four** people said *I do not understand this* and are written down as having said it; eleven of the other thirty signed something else inside a month and both facts sit in the same box. She asks Wend the lamp-trimmer, at last, which twenty-two nights the room was lit past the seventh hour, and the door was shut on all twenty-two; two of the hearings were at the ninth hour at night for a woman who works the channel. **Leda Rell comes down to the window on the twenty-ninth of the third**, reads the wall from the bottom up for six minutes, says in her own words that she will read it aloud in the room on the fourth, and tells Sarn to write her name at the foot of the third paragraph, which Sarn does that day and tells nobody about. Sarn rules that the review on the fourth of the fourth is **not a hearing of any application**, turns away a factor's man who pays two shillings to learn nothing, and reads the wall aloud in the room on the fourth with the magistrate's name at the foot of the third paragraph. Sarn posts a **witness order** through Rahl to a house in Emberwatch out of her own money, and it comes back signed on the second of the fourth, with Rahl's line that he did not ask them to be brave.

### Chapter 0035 — A Register Read Aloud

Iselle Oster's POV, the sixteenth to the twenty-eighth of the third, Millbrook Reach. Tamsin comes up the water again on her own money, and not to help: Iselle asked her to be in the room because a woman saying out loud that a document does not exist is a different act with a copyist in it than without. Iselle asks Sabra Ollett the question nobody has asked her — what the stamp is — in front of two witnesses, and hears that it is the landing office's own stamp, that it took four seconds, that the filing has no number, and that Sabra would stamp it again on Thursday. Iselle writes the chapter's argument in her own book: nobody did anything wrong; the district prints a model, tells four hundred offices to put these in a column and give them no number, and does it so a clerk four hundred miles away can count them, and that is a good system, and it has taken a gauge off her bench. She records that she turned down a gauge offered on loan through the wheelwright, on the prediction that all four shops will end up with none. The Emberwatch material arrives and rhymes: a district office that obeyed a form it was given, a trade that made its own and obeys it, and four questions she cannot trace to a person because the woman who gave the last one will not be named. On the twenty-fourth the creditor's man is in the shop and the register is read aloud, which cannot be stamped or filed, and she says out loud that she chose the day and the room. On the twenty-eighth Tace Oster asks to be asked, and asks to be asked whether the shop is worth her name rather than whether she understood a form, and Dain Oster says he wants the trade and there is no gauge to examine him with.

### Chapter 0036 — A Name on a Door

Marek's POV, the twenty-ninth and thirtieth of the third, Auremar. Pell offers him a **standing examiner of provincial filings** — no seal, no commission, a room, a drawer, and the standing to which a district registrar's answer is given — on the condition that the Venn correction is a routine filing defect and that no district's answer is framed by reference to any series in the corridor. Marek recognises the sentence as the sentence from the yard behind the Chancery on the fourth of the tenth, built into a wall, and cannot ask about the yard because doing so would disclose that Tamsin has the record. He refuses in the third gallery with the intake clerk as witness, asks for the refusal in the same form as the twentieth of the eleventh, and signs the record of it unasked, so that Pell Vey now has his name on two recorded refusals and one reply in this matter. He then works out that the standing's real power was the **letter** — one page over his own name to forty-six districts — that he could have written that night, and writes the whole of it in his book and files it nowhere, because the reason is that he would be able to, and there is no principle in it. He also does not write to Nevin Trost about the ninety words, because the rule he made was designed to protect him and is now protecting Pell, and a rule that only works in one direction is a preference. He tells Tamsin at the ninth hour, four hours after deciding, having already got it witnessed, and she does not stop copying and asks one question, *who was the witness*, and says she is not going to reward the fluency. The counterparty notification goes up to Reed Cut on the thirtieth on the water office's own paper, with the line that a dragon is notified and is not under the court's authority; Hallis Dren finds the page in his own book and writes the page number on the copy unasked.

### Chapter 0037 — Two Named Duties

Sivra Oris's POV, the third of the fourth, the Mosswake Roll Court with the benches carried onto the terrace. Rell states in the room that the keeper is a notified counterparty and not a witness, may leave, may refuse every question, and is under the court's no authority. The sworn statement in scale form has two boxes: the party swearing, and **the other party to the instrument**, which nobody can fill, because the other party is Aldren Venn and is in a ground, and the keeper's own words are entered at the magistrate's direction — that the box is blank and complete so, and is not to be treated as a refusal. She swears that the instrument created two duties and named the people who held them — the field-side upkeep and the winter seed were his, the gate and the navigation hers — and that **neither is an office**; that the alteration changed who was held by them, not the duties, by substituting a word any person may stand in. Tavi Venn asks in front of the room whether it changes anything for her, and Rell says no, and the keeper says she does not need to be useful to a lane that has a trestle table and eleven names in chalk. Four people want something and she gives none of it: Rell the occupation question, Marek the seed, a Table man the hand on the correction, and Tamsin the sentence again, and she refuses the last one. Rell then enters the court's one sentence, with her name at the foot, recording that this court cannot compel disclosure, cannot strike the correction, cannot touch the series or any standing instruction or any fire-and-water return, **cannot say and does not know and after four months of asking four offices and one district water office cannot find who authorised the sealed instruction**, and that a party may apply to the Cinder Court in the fifth month on this record and that this court cannot make that application. The gate had been back inside a quarter of an inch since the water office crew reset it in the eleventh month **under the fifth term's schedule**, which is the one part of the matter everyone in the room got the way they wanted it.

### Chapter 0038 — Nobody at the Foot of It

Tamsin's POV, the ninth to the twelfth of the fourth, the copying alcove. The **scope decision** is one page, sealed, and reads *Scope: not granted*; it answers two of five questions, refers the other three to the standing instructions of his own district, and has nobody's name at the foot of it — the middle of it is word for word the sentence Pell Vey dictated to Marek in a gallery in the twelfth, which is how the machine works: the man who taught her how to write an unanswerable answer is not the man who sent it, and the man who sent it does not know his sentence is being used. She collects the objection from the coat pocket and puts it back in her own book, because filing anything about a dragon seat's paperwork in the fourth month would merge the two documents in the file. She then files, over her own signature as a roll-keeper, **four lines** naming the **district of Glasswater** rather than the standing office, on the ground that a district has a clerk and the office has no person, and that a question put to a clerk can be answered by somebody who has to answer it while a question put to a seal is answered by a drawer. She reads it back twice and rewrites the third line once, ending on a version that is worse for her and true. The fourth month's list of dates and names comes out of the intake tray, and two of the names on it are in her book in a cooper's handwriting, and one of them is going to find out from a woman who cannot read.

### Chapter 0039 — The Fourth Question in a Room of Eleven

Nell's POV, the fifteenth to the sixteenth of the fourth, the Caudle lane, the guild's assessment room, and the Venn lane. The paper under the cloth is out: a **notice of the person who holds**, entered under the second instrument, with a box headed *the person who holds* and **Mike Caudle's name in it**, no signature, dated the second week of the twelfth; the line under the box says the person named is not a party and owes nothing, and the man on the rack put his thumb over that line. It also demands eight pounds, dated before the quarter that carries it is struck, at four times the figure in the levy book. She writes down that she saw a resemblance to the fourth term's new-signature rule and that she cannot prove it, and that the difference is that in the lane the man signed. In the assessment room she asks the fourth question of the assessor from the next province in front of eleven houses, and gets *Yes. I am paid to read instruments to people. I am not paid for the answer*, followed by a refusal to be asked anything else and the statement that she knows Nell will not build a case out of it; eleven women now know something they did not know at noon, and nothing is decided. She records the arithmetic of one woman, one room, four hours, and understands that the thing that spread is the board and not the person. She does not go to Wenna Sallow's gate, and writes down that as a decision. A letter arrives for the table from **Osa Ferren** in Emberwatch — the first thing that trade has ever written to a court, sent through its assessor, and **sent on the sixteenth of the fourth month, which is after Chapter 40's closing scene and is why Chapter 40 cannot know it** — asking what a person does who is owed an answer.

### Chapter 0040 — A Gate Is Not a Field

Marek's POV, the sixth to the tenth of the fourth, Mosswake, the Venn lane, and Auremar. The rate is struck on the sixth: four hundred and sixty rolls, parcels and households, all whole, because the process was run outside the window on purpose, and the fifth term's schedule is still nailed to the gate rail where nobody has ever come to read it. Sayer pays **two shillings and sixpence** out of the works fund as a **recruitment cost**, entered under the head *labour, examinations, engaged*, and Marek does not put his hand in his pocket. At the Venn house he reads out the letter he has owed since the tenth month: Aldren's file cannot be made complete, because the ordinary route was refused in the ninth month and the only good route needs somebody's word about the winter seed, and a scale statement is made once and its other party's box is empty for ever. Mara Venn asks whether the field-side work was her husband's, is told it was, and asks what she is, and is answered in a noun for the first time in six weeks. On the step at the gate he takes the second reading of the volume under the undischarged limited audit — the term expired seven weeks ago and the record now carries **nothing** — and it decides nothing, and costs him ash, headache, two days of not being able to look at print and a nosebleed on the rail; he writes the account into his own book on the **ninth**. **The chapter then runs backwards against Chapters 38 and 39 by five days and says so on the page.** On the **tenth**, four days after the step and a Tuesday, Rell says the review compelled nothing and created no precedent, makes him say the refusal of the certification in the past tense on the record, and **refuses Orra Dunn's early-hearing request in a room with a date**: the second Dunn hearing is the twenty-first of the fifth month, and Grice's own private promise to hold to the first week of the fourth month expired on the seventh, three days ago, and the court has no power over him. Marek files the batch's closing record in four lines, and in the copying alcove at the eleventh hour reads the bottom of the fourth column: forty-one names, thirty-two answers and **nine** non-answers, all nine of which he can say without the book, of whom the four in the furnace town are one house with **a surname and a landmark from Rahl's rider of the twenty-fourth of the second and no first names at all** — he writes *Ferren* in and nothing else — and then discovers that every thing he built is in somebody else's hands and working, and that he is the only one of them with nothing to hand anybody. The next morning is a Wednesday, and the sentence is that he will spend a year in a corridor with nothing to hand anybody.

## Volume 01, Batch 0005 (Chapters 41–50) — The Second Dunn Hearing and the Question With a Route

### Chapter 0041 — Two of the Four Are Hers

Orra Dunn's POV, the upper seed barn, the third week of the fifth month. Forty-one sacks of tilling seed, a chalk line on the floor the width of the rye that went to Coldsfoot, and a confirmation on the court wall with both her name and Nessa's on one line. Orra shows the girl her own slate, and Nessa has been counting: Orra has read the four questions to her twice and both times stopped after the fourth and looked at her. **Orra rewrites the four questions as a girl of fifteen would put them, in her own hand with Nessa's name at the top, and Nessa changes two of them**: the first from *is there work in it* to *is there work in it, and is the work mine, or the work of whoever is in the room when it is done*, and the second to *whose name is on it, and is mine one of the names on it, and is mine the first one* — because in that file she is the last name, and the last one pays. She keeps the third and the fourth exactly as they are, because those are the two she cannot answer, and she does not want her mother's handwriting making them sound better than they are. Ilsabet Kell, sixty-one, comes up the lane on her son's arm with her note in a box, three months late and refusing to be thanked; Bodo chose not to ask her to stop. **Orra's decision, which costs her: she does not ask the court to strike Grice's paper, because a served paper is not stopped by a pending duty or a line on a slate, and she asks instead that the paper be read aloud in the room, in front of the person it names, with the reading entered** — and she says out loud that she is asking a court to hear a creditor's paper rather than to stop him and that she does not know why that is right. The hearing slate turns out to work in a way nobody designed: because Orra's name is on a public board, Grice's lawyer has held back his second paper, on the barn term, for four months, and the three words in his letter are *before the hearing*. That is the only reason the seed is still in the floor, and it is not safety, it is visibility, and the man is careful and not a coward. Sarn writes Orra's request into the day's column, says she does not know whether a magistrate may read a creditor's paper into a hearing, has written the question on the board with a line number, and gets told it is the magistrate's answer and not hers. Emotional change: Orra stops wanting Nessa protected from the hearing and starts wanting her heard in it. No power, no magic, and the word *exception* does not appear.

### Chapter 0042 — In the Name of the Court That Asked

Marek's POV, Auremar, the twenty-first to the twenty-fourth of the fifth month. The Mosswake minute of the twenty-first comes down the river in six pages and five numbered findings: the court will not join Nessa as guarantor, and the public-necessity sentence is not a reason in that court without the person being heard and signing; the creditor's paper was read into the hearing at the fifth hour at the household's request and is not affected; **the court states for the first time in writing that it applied that sentence in letters and without a hearing to about sixty households in the sixth year**, and that it cannot get their names in time, and they are not before it; the court does not order the district to do anything instead, and says the cost is named so nobody can say afterwards it was not known; and the magistrate's answer to Sarn's board question is that it was the court's answer and not the clerk's. Marek builds the referral himself in a plain form: it names the correction, **series MC/FS, base issue of the second month run five hundred and sixty, amendment sheet of the twenty-second of the ninth run nine hundred and forty**, the one sworn statement's existence and date and two boxes, the review, the scope decision, the paper read into a hearing, and the four-month request that no office could answer; it states that the sheet is named and not produced and not asked for; and it closes with eleven lines of things it does not ask for, the last of which is *it does not ask this court to find a person*. **Pell Vey does not argue the merits and wins the caption**: the referral must be brought in the name of the Mosswake Roll Court, with Marek as a party and not a claimant and not at the top of the document, on the ground that a document with a clerk's name at the top of it is a document about the clerk. He signs — the fourth document in five months — and asks for nothing, and does not ask about the yard, and Marek notices that he did not ask. The referral goes down the river certified at the Mosswake window on the twenty-fourth. **Tamsin Rook tells him about the drawer**: four envelopes addressed to a lane in Emberwatch with no numbers, written in the fourth week of the first month, never sent; and she puts a choice in writing with a date on it — three go on the third of the sixth, the fourth does not, because she does not have four women's names and will not take that from the woman who said nobody has to know which one. They answer in a room, in words, with a date, as their rule requires.

### Chapter 0043 — Twenty-Three Doors and a Man Who Already Knew

Nell's POV, the Greyfen dyers' lane and a room in the next province, the twenty-fifth to the twenty-ninth of the fifth month. The fourth question asked about a **guild levy** finds a different answer than it found at Millbrook Reach: the unnamed woman who goes round with the quarter's sheet says yes first, before being asked the second half — she is paid four shillings a day to be in a room with a sheet, is paid by the quarter, and would like it written down that she read it in all eleven houses including the seven that did not sign, which is not in her wage either. At the eleventh door a woman of fifty-two reads all four back unprompted and says she has a paper of her own with nobody to ask it of; Nell tells her to ask her own boy first and to say *the lane* if he asks who told her to. **Mike Caudle has known since the fifteenth of the fourth that his name is in a box and has said nothing for a month**, and the reason is not dignity: Nery knew on the sixteenth and did not tell him because she did not want to be the house that got a man in a good coat to come and be told things by her husband. The guild's second instrument does not require anybody to ask him, and nobody is going to. Nell's whole offer is four words — read the line out before he puts his thumb on it. The Nine Elms are told what the levy actually buys: a fund holding four pounds eleven and fourpence that has never been empty, and an admission that the next four houses will be the ones paying. **The professional copyist on the Greyfen road has charged for four hundred and eleven sheets and has a waiting list**, and says she has gone from a woman with anger and a window to a woman with a queue, and that somebody else's anger went out of a door and became her week. On the twenty-ninth Nell rides the cart sixty miles, unannounced, in daylight, with no paper in, and asks the next province's assessor a question nobody has asked: whether a girl of nineteen on a bench since Michaelmas is a person in the association's book or the person who holds. The answer is that the book has eleven houses and no name column and never has, that the assessor will not put one in, and that the guild's book has the girl's name on it and is out of reach of both of them. Nine lines go in Nell's own book and go nowhere near a court. She is home at the tenth hour with a hand that will not open.

### Chapter 0044 — A Number Is About the Book

Tamsin's POV, Auremar, the passage behind the third gallery and the Forms Received rack, the fourth of the sixth to the tenth. The Craft Assessors' Guild's function answers the lane in **two lines**: it issues no notice of a person who holds, and a document entered in a book carries the number of its entry in the book, and the book in this case is the Greyfen dyers' schedule. **A number on a document is a fact about the book the document was entered in and not a fact about the document.** Tamsin copies three index cards standing up, without removing anything from the rack, and the third card is the series the notice was claimed to belong to: **AS/2, a craft assessors' instruction on entering a second instrument, second year of issue, being the third year of the sixth year back, run four hundred** — four years and four months older than the Venn correction, about tool debts in a dyers' trade, and the reason the box headed *the person who holds* exists. **The Caudle notice is not from the Venn series, carries no substituted wording and no public-necessity sentence, and nothing in the rack connects any two documents**; the assessor holds the lane book and does not know who filled the box, because an assessor's office does not keep a hand.
