Tamsin files it in a copyist's own book with no conclusion attached, sends a copy to the lane for the stone and a copy to Marl Crenn marked *read by nobody but you*, and **sends nothing to Marek**, with the reason written down: a number and a clause in one sheet is read by two people as a story about a dragon, and because she is becoming a person whose findings have her name on them and the lane's four questions have nobody's name on them, which is the thing she has given up, one document at a time, in a room with a stove in it. **Marl Crenn reads her own association's instruction for the first time in eleven years** and writes to two of her eleven houses and not the other nine, for a reason she wrote down in a book of her own in the third month, and sends four lines back, of which the last is *put it in your book with the date on it*. Tamsin posts three of the four envelopes on the fifth of the sixth, not on the third, because a thing done on the day it was decided is not a rule. On the nineteenth of the sixth the answer comes back: the second leaf of the Combination's own book, copied out in a schoolroom hand, with a second sheet listing **eleven women in four houses who had each written to their assessor and not to the lane**. The fourth envelope stays in the drawer.

### Chapter 0045 — Sixty Yeses and Four That Are Worth a District

Nell's POV, the Venn lane, the reading table, and the Mosswake Roll Court window, the ninth of the sixth. The board has two new entries: Coll Fenner has taken the four questions off the board and is taking them down the water, and the woman from the seed barns, who is seventy-one, has left a four-month-old line saying she read one out and did not tell anybody what to do about it. **The guild's finding on the levy is read in full — three and a quarter pages, against the four signing houses, because they signed** — with the Caudle's two pounds five and a quarter and the farthing read out twice, and Roke says the other half out loud in front of ninety people: thirty-six pounds a year off four houses, and in about eleven months the next four houses are the ones who will be out. Then the assessor of the eleven houses comes sixty miles in a cart with one of her eleven, and the **fourth answer is *nobody read it to me*** — the one of the eleven nobody had asked at all — and Nell says out loud before reading it that she is not going to say whether it is the right answer, and afterwards says the three things that matter, the last of which is that eleven women answered a question and the truth is that one of them was not asked. **The fourth question is then put in the open at the trestle for two and a half hours**: a hundred and four people in the lane, seventy-four asked, thirty not asked because the asking did not go round, and she puts that number on the board herself. Sixty say *yes, I am paid to read them, and no, I am not paid for the answer*; ten say *I do not know*; four say *nobody read it to me*. Three of the four give a name and put it on the board themselves, the fourth man says no twice and asks for that to be written down, and Nell's own chalk line under them is the first entry anybody has ever asked for. She then says the thing the board has never said: the questions are hers only in the sense that she is the person who gives them away, and anybody may take them off the board, and two already have. At the court window, for a shilling, Sarn reads out the decision of the twenty-first of the fifth from the board — the third finding twice — and shows her a second sheet: **Tavi Venn **was examined for canal work on the twenty-sixth of the fifth, by Odile Fenn, and passed**, and Sayer's head is on a fee paid seven weeks earlier, on the sixth of the fourth, and Nell reads the sheet twice and says nothing, because there is nothing she can do for a girl with a certificate. The chapter ends with a letter from a furnace town in a schoolroom hand that is not a question: four signatures, four lines, the same four lines four times, each saying the one man who reads is paid by the combine and there is one of him in four hundred and forty miles.

### Chapter 0046 — A List of Offices

Marek's POV, Auremar and the Cinder Court at the Crown Kiln, room four, the seventeenth of the sixth. The registrar reads the caption twice and then has to solve a problem nobody in Auremar has ever had to solve: **a seat is not a party, a device is not a person, and there is a form for it — an entry by device, used twice in a decade, whose operative line is that no inference may be drawn from the fact that a person has not been named.** He reads the whole of it aloud, seventeen minutes, and says the working rule of the compact is that rule, and that it works on a record that is nothing but names: nineteen of them, six in the room, none at the top of anything. The nine paragraphs, the roll-keeper's four lines, and the whole MC/FS series are not before the court, and Marek says so three times. Of four questions put, three are refused in a form with a name at the foot of it, and the fourth is taken: whether the sentence as printed in the amendment sheet of the twenty-second of the ninth may be the recorded reason for a decision in any district without a hearing of the person it names. The statement in scale form is read into the court **as a document and not cited**, on the registrar's distinction that the rule is against being put to the same question twice in another matter and this is the same matter and the party is not before the court, has not been cited, and is not examinable. **Then the registrar reads the four months of the record himself and stops once**, and puts a registrar's note in the minute: **a pattern of five offices, no two within four hundred miles, each of which did exactly what it was required to do** — a district water office's general memorandum, a provincial court that entered a correction, a Chancery standing office that issued a numbered sheet ten days after a sentence was stated, a landing office that stamped a sheet in four seconds, and a dyers' combination with one door. Four cannot do harm again; the third can do it tomorrow and there is nobody in it. **Four orders.** (1) No guarantor duty shall attach to any person who has not been heard in a room and has not signed, binding this court and any court taking its recognition, not reaching back, and the Mosswake decision of the twenty-first of the fifth standing because it was made in a room. (2) The sentence is recorded as **model wording of general application and not a decision of any court**, and may not be the recorded reason for a decision in any district without a hearing of the person it names; the record of a document is not a record of a law. (3) Corvin Dray's seal certificates are **suspended** on the registrar's own authority without argument, with sixty-one certificates, ninety-eight sequences, twenty-two in Reed Cut, six in the Venn file, one in the Dunn file, and the counting-house mark — a filled square, a bar, a notch out of the top left corner — against thirty-eight lines in three districts, entered **as a mark and not as a name**. (4) **The sealed First Seat instruction is escalated to a full sitting with jurisdiction on the court's own motion, over Marek's objection, which he raises and loses** — and his objection is that a thing on a list with a date and nobody's name on it is a date and not a review, which Sela Vint calls the best thing said in that room in a year, enters, and overrules. **The court states, with S. Vint's name at the foot of it, that it does not know and cannot compel** who authorised the instruction. Seryn Oris is not named, is not present, and is credited with nothing; the fire-and-water device is referred to as a device and was first named aloud in open court on the twelfth of the ninth month. Tamsin tells him on the stair that he argued it badly, that the registrar did his work in a minute, and that what he has is a list of offices, which is worse and is the only answer there is. A man in a good coat asks at a Crown Kiln counter when the minute goes on the ordinary distribution, and gets the whole of it in nine minutes.

### Chapter 0047 — One Nail and a Shilling a Copy

Ivet Sarn's POV, the Mosswake Roll Court window and the embankment, the twenty-ninth of the sixth to the ninth of the seventh. The record comes down the river in **forty-one pages in eleven days**, because a party gets the record; the **six weeks is the ordinary distribution** to forty-six addresses with a form of words on the front and an instruction to nobody, and it is not addressed to Mosswake. Sarn had ruled a line off for it **on the fourth of the sixth month, before anybody thought of asking her to**, and she puts the record under the nine feet of the trial's accounting at the fifth hour, in her own hand, with a note that a copy is a shilling and that the third order is the shortest and will not be understood. A man in a good coat reads all three things on the wall before he reaches the sill, buys a copy of the first page, asks whether the district intends to take any step, and is told the district has not decided, that thirty-four names are on the hearing slate, that four of them said the words *I do not understand this* in a room and are entered as having said them, and that a man in Greyfen may or may not have a lawyer. Then he buys the wall, which is a shilling. She writes **what the district has to do about it, which is almost nothing**, in seven numbered paragraphs: the first order is now true in forty-five other places and this court claims none of it; the sentence is not withdrawn here because only that court can withdraw its own practice; the process may be run again and has not been decided either way; thirty-four names are on the hearing slate and four of them did not understand the paper in front of them; a creditor in Greyfen has a lawyer and a served paper and a sale in a book at Coldsfoot that is right; she has not opened a window in eleven weeks and nobody's job is to open one; and the seventh is in the first person about herself, and is the sentence on the wall she calls the only inheritance she has ever been left. The **copies go out to named people and not to officials**: the Greyfen copyist at four pence with the twelfth free and her own sentence on the back; the two men off the Reed Cut bank, who now have nine; the hooded seed-barn woman; a lamp-trimmer who did not want it; **Iselle Oster, who puts forty-one pages into a book that says nothing in it is evidence** and writes the order in her own words as *no person may be put in for something they did not hear and did not sign*; two sheets four hundred and forty miles to Emberwatch; and a woman in a dyers' lane who gives eleven away and is asked to stop by a man with a tray and does not. **The paper in her own drawer** is a copy of the entry of the fourth of the tenth month about Rennie Sallow's seed, and he has never been told he can buy it, and she has decided she will carry it up the upper lane herself on the fourteenth at the seventh hour and say four words and not go in. The last line on the wall is in the first person: she would rather be frightening and indispensable at a window with a nail in it than tidy and ignored, and that sentence is the only inheritance she has ever been left.

### Chapter 0048 — Twenty-Nine, and the Woman at the Chandler's Counter

Tamsin's POV, Auremar and Candlewright Lane, the second to the fifteenth of the seventh. A distributed record is not a controlled record: of forty-one names, **twenty-nine say yes to keeping a copy of the Cinder Court's record and twelve say no**, and the twelve are the finding. Four have a creditor in the house, three cannot read well enough to know what they are agreeing to, two have a damp room, one burned it, one said that keeping a copy of a decision is a thing you do if you are the sort of person who is going to be proved right and that she has eleven hundred pounds of debt and four children and is not that sort of person, and that this is a fair position. Separately, and not among the twenty-nine, **a counting house on a row by the Kiln sheds took the record as a packet on the sixth of the seventh because a counting house takes every packet, and still had it on the counter, unused, unsold and unshown, nine days later on the fifteenth** — the most frightening answer there is, and the mark on its sign is now also in a public minute book. **Bryn Mawer, seventeen**, not on the bench, not protected, a line in a file and no wages, is copying in that building and has written asking to be examined in the copying again, in her own words, and not recorded as frightened. She gives the reason: **a copy of a decision is a weapon and it is a target**, the households that kept it are the households that can be shown to have had notice, and in four months a man with a tray will go down a lane asking who has got a copy, and the answer will be her and she is seventeen with no trade and no assessment and nobody who will stand in a room with her. She is paid a penny a sheet at a chandler's counter in Candlewright Lane, and Tamsin buys a copy there and pays a fee. **The plea of the eighteenth of the ninth month of last year is read back off the record and is not refused again**; the trial is set for the **second month of next year at the second hour in the chamber of the Auremar district court**, with four things: no release on his own recognisance, no paper except the court's own with every sheet accounted for at the door, no speaking to any of the eleven protected people except through an officer, and the trial confined to the local forgery charges, his own ledger, and the thirty-eight lines in three districts, with no count arising out of the sealed instruction. He names no client and the mark goes in as a mark. **Tamsin hands the list over**: four pages, one each to **Bryn Mawer at the chandler's counter, to Sarn, to Sayer and to Nell** — a copyist with a shop and a price, a clerk who keeps two registers, a foreman who keeps a works book, and the only one of the four who is not a clerk. The four pages **disagree in one place and the disagreement is written down on all four and nobody tidies it**: Sarn has the four first names out of Emberwatch, Tamsin's own entry is still *Ferren, fourth past the weigh-house, and four women whose first names I have not got*, and Bryn Mawer's page carries a name Tamsin will never have, from the woman with four children and no candle, who did not answer a letter from a Chancery in eight months and answered a shop window and paid four pence for a sheet. The Greyfen road copyist is **not** a holder of the list; she is the reason the name on Bryn's page exists. The rule of the twenty-fourth of the twelfth goes on all four pages: **a list of who holds a copy is not a list of people who have reported.** The sixth column is still empty and the reason is written down.

### Chapter 0049 — Nine Days in a Furnace Town, and the Fifth Question

Nell's POV, the Greyfen lane and Emberwatch, the twenty-second of the seventh to the seventh of the eighth. The coach fare is eleven shillings, the whole of a month's wages, and it has been in a cloth in a drawer since the second week of the second month because a person who has decided in February to go in July cannot say so out loud in a room with her brother in it. She leaves two lines under the stone on the trestle, one of which is that the Caudles' quarter is due on the eighth and she will not have it before them. **Bettin Rahl does not open the door.** He asks her the fourth question from the step before he will let her up, gets the answer in about a second, and gives her the whole of it, including the sentence that the rule is there so that a house has a witness and he is the only witness in four hundred and forty miles and he is not going to be turned into a stranger's errand boy by a lane he has never visited. She asks for **nine days and no advice** and gets a chair from the second bell to the sixth. She reads out two hundred and some papers belonging to about forty houses; eleven are in the model form, nine have a house in the box and **two have a person in it**, in the same trade, signed the same week, never spoken about it. The twenty-two-year-old says she asked the man who brought it and he said the box takes whoever is on the bench, and there is no line under the box saying he is not in it, and she wants to say it out loud in a room with somebody in it and there has been no room in this trade for thirty-one years and the reason is a good rule. **The four have not been idle**: they have written to two other trades, one has written back, the second trade asked a question the first had not thought of, and by the time Nell arrives a thing four women started in a kitchen in April is in **four buildings in three districts, and nobody put it there.** The finding is nine lines long: the thing that travels is not a form and not a question and not a ruling, it is **a person who turns up**; a trade with one door will find out in about a year that it has four doors; it will cost nothing and leave no series and no run and no record, and in nine years a woman in that town will not be able to say which door her visitor opened, and the answer will be that she does not know either. **The fifth question is what a house does when the assessor says no**, and nobody has asked it in eight months. Rahl has been waiting for it for thirty-one years, says in one sentence that the answer is that it does nothing, and that that is why there has been no dispute in forty years and also why four women have been sitting on copies for nine months. Nell tells him not to let a woman in a lane into his trade, gives the reasons, and tells him she would give four pounds of her own money for a second door and has not four pounds, and that is when he asks her name. He writes **Nell Kest on the second leaf of the Combination's own book, under the thirty-one-year-old rule, with the fifth question in full and his own answer that there is none** — and the rule stays in force, and the second rule is the one people will read. She comes home on the seventh of the eighth with a page of a book and nothing else she can carry.

### Chapter 0050 — A Bundle With a Thread In It

Marek's POV, Auremar, the third gallery and the Received Room, the twelfth to the twenty-second of the eighth. The file cannot be closed and the correct end to his time at the Inheritance Desk is **a transfer with a date on it**: the Registrar names **Halvard Riet**, nine years at the Desk, who asked for the Venn box and not the Kest one and got the Kest one as well, and Marek did not choose him and had no standing to object. The handover list is six items in his own hand with no heading, and item six is the second sealed page: **the second recorded decision not to open it**, with the reason that a second reader would make the woman who swore a statement in a form made once examinable about it, and a line saying the seal stays on. The **Kest property-and-debt record is formally reopened by the Registrar at the counter and not cleared**, which means any person at the counter may now buy a shilling's copy and read the property, the debt, and the four openings and closings since the fire, and think what they like about a woman whose name is a **redaction in the public roll of the year of the fire and a blank in a Chancery roll in Greyfen** — and the young clerk of the intake desk writes the time in ink and initialled it for the fourth time in a year and has never been thanked. **All five terms of the ruling are set out on the page and posted in two places** on the fifteenth, one sheet with all five and the date, in the order they were ruled: the reading steps of the Roll Hall in Auremar, and the board at the Mosswake window under a space Sarn ruled off on the fourth of the sixth, a shilling a copy, with one line in **Sarn's own hand** saying the second term ran to the nineteenth of the second month, that the day passed with nothing decided, and that Aldren Venn's file cannot be made complete and is not going to be. He moves into the **Received Room**, which is where things go when nobody wants to be the one who has them, and looks at the shelf, and finds a bundle marked *Ashfall branch, uncollated, received the ninth month of the year twelve years back, damaged, four items, uncollated*, scorched on one edge and gone over badly by a hand that was not thinking about it, never opened, because a shelf is a shelf and eleven first clerks of the year have looked at it. **A red thread runs through the collated edge of the wrapper and through the leaf beneath it**, and the leaf beneath it is not from this year, and it is a **working stitch** — a copyist's way of marking a passage in a bundle — and a way of marking a page is a person working and not a person plotting, and it does not tell him who was right. He does not open it, writes down only what he can see, and invokes the rule of the eighteenth of the ninth month about not putting two red facts in one sentence; and he does not write to Nevin Trost, and says why. He writes to **Nell for the first time since the twenty-ninth of the first month** — four lines, about the levy and Mike Caudle's four words and not about the ninth month — and records that the not writing was a decision, and receives no answer inside the volume, which is not a cliffhanger. The chapter ends on the **roll-call**: twenty names, in his own hand, of the people who will stand in a room under their own name and what it costs each of them, ending in the four women of a furnace town with four first names in four other hands and a question with no answer written under it, and one line at the foot: **there is not one line of it that says I was right.**

## Volume 02, Batch 0001 (Chapters 51–60) — The Inquiry, and the Bridge Nobody Asked For

### Chapter 0051 — Nobody Can Get At You In Here

Marek's POV, the Received Room on the second floor of the Roll Hall, the first week of the ninth month. The room has a **bar on the inside of the frame** that is not a lock, a cold stove, a table, and a window that looks at the wall of the light well, and the intake clerk tells him in the first ten minutes that the bar goes on at the fourth hour of the evening and that nobody can get at a man in here — which is the first kind thing anybody has said to Marek Kest in eleven months, and he thanks the clerk, who has written the time in the margin in ink and initialled it four times for the same man and says so, and who wrote the fourth at the counter on the twelfth of the eighth when the Registrar reopened a file and initialled it anyway because it did not need a witness that day. He counts the shelf: nine bundles, a loose sheaf, and four loose things — a slate with a chalk line and nothing else, a man's coat with nine things written inside the pocket and no name, nineteen return marks tied in string in three hands, and a tray of letters under a card reading *answered, please*. **The room is where a decision goes when the person who made it does not want to have made it**, and he has been moved into a chair that is the reason a document cannot be got at by asking. The **Ashfall bundle is on the second shelf, four feet from his own table**, and he writes about it again on his first morning, in a form he can read at the seventh hour on any morning for a year, because a man who has decided once not to look at a thing has to have a reason every morning or the decision is a mood — and this time the reason is not the rule about two red facts but about himself: if he ever opens it, it is opened by a person with no file, on a day with no argument in it, in a room with two other people in it, and he does not have such a day. On the ninth of the ninth the answer to eleven months of waiting arrives: **the request and its supplement are received and consolidated as a limited administrative inquiry into the filing practice of the district of Mosswake**, to be conducted by the applicant in his own time at his own wage, with **no power to compel any document, office, witness or return** and no power to touch any series, standing instruction or return. It is signed **Nevin Trost, examiner**, with four lines under it saying he was told to put his name to it, that a decision with nobody's name at the foot is worth a fifth of one with a name, and that he did not want to write it. Marek accepts it the same afternoon in one paragraph, asking for two cheap things — the scope fixed on the page, and the name of the person conducting it — and telling Trost in writing that he has his answer to the question he put to the Registrar on the eighteenth of the ninth of last year, which was whether that office has a person in it. He is then handed a four-line form with two boxes, required of the person holding the public copies during an inquiry. No magic, no reading, and one paragraph accepted rather than granted.

### Chapter 0052 — None, in the Second Box

Tamsin's POV, the copying alcove, the Docket of Powers' corridor, and her own fourth page, the second and third weeks of the ninth month. The form has four lines and two boxes and the second is headed **the office of the receiving person, if any**; the rule behind it is that an office holding the public copies is an office that can be asked what it did with them, and a person holding them can be managed, and the alternative to a name is not nothing but a woman in an alcove who cannot be managed. She writes **None** in the second box, signs, dates it the eleventh, and takes the forty-one pages, and counts them twice. **The four pages of the list come back the same morning**, separately, in four hands, three of which disagree with hers, and the one addition is on Bryn Mawer's chandler's-counter page: **S. Roke, dyers' lane, Greyfen**, who said the other half of the levy finding out loud in the lane in the sixth month, has been asked twice since about a copy for her house, has said no twice, and wants that written on the page. Sarn's four Emberwatch first names and Tamsin's own *Ferren, and four women whose first names I have not got* are both still right, the disagreement is still on all four pages, and she writes a third time that it is **a mess with a rule in it, and a mess with a rule in it can be read and a mess without one cannot**. The **answer from the district of Glasswater** comes on the same packet, five months late, complete and signed: the standing instructions say nothing about itemising; this court has never had a flood-season continuity filing entered in it, not one in six years; and the itemised filing of the tenth month that is in a provincial record was entered in a **river court of the same district, which is a different office from this one**, and this court has never seen it. So the third province of a three-province pattern is a district the thing never reached, and Tamsin records in eight lines that the empire is mostly full of districts where the form never came and the harm is where it did, and **does not send it to Marek**, and writes down that she did not send it on purpose and why. Then she goes to the Docket of Powers, because it has written to tell her that the person holding the public copies has a proper interest in the state of the register. The register is a bound book, one page to a year, four lines to an entry, about four hundred and ten entries in thirty years — about fourteen a year, because a reading needs a permission and only happens when there is no other way at the record. **Two gaps in this year's page, filled in by her in her own hand, with the dates, the places, the permissions, and a paragraph saying she entered them on her own initiative, that she has not asked the person named, and that this is not a finding that either reading was authorised.** She waits forty minutes in a corridor for a countersignature, and the master of the Docket tells her the book is a good book and a bad register and that in twenty-two years he has seen it done twice, and once it was him. She tells Marek at the twelfth hour — late on purpose, per the rule they made — that she has broken the first half of it, that he will want to argue with one of the two halves of what she says, and that she will not help him pick. And she tells him the sentence she has wanted to say since the seventeenth of the sixth month of last year: **the forty-one pages are in the alcove, the list is in a chest under a winter coat, the independent copy exists, was not produced, and the Docket has no power — and nobody in this book can reach a private book.**

### Chapter 0053 — The Press, and What a Clerk Never Looks At

Ivet Sarn's POV, the Mosswake window, the embankment, and the old records room, the fourth week of the ninth to the first week of the tenth. A man from the works comes about a **heating flue booked for the fourteenth of the second month**, and the third flagstone is where the press stands and the fourth is the drain, and he reads the whole of her wall standing up in about eleven minutes, including the seventh paragraph about herself, and asks the question Sarn has been waiting nine months for: *what is a bar.* She does not answer it first. She tells him where the sixty households are. **They are not in a register, and the reason is the answer**: a register of them would have been a record of the court doing it, and there is no column in this building for *a duty entered with nobody heard*. The sentence was applied **in letters**, letters go out on a **quarterly cycle**, and a cycle files its copies and presses its returns, and **no clerk in nine years has ever gone and looked at an outgoing rack.** The records room has a cold floor, a press, and a window that does not open. She takes a day and the young clerk from the intake desk, who comes down on a rider at his own cost and writes the time in the margin in ink for the fifth time. Three bundles, not four. Each press-mark is a word in a corner and an initial; **fifty-four filed copies for three cycles: thirty-one carry a name and a door, eleven carry a word and no name — *left* four times, *gone* three, *no such* twice, and twice two letters and a blot — and twelve came back with the post stamp and nothing else.** Twenty-two of the thirty-one are marked *seen* and nine are marked *told*. **The fourth cycle of the sixth year was never made**, and the retired third clerk of that year, sixty-one, with a child and eleven weeks of docket left and no replacement until the docket closed, says in her kitchen that nobody told her not to make it and that she has gone over those eleven years four hundred times and found no moment in which anybody did anything to her. So **about sixty is fifty-four plus six**, and the six are the ones a clerk did not have time to copy. Sarn enters the thirty-one in the register of pending duties in her own hand, and then takes the chalk and writes at the head of the block: **THIRTY-ONE OF ABOUT SIXTY. Eighteen returns did not come back. Six copies were never made.** And underneath, small, where a person has to come close: *nine years and I have never once gone and looked at an outgoing rack, and that is not the district's fault, that is mine.* The man from the works reads it again and says somebody should come with a bar, and she says somebody should, and the board that carries the number that will not come back is the same board as the thirty-one who might, because a board that only carries the good news is a notice.

### Chapter 0054 — The Present Holder

Hester Crane's POV, Lowcross and the Weir Cut in Auremar's lower district, the second and third weeks of the tenth and the first weeks of the eleventh. The bank has gone and the works bill is **nineteen pounds three and fourpence** against a fund holding **six pounds and eightpence**, and the figure has not moved since the third year except with the timber. Her maintenance request of the eighth of the eighth went into a box on a counter and came back nine weeks later on the reverse of it with a note: the parish box in the works' book was dissolved in the sixth year and **the column wants a body**, and the works will hear the application and then decide whether the crossing is a work of the district or of a holder, and that decision has not been made, **and the form is returned because the form cannot be entered and not because the work is refused.** On the ninth of the ninth a young works clerk, **Jonas Cray**, came out into her yard and would not put her name in a column that wanted a body, and put his finger on a box headed **the present holder**, and she read the three words at arm's length and said *that is me, then*, and signed, in front of a girl of eleven at the gate. She has signed one instrument in sixty-one years and wants that on the record in the words *he did not offer to read it to me and I did not ask*. The returned note is read aloud to her at her own table by **Rosamund Crane, eleven, who can read**, unasked, and the girl stops on the fourth line and asks whose name it is, and works out in about four seconds that the line names two and one of them is not a person, and that *or* does not say who decides — which is the second of the four questions asked by a child, of a line of a works note, and the only document in the matter that was true and that nobody had asked for. Then the walk, dated. **A works return of the fourth month of the sixth year has *the present holder* in a box headed holder of the crossing**, filled in by a clerk in a room over a cobbled yard with a hundred and forty returns to do that month, in a form that is not in any series and in an office that is not on the forty-six, **a year before the Sallow file and six years and five months before the sheet of the twenty-second of the ninth** — a word that walked, written by a person who thought it was the law, into a total that was right. Four hundred and eleven people are on the far side and the district's return says three hundred and eighty-eight crossings in four weeks, and nineteen pounds three and fourpence would do the bank, the abutment and eleven feet of kerb, and six pounds and eightpence will do the kerb and not the bank, and the bank is what goes. She is not frightened of a box. She is frightened of the fourth month of next year and of being sixty-two.

### Chapter 0055 — The Register That Is Sixty Years Old

Marek's POV, the Docket of Powers, three rooms off the corridor behind the third gallery, the third week of the eleventh. The Docket has no sign on the door and a card in the window, has kept a register for twenty-two years under a master, **Oswin Marrow**, who was not a lawyer and has never been a clerk and has never once had to enforce anything. The register is one page to a year, four lines to an entry, about four hundred and ten entries in thirty years — fourteen a year, because a reading needs a permission and only happens when there is no other way at the record. **The finding is that Marek performed two scale readings, on the twelfth of the ninth month of last year at the Mosswake rehearing and on the sixth of the fourth month of this year alone on a step at Reed Cut, both under the same undischarged limited audit, and that neither is entered, and that a reading by a person not entered is an unauthorised scale reading.** It is correct in every particular. The Docket has no power to punish and uses all three of its powers on the page: **enters the finding in a public register; bars him from any audit in any jurisdiction until examined; and refers to the Cinder Court in room four the single question whether a reading so performed was of any legal effect whatever.** It is not about falsity, and the reading is not void. Marrow has asked Marek's office twice how a clerk gets entered in the book and been told there is no procedure. Marek reads the finding, finds it correct, and learns that Tamsin entered both dates in the register on the fourteenth of the ninth, in her own hand, with a paragraph saying she did it on her own initiative and that it is not a finding that either was authorised. Marrow tells him what his copyist bought with it and what it will cost her, and then tells him a thing about himself in the nineteenth year that he has never told anybody. **Marek does not contest the entry and contests the referral**, in one page, on the ground that a finding that the readings were of no effect would not touch him but would touch the only record in the empire in which a girl of fifteen is entered as heard and a household is entered as asked, and a person erased from a record is a person nobody can call. He asks instead that the question be reframed — whether a record in which such a reading was entered is altered in its effect by a finding that the reading was unauthorised — and asks to be examined on a date, before two persons **he names**, and asks that the date not move and that he pay the room's fee. Marrow refuses him the first, countersigns the reframing onto his own finding, gives him **the eleventh of the first month, at the fourth hour, in the Docket's room**, and names his own two persons instead — **the intake clerk of the Chancery and a copyist of the third gallery** — because a man who is being examined should not be examined in somebody else's room. He walks past the Forms Received rack without stopping, for the first time in four years, and writes that down with the date.

### Chapter 0056 — What a House Does, and What It Costs in Pounds

Nell's POV, the dyers' lane, the vat flat, the trestle and the weigh-house, the fourth week of the eleventh. The guild's finding comes back on the fourth, three and a quarter pages, four houses, the Caudles first because they signed first, two pounds five and a quarter with a farthing over that was Nery Caudle's own money. She reads all of it at the trestle and then reads Roke's other half, because she promised in the sixth month that she would stand there while it was read: it is the fifth of the eleventh, Roke said eleven months, the next four houses are the ones who will be out, and the Nine Elms has a boy on a term. **She puts the four houses' children in the room**, nine of them between nine and nineteen, because the number is a wage and a number nobody has heard of gets made true by whoever is standing in the room when it is read out; one of the nine is a girl of seventeen who has a fortnight to have a different opinion about leaving. **The fifth question has an answer in Greyfen and it is not a door**: what a house does when the assessor says no is that it moves — four pounds ten each way, or eleven if you go on your own, two days lost at four shillings a day, a bench bond of eleven pounds that a house finds or gets a man to sign, and four years before a return without a second bond. One house went to Thornfen eleven months ago; eleven did not stay, not because it was asked, and they were not kept in a line because the rule is thirty-one years old and the *person* is the door. The woman at the far end of the vat gives the numbers and gives no name and asks for it written down that she gave the numbers and not the name. **At the eleventh door, Ottoline Bray, fifty-two, has a paper about her seventeen-year-old son**: the first question is yes, and there is work, and he is on the top bench and is the fastest and wants it; the second is *his own name, in his own hand, spelled right*; the third, asked in the kitchen in the eighth month with the cloth on the table, came back *if I stop, nobody answers for it, it just stops* and then *then it is mine while I am on it, and it is nobody's after, and that is the whole of what a mill is*; and the fourth — **he read it to her himself**, and said it meant a week's notice and that his week's notice is four days, and that there is nothing in this about after. It is the only paper in eleven months the four questions have made better, and she says so out loud, and then says the other half about her own cloth of eleven shillings and the four envelopes she kept for seven months. The Greyfen copyist has **nine names on a waiting list, four of them trades**, and one is a woman who came sixty miles to Auremar to ask four questions at a counter, was not in, waited two days, and left them on the step with her own name on it, and it is in a drawer. Nell writes a fifth question of her own — *and what does it cost, in pounds, to leave, and who has paid it* — and posts it to four trades. She then writes to her brother for the first time since the twenty-ninth of the first month of last year: four lines about the levy, the fifth question, and the board at four hundred and thirty miles, and **she has not forgiven the ninth month and is not going to, and will say it to his face in about four hours.**

### Chapter 0057 — The Hearing That Was Right

Marek's POV, the district court of Auremar's lower district, the second of the twelfth. It is a continuance application, not an application for money, because nobody in the room has any money. The works cannot be compelled and sent two men: **Gideon Harrow**, about fifty, with a schedule in a leather case and a warding tick at the end of every sentence, and Jonas Cray. The household sent **Silas Sowle**, a solicitor of about fifty-five who has done this district's poor business for nineteen years and says so unasked. Hester Crane is in the room because Sowle tells her a person who is not in the room is a person who has not been heard. **The district files the most honest paper any office in this district has produced in two years**: it has not withdrawn its own practice and cannot, it is aware of a high court's order and is not bound by it, it will apply the law as it stands, and **it will therefore do nothing in this matter because there is nothing in it that the district may do** — and the magistrate says out loud that this is the first of nine files in nine years where an office has told her in writing that it is powerless, and that the district has gained nothing by it. He asks whether the works could certify the crossing for the fortnight before the fourteenth and is told no, and the reason is that **a bar goes up about two days before anybody plans it** — and nobody in the room, including the magistrate, has thought about the two days. Then Marek makes the mistake: he argues for seven paragraphs, four of them about a printed sentence, and **Silas Sowle stops him with *that is a very good argument and it is about a sentence, and this case is about a bank***, and the magistrate has it read back to him, and he withdraws the submission that it entitles the application to be granted and does not withdraw the observation, and both are in the minute. **The continuance is refused with a date — the fourteenth of the twelfth month — and the refusal is correct**, and in the four minutes after it Marek stops arguing and asks Gideon Harrow one question that is not his and not the court's: has the district works ever asked this district to stand the cost of a crossing. Harrow says no, and says why: an office that writes down that it cannot do a thing has written down that a thing is not being done, and then the district has to answer it, and the district will find a reason to do nothing, and everybody will be satisfied — and *that is precisely why nobody has ever done it.* The magistrate enters it as a question asked and not answered. On the steps Tamsin tells him he argued it for five hours when there was no case, that a solicitor stopped him and was right, that the last thing he said was the best thing in the room and was not addressed to the court — and then gives him the one question he should have asked first, and it is the second of the four questions. And both of them are wrong about the date by two days.

### Chapter 0058 — The Wrong Column

Tamsin's POV, the old records room at the end of the Mosswake embankment corridor, the second of the twelfth. She goes for no court and no office: the court that refused the application refused it correctly and there is nothing in it to look at. She deliberately does not ask Ivet Sarn, and writes down the reason on the train — a district court has no business in a records room, and a clerk of that court standing in a cold room with a press is a thing that will be said afterwards in a room. The young clerk comes down again on a rider at his own cost. The Lowcross correspondence went out in the **third cycle of the sixth year** because maintenance requests go out before the autumn, so the bundle is the second on an oak shelf and is four inches thick and holds **eleven items**, which is the whole private paper life of a dissolved parish and a public bridge for one quarter of one year. She copies three things standing up, nothing removed, shelf and bundle and item on the back of her own hand. **The schedule has eleven lines and every one of them is a name in the first column**: the parish, dissolved in the sixth year, no successor; the works; the court; the trustees, whose appointment died with it; the wharf company; the river navigation body; and **four returns that read *the present holder* with nothing after them, in the right column, since the fourth month of the sixth year.** The eleventh line is Hester Crane, in the **wrong column** — under *persons from whom a return has been requested* — because she answered a form in the eighth month of this year, and a person who has been asked a question is filed under *asked* and not under *liable*. **The file names everybody the building could imagine being liable, and the only living person in it is the one family that can prove the clause was copied onto it.** She then copies the return of the fourth month of the sixth year and records, carefully and at length, that the four appearances of the substituted wording are **four instances of one practice and not four documents in a chain**, that if she wrote them in one sentence in date order a person in nine years would read a conspiracy with a beginning, and that the invented join would be the only part anybody remembered — and the finding is not a seam, it is a column. She sends three copies and **decides the order before she acts**: the household first, in plain words, by a cart in the evening, with four pence of carriage in a slot, his name nowhere on it and a separate sheet for the gate that says *do not read it to her unless she asks, and she will ask*; **Sowle second, who is the household's solicitor and nobody else's and had not asked her name once in an afternoon**, with five words on the front; Sarn third, down the river by the morning packet, behind the other two. And she writes down that she broke the first half of the rule she and Marek made, twice in three months, and why, and that he is right not to trust it. She comes up to the Received Room **at about the eighth hour of that night** with the third copy and puts the four-line custody form on his table with **None** in the second box, and Marek reads it standing up in nine minutes and says only: *you sent it to her first.* **And then he answers the second half of their rule, which he has never done, in words, with a date**: on the **fourth of the first month, at the fourth hour**, in that room, she brings him every sheet that goes out of this matter to a solicitor, a household, or an office outside the Roll Hall, and he reads it in front of her before it goes and gives any reason against one of them out loud and not on a page, and if he ever decides she is wrong again he will ask her for it first, knowing she will say no. **He tells her it is worse than the rule they made, because it is a man who has been caught twice inventing a third one**, and asks her to sign the first line so that he is in the file while there is still nothing in it against him.

### Chapter 0059 — A Duty That Nobody Owes

Hester Crane's POV, Lowcross, the Weir Cut, the far side, the twelfth to the twenty-fourth of the twelfth. **A bar goes across the Lowcross end at about the second hour of the twelfth, by a ladder and a chain, and the crossing had been open for nineteen years and four hours.** It was the day before the market; about ninety people came, sixty stood, thirty went to find out whether the towpath was passable. Rosamund did not go, because she is eleven and goes to the mill on the far side on market days and there is no school on a market day. The **four miles is a mile and three-quarters of towpath and a shoulder of the cut with a rope on it and, in the third month, a plank, a gatepost and a matter of nerve** — and she knows it that well because a granddaughter goes over it twice a week and a son of thirty-four in the works could not reach her if the bank were bad. A clerk compiles a return afterwards because nobody asked and she paid for it: eleven households on the far side carried their own four-day flour and the crossing took the sills out; four mill carts a day went from nine minutes to ninety; about fifty went to chapel and eighteen came and the vicar wrote a line about the fourteen and did not say why; and **Berta Coll, eighty-one, did not come on the second day** and a girl of nineteen from the mill went over on the plank with a sack and came back empty and is **Wyn Aldis** and says she was not brave, she was nineteen and the water was low. **Nine names on the back of a form**, read out and written down: Jonas Cray's ordinary day's order, which she will not count twice; Gideon Harrow three days with a spade, the man who put the crossing out of use, who may read this — **and he does read it, two days before the bridge is carried back, because she wrote on it that he might, and says that in nineteen years nobody at that gate has ever given him a piece of paper with his own work on it, and that he does not know what a man does with that, and will not stand there to be thanked for it, and asks her to go and find somebody to tell him what to do with it**; a chapel man six days; Wyn Aldis three times; a mill man and his wife; **the two men off the Reed Cut bank, who would not give a name, the fifth time somebody has refused her a name and the fifth time it has been right**; a woman with no name at all; and her son, who was not permitted and went four times and is not sorry. On the nineteenth **Jonas Cray comes up the yard without a good coat to write the sentence his office has never written**: that the works cannot stand the cost, that the crossing is a work of the public and not of a holder, and that the office asks the district to stand it. He reads it out, reads it to Rosamund and has her read it after him and writes down that she had, and then reads it out to Hester Crane at arm's length twice, and she asks for it in the copyist's hand and not his, and says that a piece of paper in a file in nine years will be true and will have her words in it and she will not be able to read it. He will come once a month and not in a good coat. Then the arithmetic nobody has ever entered in a column: **nineteen pounds three and fourpence every nine years, which is about two pounds two and a half a year and about a penny a day, against a fund that holds six pounds and eightpence for the quarter and about six pounds on the average of four, and which has been taken out of that fund twice in nineteen years and has lost about a pound a year in between** — too small to be a line in a fund and too large to be a household's money, and it has lived in that gap for nineteen years out of a box. Closing the gap needs four things, none of which is a hearing, and all four are a hundred times harder and about nine years long. The bridge comes back on the twenty-fourth because nine people carried it. **Hester refuses to sign a second instrument** and gives the reason twice, the second time shorter: she is the second woman in four hundred miles a word that walks about has got hold of, the first was fifteen and had somebody in the room, and she has a granddaughter who can read — *I am not going to hand her the next one*. And she gives the copyist one sentence and refuses to have it in a document, because she cannot read a paragraph and it would have her words in it and be unreadable to her in nine years. The sentence is: **you have made it true and I am the one it is true about.**

### Chapter 0060 — A Column of None Named

Ivet Sarn's POV, the Mosswake window, the embankment, and the upper lane, the first to the ninth of the first month. The register of pending duties has five columns — *Date. Household. Duty. Next review. Copy sold* — of which she has filled three about nine hundred times and cannot fill the fourth: **a next review is a date, a date is a thing that happens, and a thing that happens has a person standing at it** — and there is no review. The district has not decided, the Cinder Court's full sitting has no date, restitution is not a word this court can use, and the flue is still booked for the fourteenth of the second month. So she enters the thirty-one over nine days and puts **next review: none named** in the fourth column thirty-one times, which is the most honest thing the book has ever held and reads like a clerk who has not bothered. The first person to come with one of the thirty-one is **Iolanthe Rees, sixty**, who has paid a hundred and forty pounds of ground rent on two strips above the upper lane in twenty years, who came at the third hour so as not to be seen in a queue, and who does not want to complain: she wants to know whether she is going to be called into a room. Sarn says she is not, cannot promise no office or court ever will, writes it down, reads it back twice at the sill, takes a shilling, and says in the entry that the clerk has been wrong four times in twelve months and says so when asked. The second person to arrive at an hour when nobody could see is noted, and goes on the wall as a number. Then the debt: in the seventh month of last year Sarn wrote in her own book, and told nobody, that she would walk up the upper lane on the fourteenth of the seventh and say four words to Rennie Sallow and go — and did not, and has spent six months finding out that the reason was an ordinary day with about four other things in it, every one of them in a book, and a man in a book is a thing that can be done in six months. She puts the broken promise on the wall in the first person with the day on it — *a rule that is written on a wall is a rule, a rule that is broken in private is a preference, and I have had a preference since the fourth of the tenth month and I called it a drawer* — and on the **eighth of the first month at the seventh hour**, on his way home from the works, she walks up the upper lane, does not knock, puts the copy of the line about the Sallows' eleven pounds of seed into his hand, says **from the window, no charge**, tells him his daughter asked a question in open lane and that Sarn has never once told her the line that made it possible, does not go in, and has entered in her own book that she did not go in. The notice for Corvin Dray's trial comes down on the ninth: the second of the second month, the second hour, the chamber of the Auremar district court, with the four things attached read out to a queue and written on the board under the thirty-one — no release on his own recognisance, no paper except the court's own with every sheet accounted for at the door, no speaking to the eleven protected people except through an officer, and the trial confined to the local forgery charges, the ninety-one leaves and the thirty-eight lines in three districts, with no count from the sealed instruction or any office. And a woman of forty-eight from the lower district says: you have a mark in a minute book and a man going to be tried in a fortnight and neither of them has a name in it. Sarn's answer is that she would not take either of them down, and that this is the first sentence in nine years she has written down in advance.

## Volume 02, Batch 0002 (Chapters 61–70) — The Trial, the Mark, and a Supervisor

### Chapter 0061 — A Bar Is a Hole in a Book

Marek's POV, the Docket of Powers' three rooms off the corridor behind the third gallery, the eleventh of the first month at the fourth hour. **Oswin Marrow is in the room and is not an examiner**, and he says in advance that the two people doing it are going to have to make up a form of words because there are no rules for an examination, and that nobody in this building can be got a fair hearing; the stove is lit before Marek arrives. The two examiners are the **intake clerk of the Chancery**, twenty-five, who has written the time in the margin in ink and initialled it for the same man **seven** times and has never been thanked, and **Tamsin Rook**, who entered both of Marek's readings into the Docket's register in her own hand on the fourteenth of the ninth month without asking him. She notices in the first four minutes that the person who put a man in a register is now examining him, writes one line about it in the inside cover of her own book before she asks anything, reads it back, and says it is the practice at Mosswake and she does it in this building too. **The first question is not a question about authority: it is *what is a scale reading*, and nobody has asked Marek that in eleven months.** He answers it badly and then properly — that a reading is not a reading of the paper but of the record the paper has been entered in, that the record does not move — and the clerk writes the second answer down because he does not know he is allowed not to, and notices that the three lines he had on his foolscap are not a coincidence. The second question is hers and it is about the fourth of the fourth month, and she says she has had the register line for four months and has not asked because she did not think it was hers to ask, and he says neither of them gets to call it the proper order. **He describes the second reading out loud for the first time to anybody** — Reed Cut, about the seventh hour, alone, on a step, not asked and not stopped — and says that a person who says *the record carries nothing* in a room is a person who has been told what to think about by a thing that cannot be cross-examined, and that this is the reason he had never said it in a room. The third question is the one that costs anybody anything and the intake clerk asks it because he cannot find a fourth heading: **how does a person get entered in that book.** The procedure is one line — a written application to the Docket stating the authority — and Marek did not make one in the fourth month of last year, and says why, and the reason is the sentence of the chapter: he did not want to be entered, because a person who reads under a limited audit of a file can be asked by anybody holding the file whether he read it fairly. He does not contest the entry, does not ask for the bar lifted, says so twice before he is asked, and asks for two things: that the record be made out in the form used and filed in the Docket's file and **not in the register**, because the register is of persons who read and putting an examination in it would be a lie in the only book anybody trusts; and that a copy go to **four rooms above a cooper's shop near the Weir Cut side** — no office over the door, nothing on the door, no office that can be written to — because he learned from the custody form that `None` is a person who cannot be instructed, and he would rather be hard to find than easy to explain. Marrow countersigns one line specifically: that Marek's account of the second reading **is the account of the person examined and is not a finding of this Docket**. His copy in his own book that night: *a bar is not a punishment, a bar is a line in a book with a gap in it, and the gap has been there since the fourth month of last year, and I did not write one line to it because I did not want to be in a room, and a man who does not want to be in a room is a man who has already decided what is in it for him.*

### Chapter 0062 — Reason Not Known

Halvard Riet's POV, the third gallery and the Inheritance Desk, the nineteenth to the thirtieth of the first month. He has the **six-item handover list in Marek Kest's hand with no heading on it**, and he took the harder box on purpose. **Item three is answered, and he finds out that it was answered four months ago and that the person who answered it is the person the item is about.** He gets it out of a question asked in the ordinary way to the **clerk of the intake desk**, twenty-five and never thanked, who keeps a book of everything that comes to the gallery not addressed to a desk, with the time written down and the initials of whoever takes it; the line is the **eleventh of the ninth month**, one letter out of Glasswater, taken the same morning with **T. Rook** against it — the same packet as the custody form she signed that morning. **There is no paper to find:** she has not mislaid it, it is not on a counter, and it is two galleries away in a drawer, and the only thing in the building that says so is four words and two initials in a column headed *who took it*. He works out that item three was true when Marek wrote it in the eighth month and stopped being true on the eleventh of the ninth, in a building of four hundred people, and that nobody told the desk. He reasons out that a line saying *unanswered* protects a person and a line saying *answered* does not, and that a note against it in his own hand in his own gallery's file would be **a true note about a woman two galleries away, for ever**. So he writes to her in **six lines**, carries it down himself, does not leave it on a counter, **does not ask to see the letter and says in the note that he is not asking to**, and tells her that a line that says a thing is not so is worse than a line that is empty; she makes him read her entry back, and what she writes down is that nobody had ever thought of putting that column to that use and that she was the only person in the building who could have and had not. The real work of the week is the trial copy: the gallery's copy of the ledger has **ninety-one leaves numbered consecutively** and **the wrapper it was made from is endorsed ninety-two** in the copyist's hand of the sixth year, with the word *complete* under the ninety-one and **no figure against it** and in a different ink. He counts twice. He then works out in nine minutes what he may certify: not whether the ledger is complete, but whether **this book** is a true copy. His certificate is a quarter of a page that certifies what he compared and **expressly does not certify the completeness of the copy, and no person has asked him to**; the covering line says nothing in it is in anybody's favour and one part of it is against this gallery. It goes down a day and a half early with the figure on the outside. He reads the **Kest box**, which he took on purpose, and finds that its wrapper carries **four openings and closings in twelve years in four hands and that the third, the closing of the seventh year, has a line ruled for a reason and nothing in it**, and he enters **reason not known** in the day's book against the fourth of the first month, in the form the day's book uses, and says in the entry that he has not asked why, because a clerk who fills in a gap in a reason is a clerk who has decided what the reason was. He adds a **seventh item** to his own copy of the list and copies the six out again, in the author's words and order, on a fresh sheet, **and adds nothing to that copy** — because a preference nobody can improve is the only record of a decision that exists. Under item four, which is a matter that is not his and is his sister's, he writes two lines: *not chased. Not to be chased by me. — H. Riet, the twenty-first of the first month.*

### Chapter 0063 — Two Thirds of a Page

Tamsin's POV, the copying alcove of the third gallery, the last week of the first month to the first of the second. The Cinder Court's answer to the reframed question is two thirds of a page and is **the worst of the two available answers**: the court **will not say the record is altered and will not say it is not altered**, and records, with a name at the foot, that a Docket of Powers keeps a register of persons and not of records, that the finding before it is a finding about a person, and that the court **cannot answer a question about a record without a person to examine**, and that there is no person before it whom it may examine. **The original referral is expressly not answered, not withdrawn, and stands referred.** As to the escalated sitting, the court will not give a date, and gives the reason **in a form**: a date is not to be fixed for a matter in which no party can be named, and when one is fixed it will be fixed **with a name at the foot of it**. She writes out in her own book that the two available answers were yes and no and that either would have been a sentence a person could carry into a room in the fourth month — and that the answer **does not help Lowcross**, because Lowcross is about a person and this is about a record, and there is nobody in the crossing at Lowcross who is a record. She copies it **twice in plain words in a copyist's hand**: one to **Silas Sowle for the household** with four words on the front and no office on it, and one into the Docket of Powers' own file because the Docket referred the question; Marek reads it in front of her and gives one reason against it and she agrees and it goes anyway. A third thing goes down the river in the same packet: a transcription of the Cinder Court's **first** order for Sarn's wall, with five words on the front. The packet also brings the **notice of the heating flue on the fourteenth of the second month** in the Mosswake old records room, and she finds that the fourth cycle of the sixth year is **eighteen letters that were never written**, and offers in writing to **write** them, with the terms stated — not an officer of that court, not paid, may be refused, four days, the word *write* and not *copy* — and she does not notice that the word is *write* until she has written the letter saying so, and that is the finding. The turn of the chapter: **she has been wrong about a thing she could carry twice, four months apart, both times because she wanted a thing she could use, and both times the thing she could not use was the true one, and she starts a list** — *things I have been wrong about, and the day I found out* — with two dated entries, and then writes under them that she is not going to fill the column, because a list with two entries and a heading is worth more in a year than a list with nine entries and no heading. She opens the drawer at the fifth hour and puts her hand on the fourth envelope, which is still there, and shuts it.

### Chapter 0064 — A Question About a Receipt

Brammel's POV, the chamber of the Auremar district court and its public gallery, the second of the second month, from the second hour. He is about sixty-one, forty years in the province, eleven years a client of Corvin Dray's, and he identified the green box under the window as the working box and not the archive box on the day of the inventory, which is the reason a piece of paper with a mark on it was ever in front of a court. He has his four papers, back in a leather case with a clerk's receipt dated the **nineteenth of the tenth month of the ninth year**, which is the return in Chapter 17, and the box has been in his house for about sixteen months. **Inventory item nine was reworded at his request sixteen months ago, at a counter on the eighteenth of the tenth month of the ninth year** and now reads *one green box, four papers, of a client of eleven years* — his name is out of it, and the description is in the fourth column in the same hand as everything else with a space where a person goes, and **his mark is on the wrapper of the inventory**, which is how a box is found in a room of sixty boxes in four seconds. He can give the reason he has done nothing for sixteen months without being asked: the mark is the only way he signs, because he cannot write, and a man who gives up the mark cannot hold a box or sign a receipt or say *this is mine*. He is one of the **eleven protected people** and may not be spoken to except through an officer, so **the one thing he has come for — to be asked a question, so that he is a witness who spoke — is the one thing the order forbids**, because a question is speaking to him and the order is the same order that kept a man in a good coat out of his kitchen. He works it out four times and each time it takes less. **The gallery has nine people in it in nine benches at the fourth hour and the trial is confined to the local forgery charges, and no count in the trial names him or any of the eleven, and there is nothing in the room about him at all.** He asks the clerk at the back whether a person named in an inventory may be called, and is told, kindly and exactly, that a party is a person named in a count, that an inventory name is evidence that a thing was in a room and not evidence of anything about the thing's owner, and that if he called himself the registrar would strike it out kindly and it would be in the minute as a question asked and not answered — and he asks for it to be asked anyway, and it is. Then he thinks about going up and asking whether a person who is not a party can be added to a record on his own initiative, and works out in nine seconds that what he would get is a line in a minute about a man trying to be noticed, and does not go up. **At about the fifth hour, as the court rises, the officer at the door asks him a question**: whether the box is his and whether a leather case came back to his house on or about the nineteenth of the third month, and whether the case was returned to him or to the office. It is about a receipt, and about four inches of leather, and it is the first time anybody has asked Brammel anything in eleven months. He answers it truthfully, tells the officer that he came to be asked a question and has now been asked one, and asks whether that part is being written down, and is told no. Four other of the eleven stand about nine feet apart from each other in the corridor for three hours and nobody says anything, and that is what the order does. The closing finding: **you can be asked a question about a box and be a person, and you cannot be asked a question about a mark, because a mark is not a thing anybody in a court can put to a witness**, and nobody has ever asked him what his mark is.

### Chapter 0065 — Every Sheet Accounted For

The young clerk of the intake desk's POV, unnamed, the door and the chamber of the Auremar district court, the second hour to the seventh. He is the officer at the door because the order says every sheet is to be accounted for at the door by an officer and there is nobody else, and he is on the roster and has never been on a roster. He has a sheet of his own with five columns and **no heading at the top of it**, which he wrote himself in the first week of a job nobody explained to him, and the first lesson of the morning is that a bundle is one figure and not nine, which a registrar taught him in one sentence after a trial about a debt in the second month. He counts the exhibits in in the order they are given and out in the order they are finished with, in two columns, **because the order a thing arrives in is evidence of something**, which a copyist told him at a counter in the first month and which he wrote down and showed to nobody. Forty-one exhibits and a book. Exhibit 12 is a certificate pressed cold and not laid, with a rim, a second impression and a varying pressure, and a notch a sixteenth of an inch across that is the whole of the crime, and it is described into the record in four lines in ninety seconds because a mark cannot be read into a record. Nobody in the room looks at the card; about four hundred people think about it later, and a plate in a drawer in the Kiln sheds will be handed in on the ninth. **A limited plea is put by the court and granted and read back**, and is not absolution and not a pardon: it admits the counts and neither admits nor denies anything touching any person or office not named. The minute of the refusal of the eighteenth of the ninth is read out — four lines, to show the refusal is not made a second time — and the officer counts those four lines as a sheet. At the fourth hour and a half the magistrate puts a question of her own and enters that **no person in this court has asked this man for the name of a client or the name of a house, that this court will not ask him in this matter or any other before it, and that if anybody does it is to be entered and put to her**, with the reason: a man who cannot be got to name a client can be made to say anything by a person who wants him to, and there are people in this matter who want something from him and one of them has a tray. The defendant adds, unasked and not in the minute, that the only person who has ever said anything to him in a room is a man from a district works who put a bar across a bridge two days early. The **mark goes in as a mark** and the finding is a half page: guilty on the local counts, **not guilty of nothing; no other count was before the court and none was tried**, and the court does not know what house keeps the mark and does not repeat that as an excuse. The **sentence is four lines**: three years in the district house with the period from the ninth month credited; the ledger retained and a copy to be made and sent as the court directs; **the court has no power to restore the sixty-one suspended certificates and nobody asked it to**; and he is to be examined on the mark and the practice. Two more lines are added and read twice: **the eleven are not named in any account of the trial**; and **the original of the ninety-one leaves is directed to be produced**, because the certification from a clerk of the third gallery says the copy is short by one leaf and the copy is not that court's to make. He writes his return at the back of a corridor with the corridor going past him: two sides, a heading that is an office, and at the foot of the second side a line where a name goes, **the first piece of paper in his life with his own name at the top of it**, and he checks the two columns and they agree, and he is proud of it, and a registrar's sentence from last year comes back to him: **a document with a clerk's name at the top of it is a document about that clerk**, and he decides he would rather have the return than not have it, because there is no column on a return for a clerk to write *I would rather this were not here*. At the seventh hour a woman of forty-eight, on a copy list and on no board, says *you have been standing at that door for two hours*, and he says yes, and she says *all right*, and that is the whole of it.

### Chapter 0066 — A Matrix Is Not a Ledger

Garren Pyle's POV, a first-floor room over a yard in the Kiln sheds, and a registrar's office, the fourth to the fourteenth of the second month. He is a certifier with a press he does not own and a bench he made and about four hundred certificates, sixty-one of them suspended on the seventeenth of the sixth. **He says in the first ten minutes that he is not sorry**, and gives a copyist the two things he is sorry about in the order he wants them written, and asks that it not be written in the other order. The plate is a piece of steel a hand's size with **the mark cut in the face of it** — the mark the court in room four entered as a mark and said it did not know the house of — and **a number cut shallow into the edge** in a hand he cannot place, and no name anywhere on it. It came back eleven years ago with a four-line letter saying it was not wanted back and was to be held and would be told if wanted; the letter is in another building and the plate is in this one, and **it is the only object in the empire he is the last owner of**, because a certifier is a person other people own. On the ninth of the second month he hands it in himself with his four lines on it, including that he is not sorry and is not going to pretend to be, and a registrar tells him in a form with a name at the foot that **a matrix is not a ledger**: it proves an impression was made and cut from steel, it does not say who asked for the impression, the asking is in a book and not on this, and the book is not in this building, and **a tool is a thing anybody can buy**. The plate is entered as a plate and is not entered as a name, and the number cut into the edge is recorded as a fact about a book and not about the mark, and Pyle signs his first document that anybody in a court has read. The escalation is the week: he walks seven doors on foot over two days and three of them ask **which number is on it** rather than what he wants done with it, and a woman of about sixty who has cut plates for twenty-two years says it is a sending number out of the Table's run and that she has cut about nine hundred plates off that run and nobody has ever told her what any of them was a plate *for*, and that the thing she has been angry about is not that her certificates were taken but that nobody has ever asked her what a number on a plate is a number *of*. Pyle's sentence, said in a room on the twelfth: **a mark is not a signature; a signature says who did it, a mark says *this is one of ours* to somebody who already knows what ours is, and a mark carries a direction, and a direction is the one thing in the world that does not need anybody's permission to be followed.** **Seven plates go in by the fourteenth** — three carried in by their holders, four cut and carried in by the woman in the lane who had never been asked and had thought to ask nobody — and a registrar's note records that nine persons produced everything they held, that two of the nine held nothing that was a plate and said so, and that the reason nobody had asked them in five months is on the face of the protection order, **which protects the persons and not the objects**. Pyle keeps no copy of the minute, is thanked by nobody in a court, and goes back to cutting certificates that have a rim, a second impression, and a pressure that varies with the hand.

### Chapter 0067 — What It Costs, in Pounds, to Leave

Nell's POV, the dyers' lane in Greyfen, the vat flat, the trestle, and one yard, the middle of the second month. Four trades have answered her fifth question and one of the answers is a price: **a trade will answer a stranger in writing on its own paper for two shillings and a house it has dealt with for nothing, and the two shillings is not for the ink, it is so that nobody owes us anything.** The other three say the same thing in four hands: four pounds ten each way, or eleven if you go on the day they go, two days lost at four shillings a day, a bond of eleven pounds, and four years before a return without a second bond; one house went to Thornfen eleven months ago and is not sorry and does not know anybody here. She reads all four out at the trestle in the open with about sixty people there. Then the rule gets tested by a man with power: **a standing assessor of a third trade, fifty-one, nineteen years in it, four houses owing money and a bench he cannot fill, has written to her before she asked him anything** — that four questions at no charge is a mistake because a question asked for nothing is answered for nothing by the person with nothing to lose; and that his association will take any house pressed out of a trade, bench, standing and wage, at his own cost and without a bond; and that the offer is **not for the person who asks him, it is for whoever writes next**, and a person who is out does not write to an assessor.
