She does not read it out in the lane, and writes that down as a decision and not an instinct: **he said yes before she asked, and a concession made for a reason is not a fact, it is a door, and a bench is a thing a person can stand in front of and say the holder of the bench is the bench**, and a door with no charge on it is still a door. The number on the flat of the trestle in chalk, with no name against it: **two shillings to ask four people with power one question and be told the truth by one of them, against two pounds five and a quarter a quarter, four times a year, thirty-two pounds and fourpence out of a lane in a year** — being told the truth costs a **twentieth** of what not being told costs, which is exact, because the house's own quarter is **two pounds and a farthing** and not the two pounds five and a quarter the figure is printed as, and 480¼ pence against twenty-four is twenty and not a twenty-fifth — and the four who signed a clause about a girl leaving are not the four who will be out, **and the reason the four who signed are not is that they signed**. The four houses' children are at the back again, and the girl of seventeen who said in the eleventh month that it was going to be her in a year has not been asked again since. On the twelfth, on two days' leave out of a room with a barred door, **Marek Kest comes four hundred and thirty miles to be told about the ninth month.** It takes about four minutes, not four hours. What she says is that he was right not to tell her, that she is not going to be one of the people who forgives a thing because it has been said out loud, and what she wants is that he had to make a decision about her alone in a room with nobody to stop him and that it was not a small one; and he says it is the worst thing anybody has said to him and that she is right about it and he has no answer. Neither of them can afterwards remember what the other one said, and she writes that down in nine words.

### Chapter 0068 — The Fourth Place

Tamsin's POV, the copying alcove of the third gallery and the press room off the Crown Kiln stair, the second to the third week of the second month. **Sarn's answer comes down in four lines and refuses the offer for the right reason**: the fourth cycle of the sixth year was never copied, so there is nothing to copy, and what Tamsin offered to do was to *write* eighteen letters in the second month in a cold room, and in nine years a bundle of eighteen letters in a copyist's hand is a thing that court did in the second month of the eleventh year, and it did not, and the whole of what is on that wall is that the court wrote to sixty households and kept nothing. **The flue was cut on the fourteenth, the press is in the embankment corridor, and the eighteen are gone.** Tamsin records that she is right and she did not see it, and that the reason is the second entry on her own list. From the plate she has a **number cut into the edge, out of the run the Notaries' Table uses for everything it sends and everything it makes for a thing that is going to be sent**, and she works out there is exactly one book in the city with that run in it: **the delivery-stub book, kept in a press room, because a stub is not a record and a record belongs in a gallery.** About a hundred yards from her own gallery, in a room where a bag is made up, in a building where four hundred people work every day and not one has been in it to read anything. Getting the book takes eleven minutes and the method is the custody-form method: a room, in words, out loud, in front of somebody — the clerk of the Table **says the no out loud first and then gets his supervisor**, and asks for it to be written down that he said it in that order, and the deputy brings the book down himself with a stool and **asks whether she came for the stub or for the plate**, and is told: for the number. The stub is cardboard with four lines in a hand of sixty years — *enclosed; to whom and where; on whose instance it is sent; by whose authority it is sent* — and the entry for the ninth month of last year reads: **one sheet, sealed, with a direction; to the clerk's window, Mosswake Roll Court, by the midweek packet; on the instance of a matter in the third gallery; P. Vey.** She copies the four lines out in a copyist's hand, standing up, with the book in place, leaf on the back of her own hand. The sheet is gone, four hundred and thirty miles away, in a district court that does not answer letters about a sheet. **She then brings the finding to Marek Kest in the Received Room because it is a sheet going out of the matter to an office outside the Roll Hall, and he reads it in front of her, and he gives his reason in a room and out loud and not on a page** — a stub is a fact about a book and not about a man, a person who signs a column headed *by whose authority* is discharging an office, and a document with a man's name at the top of it is a document about that man and the room will do the only thing it can do with it, which is to hold a hearing about the man — and she agrees, and it goes anyway, and they write *agreed* and the day on it. The chapter's real finding is the last scene, in the room, at the end of the table: **the instrument the whole case has rested on does not fit.** A person affected may be heard about it in a room — the Cinder Court's first order, Sarn's slate, the second question on a board, a girl of eleven working out whose name it was on a works note — and the widow **was** heard, and she was defeated, because a third issue of a witness order went up on a board with a certificate pressed into the first line and when the record spoke the record was a thing she had understood. The person was heard. The record answered first. And a person who has been warned about a document is a person who is now afraid of it, and a person afraid of a document cannot get in front of it. Marek's name goes on the finding with the day on it and the word **accountable** and not the word *right*, on the grounds that there is not one line of this matter that says he was right.

### Chapter 0069 — A Document With a Man's Name at the Top of It

Marek's POV, the press room off the Crown Kiln stair, the last week of the second month. He asks for the room in a note of forty words, offers to bring the sheet and no one, and tells Pell Vey the question before he puts it down. He puts the stub on the table. **Pell Vey does not deny the hand and does not answer**: "A document with a man's name at the top of it is a document about the man," which is his own sentence from the caption of the referral, and the difference between putting Marek's name in the first line and the second. He refuses in the form used twice before in this matter, states that he has answered no part of the question and denied no part of it, and gives the reason: **a person who signs a column headed *by whose authority* is not admitting authorship of a thing, he is discharging an office**; and because a document with a supervisor's name at the top of it is a document about the supervisor, a hearing about the man is the only thing any room can do with it, and in nine years there will be a file with his name at the top of it and about four hundred and forty people in four offices and one trade will conclude that the danger is a man, when it is an office with no person in it, and in nine years nobody will remember that a standing office printed the sentence. The refusal is **the first one in this matter put in a form with a name at the foot for free**, and Marek cannot use it, and the reason is in the form: a refusal can be read back in nine years by a person who has never met either of them; an answer given in a room cannot. The second thing is the finding Marek cannot make work: **the sheet is gone, so the evidence is a method, not a wording**; nobody wrote *the present holder*, nobody wrote the sentence of the twelfth of the ninth, a man in a good coat used three words already in the district's mouth, and the Table sent a direction to a window, and **a supervisor who arranged a thing is not a person who wrote a clause**. Pell Vey still has not asked about the yard of the fourth of the tenth and says why, and Marek has noticed the not asking twice. The refusal is read back to him and **Pell Vey signs the record of the refusal on the back, unasked, in four seconds**, which is the practice Trost taught in the eleventh month. Then Pell Vey says the thing the chapter is for, in a room, with no witness, standing up with a hand on a chair: he did not arrange a thing, **he issued a form to a window, and a window is not a room**; and Marek will not be able to name the man who put the certificate in the first line, and **that is not a failure of his**, because a man who looks for a name for four years finds one, and then somebody will have the thing Pell has just refused to be, made out of a man. He also says his speech is worth nothing and should not go in a file, and asks not to be thanked, and says it is a morning's worth of nothing. He notices that he has signed a fifth document an hour after saying that a document with a man's name on it is a document about the man, and asks for it to be noticed that he noticed. Marek's book that night: five documents, a name since the seventh month of the ninth and a hand since the eighteenth of the second, **neither of which is a person**; the standing office is still an office with no person in it; and the reason he stopped is that building a case towards a person is the same move as building a chain out of four true facts and one invented join. Three copies, no fifth, and he wants that written down.

### Chapter 0070 — A Date With Nothing Under It

Nevin Trost's POV, the third room off the corridor behind the third gallery, and his own office, the first to the third week of the third month. **The Cinder Court gives the sealed-instruction sitting a date: the eighteenth of the seventh month, at the second hour, in room four, with jurisdiction, over the objection entered on the seventeenth of the sixth.** The notice is one side of a sheet and it is printed, and it has the device at the head of it and **nothing at the foot of it** — about a half-inch of white paper where a signature goes — and the answer of the twenty-ninth of the first month said, in a form, that a date is not to be fixed for a matter in which no party can be named, and that when one is fixed it will be fixed **with a name at the foot of it**. Trost enters the date in the day's book and then goes back and enters *the notice carries the device and no name at the foot*, because he made the record of the refusal of the sealed page and somebody in nine years will want to know it was noticed on the day. **Four months and about twelve days is what it takes the Standing Office of Provincial Continuities to print and distribute a sheet to forty-six addresses, for four districts to believe a court has decided it, and for a clerk at a window in the fourth month to reach for a form because it is the only thing in the room.** The reason he can say it out loud is not that he is clever: it is a sentence of **Joane Tranter's**, said in a district court in the twelfth month over a bridge, where she entered that the district has gained nothing and that nothing and nothing gained are not the same thing; and he writes it out on the back of the notice and then stops and puts the magistrate's name on it too. The **ninety words** are one sheet of the Notaries' Table's writing paper, ninety words, no heading, no office, no signature, handed to him in a corridor outside the fourth room on the ninth of the eleventh of last year with *this for the file*, and they are the instruction that told him to receive and consolidate the request as a limited administrative inquiry, to fix the scope on one page, to put the name of the person conducting it on the page, and to write the reason under it — **every single thing he asked for.** He has held it in a drawer for four months and has written his reason for not filing it five times and crossed out four. The fifth version stands: it is not that they are dangerous, because his four lines under the acceptance are the only thing in this matter he has ever been proud of and the ninety words would make them a complaint; filing them makes a man who reported his own office, and he loses a file he can answer for in six years, and he would like to be able to say in six years that he signed the thing that let a woman in a yard read her own file. The reason now is the date. He **files them on the sixth of the third month**, in his own hand, with the date, with a line asking that filing them be entered as a cost and not a finding — and then he **takes a copy to Tamsin Rook and asks her to hold it**, because the Docket would enter it and its master would know where it came from for the rest of his life, and because *a man who files a thing about his own office has to have it in a book somewhere*, and if it is in that book it is with somebody who cannot be instructed. She copies it, reads it back twice, and writes *held by T. Rook, no office* on the back. She then says the sentence that turns the chapter: **there is one more person who could be asked about that sheet and she is not going to ask** — the ninety words are on the Table's writing paper, a pad is not a book, nobody keeps a pad, and there is no stub for an instruction handed to a man in a corridor, so what she is describing is the absence of a record and not the presence of one. On the eighteenth of the third month **Oren Tal** comes: a retired scale notary, sixty-one or two, with a burn-scarred hand, who says he was trained in this building by a woman called Kest about thirty-one years ago and does not know what became of her and has not asked, and gives them one sentence — **the old office of First Custodian was supposed to be temporary** — a word in the operative words, and every office that has widened that office has done it by not putting the word in the sentence. Tamsin asks what that is for and **he refuses her in a form**, says he has watched three people in this matter do it better, and puts a folded leaf on the table: a copy of a schedule of the term, made about forty years ago, in a copyist's hand whose name he will not give in that office. He adds, to the clerk and not to Trost, that a temporary office is the only office in this empire that is honest about when it stops and the only one nobody in that building has ever had to administer, and he will not say what it is for. He asks not to be thanked and says he will leave if anybody thanks him. He refuses Tamsin's question about the woman Kest in four seconds, with one word, and goes down a stair that smells of fired scale. Trost does not thank him, puts the leaf in a file of his own, writes the rest in a book he keeps at home with the day on it, and ends on the notice going into a file with the device at the head of it and nothing at the foot, and on the last week of this year a district in another province receiving a sheet of a series it has received before, and a clerk in a room believing that a court has decided it, and the only thing standing in front of that being a date and a half-inch of white paper.

## Volume 02, Batch 0003 (Chapters 71–80) — The Original Referral, the Temporary Office, and a Column

### Chapter 0071 — One Side of a Sheet

Tamsin's POV, the copying alcove, the fourth to the tenth of the third month, the answer on the packet of the eighth. **The original referral is answered by the court sitting in room four, on one side of a sheet, in five points: a scale reading by a person not entered in the Docket's register at the time is of no legal effect whatever, extending to the readings of the twelfth of the ninth month of last year and the sixth of the fourth month; and the record in which such a reading was entered is a record of a court, is not altered and is not disturbed, the entry stands, every act done in reliance on it stands, and no person who acted on it in good faith is to be questioned; the bar on the applicant is not discharged and is not varied; the court names no person and states again in a form that it does not know and cannot compel the authority for the sealed instruction; and nothing in it is a finding that any person has done wrong or any record altered.** Tamsin's finding in her own book is that she wanted a document that makes a *person* answerable and has been given one that makes a *record* answerable, and that the second paragraph — eleven words, **the only sentence in this case anybody might actually be glad of** — is a permission, not a warning, and that the district which wrote to about sixty households in letters in the sixth year is now answerable to nothing. And the other side of it: **the record that stands is the only record in the empire in which a girl of fifteen is entered as heard and in which a household is entered as asked, in the wrong column**, and both of those lines are now unanswerable, and nobody can un-ask a person. She copies it three times, writes the order down before she acts, and sends the Docket first this time — with the reason written out, which is that a room three corridors away reads a thing this morning or tomorrow — then Silas Sowle with four words on the front, then down the river to a wall with five words. **The two copies that go out of the building are read by Marek Kest in the Received Room before they go, and he gives a reason out loud and she agrees and they go anyway, and *agreed* and the day are on it.** And she discovers, standing at a window with a cold kettle, that **three hundred and eighty-eight crossings in a four weeks' return is a number of a bar and four hundred and eleven is a number of people and the two are never the same figure**, and that she has been carrying the wrong one of the two for three months.

### Chapter 0072 — A Different Thing Wearing the Same Word

Marek's POV, room four and the Received Room, the second week of the third month, the eleventh and the fifteenth. The **limited audit is granted on the eleventh at the fourth hour, in a sitting of eleven minutes of which he is in for nine**, and the registrar reads every term out at the top of his voice and offers a second reading. The terms: the subject is **the two entries in the Docket's register and nothing else**, expressly not any record of any court, not the Venn file, not the instrument of nineteen years back at Lowcross, not any series or standing instruction; **one room in this city, and it may not be carried out in any district**; fourteen days, by a person named at the foot, in his own time at his own wage; a document produced of its own motion and nothing compelled, and **no finding usable in any court but the granting court**; and the bar is not discharged. He signs, and asks one question that is not about the case, and Tamsin's finding is that he would have said yes in nine seconds and read it afterwards if he had not been reading it while it was being offered. **Silas Sowle comes up on the thirteenth with the copy in his coat and wants the thing, and Marek tells him in a room, out loud, standing, in about eleven minutes, what the grant is and is not — and it is not a small version of what he asked for, it is a different thing wearing the same word, and this time the person who has to say it is not a court and is not a clerk.** Then **Sowle writes four lines and turns the sheet round**, using the rule Marek and Tamsin made with each other in the twelfth month of last year, got off a sheet with four words on the front and no office on it, and Marek answers it in a room, in words, with a date, on the fifteenth. He is told he is not going to enjoy it, and does not know until then that the rule he asked for in the fourth month of the first month works on him. What he has left to give a man who came two hundred yards to be told no is **a list of foolscap and not law**: take the file out of the coat, read out the nineteen words on the back of a returned form, ask the magistrate in advance in a room in words on a date, put in both numbers and say what each is a number of, and put nothing of Marek's in at all.

### Chapter 0073 — What a Sentence Is Worth at a Window

Sarn's POV, the Mosswake window, the embankment corridor and one upper lane, the third week of the third month. The transcription arrives on the eighteenth with five words on the front and no office and no name, and she reads it three times and writes out what it does before she writes anything else, and the sentence she writes is that she has been waiting nine years for a sentence that would put somebody in a room and has got a sentence that closes a room from the outside. **Paragraph eleven goes on the board on the nineteenth, in her own hand, as a person and not as a court**, and it says in plain words that a court has said the record stands, that it does not put anybody in a field, that it is worth nothing to the thirty-one except that the one line in which a girl of fifteen is entered as heard is now on a standing record and cannot be taken out, that the same line makes the district's own letters proper and unchallengeable, and that **the harm has not been finished and has been made permanent by a thing said in a room by people who did nothing wrong, which is the only way a harm like this is ever made permanent.** The board's chalk figures are not altered and no other figure is printed. She sells copies at a shilling, reads at the sill, and enters the sales in the fifth column. **On the twenty-first at the third hour, Iolanthe Rees comes back, and this time she is not alone, and the second woman asks the question first** — and the answer is the same and the reason is not: the reason used to be that nobody had asked, and now it is that a court has said the record stands, so there is no longer anybody who *can* ask, and that is not the same thing, and the clerk says so at the sill, and gives the new reason before the old one, and reads both numbers aloud, three hundred and eighty-eight and four hundred and eleven, and says under each of them what it is a number of, and says that the day somebody adds one to the other somebody is going to be hurt and it will not be an error. Eleven shillings. And she writes that a person who brings a witness does not have to come at the third hour, and that this is the whole of what a paragraph on a wall has done in nine years, and that it took a court to do it and not a clerk.

### Chapter 0074 — What a Document Is For

Marek's POV, the third room off the corridor behind the third gallery and the Received Room, chiefly the twenty-seventh. He asks **Nevin Trost** what Oren Tal's leaf is for and is refused, and asks for the leaf and is refused again, and the second refusal comes with a reason: **there is no form in the empire that lets a clerk of the Received Room take a document out of a file of the Examinations office, and what he would have had to put in the file if he did is the real question.** Then Marek brings **the four-line custody form with two boxes** and asks Trost to sign it as the receiving person with the Examinations office in the second box, and Trost reads it and explains in four lines what it does: **it protects a holder. `None` is a door with nothing behind it and the nothing is a person who cannot be instructed — and every protection in that building protects a holder, and every one of them is a wall against a person with a tray, and not one has ever been a wall against an office.** The drawer comes out from under a dead man's desk, and the leaf is one leaf of cream stock folded in four for forty years, **and the fold has worn a channel through the last line, and there were two lines after the copyist's rule and there is one.** Marek works out in four seconds, from a certification of his own gallery's that is short by a leaf, that a careful man does not hand a room a short copy and call it a copy, and that the man has been telling the truth by handing over a thing. **Trost copies one line out in his own hand, with the date, and gives it to him, and keeps the leaf**, and it is not to be entered anywhere and is not to be sent to anybody, and it is entered in the day's book as the only thing in that book for fourteen months that is not a refusal. And Marek catches himself about to write down what the man said and send a copy down the river, and stops, because **the form with `None` protects the person who holds the paper and does not protect a paper at all, and the reason nobody can make the man say is a form a woman signed nine days before he walked into a room.** The chapter's sentence: **a form which protects a holder protects the loss.**

### Chapter 0075 — A Bill That Was Never the Problem

Silas Sowle's POV, the yard at Lowcross and the chamber of the district court of the lower district, the third of the fourth month. **Rosamund Crane, eleven, reads the application through before anybody puts it in front of her grandmother, finds the second person's name line empty, and says so, and the grandfather's refusal comes before the form is offered** — nobody writes anything down, which is Sowle's doing. The application asks for a direction that the crossing is a work of the public and not the work of a holder, and for the district to stand **nineteen pounds three and fourpence**; neither can be made by a district court, and the refusal says in terms that nothing in it is a decision that the crossing is a work of a holder. **Gideon Harrow puts the works' answer on the table and will not read it, because it is his office's paper and not his**, and the answer says the office cannot stand the cost, cannot commit beyond the quarter, and **asked this district on the nineteenth of the twelfth to stand the cost and has not been answered and does not know whether it was declined or undecided or unseen, and has not asked, because it has in nineteen years never once written to a district to say that it cannot do a thing**; that this court is not the district; **that the office will not certify, because certifying is a promise to inspect and the office has given one in one year in nineteen**; and that the crossing is open on the labour of nine unpaid persons. **Joane Tranter enters the answer as filed and not as a concession, and enters a second line on her own motion saying the court notes the second of those three facts and proposes to do nothing with either, because a court that does something with them in the fourth month has found a way of making a district's problem into its own work.** Sowle reads both documents aloud in the yard at arm's length, twice, and his client has the magistrate's second line read to her by a girl of eleven who then asks who is going to do something with them. **Jonas Cray performs his undertaking in the bad coat and says the bad thing twice: nothing has happened in four months.** And he brings the news that a clerk of the intake desk stood in that yard for an hour and a half on the twenty-ninth of the third month, asked what the figure on the works' return is a number of, and wrote the answer in a book he made up himself. Sowle says sorry, badly, and is told that a man who does not want it is doing it for his own reasons and hers are better than his. **At the ninth hour, on the back of a form at the head of the Weir Cut, he writes that the bill has never once been the difficulty, that what the application needed was a name, that there has not been one in that district since a parish was dissolved in the sixth year and nobody in Lowcross knew because the notice went to the parish — and that he has had nineteen words on the back of a returned form in a coat for four months and nobody has ever read them out in a room.**

### Chapter 0076 — What the Figure Is a Number Of

Joane Tranter's POV, her chamber and the lean-to at the head of the Lowcross crossing, the third of the fourth month, from the sixth hour. She has read the works' return four times since the twelfth month and has never once asked what the figure is a number of, and she says so out loud in the lean-to before she asks, so that it is in the room. **Emrys Rudd, sixty-four, has kept the tally for nineteen years at a penny a day, paid whether anybody crosses or not, in a lean-to about nine feet by six, and is in no record.** The answer: **three hundred and eighty-eight is a number of times the bar went up and down in four weeks, and it is not a number of people and it is not a number of houses.** He has known it every day and had nobody to say it to. She does the penny out loud — about three pounds a year, about fifty-seven pounds over nineteen years, against about thirty-eight pounds of repairs in two lots and about a pound a year lost in between — and he says the thing she has needed: **nobody has lost anything, the money was never anybody's, and there is no line for the bridge in the fund, and a fund that had a line for a bridge would have to answer for the bridge.** And the two figures: four go over on a cart, a woman crosses on a plank with a sack three times a morning and would be three in a headcount and is one in a count of a bar, **a headcount cannot be produced from a figure of crossings by anybody, and a guess with a division in it that comes out near the right number is the most dangerous kind of thing there is.** He says in advance that a figure about him in a file in the city would be the first thing anybody has ever written about him and the first thing anybody could use. She enters it in the minute on her own motion, asked for by nobody, **as a question asked and answered and not as a finding**, and enters under the same line that she is the magistrate who did not ask for four months, and tells her clerk to find out whether the works' return is copied off his card, to want it copied off his card, and **not to put his name in the file.**

### Chapter 0077 — A Document About a Seal

Marek's POV, the great stair of the Crown Kiln and room four, the second and third weeks of the fourth month, the notice on the tenth and the room on the fourteenth. The chapter opens by disposing of the **limited audit in two afternoons in the Received Room in the third week of the third month**, which produced one sentence, entered nowhere, with **Oswin Marrow standing at the end of the table unasked because the room was in his building.** Then the notice: one side, **not printed**, a device at the head and a seal and a name at the foot, six numbered lines. The seat is entered by its device; **no person of or belonging to it is party, cited or examinable, and no inference of any kind may be drawn from the fact that a person has not been named**; the seat does not contend either way about the crossing; it asks that no lower court make a finding on the construction of the instrument of nineteen years back; **it will pay the carriage of anybody the district court thinks should be heard without asking why, and will pay it twice if the person comes twice**; and the sixth line states that the First Seat holds the old authority to call a succession convocation, with no claim and no threat. **Seryn Oris, First Seat — named for the first time in this book, and credited with nothing she did not do.** She is not present, is not a villain, and **the MC/FS circular is not hers.** Marek is not a party to the Lowcross matter and says so, and the registrar tells him what will happen and reads him the device form's own sentence about it, and then tells him out loud, in the room, that the notice **mentions no series, no run, no number, no sheet, no office, one instrument of nineteen years back, one district court, one crossing and one carter**, and that this is why it is the most dangerous paper to have come up that stair in fourteen months. Marek is heard for nine minutes, uses them badly, and says three things, the last of which is eight words: **the document is about a seal.** The objection is entered verbatim, read back twice at his request, and lost in four minutes with no reason on the bench — **the second time an objection of his has been entered in a room in this matter.** In the corridor, Sela Vint says that a seat has found the only door in that building that it fits through and that she published it and has never been sorry once in nine years, and that she cannot refuse a form her own court published because a judge who refuses one has to invent a reason and an invented reason in that minute book cannot be told from a real one. And she answers the question he actually wanted to ask: **a document with a name at the top of it cannot make a person examinable. A person who signs for a seat is discharging an office, and the thing he wants is a seat, and an office cannot be examined. It is a human clerk's instrument and it does not cross a species.**

### Chapter 0078 — A Date and an Initial

Tamsin's POV, the copying alcove and the Received Room, the fourth week of the fourth month, the twenty-second to the twenty-sixth. She starts from a sum on the back of a form: **the Cinder Court's first order says no guarantor duty attaches to a person not heard and not signed, it is the best sentence anybody has produced, and in four hundred and thirty days it has not spared one household**, because of Chapter 68's finding — the person was heard, the record answered first, and what actually failed was the *order of the day*: somebody put a document in front of the widow before anybody asked her anything. **So a person has to be in the room before the document exists**, and that is not a new idea, it is the trestle table and a girl of eleven at a bridgewarden's table and nine days in a furnace town advising nobody. She goes and stands at the end of the intake counter for nine minutes and does not say anything, reading upside down a book that has been kept for two years with **one column, a time and an initial, no name, and no heading at the top of it** — the cheapest piece of evidence in the empire, never named, never asked for. Then she puts the sheet on the table: two ruled boxes and three lines of rule, no number, no series, no seal, nothing at the head of it, **because a form with a run on it is a model waiting for a district.** Box one, *what the person was told, in the person's own words*; box two, *the office of the receiving person, if any*, and `None` when there is none; three lines, *you are not asked anything and nothing is asked of you; you are not to be given advice; write the day and your initial on the other side and hand this back*; and the other side, *the day. Your initial.* A separate column at a separate counter with one heading, **Given**, after four others were written and crossed out. **Four pence a sheet**, costed standing up, with the reason in her book: a penny is a trade price and four pence is a price that lets a person with nothing answer without owing anybody. She then says the limits out loud — it stops nothing, it opens nothing, it is not evidence, it cannot be used at Lowcross, it is adopted nowhere, **and it is not sent to anybody because the undertaking of the fourth of the first month says a sheet about this matter in an office's hand is a document with an office at the top of it** — and says the one that matters: **it is beaten by the case it was made for, because the widow had four months' warning and being told the truth is the warning; it fixes the next thing, and the next thing is the one where nobody warned anybody.** Marek wants the limits on the sheet and she refuses and puts them in the book, and records that he does not agree. And then: **they do not fill in the first one, and that is the finding.** He is in the Docket's register and three minute books and a grant and a return at a door; she is in two registers and a list of forty-one names in four hands; **two people cannot carry the first one because the two of them are the two people in this matter that a document is about.** He does not ask her to put his name on it and she does not. And he does not go and ask the clerk of twenty-five to write a heading on his book either, because somebody has to leave one thing in the book without asking.

### Chapter 0079 — What a Caught Person Is Worth

Garren Pyle's POV, a first-floor room over a yard in the Kiln sheds and four streets, the second to the fourth of the fifth month. **The examination of the man convicted on the second of the second month, taken in the second week of the fourth month, is forty-one pages and names no client and no house**, and what came out of it is a practice, eleven lines long, and the eleventh is that a set of people is a thing that can be counted. **About nine certifiers learned what a mark is for in one week in the second month; about forty more have learned in the last three weeks what a name is for**, and none of the forty learned it from the man who was caught. He is a certifier of about sixty, of a town not named, who held a client's name under his own certificate in his trade's book since a year he will not give, and put it in a form of his own on about the first of the fifth; it went in at the second hour and was read back and initialled at the fourth; **he is not sorry, gives the two things he is sorry about in an order, and is Pyle's own condition from the other side.** Pyle goes and listens in a room with two windows for about an hour on the wrong chair and says nothing that is not a question, and comes out and says out loud that **a man who is caught is a quantity, worth four pence a sheet and a clerk's half hour, and is the end of it and not a source.** And the price list: **a name is worth about a plate, the price is public, and it was set on the ninth of the second month by a registrar who meant no harm by it — a matrix is not a ledger and a tool is a thing anybody can buy. He was talking about steel. He was talking about all of us.** He walks four doors on foot and says the same five things at each: one says thank you and shuts the door, one says nothing, one says he will think about it and then asks *if I hand it in, does anybody come to my house* and then says it again, one is not at home and he leaves nothing and does not knock twice. **The list of which is which is in a book with no office on it and in nine years nobody will be able to get at it, and if somebody does, the first thing they will do with it is not find out about a plate.** The chapter's sentence: **a name is the only thing a person has that is theirs, nothing in this book or in this empire can make a man put his name down, and the reason is not a legal rule and there is not one** — and the instrument that finds people does not work on a man who will not write his own name, and there is no appeal from a word. And on the fourth he stands across the road on the Kiln row for a quarter of an hour and does not cross it, because **a mark on a board is not a door, and a girl of seventeen on that counter already knows what a copy is.**

### Chapter 0080 — A Number in the Corner

The woman of about sixty who has cut plates for twenty-two years in a lane, unnamed, the fifth to the seventh of the fifth month, in Auremar, a lane off the Kiln road, a bench under a window and the yard behind. A clerk of the district court comes on the fifth with a form and does everything right and is told four words: **nobody gets my name.** And the form goes under the weight with about nine hundred others. Then the reason, which she has never said to anybody: a number in the edge of a plate is a **sending** number, a number of the run and not of the plate, and **the run does not stop, and there is one year in the middle of it where a run changes because a counter changes or a hand changes, and she was in the room when it happened and the man who was doing it before her is dead** — so she is the only living point in the empire at which a number can be broken into hers and not hers, and clients have numbers too and they go out on the same run. **If she gives her name she is a person in a file. If she gives the year she is a ledger.** And the other thing she has never said, eleven years old: a woman at the far end of the lane has been bringing papers to be stamped three days in ten for eleven years and cannot read and has never said so, and eleven years ago a certifier brought a plate to be cut here with a card made out to a firm of about four words, and she cut the number into the edge because the run is the run and did not look at what the matrix was for, **because nobody has ever asked her what a matrix is for and she is a plate-cutter and a matrix is steel and a plate is steel.** She thought about it for a year and stopped, because there was nothing to think about it with. **About four hundred people have told the truth in a room in this matter and she is one of about four who has not.** On the sixth, a girl of about thirteen walks two hours and asks whether a certificate is hers; the plate-cutter takes the edge of the card between thumb and forefinger the way you find a cut in a coin and says no, and will not say whose it is because she has cut about nine hundred plates off that run and not one carries a name, and then writes the number in pencil, large, in the unprinted corner, and the day on the back with **three short cuts in a triangle** beside it, and tells a person of thirteen out loud that that is a mark and not a name and somebody will come to her house with a tray in about four years. **There was no box and no form and no heading. She was the receiving person and had none, which is the word `None` on a form that has not been written and has not been sent to anybody, and the day and the mark on the back of a piece of paper is the whole of the second instrument, arrived at on its own, in a lane, eleven years late, in about four minutes.** And at the seventh hour of the seventh she writes the volume's question for its final two batches in the plainest words she has: **what happens to a rule about records when the person it is about cannot get to the room** — and the answer is nothing, nothing has always happened, there is no rule about records that reaches the far end of that lane, and the only thing that changed in three weeks is that a person can now say out loud what a number on a card is a number of. And the fear, in her own hand with the day on it: about forty people have learned what a name is for, she is the only person alive who can break a number into hers and not hers, and **the only thing standing between those two facts is that she is sixty, she has three cuts in a triangle, she has no office, and a man with a tray has to knock on a door to get at her and he does not knock on doors — and she has got about three weeks of that left.**

## Volume 02, Batch 0004 (Chapters 81–90) — The Seat That Is Not a Person, a Forged Seal, and a Finding That Binds Nobody

### Chapter 0081 — Four Pence and a Column

Tamsin's POV, the copying alcove and the Received Room, the tenth and the eleventh of the fifth month. She goes through the three ways a sheet can be moved and finds that two of them are refusals dressed as methods — to an office it becomes a document with an office at the top of it, by post to a person it becomes a document about her — and that the third, **a list of people who can hand it over, is a list of doors**, and a man four streets away has four doors in a book with no office on it and has written down that the first thing anybody would do with such a list in nine years is not to find out about a plate. She writes the reason down, tears the list up, and puts the pieces in a waste box under the counter. In the Received Room she asks Marek what a thing is for before anybody has used it twice, and gets the plainest answer of the year: **it is for somebody, and the somebody is almost always the one who is least able to come, and the work is to find out who is going to hand it over, who is going to keep the column, and where the four pence comes from.** Then the money, standing up: foolscap about a halfpence, about a minute and forty seconds a sheet, so three and a quarter pence a sheet is not paper and is not ruling and is not her hour. **Four pence is the smallest sum a person told about a thing once can hand over without owing anybody afterwards, and she has priced a rule and did not know it, and it has to come out of her own account, because there is no fund in this matter.** She rules eleven sheets in a dead hour, corrects her own costing without crossing the wrong figure out, and gives three of four away at the end of the intake run: three given, one returned by a man of sixty who said out loud in a queue that it was the only thing in the building that did not want anything off him, and then asked whether a person was allowed to take two. **The column headed Given is ruled in her own book and the keeper is a person who can be found, on purpose: a column kept by somebody nobody can find is a column that can be denied.** And the last of it is the first number about herself she has ever entered in that book, and she read it back twice and did not cross it out.

### Chapter 0082 — A Hearing That Cannot Fail

The clerk of the district court of the lower district, unnamed, Auremar's lower district, the fourteenth and the twenty-first of the fifth month. A refusal gave a date to be fixed on seven days' notice; a date needs a person to serve it on; the schedule of persons who may be answerable has eleven lines, ten of them offices or a parish dissolved in the sixth year, and four of them reading *the present holder*. **So the notice went to the holder, and the clerk read it and thought for about four seconds about the word at the top of it and then sealed it, and that is the whole of what a person in this empire does that they afterwards wish they had not done.** It is served at the yard on the fourteenth, and served properly, and the officer writes on the back that it was read aloud at the request of the person served, who cannot read and says the service is not to be taken as her assent. Hester Crane is served, does not come on the twenty-first, and does not refuse. **The magistrate will not let the clerk enter *served* and will not let him enter *refused*, and enters instead that a court cannot distinguish a person who has refused from a person who has not come, because both of them are an absence and an absence has no date and no initial on it.** Emrys Rudd, sixty-four, is in the room because the clerk sent him a card four days earlier with his own name at the foot of it asking what the crossing costs a day; he says the whole of it in nine sentences — he has signed eleven papers at the end of that bar in nineteen years and was right every time, and this is the first that anybody has written down, and in four years somebody will come to that yard with a tray and say the holder of that crossing is a woman of sixty-one who cannot read and has a granddaughter — and adds that he would not have said no to a man with a paper in that yard, because in four years there are going to be a great many people saying that people like him signed things they did not understand, and about half of that is true. **No date is fixed.** The clerk writes his own account that evening, in a book with no office at the top of it, and the finding is that a word that walks about has been put in an envelope and sent by post by a clerk who did the ordinary thing correctly, and that the word is the only part of the notice that will still be true in nine years.

### Chapter 0083 — The Respondent That Cannot Be Served

The registrar of the Cinder Court, unnamed, the great stair and room four, the twenty-sixth of the fifth and the first week of the sixth. A seat's wing asks the court to enter it as a respondent in the exact form this court published in the reign of the third of the Ashfall line, and **he says out loud, before he does it, that he is going to do it and why, and has the clerk enter the reason in the day's book and initial it** — a judge who declines a form her own court published has to write down why, and there is no why, and an invented reason in that minute book cannot be told in nine years from a real one. The difficulty is four minutes long and it is the column for the person served: a device has no address, no person, and no embarrassment behind it, and can never be served. Then the finding, from the three documents with one mark on them — the instrument of nineteen years back, the sealed instruction, and the seat's notice: **the alteration has two authorities in it and neither of them is a person, one a standing office that prints a sheet for forty-six addresses with nobody at the top of it, and one a seat, and in nine years the only two things anybody will be able to say about the alteration are offices, and the correct conclusion from those two facts is that there is nobody to conclude anything about, and that is a worse answer than a man in a good coat.** He enters it, on his own motion, with his name at the foot, and then writes four words in the register of parties where an address goes: **no person. no address. this court cannot serve it.** In the register he also notes that the notice's fifth line — the carriage — is the only line with a person in it, because money has to be paid to somebody. On the great stair, to Marek Kest, who is not a party: **a seat that is not a person cannot be refused, cannot be examined, cannot be embarrassed and cannot lose, and it does not have to be sorry about it, and that is worse than the man in the good coat because we could have refused him — and it is also more honest, because it told the truth about itself in its own second line and did not pretend, and I would rather deal with the honest one that cannot be refused than the dishonest one that can, and I understand perfectly well that that is the worst of it.** Then Marek's own book, that evening: **a document with a mark at the top of it is a document about nobody, and a document about nobody cannot bind a person who has not been heard, because it has not bound anybody at all** — and he writes that he does not know what to do with a thing that is not a person.

### Chapter 0084 — A Question and a Form

Tamsin's POV, the copying alcove, the Received Room, and a notary's table at the Kiln end, the fifteenth and the sixteenth of the sixth month. An **instrument of appearance** is a sheet, made at a notary's table in a House Oris chamber about two hundred miles away and put to a notary's table here at the same hour, with a person at each end and a witness at each end, and the seal laid and the assent spoken — **eleven seconds, and the whole of how a true seal is made, which is the reason a true seal cannot be forged, and no clerk in this city has needed to know that until a card with no rim went into a district court in the second month.** Vaunt Oris says, before he is asked and in the words the notary of this city will write down, that he is a House Oris notary in his own right and **is not the First Seat and is not her agent and has had no communication with her in this matter, and that the same surname is a fact about his family and nothing whatever about her standing, and that he asks for the second thing to be recorded because in about four years somebody will see the surname twice on a sheet and join them.** The question is one question with a form behind it: where a seat is entered by its device and no person of it is party, and a court is asked to hold a matter, **to whom is the order served; if there is no person, by what means is a person told; and if neither is possible, what is the standing of an order so made against a person who has not been heard.** The undertaking of the fourth of the first month is exercised and catches a word: Marek reads the transcription in front of her and says she has written *suspend* four times where the original says *hold*, **and a court does not suspend anything, a court holds a matter, and no court in this empire can stop a thing happening by writing a word** — and she changes the four and strikes the word out without inking it, and the third time that undertaking has caught something it has been a word and never a fact. The answer: the order was served on no person and cannot be served; the carriage offer is inoperable because **the district court has not been asked who it thinks should be heard and the notice's own terms require it to be asked**; and an order not served on a person does not bind that person. **The First Seat's own counsel loses a day and a half, and the cost nobody entered is that the carriage was not offered for a day and a half and four women who had said they would come on the twenty-eighth of the fifth did not.** Vaunt Oris is satisfied with the second answer and not the first or the third, and says why: the seat's order has bound nobody for a month and a half and its House has behaved as though it bound everybody, and — the real reason — **your order of the seventeenth of the sixth is a door with the hinges on the wrong side, and it lets a person in after the thing has already happened, and my House is short one sentence and has been since the twelfth of the ninth month of last year.**

### Chapter 0085 — The Third Time

Marek's POV, the Received Room, chiefly the nineteenth of the sixth month. **A certifier of about forty, unnamed, and expressly NOT Garren Pyle of Chapter 0066** — he now has four hundred certificates and thirty-one called in on the fourth of the second month of this year, and **the sixty-one suspended on the seventeenth of the sixth month belong to Pyle and to Corvin Dray and must not be put on a second man** — with a press he does not own and a day-book in his own hand brings a card and puts it on the table. It carries a scale impression with a rim, a second impression, and pressure that varies across the width the way a hand goes — **it is exactly what a true seal looks like** — and a number cut shallow in the edge of the die, **44,819**, which is a number of a book in a press room about a hundred yards from where Marek sits. He is not a villain: he read the return of the fourth-month application, he knows the works man is right that certifying is a promise to inspect, he has stood at the end of that bar twice on his own time, and **he wants to certify for nothing that the crossing is a work of the public of that district and not the work of a holder, because a certificate ends a dispute, and that is what a certificate is, and it would be finished in a week.** Marek says two things and is fair on both: you are right, and a man who crosses that water every day knows things a file does not; and I am not going to use it, because the seal was made by cutting a die out of a laid impression, and when you cut a die out of a copy the fault is in the card twice, in the same place, at the same depth, in the same order, **and a hand cannot repeat a pressure** — and the book that would say who asked for the impression is in a press room and cannot be got, and a document that cannot be produced cannot be protected. The certifier's answer is the best in the chapter: *I believe the reason and I do not think it is good enough, and I am not going to argue with you in this room because I can see that you would let me.* Then Marek counts his three refusals out loud — the ninth of the second month, an absence and not a decision; the eighteenth of the second month, four lines of a delivery stub and a man he did not build towards; and this — **in the same words, because the reason is the same, and a document that cannot be produced cannot be protected and a mark that cannot be read can only be described and a description does not name anybody** — and then says the other half: **a man who refuses the same thing three times has a habit and not a principle, and a habit is cheaper to refuse than a principle is to hold, because a principle is a thing you must hold in a room where the room is against you, and he has been in three such rooms in fourteen months and this is how he got this far.** The impression is described into a record in four lines, because a mark cannot be read into a record and can only be described into one. The bill is still nineteen pounds three and fourpence and is not paid.

### Chapter 0086 — A Bench Under a Window

Joane Tranter's POV, a bench under a window in a lane off the Kiln road and the chamber of the district court, the twenty-fourth of the sixth to the second of the seventh. The court sent for two people and neither came. Emrys Rudd came and was no help. The woman of about sixty **declined, in the same four words, and gave her reason: a person who can tell a cut die from a hand-cut one is a person anybody can find, and being unfindable is the whole of the protection her trade allows her, and she is not handing the year over to a court to be polite** — and she counted the visits, and said the number is two now and it is not going to be three while she is cutting. The magistrate goes and buys a person she can find: a plate-cutter of about thirty-four with a bench of her own, a trade name over the door, a customer list and a day-work book, **four things that are all things about a person, where the woman of sixty has none of them, and that is the whole of the difference.** She is **warned on the record before she begins**, with the magistrate's name at the foot of the warning: from today she is a person in a file who can answer this question, the only protection her trade has is that a person is not findable, and this afternoon is not what makes her findable. She asks whether anybody in the court thinks a day of her time is worth more than a shilling, is told the figure — **one pound fourpence and the carriage, about four days of her bench** — and says she will do it for the four days and not for the kindness. Then four seconds: **it is a mirror.** The card's mark is heavy on the left and the die's shoulder is heavy on the right, **a die is the instrument by which a mirror is made, and a mirror is a scale impression, and the whole of the variation of pressure in a seal is the record of a hand, and the die therefore carries the record of a hand reversed, and no hand can produce its own pressure reversed** — and so the original is real, and this is not a false seal but a true one photographed by a person who has never seen a hand come off a card, **which is worse, and is not a crime yet.** Four lines go into the minute; no house, no client, no person. Then, in a lane, she says in advance that she will not say the year, and gets it entered that she said it in advance, and is told there will not be a third card. **The finding's cost is entered in the magistrate's own hand: the court took a woman's protection and paid for what it took and wrote down that it had taken it, and it has still cost her, and the only mercy is that the fund pays for a service and not for a person.**

### Chapter 0087 — A Document About Nobody

Marek's POV, the third gallery, the intake counter, a street and a chandler's door, the ninth and the eleventh of the seventh month. The card had been **used** before it was offered, and the record of the using is a sheet of foolscap with five columns made by a clerk of twenty-five — a count, a time, a sheet, where it went, and five strokes — and **a five-column return counts sheets and not people**, which the clerk says in about half a second when asked, and Marek does not push and does not ask for a heading on the book. **What a row in such a book carries about the person who brought a thing is: nothing. A carrier is paid eleven pence to carry a sealed card off a hook at a counter with about thirty things on it, and he is found in a day and asked one question and answers it in a minute and a half, and he is not a party and is examinable by nobody, and he says the trade is a man does not look, because looking is not what the eleven pence buys, and he would carry it again tomorrow.** Then the best thing anybody says in the chapter, for free, on his way out late: **I am not a person in this; I know what it is not, because the eleven pence is bought for not knowing, and the day a man in a good coat can take a thing out of a bag and read it and put it back and the carrier does not know, he is not a thief, and he will say he only looked, and he will be right, and every one of you will wish he had been something worse.** And the finding, in a room at the ninth hour, and the refusal that is its price: **a document with a person's name at the top of it is a document about that person, and a document with a mark at the top of it is a document about nobody, and the first thing you do with a document about nobody is nothing, and a document about nobody cannot bind a person who has not been heard because it has not bound anybody at all.** He writes down that he is not going to the lane off the Kiln road and why, and that he is not going to write the other three appearances of the word that walks about in a row, **because the moment four of them are in a row somebody will draw a line, and a line is a chain, and a chain is an invention, and the whole finding of this matter is that there is nobody at the end of it.** He writes down where the hook ledger is and does not go. He does not read anything; the word *suspend* is not used; and the eighteenth of the seventh is six days away and nobody in that building has been told anything about it.

### Chapter 0088 — The Woman With the Barrow of Hot Water

The woman of about sixty who cuts plates, unnamed, her lane and a lane four streets off, the twenty-first to the twenty-fourth of the seventh month. The woman who brings papers to be stamped has stopped coming to the bench and has found **a woman with a barrow of hot water at the end of Weaver's Cut**, and the plate-cutter goes and stands at the end of that lane for a quarter of an hour and says nothing. What she watches is exactly what she does: an old man of about seventy with a demand from a district water office for two shillings and a farthing, which he cannot read, and the woman with the barrow reads it twice and asks him four things — **is there work in it, whose name is on it, if you stop whose name answers, and who read this to me and do they get paid if I sign** — and writes what he said in two ruled boxes, and writes the day on the back **with three short cuts in a triangle that are not her mark; she copied his.** And the second thing the plate-cutter did not invent: the four questions came off a stone four hundred and thirty miles down a river in chalk in the sixth month of last year, and the woman with the barrow has had them about four months and does not know where from, and Marek Kest asks why the plate-cutter did not ask, and she says she is not going to, **because then the next person who thinks of one of those four things has to hide it.** Then the cost: `None` is in the second box and **the woman with the barrow does not know what it means, and it is not a word about an office, it is a person who cannot be instructed, and it only works while the person writing it knows that, and a person who copies the shape without the meaning will one day put an office in that box and the door will be behind the questioner** — and there is no way to send a correction, because there is no form, no series, no run, no counter and no addressee, and a form with a run is a model waiting for a district, and the only thing she could do is be a person in this matter that a document is about. The chapter refuses to be a vindication: eleven weeks, three women, no form, no sender, and **a method travels faster than its meaning, and that is how every method in this book has ever travelled.** And the other cost, which is hers: the three weeks of being not worth a tray came and went, nobody knocked, and two visits from a court have made her the best-known bench in a lane — **she has not become worth a tray, she has become famous, and fame in a lane has a radius of about a quarter of a mile.** The eighteenth of the seventh has come and gone and nothing has come up that road, and nobody has been told anything, and she is sixty and it is a long way.

### Chapter 0089 — The Half the Man in the Hut Cannot Answer

The woman of about seventy at the far side of the Lowcross crossing, unnamed, a lean-to at the head of the bar and the chamber, the twenty-eighth of the seventh month. She crosses twice a week for flour and has for fifty-one years, and **she does not go past the bar.** The magistrate was not looking for what she found: she went to certify a service she could not certify unseen, the yard was shut, and she went four hundred yards to the crossing in the rain and sat in a lean-to for about eleven minutes. She asks Emrys Rudd the one question that matters — *if a person crosses that water without the bar moving, does your card know* — and he says no, and says the man who opens the gate at the fifth hour is a carter on a round and is not paid by this district, and then that he has been wrong for a long time and nobody told him, and then: **ask her, she goes over twice a week for flour and I have been here nineteen years and I have never once asked her a question because I have been paid a penny a day to sit, and asking is not sitting.** Her answer, in nine sentences: there is a gate at the mill end with a chain and a **padlock that has been broken for longer than any person now living**, and a plank, and the water is low at some seasons, and **a person can cross that water and the card does not change at all, and it is not a trick and it is not a fraud and I have never once thought of it as anything, and I have known it every single morning for fifty-one years** — and a woman at the mill who comes through a yard at night, and men who fish the shallow for two hours, and a boy of nine who goes over on his hands. **A count of a bar omits every person who crosses without the bar moving, and the omission has always been in the figure and is nobody's conduct.** The magistrate enters it on her own motion as **a question asked and answered and not a finding**, with no name in it, stating that the court did not ask for one, and again entering that no person in that lean-to is paid anything and none is named. What it cost is in her own account and not in the minute: a line in a file about a woman of about seventy with no name in it, **the first such line in nine years, and the only person alive who will know it is about her is the magistrate, and a line about nobody is a line that can be denied by anybody who reads it in a bad light** — and she would rather leave a true thing that can be denied than a true thing that can be used, and she has to go and tell the woman in person because a letter is a document and a clerk cannot be sent. The bill is still nineteen pounds three and fourpence and the tally has cost about fifty-seven pounds over nineteen years and nobody has ever claimed a penny of it. **Nothing has come up that road about the eighteenth of the seventh.**

### Chapter 0090 — Open to Challenge

Joane Tranter's POV, the chamber of the district court of Auremar's lower district, the yard, the lean-to and the foot of the crossing, the fourth, fifth, ninth and tenth of the eighth month. The further application comes on seven days' notice given by the solicitor, not by the court. **The order of the day is made the day before, in her own hand: the person affected is addressed before any paper is read, and if a paper is put in front of her it is put after she has spoken, and the magistrate will say out loud what is about to be read and who wrote it before it is read, and a person who cannot read a paragraph is read to by a person of her own choosing and not by an officer.** Hester Crane asks for one line in a copyist's hand — *my granddaughter reads, and not you* — and then, before anything has been read, says the thing she came to say: **a word keeps coming to my gate, and it is a word and not a person, and the parish it belonged to went in the sixth year, so it comes to me, and it has come eleven times and I have said no to it twice and the other nine times I signed for a thing I could not read because I did not know there was going to be a no.** Then six documents go in in twenty-two minutes, each described aloud before it is read, and the works' answer is unchanged at four months. Silas Sowle reads out, for the first time in a room, the spine sentence on the back of a returned form — *the form is returned because the form cannot be entered and not because the work is refused* — twice, **which is seventeen words and which he had called nineteen for four months; Joane Tranter counts it aloud in the room, the clerk enters seventeen, and the file goes on calling it nineteen.** and says the bill has never been the bill and that what the form wanted was a decision and a decision needs a person and there has not been one since a parish was dissolved. He asks for the smallest thing anybody has asked in this case and is ashamed of: **that a person affected may be heard about an instrument in a room, in a place that recognises the room, before it binds anybody.** She refuses everything else correctly and in six minutes — cannot direct the district, cannot make a finding on the construction of the instrument, cannot certify the crossing, cannot order a continuance — and then makes two findings. **First, the precedent is open to challenge: a person affected may be heard about an instrument in a room, in a place that recognises the room, before it binds anybody, and not only afterwards. Second, the order of the seventeenth of the sixth of last year does not by itself provide that a person is heard before the instrument takes effect upon them, and a person who has been warned about a document is afraid of it and cannot get in front of it** — entered with the magistrate's name at the foot of it, and it is Chapter 68's finding put into a minute book with a judge's name on it. And then the cost, said out loud in the room: **every precedent in this empire is now open to challenge, including the ones that are right, and there is no way to charge it to anybody.** Four sentences in fourteen months and not one has paid anybody. In the yard a woman of sixty-one asks one question at the gate — *is my name on it* — is told no, is told the finding in plain words at arm's length, and is told the other half: **you came for the nineteen pounds three and fourpence and nobody has given you the nineteen pounds three and fourpence**, and the bar goes up at the end of the day and the chain goes on the gate and nobody is going to mend the padlock. At the gate she says *he read it out; nobody ever read it out*, and then that she did not have to be brave, because there was a chair and it was empty and the woman told her it would be empty. And on the tenth a clerk comes down from the city and asks whether anything has come of the eighteenth of the seventh, and is told, in a corridor, in twenty seconds, that nothing is before her and that **there is no form in this empire for a magistrate of a district court to be told about a sitting in a court above her**, and the answer goes in the day's book.

## Volume 02, Batch 0005 (Chapters 91–100) — The Certified Word, the Bar, the Watch, and a Rail at Reed Cut

**This is the last batch of Volume 02. The volume is closed at Chapter 100.**

### Chapter 0091 — The Fourth Form

Sela Vint's POV, room four off the great stair of the Crown Kiln, the second week of the eighth month, with the sitting of the eighteenth of the seventh read back inside it. The court has four days a week on which a person may come and sit in room four while an order is read; about nine come, and an unnamed registrar who has been in the building twenty-six years writes their names in a new book headed *came*, with his own name as the fourth line, and reads out the four questions four people in four buildings have asked about a sitting nobody was told about — **there is no form in this empire for asking a court what came of its own sitting, and a court that publishes nothing cannot be asked for nothing.** She then reads the order of the eighteenth of the seventh, in five entries with times against them: **a court may address what an instrument does and may not address the mind of the office that issued it; the sealed instruction is not void, not invalid and not set aside; the court does not know and cannot compel the authority, in a fourth form, and the fact that no person can be named is not a defect in the proceeding; a device is not a person, is not a party and cannot be served, and an instrument of which no person is named cannot be produced against a person and has no operation upon any person until that person has been heard about it in a room; and the matters touching the instrument of nineteen years back at Lowcross remain held.** It names no person, finds no record altered, finds no person at fault, and makes nobody liable. She reads the registrar's own entry of the twenty-sixth of the fifth against the third entry, says the form was drawn by this court in the reign of the third of the Ashfall line and is the best drafting in the file and the reason a seat can be in a room without being a person, and then finds out loud in the room that **the order is one sentence short** — a person may be heard, and it does not say when, and a dragon notary asked for the rest of it from two hundred miles away in the sixth month and the court has now given his House the half that is the useless half. She says the price of the whole thing out loud: the sentence binds nobody, there is no form for compelling a person to be brought to a room, the seat's carriage offer has been live and inoperable for four months because nobody has asked the district court who it thinks should be heard, and **the whole of the work the sentence creates is done by clerks at counters and women with barrows, none of whom are in the room.** A clerk of the lower district court asks whether he may put it in a minute, is told a fifth of a line, is told he may not decide the person before him is affected, is asked what his magistrate found on the ninth of the eighth, and says nothing, and is thanked. The last thing in the chapter is a marginal note in the registrar's own hand: this court has granted four things and entered five, the fifth is not a grant, and a clerk in nine years will add up the grants and get five instead of four, and the judge tells him to enter it with that reason and let him add it up wrong.

### Chapter 0092 — What a Sentence Is Worth at a Counter

Tamsin Rook's POV, the copying alcove, the intake counter, a corridor and the Received Room, the third week of the eighth month. A packet brings the order, the certification of it, the second finding of the lower district, and the notice of the sitting of the sixth of the third with the device on it and nothing at the foot. She copies the order in plain words, and the undertaking of the fourth of the first month is exercised and **catches a fact for the first time in fourteen months**: she has written *cannot be a party, cited, or examined* where the instrument says *is not a party, cited, or examinable*, and a copy that strengthens a sentence is a copy that will be quoted as the court, and she puts it back. The certified copy goes down the road and four plain copies go out at four pence at the end of the intake run: a man who works out what the sentence means for him, a woman who takes one unread, a young man told that there is a name on the *bottom* of it, and a man of thirty-four with a mark on a card of his own who is told **the way** to find out whether a mark is his and **not the lane**, because the woman who cuts plates there has spent eleven years arranging not to be a person in a file. A clerk of the lower district walks four miles to ask whether he may put it in a minute, is told yes and told the limits, and asks what it is for, and is told: **it is for a person who has been given a paper and is not in the room where the paper is being used, and the whole of the matter has built a sentence that gives a person eleven seconds and a sentence to say, and the sheet with two boxes is the reason the sentence is worth giving away.** She says the finding out loud to a stranger in a corridor: **a finding that binds nobody who has not been heard is a sentence with no supply of hearings in it**, and there is no form for compelling a person into a room. He asks for the plain copy for his counter and not the certified one. At about the tenth hour she puts the two documents on the same table for the first time and sees that **they are the same sentence in two halves, nine days out of order, four miles apart, and the half the high court gave is the half that does not work**, and she gets Marek out of the room and reads him both and he does not interrupt her once. The chapter closes with four entries in the book of the four sentences and **a written decision not to enter the fifth**, because a copy at a counter in a copyist's hand is a document about the copyist, and she chooses to be the one who copies this one and says why.

### Chapter 0093 — The Same Words the Other Way Round

Joane Tranter's POV, the chamber of the district court of Auremar's lower district and the yard at Lowcross, the fourth week of the eighth month to the first week of the ninth. A certification comes down from the Crown Kiln in a seal that is not the fire-and-water device, carried up the stair by a man who will not give a name. It is **a certification and not a grant and not counted among the four things the court has granted in nine years**, and it certifies the ruling of the twelfth of the ninth as operating **in the district of Mosswake, at the field, the channel, the Reed Cut gate, the bank works and the household named in it, and in no other place whatever**, and expressly not as a rule for the empire. She reads it out to about nine people, saying what it is about, who wrote it and how it came, and then says out loud the sentence the chapter is named for: **this is the same sentence this case has spent fourteen months trying to get somebody to say with the direction of the words reversed**, and that she agrees with it because of what she found on the ninth of the eighth — a thing open to challenge is a thing challenged where it is applied, and if the ruling travels into a district that never heard of Aldren Venn then a girl of fifteen there is a guarantor because a man in another district was, **and that is the echo, and the echo does not care about the addresses.** She notices that four of the five places named are things a person can be shown and the fifth is a list of work, and that a list of work is the only kind of sentence anybody can act on the same day, and that nobody has looked at the fifth term's schedule.
