She then finds, by accident, in her own file, that **the order of the court sitting in room four of the eighteenth of the seventh is one half of the sentence she found herself nine days later and neither court has read the other.** Her minute enters that, and that the finding of the ninth of the eighth is not varied, and the cost in figures: worth one place, about four hundred and sixty households, forty-six district courts and one landing office not bound, about four hundred and forty people untouched, the sheet of the twenty-second of the ninth in nine hundred and forty copies expressly not touched — and that **the movement asked for it and the asking was the finding.** At about the half past four she reads it out at the gate at arm's length in about four minutes, the fourth time she has done that and the first time it has been good news, and Hester Crane asks *is it about me* and is told no, and then the magistrate says the other half, and says the bill is unpaid and the fund has no line for a bridge in it, and Hester Crane says *read it out to somebody while you are still able, that is my grandmother's whole of it and she was not read to either.*

### Chapter 0094 — One Line Long

Marek Kest's POV, the Docket of Powers and the Received Room, the second week of the ninth month, chiefly the twelfth. Oswin Marrow says his three powers out loud in advance, as he does, and none of them is what Marek has come about, and the fourth thing is the register: one page to a year, four lines to an entry, about four hundred and ten entries in thirty years, about fourteen a year, **because a reading needs a permission and a person only agrees to one when there is no other way of getting at the record.** A bar is a line in a book with a disposition against it, and the only procedure for being entered is a written application of one line stating the authority under which the person reads, about twelve hundred years old. Marek says the reason he did not write one in the fourth month of last year, and Marrow asks for the other reason, and Marek says it: **a register of persons is a list of doors, and the only difference between this door and the four in a certifier's book in the Kiln sheds is a rule at the top of it and a name at the top of the entry and a person who keeps the book** — and he is the man who wrote down that a list of people who can be found is a list of doors. Marrow says the office cannot answer whether a man with a tray is more dangerous with a register in front of him, and that all this office can do is stop a person auditing, ask nobody anything, produce nothing, put nobody in a room. Marek writes the line, and the fourth line of it names **an authority that has ended**, and he asks for the entry to say that the bar was discharged by his own line and by nothing else, because a clerk who fills in a gap in a reason is a clerk who has decided what the reason was. Marrow says the sentence out loud: **a bar is discharged by a person putting his own name on a page, and the line has been there for about twelve hundred years, and he had known about it since the third week of the eleventh month and did not write it for nine months.** Tamsin Rook is in the doorway and says the one thing: **this is the first instrument in fourteen months with his name at the top of it that he asked to be inside**, and in four years a man with a tray will be able to say *there is a court order about him and you may read it*, and the answer will be yes, and there will be no second question. That night he writes that he had a whole day with nothing in it he was allowed to read, and that he was not disappointed, and that the not being disappointed is what he has been afraid of for a year. Marrow then says the fourth thing: there is one more power and he is telling him before he uses it, and he can refer one question to room four.

### Chapter 0095 — In One Sentence

Marek Kest's POV, the Docket, the Received Room and the copying alcove, the third week of the ninth month, chiefly the nineteenth. Marrow's referred question is one line and its last clause is the design of the chapter: **whether the eligibility and the terms are to be in one instrument or in two**, because that court has been asked twice in fourteen months to move a finding and has moved it twice. The answer comes back in **one sentence with the eligibility and the watch in it, deliberately**, and a training that is not a certification: a reading of a file, in a room, with a person in it who is not the person being trained, on a day fixed, out of an afternoon, with no fund because there is no fund. The watch: a person from an office, in this matter unmentioned, may read anything the watched person writes, and the watched person may not be in a room without a person in it who is not from that office — **and the office is not named and cannot be named, because a person who signs a column headed *by whose authority* is discharging an office, and an office cannot be examined, and an office that is named and cannot be examined cannot be instructed.** Tamsin Rook says the sentence aloud once and then the one sentence that matters: **this is the first instrument in fourteen months with his name at the top of it that he asked to be inside**, and a document with a person's name on it is a weapon and a target. The undertaking is exercised and he gives no reason, the only time he has given none. She asks the only question: have you read the sentence about the room, and the only person in this matter who is not from the office is a copyist of the third gallery standing in a doorway. Then she says the thing she has been carrying for eleven months: **for eleven months he has been the only findable person and she the only one who cannot be instructed, and between them they have been an office of two people with no office on it, and now a court order has made her the mechanism of a watch, and she has decided to say that out loud rather than decide.** At the Docket, at the door, Marrow answers three questions in the order asked: the watch has no date; the only person who can end it is Marek and the only person who can hear him is the copyist and neither can do anything with the answer; and what it is for is that somebody, in about four years, is going to have to write down that a person was heard. Then: **a bar and a watch are the same instrument, and the difference between them is a column and about four hundred yards.** That night he writes the watch as the price and not a qualification, and the sentence that a person who wants protection against a seat cannot have it without also having something he cannot get rid of, and that nobody has asked him whether he wants the eligibility or the watch, and there is no form for asking a person whether they want a thing a court has just done to them.

### Chapter 0096 — The Office That Does Not Exist

Tamsin Rook's POV, the copying alcove, the Received Room and a lane off the Kiln road, the fourth week of the ninth month to the first week of the tenth. Her custody form of the eleventh of the ninth month of last year is four lines and has `None` in the office box, and for eleven months the reason it was safe was that nobody can send anything to a person who cannot be instructed — and a court has now entered that **a room with a watched man in it has to have her in it**, and the reason she is safe is about to be the reason she is useful. She writes the reason out and finds it is not a reason any more. She then writes four lines of her own with a duty and a limit and no office: she holds the copies, anybody may have one at four pence, the column headed Given is in her book with her hand against every line and she can be found on purpose, and **the office of the receiving person, if any, is `None` — and on a page about herself that is not a box on a sheet, it means she is the receiving person and there is no office behind her.** The limit: **anybody may ask her to stop, with a reason, written under the column with the day, and on that day the copies go to the four holders in four hands and the column goes with them** — a girl of seventeen at a chandler's counter, a clerk at a river court, a works foreman, and her own colleague's sister, four pages that do not agree and that she is not going to tidy. Then she writes out what such an office is worth: it cannot be compelled, instructed, transferred or audited, it cannot be paid for, given a wage, given a room or given a day off, it cannot die in office, **and it can only stop** — and that an office that cannot be instructed cannot be instructed to hand itself on, so there is no successor, and the only way to stop one is for the person in it to stop. Marek gives one reason and it is a fact: **the sheet says in its third line that a person is not to be given advice, and this page is the only page in the matter about what she does to the people who come, and if the two are in one hand in one book then a woman handed a piece of paper in a lane is also a person who has been told what this office is — that is advice by proximity.** So the sheet is ruled at her bench and the office is kept in the locked book, in two places. She walks down the lane off the Kiln road, stands at the end of it for a quarter of an hour and does not knock, and writes down three reasons, the third being that **the woman with the barrow of hot water has been writing `None` for four months without knowing what it means, and there is no way to send a correction, and if she says the word out loud tonight the next person who thinks of it has to hide it.** The rest of the week is ordinary: four sheets and two and one returned, a woman who thought there were three boxes, the four pages coming back still disagreeing. On the last day of the ninth month she uses the rule they made with each other, for the first time in four months, about whether the word *office* is allowed to appear on the page, and he says yes, because it is not an office.

### Chapter 0097 — A Letter Is Not an Instrument

Ivet Sarn's POV, the clerk's window at the Mosswake Roll Court, the board, the register of pending duties, the embankment corridor and the upper lane, the second week of the tenth month. Two transcriptions come down the river, both with five words on the front and no office and no name, and she writes out what she is reading *before* she copies anything. She puts the board's figures up unchanged: **thirty-one of about sixty, eighteen returns did not come back, six copies never made**, twenty-two seen and nine told and neither a hearing, eleven with a word and no name, twelve back from the post with a stamp, fifty-four for three cycles and six in a fourth cycle never made. She writes out what the answer of the eighth of the third does, in five points, and says the sentence the chapter is named for: **you were sent a letter and a letter is not an instrument, and there is no sentence in this empire addressed to a court about a letter it wrote.** Iolanthe Rees, sixty, of the thirty-one, comes for the third time at the third hour so as not to be seen in a queue, puts a shilling on the sill, and asks for the third time whether the wall is for her. Sarn takes the shilling before she answers, because **a person who has paid can answer without owing anybody** and gratitude is a debt with no figure on it. She tells her, and eleven people, that a hearing will be offered, that it will end with nothing different in her house in four months, and that if she wants it she is to come and ask and Sarn will not tell her what to do about it. Then the two things that are hers: the letters of the sixth year were written at that window or above it and she is the clerk and does not know which hands, and **she is a fifth instance of the thing two courts have now said four times, at a lower desk, and the fourth column of her own register is empty thirty-one times because she did not ask.** And the entry about the fourteenth of the seventh: she walked up the upper lane with Rennie Sallow's own line in it and said four words and it was nothing, and the reason is that **a line in a register is not a duty, and he is nobody's duty, and what he is owed is an apology, and an apology is not a thing a court supplies and there is no column for it.** The embankment corridor: the flue cut on the fourteenth of the second month, the press under an oiled cloth, the fourth cycle gone for ever, the refusal of the eighteen letters in four lines, and the last line written at the foot of the whole wall: **a wall is a thing that a person looks at, and this is the first time I have written on one what it cannot do, and there is nobody in this room.**

### Chapter 0098 — What a Conviction Is For

Silas Sowle's POV, the chamber of the district court of Auremar and his own office, the third week of the tenth month, chiefly the eighteenth and the twenty-first. The original of the ledger is produced on the direction of the trial and goes on a bench. The book has ninety-one leaves numbered consecutively; the wrapper it was copied from is inside the back board, endorsed ninety-two in the copyist's hand of the sixth year, with the word *complete* against ninety-one and no figure against it. The certification of the copy is read out. The court enters four lines and then adds, on its own motion, that **this court makes no finding as to how the difference arose and directs that nobody be asked about it in any proceeding in this court or elsewhere.** Marek waits in the corridor with a sheet with four words on the front, which is their own rule, and puts a question and a date to it, and tells him out loud, on a stair, what he thinks Sowle will say. The answer, in Sowle's own office, on the twenty-first at about the sixth hour: **there is a use and it is a shilling a copy and it is the only one**, and the finding is that **a conviction is a fact about a person in a room and is not a licence to go and find out who his clients were, and the line between those two is the whole of the difference between a court and a mob, and the line is about eleven seconds wide, because it is the length of a person standing up** — and a woman of sixty-one who has said no twice and signed nine things she could not read will be told about a man in a good coat, and the lane will be hearing about a word, and the word came out of a printed sheet in nine hundred and forty copies before the man was born. The use: a copy of the record of the trial in plain words at a shilling, sold at a counter, **not read out in a lane**, so a person with a mark on a card can put it in front of a trade assessor. He reads the record out in full, including the limited plea and the court's own entry that no person in it has asked for the name of a client or a house and will not, and says that in nineteen years he has never had a document he could hand to a woman that nobody can be sent to because of it. The clerk of that court asks whether the movement may use the conviction to find out who sent the man the work, and is told no, and the reason is not kindness: **a person who will not write a name cannot be compelled to, and that is the whole of the protection, and a process that gets at names is a process that gets at anybody's**, and four or five certifiers worked that out in about three weeks by doing nothing at all. Marek's book that night records the finding, the gap in the copy, and the fact that he is not going to look for a ninety-second leaf.

### Chapter 0099 — An Account and Not a Finding

Halvard Riet's POV, the Inheritance Desk in the third gallery, a bench and a day's book, the fourth week of the eleventh month to the first week of the twelfth, chiefly the second of the twelfth. Nobody asked him for it. He took the Kest box on purpose in the first week of the ninth month of last year and the only record that he chose is a clerk's handwriting in the margin of a day's book. He writes nine heads of figures, every one of them already on the page: the four sentences in fourteen months with what each cost and paid, and the order of the eighteenth of the seventh as the other half of the fourth; the two things refused in a room with a witness and what is in their place, including that not one shilling of the nineteen pounds three and fourpence came out of a bar, a watch, a grant or a refusal; the die, 44,819, a number of a book about a hundred yards away that nobody has read; the plate, seven plates and none identifying a house; the gap in the copy, ninety-one and ninety-two, and the fact that the only proof the original was ever ninety-two leaves is a figure in a copyist's hand on a wrapper; the thirty-one with every figure of the arithmetic and the fourth column and the flue and the fourth cycle and the refusal in four lines; the crossing, with the bill, the fund, the absence of a line for a bridge in nineteen years, about fifty-seven pounds spent on a tally and never claimed, four hundred and eleven people and three hundred and eighty-eight crossings that is a number of a bar; the sheet, four pence, three and a quarter pence above the cost, out of a copyist's own wage; and the schedule nailed to a rail at a gate, which nobody has sent anybody to read, because a schedule is a thing you read when you are about to do the work. He then adds the figures, gets nothing, adds them again in a different order, gets a different nothing, and writes both down and enters neither. **The finding is that the figures do not add because they are numbers of different things, and a clerk who adds them makes a person liable, and an account that reconciles is a lie.** He then lists six figures he could not check with the reason against each, all of them ending in *nobody can ask*, and adds an eighth item to his own copy of the handover list and nothing to the copy of the six. The last entry in the day's book says that an account is a statement of figures and not a finding, because a statement can be checked and a finding cannot, and that **he is not going to write one line of this that says anybody was right, and the reason is that in about nine years the person who has to answer for this box is not going to be him.**

### Chapter 0100 — The Fifth Term's Schedule

Tavi Venn's POV, Reed Cut: the gate, the rail, the platform and the bank, the second to fourth week of the twelfth month, chiefly the twentieth at the seventh hour. The schedule is on good paper, folded once, held on by four nails, tied on with an oiled cord on the evening it went up; it carries the water office's device, a memorandum number, a date, and a foreman's name at the foot, and **no person's name at the top of it and no price on it at all.** Tavi is seventeen, has been on that bank since she was old enough, was examined for canal work on the twenty-sixth of the fifth, and can be taken on by a master on a written engagement. She reads the sheet out twice in plain words — ditch, bank, abutment and kerb, gate reset to a maker's gauge, the rail's new pins, with two men and a boy against each and days against each. A girl of about eleven comes to the rail with a pail at the seventh hour and asks the first question: *is there work in it.* Then the second: *whose name is on it*, and is told there is nobody's name on it and that a mark is not a person and a device is not a person, which Tavi knows because her brother has explained it to her four times and got slower each time. Then the third: *if you stop, whose name answers for it*, and is told nobody's, because the office's name answers and the office is not a person and a list that goes on a list again is the safest paper in the world and the easiest to forget. Then the fourth, at the speed of a thing carried a long way: *who read that to you* — and is told she read it to herself, and that Tavi is not paid, has not signed anything, and that **there is nothing on that rail for you to sign**, which is the whole of what the paper is. Then the fifth, which Tavi does not answer: **and who is it that reads it to the ones who cannot read it.** Tavi does the arithmetic instead and says the figure is fourteen months, and then says the reason nobody came is that it is a schedule and a schedule is read when you are about to do the work, and the work was done, and a thing that has been done does not get read, and a rail is a rail. Then she says the volume's question out loud and does not answer it: **if the water office cannot always do it, then somebody has to, and the duty handed down to a person who has not been asked comes out as a child.** The girl says that then somebody will come and ask her to do it for nothing, and will say it is her mother's river, and Tavi says she has been waiting fourteen months for somebody to say that to her and would rather it had been a man in a good coat. Nothing is fixed. The last page names the four questions as they came off a stone in a lane four hundred and thirty miles away, adds the fifth, and ends on the rail, the four nails, the gate and the river, and the fact that neither of them is a court and neither has ever pretended to be.

## Volume 03, Batch 0001 (Chapters 101–110) — The Slade Cut, and a Duty a Person Can Stop

**This is the first batch of Volume 03. The volume is open at Chapter 110 of 150.**

### Chapter 0101 — A Name at the Foot of It

Halla Wray's POV, the Slade Cut, the bank, the platform and the tool shed, the first week of the first month, chiefly the seventh. The Slade is a ditch four miles above the Reed Cut gate and its bank is eleven feet high in the second month and about five by the third week of the first, and a third of the way along it the outer face has gone over nine inches twice in nine years. The Slade Cut Company is a cooperative of about ninety-one households with about sixty children under sixteen, kept in a book and not in any office. There is a shed with a nail in it and a list of works on the nail that has the crew's names on it and **has never had a name at the foot of it in sixty years.** The crew is five men and a boy at **eight pence a man and five for the boy**, which is **forty-five pence a day on the five and one**, a fortnight at **two pounds twelve and sixpence**, and the works fund's head has been empty since the third week of the twelfth month. Her brother **Piotr Wray**, twenty-nine, asked whether she was going to put the Company's name on the list and was told it was not a question he had standing to ask. She copies the list out again, **writes her own name at the foot of it**, and says the condition out loud in the shed to five men and a boy: a list of works gets a person's name at the foot of it, a person may read it, a Company is not a person and cannot read anything and cannot be asked whether it agrees. Then she says the volume's question before any lawyer has come up the river: **she will not put a household in a box and she will not put the Company in one either, because a company cannot be asked and cannot say no and lasts longer than she does, and the only people left in a company after everybody in it is dead are the children.** She takes the list to the weigh-house and the weigh-master writes a line under it saying the money is to be paid to the men on the list and not to any body, with the day on it, and that any person may come and read that line.

### Chapter 0102 — The Box at the Water Office

Sivra Oris's POV, the Reed Cut district water office, the counter and the back room, the second week of the first month, chiefly the ninth and the tenth. The office stands two hundred yards above the gate so a man can see the water and the office at once and issues flood memoranda, gate schedules and inspection dates for about four hundred and sixty households' worth of channels. **A district flood authority is nine or ten things a year and the only one anybody feels is a stop, and it can stop a crew and cannot make one.** The form is about a hundred and forty years old and its box is headed **who is to answer**, and nine years of practice is to enter the body that does the work, and she has done it twice herself and can name both. On the ninth, standing with four people in the room, she says the finding out loud: **an office can be instructed, a person can stop, and a body can do neither**, and that is the whole of what a body is good for and bad for, and that the box is a body only because finding a person was inconvenient. **She enters H. Wray and P. Wray without asking either, and writes on the face of the sheet that she asked nobody, and leaves the boy Raff Ordway out of the box on purpose and enters the leaving-out as the only protection the office was able to give anybody.** The four lines give the district's flood authority to those two persons and to no body, and enter that **this office cannot compel the works, does not claim to, and cannot be compelled to do them itself**, and that if the bank goes in the fourth month there is nothing here to compel anybody with. She refuses, in a room, to let her own sworn statement of the third of the fourth be used, unasked. The sheet goes up the river on the tenth.

### Chapter 0103 — Nine Hands

Rennick Adley's POV, a hired grain store off the Slade, the thirteenth to the fifteenth of the first month. The store is a stone building with the floor a foot off the ground, rented at a shilling a quarter, with the key in his sixty-eight-year-old mother's keeping for eleven years. On the bench is a book about two inches thick with **no heading at the top of it**, kept for about sixty years in nine hands, of which his is six, his aunt's nineteen, and one a man who could not write whose wife wrote the page and ran out of room in the middle of a year. The district's own book carries the Slade in nine years out of nine as *crews sent* and *works done* and has carried no person since the district was last mapped, and the district has been mapped twice since he was born. He works out that the Company's book is the only record anybody has, spends an afternoon proving it — four names off a random page, three answers in an hour and the fourth findable with an address — and writes down both halves: that a list of people is the only list anybody can act on, **and that a name is findable because somebody wrote it while the person was still alive, and that if the district had kept its book this way it would have known in four minutes who to write to, and instead it writes to a dissolved parish, and that is the whole of that district's harm for two hundred years and nobody called it harm because nobody kept the other book to compare.** He writes what the book is and what it is not on the first leaf with the day on it, reads it back twice, crosses nothing out, **rules a line under the six years he has kept it, and puts no name under the line on purpose**, because a book that says *nobody has taken this on yet* is a different sentence from *nobody will ever*. The ninety-one-year page carries eleven works and eleven names and a line saying the terms are in the instrument and not in the book, and **the sixth name cannot be read with certainty.**

### Chapter 0104 — No Resolution

Doreth Ferrow's POV, the mill yard off the Slade, the twentieth of the first month at about the seventh hour. About forty people come; nine others were not told or were told on the day. **The miller Aven Tace stands in his own door with his arms folded for the whole hour and says nothing at all, and afterwards gives the reason: there is no form for a miller to say *you may not stand in my yard*, and about forty of his customers were standing in it.** She opens by saying there is nothing to sign, and that about four of them have come to sign something. What happened in the preceding thirteen days is that **a keeper came up the river on the thirteenth, stood in a shed for twenty minutes, and told about forty people in plain words that she had put two people's names in a box without asking either, had written on her own sheet that she had asked nobody, had left the sixteen-year-old out of the box deliberately, and had given away the only power the office had** — and said both of the first sentences, that she is not the First Seat and not that seat's agent, before anybody asked. She also refused her own sworn statement to the yard without being asked. Piotr Wray speaks for four minutes and then says, in nine words, **Nobody asked me, so I am not going to sign**, and adds that the box was the right box and the name was the wrong one. She lets it sit half a minute, being a chair of nineteen years, and then says the two things the Company will do — keep the book, put a person's name at the foot of a list — and the two it will not — sign as a Company, put a household in a box — and **counts the yard out loud, and says there are about sixty children under sixteen in that cut who were not in the yard and cannot be in it, and that if anybody agrees to a thing tonight those sixty have been added to it without being in the room, and that is the same case that has been running for fourteen months in a city and it is running tonight in a mill yard with a body instead of a family.** She ends by saying there will be no resolution and that she is stopping because she has nothing else. Afterwards, at home, coat on, she says that the way to win a thing in a kitchen is to let the loudest person have it, and that tonight the loudest person was right, and that she has to hold both of those. The spring work is four weeks off. The book's whole annual budget is four shillings a year for a store.

### Chapter 0105 — A File Is Made of Other People

Marek Kest's POV, Auremar, the Docket of Powers' third room and the Received Room, the nineteenth to the twenty-third of the first month, with the training on the **twenty-second at the second hour of the afternoon.** He has never been in the third room. Oswin Marrow says his three powers out loud, says none of them is what he came about, and has found no fourth in four months; then he says the cost before he starts: **a training is a reading of a file, it is not a certification, no certification exists or is asked for, and the hour after the nine minutes is an afternoon of his own and there is no fund for it in this office or any other, and the court that ordered it entered that it knows who gives the afternoon in nine cases out of ten and does not know in the tenth, and this is the tenth and nobody entered it.** Tamsin Rook is in the room because the watch requires it and neither of them says why. The file is the Docket's own: two register entries, the counter's card, the bar, nine pages of examination, the Cinder Court's answer, the limited audit, the referral, the eligibility and the watch, and at the back a sheet headed *the file on the person* with nine dated items. The finding: **there is not one line in the file that he wrote; his name is on about eleven lines and the only decision of his anywhere is one line in a different book in a different room** — and a file is a box of what other people did, and putting a name on the box does not make the box about the name, it makes the name findable. Marrow gives him the other half, unasked: **a file is the only thing that can put a person in a room who did not want to be in it, a register cannot, a file does it with other people's hands, and a file only has to be produced.** He asks what he wants to be able to do and what he is willing to have produced, in that order. He asks for the training to be entered in the day's book with the day, as a training and not a certification, and **with the master's afternoon on the record because there is no fund.** At the door Marrow says the fourth thing he says to a person on the way out, and says that the instrument and the protection are the same person this afternoon and that he would like it written down by somebody who is not him. The chapter closes with his own book: the method he takes out is **write down what you are reading before you copy it and say who is not in the room**, which he has been getting wrong for fourteen months and which a woman at a clerk's window has been getting right for nine years; he has read no instrument and wants that written down because in four years somebody will say an auditor read something; the **fourth envelope is in a locked drawer and is not sent and he did not open it**; and he is going four hundred and thirty miles.

### Chapter 0106 — The Fourth Question at a Table

Tavi Venn's POV, a kitchen at the Slade end, the last day of the first month. She is seventeen, has been on the Reed Cut bank since she was old enough, was examined for canal work on the twenty-sixth of the fifth of last year, and **nobody asked her to come, nobody paid her, and there is no form of any kind in that office that says a person reads a sheet to anybody.** She came because a woman of sixty-four wrote on the twenty-fourth saying there was a sheet with two names in a box, that four men on that bank could read and one had said no, and that she would pay nothing because she had nothing and was not asking for advice. She puts the sheet flat with the writing turned for the people on the other side and reads it out **twice**, naming the box out loud, the first time as it is written. All four questions are asked and answered, and the fourth is asked by the woman of seventy-nine who can read about nine words: *who read that to you, and do they get paid if I sign* — and the answer is that nobody read it to her, that she is not paid a penny, and that there is nothing on the sheet for either of them to sign. Her answer to *if I stop, whose name answers for it* is **nobody's**, and she says out loud in a kitchen that this is the first time she has said it in a room. A woman of thirty-one asks why the sixteen-year-old is not in the box, and Tavi answers that a box is a place where a person goes and a person can be found and can say no, and that **a body in a box is a place where children go, and a body in a box is about everybody who will ever be born into it, and nobody has ever been asked whether they wanted to be born into a box.** Then she gives the answer to the fifth question of Volume 02, which she has been carrying for four weeks and has told nobody: **the answer is a person who turns up, and the whole of the cost of that answer is that the person who turns up is never paid and cannot be**, because a body with no money cannot pay a person. And her condition, said out loud to eleven people: she will read it to anybody who comes and she will not tell anybody what it means, and if somebody wants a name in a box she will say no and will not care whose name it is, including her own.

### Chapter 0107 — A Copy of a Copy

Ivet Sarn's POV, the Mosswake Roll Court's clerk's window, the board and the embankment corridor, the fourth and fifth of the second month. The wall above the sill already carries the order of the eighteenth of the seventh, the certification of the fourth week of the eighth, the answer of the eighth of the third, seven paragraphs of what the district has to do about it, four first-person lines about a clerk who did not ask in the sixth year, and at the foot in half-size letters the only instruction on it. **Marek Kest comes up the road with Tamsin Rook a minute behind him, and turns round and says everything that matters to her first, and she notes it and does not ask why and writes down that she did not ask why.** He puts a shilling on the sill before the hour is over, and she takes it before she answers, because a person who has paid can answer without owing anybody. She reads the district's flood sheet and then does the thing the afternoon is for: **she writes down what she is reading, before she copies anything, in the first person, and says who is not in the room** — a district flood sheet with two human persons' names in a box, a list of works with a foreman's name at the foot of it, and a note that the keeper entered the second name without asking. Then she tells him, unkindly, that he is going to do that for the rest of his life in about nine rooms that do not know about each other, and that the method came from a woman called Ansel who made her do it every day for four years and never once explained it. The answer is no, and it takes about nine minutes: **a scale instrument is certified by a notary's table and nowhere else, and that is the only thing that makes it worth anything against a dragon party, and a copy made by a district clerk proves that a district clerk copied something**; the certified copies are in a table in a city four hundred and thirty miles away in a run, and a run is a number of a book. **The only door in the empire is a door a person walks through, and the Company cannot ask, and Halla Wray cannot ask because nothing in the instrument touches her, and Adley cannot, and the boy cannot, and Ivet Sarn cannot on any of their behalf** — and then the second half: **a document about nobody cannot be asked anything by anybody either, and nobody is not somebody.** She says out loud that when somebody on that bank is harmed the door opens, and dates that, so that it cannot be said nobody thought of it. Marek Kest tells her the sentence cuts both ways and refuses to write it on her wall; she tells him it is her wall and she is writing it herself, in the first person, tonight. Tamsin Rook gives up a name and a lane in a room on purpose and Ivet Sarn refuses to be the woman who unmasked a person. One line is entered in Sarn's own book with the day on it, and it is the first line she has written in a year that is not about a letter a court wrote.

### Chapter 0108 — The Wrong Form

Leda Rell's POV, the Mosswake Roll Court on the flood terrace, the sixth to the ninth of the second month, sitting on the ninth. The application is made in the ordinary form for hearing a **body of persons** and the form has a line at the top for the head of the body, and there has never been a head of the Slade Cut Company, so the line is blank and nine names are in the body of it. A clerk of two years says it correctly — she cannot admit an application, only a magistrate can, and only with the people in the room — and Doreth Ferrow says admit it and enter that it is the wrong form, and she says she cannot do that either, and Leda Rell enters it on her own motion **before anybody has spoken, because if she does it after nine people have spoken it is an answer to nine people and she does not want it to be that.** Nine people are heard for two hours and a quarter: a chair of sixty-four, a book-keeper, a foreman, the brother who said no, a miller with a paper nobody has ever asked him for, two men off the Slade, a woman with two children, and a man of twenty-nine from a city who is not admitted, is not on any list, is not a party, is not a witness, is not named in the minute, **and says nothing at all — and the magistrate enters that he said nothing, and reads the transcript out afterwards because of it.** **About forty did not come**, and the clerk enters that because nobody asked, and the reason is that a body has no clerk and no door and no list and there is no board anywhere in the district with a name on it about a bank. The minute has four entries: **a body of persons is not a person and this court cannot make it one and neither can any other and there is no form that does it**; a hearing that brings nine persons out of ninety-one binds nobody who was not in the room; the flood sheet is not questioned and the keeper is not a party and is not examinable; nothing is ordered and nobody is liable. After it she reads out, standing, the **finding of the ninth of the eighth month of last year from a district court four hundred and thirty miles away** — a thing in general use may be challenged by a person it affects, in a room, before it binds anybody, and it binds nobody who has not been heard, and the price is that every precedent in the empire is now open to challenge including the ones that are right — **and the second finding of the same minute, that a person warned about a document is afraid of it and cannot get in front of it** — and says that the first half is nine days older than the half that works, that neither court has read the other, that she has had the transcript in a file for six months and never read it out, and that the reason is not humility and is that reading it out would oblige her to say what she thinks about it. Then the sentence, on her own motion: **I do not know whether I was right, and not knowing has not stopped me for nine years**, and this court will hear every application a body brings through a wrong form until she is told otherwise. A second own-motion entry reads out of her press an instrument of about nineteen years back, in scale form, with a seat's device and no person's name, whose eleventh, fourteenth and nineteenth clauses give the work to *the body of the households from time to time having the use of it* and the cost to *the same, and by none other*; it has been applied in about eleven matters and never to a body; a balance has stood in about a hundred and forty files for about nineteen years; **and the amount is not owed by anybody and has not been added up**, and the minute expressly enters that this instrument is not an instance of anything else that has been before the court and is not joined to the substituted wording, because a line between two things is a chain and a chain is an invention. The cost is entered with a figure. The clerk of the court asks whether she wants the number and is told no, and the reason is entered: a clerk who thinks somebody owes something is the beginning of everything that has gone wrong in that district since the sixth year.

### Chapter 0109 — The Weigh-House

Marek Kest's POV, the weigh-house at the Slade end, the fourteenth to the sixteenth of the second month. The weigh-master is unnamed, about fifty-eight, and explains in four minutes that eight pence a man and five the boy is **forty-five pence a day on the five and one**, that a fortnight of it is **two pounds twelve and sixpence** and that the list has not said a fortnight since the north ditch went on it and now says **nineteen days, which is three pounds eleven and threepence**, and that the list has to be the list, and that the works fund's head has been empty since the third week of the twelfth month, and that the Company spends its entire annual money — **four shillings a year** — on a store in a mill yard to keep the book dry. He has had a sheet in his coat for nine days and Tamsin Rook asks him for it in one word, and reads it standing at the bench, and understands it in four seconds. **Her reason against it is not that it is wrong: it is that it is the best piece of drafting anybody has put in front of her in fourteen months and she has been waiting since the twentieth of the eighth month for it and was wrong about wanting it.** It would move one specified consequence off two named persons onto ninety-one in rotation, every one of them a person who can be heard and can say no — **and it would make the Company the guarantor of every flood on that cut for ever by never asking anybody twice**, so that the ninety-first household is told by its neighbour it is nothing and the child of seventeen inherits it the way a district inherits a dissolved parish. He has not brought it to her for nine days because he knew she would say no and he would have had to do something about her no. **The undertaking of the fourth of the first month is then exercised for the fifth time and catches an absence**: she asks whether he has asked the ninety-one households, and he has not, and he says so, and says that not one of the four times anybody asked him whether he had asked anybody he had been able to say yes. She reads her reason out loud, gives no name, and asks that the fact of her having given a reason in that room be entered in the Company's book by its keeper, in his own hand. Halla Wray asks him the fourth question — *who read that to you, and do they get paid if I sign* — and is told nobody, and that he is a person who turns up, and is told the whole of his standing: eligible, untrained, no certification, no office, no fund, a grant that is over. **He pays three pounds eleven and threepence out of his own wage, having worked the sum out himself in front of the weigh-master and entered in his own book that he has not added up a bill in fourteen months and did it in a weigh-house; it is three weeks of a wage of seventeen shillings and a penny the week, and he says that figure out loud too, and will not let the money be called a gift or a fund, and asks that if anybody ever says a fund paid for the spring bank that it is a lie with a clerk's name on it.** The weigh-master asks what word to use in the ledger and four people argue for four minutes and settle on **held**. **There are no unpaid days: the crew worked nineteen days and were paid for nineteen days.** His own book at about the ninth hour records that he could write the instrument and that he is not permitted to, that the watch is the price and not a qualification, that being powerless has stopped feeling like a wound and started feeling like a fact, and that the best sentence anybody said in that room was said by a person who was not talking to him.

### Chapter 0110 — Eleven Names

Halla Wray's POV, the Slade Cut — the bank, the shed and the grain store — the twenty-first of the second month to the second of the third. The work took nineteen days instead of the fortnight the list was written for, because the north ditch was in the ninety-one-year schedule and had not been in anybody's list for about thirty years, and a foreman who cannot read a paragraph read the eleventh page of a book aloud to about nine men, and the ditch is in the list. **The bank holds.** It cost three pounds eleven and threepence out of a clerk's wage; the fund is empty; the district's flood authority is two people's names and a dragon who has given it away in writing; **and none of those three facts was fixed by the work being done.** The chapter restates at a distance, without reconciling, what is unpaid: the fifth term's schedule on a rail with a foreman's name and no person's name at the top, the fourth term as a new signature, a bridgewarden of sixty-one who has refused a second instrument three times, **nineteen pounds three and fourpence unpaid with no line for a bridge in the fund and a penny a day out of a kitchen box for nineteen years**, and the thirty-one on a board four hundred and thirty miles down the river with a fourth column reading *next review: none named* thirty-one times. On the twenty-second she holds the work in her own name with a date against it — the third week of the third month — and the Company is not entered anywhere, and the book-keeper enters it and **puts no name under his own entry**, and the foreman says the volume's finding out loud in the store: **a duty held by a person can be stopped, and the reason nobody on that bank could say yes or no for sixty years is that nobody was holding it, and a nobody cannot say no and cannot be asked** — and that the keeper's two names were not a kindness, they were the first honest thing anybody has done on that cut in sixty years, and honest things look exactly like a punishment. On the second of the third month the book-keeper reports that he has found the eleventh of the eleven names — **Tarn Skell, of the Slade** — in a map of nineteen years back as the head of a household of nine at the north of the ford, and in four houses of Skells there now. **And he does not go, and says out loud why, and the reason is that going would make a person a party.** Marek Kest answers the door three times and says it slower the third time: a person who is not a party cannot ask a notary's table for a certified copy; the Company cannot ask because a Company is not a person and a magistrate has entered that it cannot be made one; Halla Wray cannot ask because nothing in the instrument touches her; **and a document about nobody cannot be asked anything by anybody either, so the same shield that keeps ninety-one households from being joined is the shield that keeps them from ever finding out what they owe, and the only route to it runs through a person being harmed, and nobody on that bank has been harmed yet, and that is the good news and it is also the whole of the difficulty.** Tavi Venn reads the ninety-one-year page out loud twice for nothing, reads the sixth name both ways rather than guess, and a man off the lower Cut with four children asks whether there is anybody he can thank for the boy not being in the box, and is told the keeper's reason, and gives his name to the book, and the book-keeper says he is not going to start a column called *who* either. The last page: **the child of thirteen is not a debtor, and the reason is a form with two boxes on it, and the reason that form does not travel is that it has no run and therefore no addressee, and the reason somebody on a river knows the meaning of the word in its second box is that a copyist did not go and tell her, on purpose.** The water is in the fourth month.

## Volume 03, Batch 0002 (Chapters 111–120) — A Person Who May Be Made a Party

**This is the second batch of Volume 03. The volume is open at Chapter 120 of 150.**

### Chapter 0111 — What a Bay Costs

Rennick Adley's POV, the mill yard off the Slade and a hired grain store, the sixth day of the third month, at about the fourth hour of the afternoon and then from the fifth hour. **The north bay of the store building is thirty-one foot by nineteen, holds about thirty, has a bench against the north wall and a door in the west end whose lintel has dropped about half an inch in about forty years and does not shut.** It is not hired. It is empty from the middle of the fourth month to the middle of the ninth and has been unused for about nine years. **On the sixteenth of the first month a man said it was there and Rennick Adley said no, then said yes, then said he would have to ask somebody, and then said nothing at all for about eleven minutes, and nobody asked him again for seven weeks.** He asks the miller, **Aven Tace**, about fifty, standing in a door with a fanlight painted shut. The price is **nothing**, and the difficulty is that being free is the difficulty: the Company has four shillings a year and all of it is the rent of the other end of the same building, and a body that cannot be paid for cannot be compelled, and a room given to a body is a room nobody can be asked to open, and a thing which is free is a thing nobody can refuse. Tace gives him a paper too — **his own, written in the sixth year, four hundred and forty foot of outer face at the third-of-the-way place, nine dates in a miller's hand on which he saw the face move, two he can prove and seven he cannot with the reason given, and at the foot a line saying he sent it to the district works four times and it came back four times and nobody has ever come for it** — and says out loud why he never handed it over in six years: **the moment his name is at the top of a paper about that bank, that bank becomes a thing that can be asked about his name, and the seven he cannot prove are what somebody will want**, and he worked it out alone, in a kitchen, with nobody to ask, **and nobody had ever told him he was right.** At about the seventh hour, in the store, with the ninety-one-year page on his knee, the book-keeper writes a heading at the top of a fresh leaf for the first time in about fifty-nine years, and four lines about what the Company holds, and then **the first name against an entry in that book in sixty years: Aven Tace, of the mill yard, as a person and not as a crew and not as a debtor, with the day on it.** He says out loud that he is not going to start a column called *why not* and not one called *who*, and that a name in a margin is not a debt and not a claim and not a vote and not a party, and that **it is findable, and the price of being findable is that a man with a tray can come to that store and ask about the bay and the paper and a foreman's list and anything else in the book for eleven years, and that the honest thing to do about a body that cannot be compelled is to be easy to compel yourself.** He writes a foreman of the district works a four-line letter asking one question — *can the district works put a person's name against the Slade, and what is done to that person afterwards* — and seals it with a stone off the yard wall, which is the miller's seal, on a gatepost, and is not the Company's.

### Chapter 0112 — A Column With No Heading

Wendt Sayer's POV, the Reed Cut district works, a yard, a slipway, a wet rope and a book, the seventh and eighth days of the third month. He is the wrong side of sixty, eleven years in this office and thirty-one in the district, and holds **about four hundred names in the four pages of the copy list**, and paid two shillings and sixpence out of his own fund for a girl of seventeenth examination seven weeks before it was needed. **The district's return book has four columns and the fourth has no heading over it and has had none for thirty-one years**, because the man who ruled it in was not told to write one, and it holds **the name of whoever reported the work** — **a column of *who spoke* and not of *who answers*, and that difference is the whole of why nobody from the Slade has been in a box for nine years.** The letter arrives on the seventh at about the seventh hour and he reads it four times and does not answer it, because he cannot answer it at all. On the eighth he reads the last year of his own book and finds the Slade entered nine years out of nine as *crews sent* and *works done* — a crew is a body and a work is a list — with about four hundred people in the fourth column in three years, every one of them a person who walked into a yard and said a thing, **and the district has not had a person against that bank since it was last mapped, and the district has been mapped twice since he was born.** He rules a leaf at the back and writes, on his own initiative, asked for by nobody, that the spring bank was done by a body of households and that his office sent nobody, paid nothing, was not asked, and **could not have been asked because a company is not a person and cannot ask an office for anything**, and puts **his own name at the foot of it for the first time in nine years**, and writes in the same breath that the reason is that a name under an entry is a name a man with a tray can ask about. He says it out loud in the yard to four men and a boy: **a column with no heading over it is findable without being answerable, and he has thought in the first month of every year for nine years about writing a heading over it and has thrown the paper away twice, because the day there is a heading every one of those about four hundred people becomes a person who answers for something, and he has got the names.** He puts the cost on the page: the works' return goes up on the first of the month in a bundle with two other yards, a man above him has asked four times whether his figures are complete, and in eleven years no yard has ever returned *nothing done, not asked, could not be asked*. He answers the letter in four lines on the ninth. It is read out in a bay nine days later and it is the first thing anybody has said in nineteen years to tell a woman of sixty-four that her no was the right one.

### Chapter 0113 — Not One Day Past It

Piotr Wray's POV, the Reed Cut district water office — a counter, a stove in a back room, and about nine hundred forms in a shelf — the tenth day of the third month, at about the seventh hour. He has come to be taken out of a box and he is not taken out of it. **Sivra Oris gives him the correct procedure in four words: *you say no, I enter void, I enter the day* — and then the price, which is that the sheet of the ninth of the first month is the only instrument in the district that gave a flood authority to a person instead of a body, and that voiding it in the flood season leaves the district's flood authority undispensed, and **the office has already written on the face of its own paper that it cannot compel the works, does not claim to, and cannot be compelled to do them itself**, so there would be no name on that bank anywhere at all behind nine acres and thirty-one households.** She then says the structural thing: **every form in this empire is a form of one of two sentences, a person accepts or a person refuses, and there is no form for a keeper to say *give it back* and no form for a person to say *I am here and I do not want to be asked again*, and those are the same absence twice and it is the only authority this office has ever given away and there is no form for getting it back.** He asks for the third way and it can be done: nine words in her own day's book in about a minute, **and they will not be available to anybody, not to him, not to his sister, not to a court, not to a clerk, and that is the whole of what this office has ever been able to give anybody in nine years.** He is given a date he did not ask for: **the last day of the third month, and not one day past it**, because the flood season's memoranda begin in the fourth week and the standing of the sheet is settled upstairs and not by him, and **she is not going up in the fourth week to find out which way it has gone without her in the room**, and a refusal is worth more with a day on it and worth nothing without one. On the way to the door he asks whether she is the First Seat's, and she says no, and says she said it once in a shed on the seventh of the first month before anybody asked and is not going to repeat herself in her own building, **because the moment a keeper begins to repeat herself she has started to need somebody to believe her.** He walks back down the two hundred yards and works out the thing he has not once thought about in seven weeks: **the box was never for him. The box was for the bank.** A clerk of the office, about twenty-three, has read the nine words upside down and has said since that this is the first thing in two years of that job that has made her want to come in on a day she is not paid for.

### Chapter 0114 — In What Way the Instrument Touches You

Tamsin Rook's POV, Auremar, the Notaries' Table, the fourth counter and the passage behind it, the nineteenth and twentieth days of the third month. **The Table does not answer questions; it takes one, in a form, from a person, on payment, and answers it in about eleven weeks.** Marek Kest is four feet behind her, and she is there because a court order says so, and neither of them says it. The clerk at the fourth counter is **Marn Ottery**, thirty-four, eleven years in it, not a lawyer — and she says at the counter, before anybody asks, that **a foreman can be sent for and a clerk cannot, and about nine people a year find that out, and it is the worst fact about that room and the reason it has not been able to do anything wrong in eleven years that anybody could put in a minute.** The form is a third of a sheet, a shilling whether it is filled in or not, with four lines: a name, where the person may be found, the instrument by its day and year and what it is called, **and in what way the instrument touches you** — and everything up to the fourth is a fact and the fourth is the whole of the door. There is a fifth thing that is not a line: a place to put a day, **and the day may be a day before the instrument takes effect upon the person putting it in, and that is used about four times a year in this empire and is not a rule of the Table and is not in the form and cannot be put in by the Table.** Marek Kest says out loud, in words and not on a page, why: the finding of a district court of Auremar's lower district of the ninth of the eighth, **whose two halves are nine days out of order and neither of which has read the other, and he has read both and has never been in a room with both, and has tried four times in nine years to write to the other court and has never sent it.** The clerk writes nothing down, because nothing was said in a form, and says that if that finding is the whole of the reason, then every one of the about four times a year is a person gambling on a sentence half of which nobody has read. The fourth line takes eleven minutes and gives the batch's best sentence: **the form wants a person and the form is right to want a person, and the trouble is that what the instrument does was done to ninety-one households at once, and no person can say that in a line with their name at the top of it, and the reason they cannot is that the day they say it they are a party.** Tamsin Rook does not put her name to it and says so out loud in about four minutes — **no instrument in this empire has ever touched her, she has been findable on purpose for fourteen months, and she has never once been a person anything happened to** — buys the form for a shilling out of her own hand and does not fill it in, **because a form bought and not filled in is four pence of proof that the question was asked**, and **puts the price of the matter into a room and does not soften it: a wage of seventeen shillings and a penny the week, three pounds of it being three weeks, nobody paying his carriage, and a man of sixty losing a day and a half of his own afternoon to a watch, so that the whole of what it costs is about four afternoons and three weeks of one man's wage.** Marek Kest says he will not thank her, because that would make it a thing she did for him. The last thing is four words long and is asked of the clerk and not of the man: *who was the last one you had* — and the answer is **a woman of sixty-one, lower district, the fifth month of last year, with a stamped paper she could not read, who did not fill it in either, who has not been back, and whose blank the clerk has kept in a box of about four hundred under the counter, because a Table is the only place in this empire where a person can come up, ask a question, and walk away with a piece of paper that says they asked it.**

### Chapter 0115 — What It Is For

Marek Kest's POV, Auremar, the Docket of Powers' third room, the twenty-first day of the third month, from about a quarter before the second hour of the afternoon, and the Received Room at about the ninth hour the same night. He carries the wording of the fourth line and a blank form in a bag behind him. **Oswin Marrow, about sixty, says the three powers in advance and there is not a fourth, and says the cost before he starts: the third power is the only one that does anything, and referring a question costs him a day and a half of his own afternoon and there is no fund in this office or any other, and this is the fifth person he has done that for.** He says what a referral is worth: **nine days, a page, and nothing else in this empire** — the court sitting in room four has an order that binds nobody who has not been heard, there is no form for compelling a person to be brought to a room, about four people sit in a room on an ordinary afternoon, and the whole of the work the order creates is done by clerks at counters and women with barrows, none of whom is in the room where the order was made. He enters, of his own initiative, that a short question gets a short answer, and that about four people a year bring him one, and none of the four lines that comes back is about their bank. He refers a long question and reads it back and makes Marek Kest read it back. Then he explains the register, unasked: **it keeps persons and not records, which is the whole of why it is safe and also the whole of why it cannot help; about fourteen people a year agree to have a name in it; a name in it is a name a man with a tray can ask about for ever for anything, and it is the one door in this empire a person cannot shut; and the only procedure for being entered is a written application of one line stating the authority under which the person reads** — and Marek Kest has no authority, does not write the line, is not entered, and does not ask to be, **and the reason Marrow does not ask him to is that it is one line and could have been written in about nine seconds with a false authority in it, and a good day is not worth a name in a register put in for a man who was not entitled to it.** In the room, standing, in about four minutes, with a copyist in it, Marek Kest states the price of the one instrument he is not permitted to write, in four lines: **one specified consequence moved off two named persons onto ninety-one named persons in rotation; a term, a date and a right of refusal; the whole of its cost is named labour and named liability; and it is not a gift and it is not a fund and he is not paid.** And then, when the master asks him in the ordinary way **what it is for**, he cannot answer, and works out in the room that he knows the price and not the purpose, and that a document about a bank is a fact about a document and a person being heard is a fact about a person. Tamsin Rook says one sentence from the door: **a cost nobody can be shown the bill for is not a smaller cost, it is an unpriced one, and an unpriced one is a cost somebody else finds out about later.** At the door Marrow says the thing he has now said twice: a register keeps persons and not records, and it is safe because it cannot say what a reading did to a file, and it cannot say what a form does to a person either, **and in this empire the only two people who can say are one who has already been harmed and one who has not, and Marek Kest is the second of those and it is not a small thing to be.** In the Received Room that night he writes out the afternoon and crosses nothing out, and writes again, without crossing it out this time, **that there is a document in a room he works in that he has not opened, and that the reason he has not opened it is the reason he has not started a case, and that he has now written that sentence twice and both times because somebody in a room said something better than what he had.** He read no instrument, took no reading, and carries no mark.

### Chapter 0116 — A Rule That Certifies Itself

Doreth Ferrow's POV, the north bay of the grain store off the Slade, the twenty-eighth day of the third month at about the seventh hour. The bay is the largest room within two miles of the Slade end that anybody in ninety-one households can stand in, it is cold, and it is warmer than nine kitchens because nine kitchens have eleven people in them and this has thirty. **Nobody rented it. It costs nothing.** She counts the room out loud at the start, the way she has counted forty meetings, **and then counts the people who are not in the room, which she has never once done in nineteen years, and writes the two figures together and not apart on the back of her hand.** She has written a rule for the bay in her own hand and **cannot read it, and makes Rennick Adley read it out twice, because the first time is for the people who can see the words and the second is for the people who cannot, and nineteen years of meetings in kitchens have been one time only and the one time has been for the people who can read.** Five lines: a meeting when a person says so, with the place and the day written down; the chair counts the room and the not-room; **nothing is agreed to — anything a person wants agreed to is written down and read out and the chair says what it would cost, and then it stops**; any person may come and a person who cannot read a paragraph may come; and **this is a rule and not a resolution and not an instrument and not a vote and not a demand, it is issued by nobody, it binds nobody, and any person in the room may stand up and say they never agreed to it and be believed.** She says out loud what it is: **a certificate of a kind that certifies itself, issued by nobody, binding nobody, deniable by anybody, and the first paper this body has ever had, and in nineteen years everything she has done in a meeting has been something people could have stopped and she has never once had a thing they can go back on in the morning.** Then the second thing, about the free room: **a body that cannot be paid for cannot be compelled, a room given to a body is a room nobody can be asked to open, and a thing which is free is a thing nobody can refuse** — and a thing that is free is also a thing nobody can take off you, and if the miller is not decent about it she finds out in the ninth month, which is too late, and she has a year. Then Rennick Adley reads out a four-line letter from a foreman of the district works, twice, and about nine people follow the second reading, and she says the fourth thing she has said in this volume: **that is the first time in nineteen years that anybody has stood up in a room and told her she was right, and he did not say *you were right, Doreth* — he said the district could not have been asked and put a number on the reason, about four hundred people in three years who walked into a yard and said a thing and are not answerable because a man ruled a column thirty-one years ago and nobody told him to write a heading over it, and that is the shape of the twentieth of the first month.** Then she has the bench carried out of the wall and into the middle of the room, about nine foot and heavy, four people and four minutes, puts the miller's paper flat on it and stands back, and says **a thing on a bench is findable, and from tonight there is a bench in the middle of this bay and things get put on it, and it certifies nothing, and I have been a chair for nineteen years and I have never once had a bench** — and that she has made it so nobody is answerable for it and anybody may deny it in the morning, and that this is the only way she can think of to have a paper and not have a debt. About nine people spoke and nothing was agreed to. A foreman of fifty-one was in that bay and **her term had run out in the third week of the month and nobody asked her to hold it again, and Doreth Ferrow noticed and did not ask, and has not told anybody she noticed.**

### Chapter 0117 — Nine Stones

Tavi Venn's POV, the lane of channel-side houses at the north of the ford on the Slade and a landing below it, the twenty-ninth day of the third month, about the sixth hour in the morning to about the fifth hour. **The seventh work on the page from ninety-one years back is the ford, and the stones, and the two posts, and it is on nobody's list and has not been on anybody's list for about thirty years, and the only reason it is on a piece of paper anybody has read this month is that a girl of seventeen can read a paragraph.** Somebody said the ford wanted looking at, in passing, about the seed, to nobody in particular, and it has not been repeated. **There is no form of any kind that says a person walks two hours to look at a ford, and she is not paid and nobody asked her.** What she finds: **the two posts are not broken, they are out of their sockets and squared off and stacked on the low side, and nine stones are out of the throat of the ford and stacked too, four and five, in the way a man stacks things he means to come back for.** A man of thirty-eight with a barrow says it straight: he took them in the fourth week of the second month, came back in the first week of the third, **nobody has asked him to stop, nobody in this district knows the ford is down, and he did it because he had a cart and a boy and no other hard stone within two miles, and he had not looked at the posts in about eleven years** — and he has not put them back, and says why: **if he puts them back the ford is the ford again and he is the man who took nine stones out of it, and the ford is the ford whether they are in it or not.** A post in a ford is a depth mark, and **a ford with no depth marks and no hard stones in the throat is not a ford, it is a place where the water happens.** Tavi Venn **does not ask his name and says out loud why** — the moment a name goes on a paper about a ford it is a name anybody can ask about the ford, she has watched two names go into a box and one man refuse in nine words, **and this is a condition she stated in a kitchen and will not care whose name it is.** A woman in a doorway says the stones went down to a landing below the ford, and that anybody in the district may read the book there. Tavi reads the miller's paper out of her own bag, twice, in a lane, and will not say what it means. A woman of about seventy-one in the fourth house, who cannot read a paragraph and has not pretended to for fifty-five years, comes out into her own door, hears both readings, asks one question, is told the girl will not tell her what it means and would not be able to put it anywhere, says *I know you are not, that is why I am still standing here*, and goes in and shuts her door. On the way back she stops at a landing and reads a **slate in a wooden frame on a nail in the open on the end post**, kept there for about nine years because a customer wants to know whether his stone has gone: **a record and not a register, and not evidence of anything against anybody.** It carries *third week of the second month, nine stones, hard, off the ford, north of the crossing, the throat. Hob Bray. Thirty-eight. Paid.* — **and that is the first person made findable in this matter by a record nobody made on purpose, and a girl who declined to ask his name got it off a nail in the weather and told nobody in the lane that she had it.** Standing at the landing with her hand on the wet rope she works out **the second half of the answer to the fifth question of Volume 02: a person who turns up has to be told what she is turning up about, and nobody told her, and she went anyway, and the going is the whole of the cost and it is not paid and cannot be** — and does not tell anybody, and will not, because stating it in a room would be asking somebody to pay her, and she would then be a person who reads for money.

### Chapter 0118 — A House for Sixty Years

Bel Skell's POV, the lane at the north of the ford, the thirtieth day of the third month, morning to about the second hour of the afternoon. She is about seventy-one, in the fourth house, cannot read a paragraph and has not pretended to for fifty-five years, has been in that lane sixty years and her mother was in it before her, and **nothing has ever been written about any of the four Skell houses by anybody, in sixty years** — and a Skell in a map of nineteen years back and a Skell on a page from ninety-one years back have never been put in the same sentence by anybody including her. In flat light a two-wheel went into the ford at the shallow place **because the shallow place is not there any more**, and a boy of fifteen went in to about his waist and got out, and cannot stand on that leg. She gets him out of the wet, gets nine people out of nine doors in one sentence, and **then has the rest of it, which is about a boy and not a leg: the boy is her sister's boy, not by the book — his mother was Sef Skell and died in the fifth year and married a Bray at the fourth house below the mill, and he has gone by Bray ever since and slept at her house since he was four, and he is fifteen and he is a Bray and he is a Skell and she is the only person in the lane who could say which, and she said it inside four minutes because she is not going to be asked for it later.** Then the hard part: **she has never given a name to a piece of paper in sixty years, because she cannot read a paragraph and if her name is on a paper there will be a paper about her leg and she will never know what is on it and somebody will** — and she gives one, out loud, in front of nine witnesses, unasked, for a doctor, and says what is happening to her: **she has been a house for sixty years and being a house has been the safest thing she has ever been, and the moment she says a thing out loud she is a person.** The doctor comes in about three hours, looks at the leg for a quarter of an hour in front of nine people, says he has seen it before, and **says nothing about the future**, which she has since described as the best thing anybody has done in that lane in her lifetime. **There is no count of that ford and there never has been: a count of a ford is a number of crossings and not a number of people, and a ford crossed on a plank and not on the stones has no count at all, and nobody in this district can be asked for one**, so the lane counts itself, twice, in two hours, and gets the same number both times. At about the fourth hour **Halla Wray comes up the lane having walked it**, and in about nine minutes writes on the back of a foreman's list — the two posts in their holes to the depth as they stood, the nine stones bedded, the ford looked at as a work and not as a place where a cart went in — and **puts her own name at the foot of it and a date against her name and reads it out once to the lane**, and says out loud that her term ran out in the third week of the month, that nobody asked her to hold it again, and that she is not going to let anybody take it that she was asked. The lane's second question is asked at about the fifth hour by the woman of about thirty-one with two children: **whose name is on it.** And Bel Skell says only the foreman's, and then says the thing the chapter is for: **nothing has ever been written about these four houses in sixty years and that was not a protection, it was a luck — it was the luck that nobody had ever needed a name from that lane, and a lane nobody wants anything from is the safest place on this river, and now a cart has gone into the ford and a foreman has come up with a list, and about nine people have heard their own lane said out loud as a place where a thing happened, and a house that is known to be a house where a thing happened is findable, not for that, for ever, and for everything, and not only for that** — and that the boy is fifteen and there is not one form in this empire a boy can be kept out of if a person is named somewhere else in it. And then the finding she came out of two days on a wall for: **a name is findable and a family is not. *Skell* is a list of four doors and Tarn Skell's son is not a thing anybody can look up**, and the good half and the bad half of it are the same fact and she has not been able to get them apart.

### Chapter 0119 — What a Party Is

Halla Wray's POV, the Slade Cut — the tool shed at about the seventh hour on the thirtieth day of the third month and the bank at the third-of-the-way mark after that. **Her term ran out in the third week of the third month, she has said out loud in four rooms that she would rather be asked than not, she has not been asked, and she did not know until this week that she could not say yes twice.** Her own book-keeper asks, having worked out that a man who keeps a book is not a person who can ask, and Piotr Wray points out in the silence that this is the first time anybody has asked. **She says yes, with a condition: the crew is paid out of something with a figure on it and not out of a man's wage in a city, because she is not going to pay a crew twice in one year out of two men's pockets and say nothing about it, and if nobody can pay for it the work waits rather than be done a second time for nothing** — and she puts the date on it herself, **the second week of the fourth month, and not one day past it, because that is the last week before the water.** Then, standing, in about nine minutes, with nine people in the shed, she says what a party is: **a person with a door — a person who can be found, who can be served, who can be put in a room, and who can say no, and the last of those is the only one worth a farthing and it is the one nobody asks about**, and that she has been one for ten weeks.
