She gives out the four words she has from a copy of the order of the court sitting in room four bought for four pence out of the shilling — the first thing that book has cost in eleven years except the rent — which she cannot read and which her brother read to her twice in a kitchen: *has no operation upon any person until that person has been heard about it in a room.* **That is a fact about a person and not about a document, and it is the one thing available before the water, and it is available to one person at a time and never to ninety-one households at once**, and she has been trying for ten weeks to get a thing available to ninety-one households and there is nothing and there never was going to be anything. She says the price out loud because she has just learned it: **asking four households makes them parties on the day of asking and findable from that day, for ever, and for everything, and not only for the thing at the ford, and a household that is findable is a household a man with a tray can ask about, and none of the nine people in that shed can know in advance which tray it is.** And she says where she learned it: **Aven Tace worked the same thing out alone in his own kitchen in about six years with nobody to ask, and the reason he could not hand in his paper is the reason she has just said, and she has been doing it to him for nine years without ever thinking about it, and she did not know that was a thing a person could be caught doing, and that is the whole of how anybody in this matter has ever found out anything.** Then the decision: **four households at the north of the ford are to be asked, in advance, whether they will be heard — four households, and this Company is not going to choose them, and the four are the four because those are the four and not because anybody chose them, and if this body had been allowed to choose four it would have chosen the four that come to meetings, the four that can read a paragraph, and the four that have not had a bad thing happen to them yet, and the four everybody picks are never the four the thing is about.** Nine minutes, and six of the nine repeated it afterwards. She refuses to repeat a sentence about a seat, says if anybody wants it they can go and ask a keeper and she will not repeat it either, **and one hearing is enough for both of us.** The entry goes in the store at about the ninth hour with the day on it, and the book-keeper writes that nothing is agreed, nobody is chosen, the Company is not a guarantor and not a party and not answerable, **and that there is no name under the entry because he is not the holder of anything.**

### Chapter 0120 — The Line at the Top of It

Rennick Adley's POV, the Mosswake Roll Court on the flood terrace, the clerk's window, the counter, the sill, the embankment corridor and the press behind it, the last day of the third month, from about the ninth hour to about the fourth hour, having walked four miles up a towpath the night before and four miles back in the dark. At four doors at the north of the ford: **two names in under a minute each, one in about eleven minutes after four questions about what the paper was for, one given before the asking was finished by a woman of about seventy-one who said that a paper going into a building that recognised the room was a better paper than most things that had ever come up that lane**, and **one refusal — a woman of about thirty-one with two children, who said her name was on the back of a thing in a shop in the district below the mill and that she did not know what two of anything was for, and let herself be described.** At all four doors he asked whose grandfather was Tarn Skell, **and nobody could tell him: one had been given the name in a kitchen and had never asked, one said there had always been Skells, and one said the name was not on any of the stones in the lane and that a man who wanted a family should try a churchyard instead of a lane.** The form is the ordinary form for hearing a body of persons and **the line at the top of it is for the head of the body, and it is blank, and it was blank on the sixth of the second month and on the ninth, and there has never been a head of the Slade Cut Company and there is not going to be one.** **Ivet Sarn, thirty-eight, nine years at that window, says before he has the paper out that she is not going to ask him to fill in that line, and then says the thing: a clerk cannot admit an application; on the ninth of the second month a magistrate did it on her own motion before anybody had spoken; there is a magistrate in that building and she is not in the room and is not on the floor, and she does not have to be, and there is a written undertaking in this court's own minute book of the ninth of the second month saying this court will hear every application a body brings through the wrong form until she is told otherwise, and it is not a form and it does not require anybody to be in the room** — and Sarn enters on the face of the form that it is admitted in the wrong form under that undertaking with the magistrate not in the room and not asked. Then she makes him work out the cost of a form at that window in about four minutes, and it is written at the top of it in her own hand: **these four persons may be heard and are parties from the filing and cannot be un-named, and the filing is the making of them parties, and a decision with nobody's name at the foot of it is worth about a fifth of one with a name** — the first figure she has ever put on a form, out of her own nine years, and she writes it on the face of the paper because she will not let him do it in a cold way. He writes the application out in about forty minutes, with the head of the body entered as **none, there is none and there cannot be one**, and four persons to be heard, two named and two described, with the court entering that **a described person is a person and is harder to serve and easier to lose and is worth a good deal less than a fifth of a named party, and the alternative is that she is not there at all.** The application also enters that it binds nobody who has not been heard, that about thirty persons of about ninety-one households came to a room on the twenty-eighth and about sixty did not and are not parties, that a body cannot be summoned, cannot be be a party and cannot ask the court for anything, and that the four are the four because there are four and not because anybody chose them — and when the clerk stops him four lines from the end to point out that he has copied **her own transcription slip, *can be be a party***, out of nine years of day-books and into a document with somebody's name at the top of it, she says keep it, and that she is going to enter that she noticed and let it stand. Three dates are in the matter and two are on the form: **a sitting in the first week of the fourth month, a water in the third week of the fourth month, and a third thing on the last day of this month that he does not know about and has deliberately not tried to find out, on the grounds that if there is a thing in this matter a book-keeper of a company is not to know about then that thing is probably the only thing holding that company together, and nobody in this matter can move any of the three.** He pays the shilling and takes the copy himself and does not send it to the Company, **and is told why that is a decision: a copy that goes to the Company is a copy about ninety-one households, and a copy in the hands of one man is a copy about one man, and he is the only person in ninety-one households who can be asked about it, and he is now findable in a building that recognises the room.** In the embankment corridor, for the stamp, **he puts a hand on a file of the sixth year in the press at about knee height with a routing slip in her hand of nine years ago, and does not open it and does not pull it out and does not ask what is in it**, and asks her instead for one line in her own book with no office on it, which is the only line in nine years about a thing nobody asked her about. She writes it and says that it is not that the file is a secret: **nobody has ever asked her, and a person who is not asked has not been given a duty, and that is the fourth time in nine years that sentence has been true about her and the fourth time has been the easiest to write down.** He goes back up the towpath at about the fourth hour with a copy in his coat and four doors in a list, and the bank is eleven feet high and has been since the twenty-first of the second month and will be until the third week of the fourth month.


## Volume 03, Batch 0003 (Chapters 121–130) — The Flood Season, and a Man Who Cannot Say What a Thing Is For

**The second batch of Volume 03. The volume is open at Chapter 120 of 150 at the close of this batch.**

| 121 | A Letter Nobody Sent Him | Piotr Wray | last day, 3rd – 1st, 4th month | Four words said standing in a water office, a void sheet, and the price of the no arriving overnight on foot in a form with a body in a box |
| 122 | Nine Acres and a Dead Sheet | Sivra Oris | 2nd & 4th, 4th month | A dead sheet carried four miles upstairs, a district flood authority entered undispensed, and a dragon's name at the foot of a paper about her own office |
| 123 | Four Persons, and Sixty Who Are Not | Ivet Sarn | 5th–7th, 4th month | A two-hour sitting in the wrong form, four persons heard, about sixty entered as not bound, and a direction to serve notice on four of whom two are described |
| 124 | What a Wagon of Kerb Is Worth | Aven Tace | 9th–10th, 4th month | Nine inches of toe gone at the third-of-the-way mark, a price named and refused on purpose, and a notice read out twice in a cold bay |
| 125 | The Four Lines, Sent | Marek Kest | 11th–12th, 4th month | Nine pages written, an undertaking catching an absence and then an answer, four days of not acting, and a paper through a gate because a paper with a name on it is the only thing that goes through one |
| 126 | Seven Shillings and Sixpence | Halla Wray | 13th–15th, 4th month | A third no, a ford that lapses with two figures on a lapsed list, and a district foreman who cannot answer a question in the ordinary way |
| 127 | A Household That Has Just Been Helped | Doreth Ferrow | 16th–22nd, 4th month | The water survived, nine people watching a yard they do not belong in, and the finding: a burden moved onto ninety-one people is distributed, not shared |
| 128 | A Body Cannot Answer | Tamsin Rook | 4th wk, 4th month | A body asked in a room of about thirty and no answer, and four minutes of the whole price of this matter read aloud to a woman who cannot read a paragraph |
| 129 | What It Is For, the Second Time | Marek Kest | 1st wk, 5th month | Nine seconds of the right answer, a foreman who says no before she says yes, twenty minutes on the back of a lapsed list, and about two inches of line on a wrist |
| 130 | A List of Works | Rennick Adley | 2nd wk, 5th month | Nine entries in a book with no heading, four records not lined up, a copy going up a towpath in a hand, and a letter standing unopened with a body's name on it |

### Chapter 0121 — A Letter Nobody Sent Him

Piotr Wray's POV, the Reed Cut district water office, the counter, the back room with the stove and the front desk-book, **the last day of the third month from about the seventh hour to about the fifth, and the first of the fourth at about the sixth hour.** He is twenty-nine, he has been the person in the box for eleven weeks, and in the first month he said in nine words in front of about forty people, *nobody asked me, so I am not going to sign*, and has not been asked anything in fifty days. **The keeper tells him before he arrives that she is not going to ask him about it and that nobody in that building will, and that if anybody does he is to say a keeper gave him a date and did not give him a morning.** The only thing standing in the room is the arithmetic of what his no will produce, and nobody persuades him and nobody asks him not to. He works out that a sheet which stands with a live refusal in it and no date on it is the thing that keeps him findable, and that the refusal is worth exactly nothing without a day on it and worth a great deal with one, and **so he waits until the last hour the keeper will hold it open, which is *not one day past it*, and says the word standing, in about four words, and does not explain it afterwards.** The keeper writes the void in the office's own book in her own hand with the day on it, at about the fourth hour, and **the sheet is void, and the district's flood authority in respect of the Slade spring works is undispensed, and the season's first memorandum for the Slade Cut goes out with a body in the box headed *who is to answer*, and the body is the Slade Cut Company, and it was made in about four minutes by a clerk who was given a box and no word of what to put in it.** The keeper does not repeat that she is not the First Seat and is not that seat's agent and nobody asks her to. He is **neither a martyr nor a coward**: he is a person who said a thing he was entitled to say on a day somebody gave him for saying it, and nobody is going to thank him and nobody is going to be sorry for him either, and both of those were promised to him in advance by a woman with a stove in a room. On the morning of the first of the fourth he is still in the building because he slept at the far end of the cut and came back up, and **a man of the district works comes up the road four miles in the wet with a sheet nobody in this matter sent him**, and the chapter is named for that sheet.

### Chapter 0122 — Nine Acres and a Dead Sheet

Sivra Oris's POV, the water office and the yard and gate of the Reed Cut district works, **the second and fourth of the fourth month.** She is a dragon who has given away her own office's flood authority in writing and cannot get it back, and the thing she is carrying is the death of the only instrument in the district that gave a flood authority to a person instead of a body. **She writes the void in the front desk-book in her own hand with the day on it in about a minute, and puts the sheet flat on the counter with the writing turned for the person standing on the other side, which is a thing a girl of seventeen taught her by turning a sheet round in a kitchen for eleven people and has never once been thanked for.** Then she takes it four miles upstairs and puts four lines and a refusal and a day in front of a man and tells him the truth: the district's flood authority is **undispensed**, and that is entered with a date, **and not *no person available*, because a sentence about people in a return of that office would be read in nine years as a finding that nobody was willing.** She **gives her own name at the foot of a memorandum about the district and about her own office when a foreman asks for it, and says the difference out loud: a keeper's name on a paper about a bank makes that paper a thing the bank can be asked about, and a keeper's name on a paper about her own office is a thing the office can be asked about, and she is the office.** The chapter's other finding is the **third absence in this matter of the same kind: there is a form for a body and no form for nobody, and a district can be told to go and look for nobody and has done it before and it cost a year.** A void sheet and a box with a body in it are the same fact seen from two ends, and the second end is worse, because the first was a person deciding and the second is a clerk with a form and four minutes. Behind four hundred and forty foot of outer face there are nine acres and about thirty-one households and there is no name on that bank anywhere at all.

### Chapter 0123 — Four Persons, and Sixty Who Are Not

Ivet Sarn's POV, the Mosswake Roll Court on the flood terrace, the court room, the clerk's window, the board above the sill and the counter, **the fifth, sixth and seventh of the fourth month, with the sitting on the sixth at about the tenth hour for about two hours and a quarter.** Four persons are heard through the wrong form, with the line at the top of it for the head of a body blank and staying blank, and the court enters that they are parties from the filing and cannot be un-named and that **about sixty of about ninety-one households are not bound, are not served, and are not anybody's debtors.** The chapter's finding is said out loud in the room by the person it is about: **being heard is a fact about a person and not about a document, it is the only thing available before the water, and it is available to one person at a time and never to ninety-one households at once.** The turn is the magistrate's own motion: **the four persons are to be given notice that they are parties and of what was said about them, because a person made a party by a form is entitled to know it before the water and not only afterwards** — and **Sarn admits the direction in the wrong form for the third time in this matter, under the same written undertaking of the ninth of the second month, with the magistrate not in the room, and says so out loud before she does it and writes in her own book that it is the third time and what the third time is.** She also works out that the undertaking is a sentence written by a person in nine years, used by anybody with a pen, about a matter the writer is not in, **and that the reason it works is that it is written down and the reason that is dangerous is exactly the same thing.** There is no form for notifying a person who refused a name and none for a person who cannot read a paragraph, so the notice is written out by hand in a clerk's hand with the day on it. **Leda Rell is in her own court on her own motion and was not told this was on, and says nothing new about whether she was right**, and enters that she was told about the second use of her undertaking, was not asked to agree to it, is not going to refuse it, and is going to enter that she was not asked.

### Chapter 0124 — What a Wagon of Kerb Is Worth

Aven Tace's POV, the mill yard off the Slade in daylight and the north bay in the same building, **the ninth and tenth of the fourth month, from about the sixth hour to about the fifth.** The miller is about fifty and has run the mill since he was thirty-one and can read. He measures four hundred and forty foot of outer face with a pole and a line against a cross he cut himself in the second month of the year before last and writes, on the back of his own paper about the bank, **that at about two foot back from the third-of-the-way mark the toe is nine inches lower than that cross, that he put a pole to it eleven times between the cross and thirty foot either way and got between eight and ten inches, and that nine is what eleven of them say, with the day on it.** The ordinary competence that keeps winning in this matter is a man with a pole, a line and nine years of looking, and it wins by producing a number nobody had, and **the district works has no instrument that takes a measurement.** He then prices nine tons of his own kerb and coping delivered with five men and a boy for two days at the Company's own rate — **seven shillings and sixpence** — and **refuses to give it, on purpose, and says the reason out loud in about nine minutes: a gift is money with no figure on it, and if he puts nine tons of stone in that ford for nothing then there is no price on it anywhere and the only person who can be asked about that ford is him and he will not be able to answer a word of it, and a thing with a figure on it can be argued with and a thing with no figure on it can only be found.** He puts it on the foreman's list with a date and writes under it that she has not got the money, **so that the list does not lie.** A notice of a court is read out twice in the bay by a girl of seventeen who is not paid, and the miller asks her the fourth question — *who read this to you, and do they get paid if you sign* — and she answers in four words: **nobody read it to me, I read it, I am not paid and I am not going to be**, and says that the moment she is thanked in a room she is a person who reads for thanks. **About nine people in that bay were spoken to by a person sitting next to them without being asked whether they wanted it.** He finds out that in one month a book-keeper wrote his name against an entry in a book with nine hundred and forty names in it and no heading, and that **that name is now the first name anybody will come to, and he had thought of the paper and not of the book.**

### Chapter 0125 — The Four Lines, Sent

Marek Kest's POV, the Received Room and a room in the Roll Hall corridor, **the eleventh and twelfth of the fourth month, from about the ninth hour of the eleventh to about the fourth hour of the twelfth, and the courtyard at the end.** He has had the shape in his own book since the sixteenth of the second month in four lines, and has stated its price in three rooms, and could not say what it was for when a man of sixty asked him in the ordinary way on the twenty-first of the third month. The page has come back and says what it said: **the question was examined and not answered, no person was named, no document was produced, and a day and a half of the afternoon of a man of about sixty is in his own day's book with no fund against it — and about four hundred names are on it and his is one of them, and about four hundred names on four such pages are a trade in about four years, and the trade is not made by anybody and is not stopped by anybody.** Then the difficulty that took him nine minutes to reach: **a rotation of ninety-one persons needs ninety-one persons, the Company is a body and cannot be made one, there is no list, and the only thing that exists is a book with nine hands in it, about nine hundred and forty names, no heading, and no name at the foot of anything until the sixth of the third month, and it has never once been chosen from.** He writes it in four lines and then nine pages. **It is technically good. It moves one specified consequence off two persons named in a void sheet and off the district onto the Slade Cut Company and onto the persons named in the Company's book, in rotation, for a term, with a review date and a right of refusal that costs the person refusing nothing — and it puts in the first recital, in a sentence of his own drafting, that a body cannot be asked, and a body that is given a duty and cannot refuse has accepted it.** Tamsin Rook reads it out in the room and gives four things, three of them the price: the schedule runs on a book nobody has ever chosen from and about four hundred of the names in it are dead; the duty goes to a body; the cost to a person is a wage and he has not put a figure on it; **and the part he has got right, said out loud, because it is the only reason she is still sitting there — nothing in it survives the last term of the last name in the schedule, and nobody in this matter has ever written that sentence down.** The fourth is the undertaking: **the sheet is going to an office outside the Roll Hall, it is the undertaking, it has applied since the fourth of the first month, and he has had it in his coat for eleven days and she has been in the room for two hours.** He says she is right. He uses the rule and puts the choice in writing with a date on it, and **she answers in a room, in words, with a date: *two, he sends it*, and the reason is not a legal one — the water is in the third week of this month, nine acres and thirty-one households are behind four hundred and forty foot of bank with no name on them anywhere, and there is no other instrument in this Empire that would answer it — and she asks for it not to be written down, because a state of mind is the one thing in this matter a copy can take away from him and nothing else can, and a room is the only place a thing can be said and not kept.** He does not give it to her, says so out loud, honours it, does not act on it that day, and sends it on the twelfth at about the fourth hour of the afternoon **to the foreman of the Reed Cut district works and not to the water office, because a paper with a person's name at the top of it is the only thing in this Empire that can be put through a gate, and a district works is a body and cannot refuse, and a person at a gate who has never refused anything cannot begin either.** He writes four lines in his own book and none of them is about why.

### Chapter 0126 — Seven Shillings and Sixpence

Halla Wray's POV, the tool shed at the Slade end and the bank at the third-of-the-way mark, **the thirteenth, fourteenth and fifteenth of the fourth month, from about the seventh hour of the thirteenth to about the eighth hour of the fifteenth.** She is fifty-one and has been foreman of the Slade Cut for nine years, and her condition — that a crew be paid out of something with a figure on it and not out of a man's wage in a city — is the right condition and it costs her the ford. The bill for two days is **seven shillings and sixpence** at forty-five pence a day on five men and a boy, and the Slade Cut Company has **four shillings a year** and that is the rent of the store, and **nobody in ninety-one households has got seven shillings and sixpence.** A man of the district works comes four miles up a road in the rain with nine pages, and she says no in about eleven seconds, and the asking is not the same kind of asking as the last two and she is the only person on that bank who can tell the difference. **She says the price out loud in about nine minutes: named labour and named liability are the whole of a cost and a fund is not, and a duty with a fund behind it stops when the fund does, and the works fund's head has been empty since the third week of the twelfth month.** The ford work lapses on the last day of the second week of the fourth month, and she writes both figures on the lapsed list and enters the word *lapsed* and her own name and the date, and says out loud to nine men that **a foreman who lets a work die rather than lie about where the money came from is a foreman somebody can be shown to have been right**, and that she is going to be asked again and is going to have to say it again. She is asked about the spring bank as well and says no for the same reason in fewer words. The second time this batch touches the wage the whole cost is on the page: **three pounds eleven and threepence out of a clerk's own wage at seventeen shillings and a penny the week, being three weeks of it, paid in full, and there are no unpaid days in this matter.** The chapter's hinge is hers: **it is technically good, and I cannot find the wrong in it, and I have been in this matter eleven weeks and I know exactly what being on the wrong side of a thing feels like, and this feels like it, and those two facts are sitting in the same room and they are not going to get on.** And at about the fourth hour of the fourteenth she finds out **that she was never asked about the flood authority in the first month either, and that her brother's word took it off her without her being asked, and that she has been relieved of a decision she never had.**

### Chapter 0127 — A Household That Has Just Been Helped

Doreth Ferrow's POV, the mill yard, the north bay and the bank at the third-of-the-way mark, **the sixteenth to the twenty-second of the fourth month, the water being in on the nineteenth at about the second hour of the morning.** She is sixty-four, has kept this meeting nineteen years, cannot read a paragraph, can add and can count a room. **The water came in at about the second hour of the morning, there were about eleven inches of it in the middle of the bay floor by the fourth hour, about nine people got the carts and the sacks and the door off its hinges without being asked, and the bank at the third-of-the-way mark stood eleven feet high throughout, and nobody on that cut drowned, and the Tace household is dry.** At about the seventh hour on the morning after, she goes out into the yard to count the room the way she has counted forty meetings, and counts **nine people from the bay, standing in a mill yard at about the seventh hour in a yard that is not theirs, not carrying anything and not asking for anything, facing the mill the way you stand at a gate.** She sends the book-keeper out and makes him count and then makes him tell her how many come to the bay — about thirty — and writes the two figures on the back of her hand together and not apart. Then she asks four questions in the ordinary way and gets the finding out of a book she cannot read: **the rotation runs on a book with about nine hundred and forty names in it that has never once been chosen from; a name in a book against a household is a name of a person; the person is in the house; the people in the house are the person's — so about sixty children under sixteen, in about ninety-one houses, are inside a duty they were not born into, and she did not know until about a minute ago that a name in a book is a chain round about sixty necks, and it is not a chain anybody has laid, it is a chain of nine hundred and forty names in a book with no heading.** And the Company: **a body cannot refuse, and a body that is given a duty and cannot refuse has accepted it, and the acceptance is on the face of the paper in about nine words that nobody in ninety-one households ever said.** She says the sentence the batch ends on, standing in the mud, to a book-keeper and to nobody else: **a burden moved onto ninety-one people is not shared. It is distributed. And distribution is what makes it permanent.** A woman of about thirty-one comes down two hours of towpath in the mud with a boy of four on her hip to ask one thing — *did the water come into my house* — and is told no, and told that whatever the paper says about her it did not save her house, the bank did, and the man on the bank was on it because his name was in a book on purpose six weeks ago. **She does not tell the miller. She will not tell him, and she has not told him anything since the sixth of the third month.**

### Chapter 0128 — A Body Cannot Answer

Tamsin Rook's POV, the north bay of the grain store, **the fourth week of the fourth month, from about the seventh hour to about the ninth.** She is twenty-four and she is in a district four hundred and thirty miles from her own counter because a court order requires it, and he is in the room because she is, and both of them know that and neither has said it in four months. The bay asks the only question anybody on that bank has ever been able to put to the Company — **whether it will accept a duty** — and there is no answer, and about nine people answer it in about nine different ways, and Doreth Ferrow says the answer a third time and it is the same one: **it cannot refuse, so it has accepted it.** Then Tamsin Rook says the thing about the bill, in about four minutes, and **Rennick Adley reads it to Doreth Ferrow because a woman who cannot read a paragraph has to be read to, and the second reading is the one she does not skip.** The price is the whole of it and it is not softened: **a wage of seventeen shillings and a penny the week, three pounds eleven and threepence of it for nineteen days being three weeks, a second instrument with no fund behind it, a day and a half of a man of sixty's own afternoon for a watch, about four hundred and thirty miles of carriage nobody has paid, and about nine pages now on a bench that nobody asked for and that four or five people cannot read at all.** She says out loud, standing, before anybody can call it kindness, that **she is not advising anybody, that her office says she keeps the copies and advises nobody and asks nothing, and that advice by proximity is the ordinary way advice happens and it is why she has not said one word to one person in that room, and that everybody in there has heard a price and not an opinion** — and then says that anybody who thinks otherwise should say so while she is standing there, and nobody does, and she has not asked whether anybody thinks so because asking is advising. She says the second thing, about the copy: **a copy in the hands of one man is a copy about one man, a copy sent to a body is a copy about ninety-one households, and the difference is about four hundred and thirty miles of carriage and a clerk's decision**, and the copy of the sitting of the sixth of the fourth went to one man who bought it. Her finding is that **the price of this matter is a number and she is the only person who can produce it, and a number that one person can produce is an unpriced cost with a witness in it** — and that **not being objected to is the loneliest thing that has happened to her in this matter.**

### Chapter 0129 — What It Is For, the Second Time

Marek Kest's POV, the north bay of a hired grain store, **the first week of the fifth month, from about the seventh hour to about the fourth hour of the afternoon.** He is thirty-eight and has been on this bank for eleven days and has slept two nights in the south end of a store with a stove in it, and he has not asked for a wage to be advanced and there is no fund in this matter and there has never been one. **At about the ninth minutes of his being in the room a foreman of fifty-one asks him, in the ordinary way, at the west end, with about thirty people in it, what it is for, and he has eleven seconds and has had seven weeks to have them.** He answers: **it is for a water, for a bank that is eleven feet high with nine acres and thirty-one households behind it, and for a gate a person can shut; and a public duty with no name on it gets carried by whoever is standing nearest, the nearest is a household, and a household that holds a duty produces a child that holds the duty** — and that he did not know this on the eleventh and knew it on the fourteenth of the third month and did not look, because looking at it was in the only direction he had been forbidden to go for nine months. It takes about nine seconds and it is the right one and nobody in the room can tell. Then **Halla Wray says no first**, in about nine sentences, in a cold bay, to about thirty people, and nobody in that room had expected it: **nobody asked her in the first month whether she would hold the district's flood authority either, and she was put in a box by a keeper who did it on purpose and stood in that box for eleven weeks with nobody asking her a single question, and then her brother said one word in a water office four miles up the road and the box was opened from the outside.** She then sets one condition and it is not about the work: **write down that I was asked, that I said no, and the day of each, and put both days on the list of works and not only the second one, because in about four years somebody is going to read the word *consented* on that paper and there is going to be a day against it, and it was not agreed, it was asked twice and answered twice, and I want the no in the same hand as the yes.** He writes it on the back of the lapsed list in about twenty minutes with four people reading over his shoulder: **one specified consequence, different from the first, moved off the Company, off the schedule and off the rotation onto one person, M. Kest, of the Received Room, who is not of this Company, is not of this district, has no house here and no household here and no child here, so that there is no household in ninety-one households into which it can pass; term one year with a review on the day, and if nobody comes to the review then this stops on the day and there is no successor and nothing carries; the labour named at forty-five pence a day on five men and a boy, about two days, seven shillings and sixpence, out of a wage of seventeen shillings and a penny the week, out of his own hand, and not out of any fund, of which there is none; the liability named — I am answerable for it alone, I can be found, served and put in a room, and I can say no, and I am not going to be able to say no to a person standing in front of me, and I would rather be able to.** About nine minutes after that a line about two inches long appears on the inside of his left wrist, and the chapter is explicit that **the counter-seal is the record, the exception itself with his name at the top of it and a witness in the room, and it is a human clerk's instrument and does not cross a species and will be produced in about four years by anybody who wants it, and the counter-mark is a symptom and not a power: it reads nothing, it decides nothing, it imposes no geographic tether, and it does not know where the ford is. The mark is not the price. The wage is the price.** Tamsin Rook says four things about the mark in about nine minutes and refuses to be tender about any of them, and the fourth is the one she would like written down and will not write down herself: **in about four years this is going to be the only gate on that bank with anybody findable behind it, and the reason will not be courage; you have not made that ford safer by being brave in a bay, you have made it findable, and the finding is the whole of what happens next, and I am saying it to you in a room with about thirty people in it about four days before it happens rather than about four years after.** Doreth Ferrow asks one question in nine minutes and it is the only thing she says: **your term is a year, what happens to the ford on the day the year is up and you are not in it.** He wrote the review day on a piece of paper in a bay and wrote that if nobody comes then it stops, **and that is the first time in nineteen years that I have seen anybody put the day on a thing.**

### Chapter 0130 — A List of Works

Rennick Adley's POV, the south end of the hired grain store and the north bay in the same building, **the second week of the fifth month, from about the seventh hour to about the sixth.** He is thirty-eight and has kept the book for six years; his aunt kept it nineteen before him and before her a man who could not write whose wife wrote the page. **He has nine things to go in the book and he does them in the order he learned in nine years, which is: write down what you are reading before you copy anything, and say who is not in the room.** The entries: **the rule of the meeting entered a second time with the day on it, above the entry of the twenty-eighth of the third month, because a rule a body writes for itself with a date on it is the only paper that body has ever had and it should be in the book and not only on a bench**, and he reads it out twice, says *I wrote it and I did not agree to it and I have never agreed to it*, and says he is not going to start a column called *why not* or one called *who* because a name in a margin is not a debt, not a claim, not a vote and not a party, and the honest thing to do about a body that cannot be compelled is to be easy to compel yourself. Then the water: in on the nineteenth at about the second hour of the morning, down over the twentieth and part of the twenty-first, nobody drowned, the bank eleven feet high throughout, the ford full. Then the lapsed list, written out in full with the two figures, the word *lapsed*, the reason, the foreman's name and the date, read back twice including the second reading for the nine who cannot read a word. Then a line about the ford, the stones and the two posts, and **a clerk of the Inheritance Desk in a city four hundred and thirty miles away, whose name is at the foot of it with a date and whose term is a year and whose review is in the first month of next year** — and he entered it in the Company's book and then **sat on the bench for a while with the pen out and understood the whole of what he had just done in about eleven seconds and put the pen down and wrote a sentence instead: the fourth of these entries is not of this Company; it is put in this book because a list of works is the only paper on this cut that anybody can point at, and because a thing kept off the book is a thing findable in one place only, and I would rather it were findable in five; he was not asked and cannot be asked.** Then the new list of works with the foreman's name and a date of the second week of the fifth month and not one day past it, and **the only figure in ninety-one households anybody can say is a day rate of forty-five pence on five men and a boy, which is two days at seven shillings and sixpence, and the Company has four shillings a year of which the whole is the rent, and nobody has offered to pay this and nobody is going to be asked.** Then **the four records on one page and not lined up** — the district works' return book four miles away, this book, a slate in a wooden frame on a nail in the open at a landing, a minute in a court on a flood terrace — and nine sentences out loud: **a line between two things is a chain and a chain is an invention, which a magistrate entered in the ninth of the second month, and I am not going to line these four up, because the day somebody does, all four of them become evidence of one thing instead of four, and about four hundred and forty foot of bank will then be a matter with a record behind it, and we have not got one and we are not going to get one by joining four things that were never joined.** And then the thing he has been not saying since the second of the third month: **there is a name in this book on a page from ninety-one years back, Tarn Skell, of the Slade, the eleventh of eleven names, and a map of nineteen years back has him as the head of a household of nine at the north of the ford, and there are four houses of Skells there now; I found that name on the second of the third month and I did not go, and I have not gone since, and I have asked at all four doors and nobody at any of them could tell me whose grandfather Tarn Skell was. A name is findable and a family is not. *Skell* is a list of four doors and *Tarn Skell's son* is not a thing anybody can look up. I have had about nine weeks to do something about it and I have done nothing, on purpose, and I am not going to do anything in the second week of this month either, because going would make a person a party.** Then a copy of the sitting of the fifth of the fourth month goes up the towpath on the eighth in a hand and not in an envelope at four pence, and no carriage was paid because the person carrying it walked. The chapter ends on the letter: **a paper has come to this cut addressed to a body, it wants a person's name and a person's answer, a body cannot be served, cannot refuse and cannot open its own post, and there is no form in this Empire in which a body may say *I have no person*, and there is no head of the Slade Cut Company and there has never been one — and it is standing against a wall in a tarpaulin and nine people have read the outside of it and nobody has opened it.**

## Volume 03, Batch 0004 (Chapters 131–140) — A Guarantee With No Price On It, and a Name at the Foot of a Form

**The fourth batch of Volume 03. The volume is open at Chapter 130 of 150 at the close of this batch.**

| 131 | A Body Cannot Open Its Own Post | Aven Tace | 2nd–3rd wk, 5th month | A letter opened by a person on a body, a guarantee at no charge, about sixty children in the labour clause, and nine days that are only for saying no |
| 132 | The Free Door | Marn Ottery | 3rd wk, 5th month | Nine lines on what a table may certify, about four hundred blanks counted under a counter, and the finding that the cheapest door in the empire is the one that makes a person a party |
| 133 | The Office That Cannot Give It Back | Sivra Oris | 4th wk, 5th month | A refusal in a form with a reason and no why, a yes in the wrong place on the front of it, and nine hundred forms with no box that says *none* |
| 134 | What Is on the Ground | Vaunt Oris | last wk, 5th – 1st wk, 6th month | A House redrafts its own request into a sentence that is true of a book, and a notary of fifty-one or two asks to see the ground before he seals anything |
| 135 | A Form With a Reason and No Why | Halla Wray | 1st–2nd wk, 6th month | A notice of a seat with a foreman's name at the top of it, declined in nine words, and a shed where nine men are asked whether anybody has had a thing they could not put down |
| 136 | The Names in the Press | Leda Rell | 2nd–3rd wk, 6th month | About a hundred and forty files opened by the person who put them there, three categories never made answerable, and the first answer this court has ever given to a seat |
| 137 | A Figure, and a List With It | Aldis Kern | 3rd–4th wk, 6th month | The first real figure anybody has put on that cut, a list of persons with a line at the foot of it, and a factor who says out loud that he cannot refuse it |
| 138 | The Fourth Line, and Who Can Fill It In | Marek Kest | 4th wk, 6th month | The door this volume's own work opened, five people who could fill in the fourth line and no one who should, and being told to stop treating his body as a place to store public cases |
| 139 | What a Seal Is For | Vaunt Oris | 4th–last wk, 6th month | A split instrument: one limb sealed, one refused, and a hundred and forty years of a House's licence on one reach entered as lapsed with a notary's name at the foot |
| 140 | A Name at the Foot of It | Doreth Ferrow | last wk, 6th month | A dragon's name read out twice in a bay, a person who says *I wanted to see if it worked*, and a copy going four hundred and thirty miles north to a list of Sorn border households |

### Chapter 0131 — A Body Cannot Open Its Own Post

Aven Tace's POV, the mill yard off the Slade and the north bay in the same building, **the second and third weeks of the fifth month, from about the seventh hour to about the ninth.** The letter came on the eighth day of the fifth month in a cart with some seed, addressed to **the Slade Cut Company, Slade End**, and it has been in that yard five days, under a tarpaulin against the south wall, and nobody has opened it. On the outside of it, under the address, in the sender's own hand: **a thing is to be entered against a person and not against a body** — and his first finding is that whoever wrote that has already hit the same wall four hundred yards down the road and has put a note about it on the outside of his own envelope instead of hitting it again, and sent it to them anyway. He has worked out in his own kitchen in about six years, with nobody to ask, that **a name at the top of a paper about a bank is a name anybody can ask about the bank**, and he has never once put his own name at the top of a piece of paper about that bank — and he knows by the ninth that opening this one makes him the door to it, because he is the one whose hand has been on the outside of it for five days. **Doreth Ferrow does not ask him. She says out loud that she is going to say *open your post* and that it is not a question, and that a woman of sixty-four who cannot read a paragraph is very useful to everybody and expensive to consult, and that she wants it in the book with the day on it.** He opens it in the bay at about the ninth hour of the eleventh with about thirty people, cuts the string, **turns the sheet round and puts it flat on the bench with the writing facing the far side, which is a thing a girl of seventeen has done about nine times in that bay and he has now worked out why: a sheet lying the wrong way round is a sheet nine people cannot read.** He reads the outside out loud first and says, before anything else, that **it is a good document, that he has had a hand on about four hundred papers in thirty-one years and this is the best-made one he has ever had, and he is not going to stand in a bay and tell people it is a bad document because he cannot make it say what he wants it to say.** The guarantee is then read out in full, once, and the operative parts are these: the holder of the fire-and-water device will hold the water of the Slade Cut against flood for the length of the works, will stand the cost of the bank, the ditch, the scour and the works at the ford, **without charge, and will not ask any household for any sum**; and **the labour of those works is furnished by the Company and by such persons as are of the Company at the time the work is done; a person who is of the Company at one time and not at another has furnished the labour of the works done while he was of it; the Company is not discharged by the death of a member and is not discharged by the coming of age of a member; and nothing in this instrument is a debt of any household and nothing in it may be sued for by any household or against any household, and no child, widow or person not of the Company is named in it, is party to it, or may be sued upon it.** The fourth line is required, in what way the instrument touches you, within nine days of the day it is opened, and **if no answer is given within that time the instrument stands offered and unanswered and may be taken up at any time afterwards by a person answering in those terms.** Tavi Venn reads the whole of it out twice, once at the speed of a price list and once slower, is not paid, is not thanked, gives four people who ask her what it means the same four words — *I read it, I do not say* — and the miller asks her what the nine days is for and gets the same four words twice. Then **Rennick Adley says the thing four people have been thinking and nobody wanted to be first at: the nine days is not for answering, it is for saying no; a deadline is a thing that stops something, and that is a window on a thing that does not stop; it has been true since the eighth; nobody had to do anything; and the offer does not need an answer to be true.** And then the counting, done out loud by the chair with the back of her own hand: **a person is of the Company if the person is in a house of the ninety-one; so a child in a house at the north of the ford is of the Company, and a child born in the eighth month of next year is of the Company, and there is no age in it** — her count of nineteen years stops at sixteen and the paper does not — **and the sentence that says no child is party to it is the fourth one down and comes after, it is honestly drafted, it is true about suing and it is not true about being in it, and whoever wrote that paper put the one that takes the children in first and the one that says nobody may be sued afterwards, and I do not believe he did it on purpose and it would make no difference if he had.** The miller then says the nine words that turn the chapter: **I gave you a room on the sixth of the third month and it costs nothing, and I have said out loud that a thing which is free is a thing nobody can refuse, and it is not a room, it is a letter with a body's name on it, and I have just opened it, and the paper was under my own tarpaulin, and I am the only person on this cut anybody can put a name on.** And Adley gives the other half: **you are right, and a House has answered me with a figure of nought, and nought is a figure, and it is worse.** Nobody answers the letter, nobody fills in the fourth line, nobody gives anybody's name, and Adley enters it in the book with the day on it, and **does not line it up against the four households already parties to a form filed four miles down the road, because a line between two things is a chain and a chain is an invention.** On the twentieth, four days after the window shut, a courier comes up the road with a second sheet in the same hand addressed to a notary's table, asking that counter whether it will certify the guarantee and asking first to be satisfied of one fact, **in what way does this instrument touch the Company**, and carrying at the foot, in bigger letters, that the practice has also written to the keeper of the district water office and that answer is required before the table is answered.

### Chapter 0132 — The Free Door

Marn Ottery's POV, the fourth counter of the Notaries' Table in Auremar, the room behind it, the register of concessions and the fee board, **the third week of the fifth month, from about the seventh hour to about the sixth.** The counter has been hers for eleven years and she came to it from the second, where estates are, and she has never been a lawyer and has never wanted to be one, and the fee board has four things on it and none of them is time: a question is a shilling, a copy is fourpence, a certificate is four shillings, attendance by arrangement; a blank form is a shilling whether it is filled in or not; an answer is about eleven weeks. The courier puts down a sheet that says it is a refusal with a question on the back of it: **a water-notary practice is asked to be satisfied of one fact, namely who is of the Company, and the Company is not on the register of bodies, has no head, and may not be made a party.** She answers anyway, for no fee, because nobody has ever walked up to that counter without a shilling in the hand and it has made her the only door in the building and she has never minded until about a fortnight ago. Her nine lines, in her own hand, dated and signed on the back of a blank and declared to be a clerk's own note and not a certificate: **a table may certify what an instrument is and when it was made and what it is called; what a book is and what is written in it; what a party is; what a document does. It may not certify that a person ought to do a thing, who ought to answer for a thing, or what a body is made of.** And then the sentence that is the chapter: **the fourth line asks in what way the instrument touches you, it has always asked about one person, it was never intended to be about a body or a household, and it has never had to be, because in the hundred and forty years of this table no body has ever been able to put a person in front of it.** Then the box under the counter: about four hundred blanks, about two hundred and sixty-one with the fourth line empty, **a hundred and forty with it filled in, and in a hundred and twenty-six of those a person's name — and in fourteen the box does not say a person's name, and in eleven of those fourteen there is a person's name and a date underneath the fourth line, at the foot of the form, in a different hand.** So: **the form was not filled in by a body; it was filled in by a careful person who wrote the body in the box, and then a person signed underneath and answered for it, and nobody at this counter ever asked whether that person had been asked.** And then the trap, which she finds sitting at the counter: **they asked her for the third thing and she said no and was right and was proud of herself for nine seconds; the first, third and fourth things on her list are the same question three ways and all about documents; the second one is the one that is not. A House does not need her to certify who is of ninety-one households. It needs a seal on a true sentence. And a true sentence about a book is on her board at item two and she would be saying it in her own hand and it would be true** — and the sentence, which is one hundred and forty years of table-craft: *the book produced is a true record of the members of the said Company, being the persons entered in it.* **A seal does not say *these are the members*; a seal says the paper and the ground are the same thing; so the seal says the ground is the book, and after that there is no difference left to argue about, and the difference was the only thing anybody had.** And: **a table may refuse a question and be entirely right, and a party may put the same question differently and be served inside four days, and the only defence anybody in this Empire has against that is a person who is in the room when the second version is written. There is no form for that. There is a person for that.** She writes it all out on foolscap, dates it, signs it, **decides not to put it in the file because a file is a place a thing goes to be looked up and this ought to be in a place where about nine people can find it**, and gives it to a carrier on the sixth of the sixth month with four words on the front — *About the fourth line* — at four pence out of her own hand. A woman of twenty-four comes down the passage from the third gallery, reads it, and says in eleven seconds that it is true, and then says the thing that is not in the note: **a person who turns up is never paid and cannot be, and I have just found out the same fact about a form; a thing nobody is charged for is a thing nobody thinks about, and a thing nobody thinks about is the thing that takes a child into a guarantee in a bay four hundred and thirty miles from here.**

### Chapter 0133 — The Office That Cannot Give It Back

Sivra Oris's POV, the Reed Cut district water office — the counter, the back room with the stove, the front desk-book, the shelf of about nine hundred forms — and the Reed Cut gate, **the fourth week of the fifth month, from about the seventh hour to about the ninth.** A boy of about fourteen brings up a question from a notary's table of a House: **we are asked to be satisfied of one fact, namely who is of the Company; we are told it is not on the register of bodies, has no head, and may not be made a party; we ask this office to certify the fact; the answer is required in the form provided, with a reason, within the time stated.** The clerk is about twenty-three and gives the boy something out of a jug that is not tea. She takes the paper into the back room and shuts the door, which she has never done for a piece of paper in nine years, and stands by the stove for about four minutes. **The thing the House has asked her for is the thing she signed away in the first month.** Not the flood authority over the works — that is a different absence and she could have given that. **For eleven weeks the only office in that district that could have said anything about the water of that cut with a seal on it was this office, and this office put two names in a box on purpose and gave the authority away in a sheet of foolscap to keep a boy of sixteen out of it.** She fills the form in standing up in a minute and a half, and the box on that form is two lines deep and what she has to say is not: **Answer: refused.** Reason: this office cannot certify who is of a body, has no power to, never had, and there is no form in this district or the Empire by which it could acquire it, and it is entered in this district's own front desk-book in this keeper's hand with a date that the office does not hold the flood authority in respect of the works in question and cannot be instructed to act in respect of them. And then she writes **on the face of the form** that the reason is longer than the box, is on a page in her own front desk-book in her own hand with the day on it, and any person may come and read it. And under it, on the back: **there is no space on this form for why. Nobody has ever wanted one and I have wanted one for nine years and I am not going to invent a box.** Then she reads out the second answer, which is on the paper and not in the box, and which is **yes**: a body may not be made a party, and the answer is true in every court in the Empire because there is nowhere in it for it to be otherwise, and **take the yes back with you; it is free and it is correct and it is worth about four hundred and thirty miles of carriage and I am giving it to you for nothing because it costs this office nothing to be right about a thing that is already written down in nine books.** She then re-enters the void in the front desk-book, referring to the entry of the last day of the third month, because a House is now asking about the same works and somebody four hundred miles up a river will want to know whether this office gave that sheet out again; it did not; **the flood authority is entered as undispensed and not as *no person available*.** The rest of the day is ordinary and is worth a page: a man of about forty who wants a sticking gate linkage written down, and is told what this office's book is for; a woman of about thirty-one about a boundary stone, done in nine minutes for a nail and half an hour of somebody's afternoon, and **the only thing this office did that day anybody was ever going to be satisfied with.** And then, at about the fifth hour, a man from the district works comes up the hill with a slip and a bad temper and four days behind him, because he has four men at a gate and a list that says nothing on it, and asks for another sheet. **That is exactly the sentence she has been waiting since the last day of the third month.** He gets it: the authority is undispensed, this office cannot give a sheet, if it did it would be a sheet with a name in a box and there is nobody to put in the box; *then who has* — **nobody**; *then who do I ask* — **ask the district works**; *I am the district works* — **then I have told you the whole of it and there is no more of it than that.** And she sends him down the hill with a sentence to write in his own return book in his own hand with the day on it and **his own name at the foot of it, because he has not done that in nine years.** About nine hundred forms on that shelf are gone through with a thumb: **a form for a person, a form for two persons, a form for a household, which is a form about a head, and about nine hundred forms with no such box at all, and none of them that says *none*. There is a form for a body and there is no form for nobody.** She does not write one, and could not have told anybody in four minutes why, until she did: **a form made by this office saying that no person is available would be read in nine years by somebody in a corridor as a return, and a return about people is a finding about people, and I have spent nine years not making findings about people.** Then the day's own finding, entered in one line: **I have today made two people findable — a House, by a true sentence, and a foreman of the district works, by telling him to sign his own name. I record it because nobody else is going to and because the second one I did on purpose and the first one I did not notice, and the whole of the difference between them is about eleven seconds and I did not see it happening.** A House's own practice does not ask its own table whether its own seal is good, and the person in that room was a notary of fifty-one or two with nineteen years in that chair, and it took him about four minutes to say he would look at it and nine days to ask for one thing. **He asked what is on the ground.**

### Chapter 0134 — What Is on the Ground

Vaunt Oris's POV, a chamber of House Oris about two hundred miles from the table where the fourth counter is, **the last week of the fifth month and the first week of the sixth, from about the ninth hour.** The chamber is two rooms and a passage, a stair that does not turn, a window that looks at a roof, and nine things in the outer room, one of which is a book about ninety years old with a crack in the spine. **He is a notary of House Oris in his own right, fifty-one or two, nineteen years in that chair, and he has certified about four hundred instruments a year and has never certified one he had not seen, which is not in any rule of the house and is in him.** He has not repeated one sentence in thirteen months and nobody in this matter has asked him to. The request comes up the stair brought by a **trade assessor**, who is not his clerk and is nobody's clerk and stands between a house and a guild and has done for twenty-six years, and it is four lines long and is from the practice and not from a person: **be satisfied that the persons named in the book of the Company working the Slade Cut are the persons of that Company, so that the guarantee may be certified; and be satisfied that the Company has accepted the duty, the acceptance being on the face of an instrument drawn in a district four hundred and thirty miles away and placed there on the twelfth of the fourth month.** He separates them in about nine seconds: **the second one is true, and it is not an opinion, it is arithmetic — a body cannot say no, a body that is given a duty and cannot say no has accepted it, nine words on a piece of paper in a hired bay are the whole of the acceptance, nobody in ninety-one households said them, and the paper itself says the acceptance does not evidence a consent, which is the only reason the nine words are honest — so the second one is true, and it is worth nothing, and I can put a seal on it tomorrow. And the first one I have to go and look at, and I am not going to look at it from a piece of paper.** The assessor makes him ask it properly, in the words he would use in front of a witness, and he asks: **I am asking what is on the ground, meaning I will not put a seal on a statement about a book I have not been shown, and if it does not come in an answerable form then I will say no and it will be my no and not my House's** — and then, to the assessor, **I do not know whether it will be my no or my House's, and that is the honest position and I have been in it for about nine days already and have not said one word about it to anybody.** The answer comes by three routes in nine days and takes about eleven minutes to hear: a company of about ninety-one households, no head, not on the register, may not be made a party; **a book in a hired stone store at a shilling a quarter, nine hands, about nine hundred and forty names, about sixty years, no heading at the top of it, no list of persons in it, saying on its first leaf in a hand about six years old what it is and what it is not, and about four hundred of the nine hundred and forty people in it dead; the figure of about ninety-one households and about sixty children under sixteen is not in the book at all and is a count off it written on the back of a person's hand by a person who cannot read a paragraph; and no person has ever been chosen from the book, because it is not a rota.** Plus one more thing, in a different voice, from a different person: **on the sixth day of the third month, for the first time in sixty years, a name was written against an entry in that book, and the name is of a person and not of a crew and not of a debtor, and the man who wrote it wrote it on purpose so that the Company would be easy to compel itself, and he has said so out loud in a room of about thirty people, and he put it in a book with no heading so that nobody could tell what it was for.** And his finding, in nine sentences, to three people: **a book with names in it is not a list of persons. I have read human inheritance records for nineteen years. In nineteen years I have never once been asked what a company's book was, and I have certified about four hundred instruments a year, and not one of them has ever been about a cooperative, because a cooperative cannot produce a person to be certified about. That is the whole of my profession: I am asked what a thing is and I go and looks at it before I say. And the request on that sheet is not asking me what a thing is. It is asking me to say that a book is a room, and that a room is the Company, and that everyone who ever walked into that room is still in it. About four hundred of them are not. The difference between a record and a register is about eleven seconds wide and it is the width of a person standing up deciding which one they are doing.** Then the practice tries to help, twice, and fails twice, and both failures are on the page. **First:** the practice's clerk of about thirty-four redrafts the request as *the book produced is a true record of the members of the said Company, being the persons entered in it* — the second thing on the counter's board four hundred miles away, a thing tables certify every day — and Vaunt Oris says: **that is exactly the sentence I was going to write myself, and I had it in my own book on the fourth day, and I would have arrived at it, and the fact that your office arrived at it first does not make it somebody else's; will you seal it? No. Then I do not know what I did wrong. You did nothing wrong and that is the difficulty — nobody in this house did anything wrong; the request came in a form I cannot seal and then, four days later, in a form I can, and both forms were drawn by careful people, and the second one is shorter, and it is shorter because it has left out the only question there is.** **Second:** the House sends nine lines down about a week later, unsigned, because a seat's paper does not have a person at the foot of it, and leaves it on his table and takes it away and leaves it again, twice — that the guarantee is a good instrument offered without charge; that the practice is the oldest on the reach and the concession is not a formality and is the only reason anybody in that district has ever been able to have a scale instrument put in a hand at all; that the request as drawn is reasonable and a clerk four hundred miles away would seal the redraft without reading it twice; that a person in this chamber who cannot seal a request his own House has put forward is not being asked to be difficult and will be told so in the practice's own book — and the last line, which he writes out in full in his own book with the day on it because he was told to: **nothing in this paper requires the practice to answer. The paper is not a question. An institution that sends a thing to be answered and does not require an answer has sent a thing to be complied with.** He has, in another city, said in a chamber fourteen months before this that an institution is not told things by its notaries in the middle of an afternoon, and that it is told in about four years by somebody else at more expense, and that his question had cost his House nothing. **He was right about the timing; it took about nine days. He had been wrong about the price for about thirteen months without ever once thinking about it, and the price was not a house and not a fund and not a wing: the price was a person, and it was going to be himself, and it was going to be his own name at the foot of a page in a book of about a hundred and forty years, and the House was not going to know it had happened for about four years either.** He leaves the sheet on the table for nine days and writes four words at the bottom in his own hand and sends it down the stair: **Noted, and not done.** And he learns what happens to a concession from the practice's clerk, who says without any weight on it at all that **a concession is held and not owned, and a House which can lose one without saying who lost it will lose it quietly and will lose the next one quietly, and a House that is going to lose one anyhow might as well have it written down** — and that a person in that chamber who has not refused anything has not had cause to think about the rule. And then, once, in that room, to two people, the sentence he has wanted since the twelfth of the fourth month and has told nobody living: **I would rather it held. The rotation, the list, the term, the review, the person in the book. I would rather ninety-one households were on a schedule with a name at the foot of it than under a House guarantee that costs them nothing and takes their children. I am not going to pretend that is a neutral thing to want.** He is told an answer in two parts is an answer in one part with a refusal in the middle of it, known as a **split instrument**, and that there is a column for it, and nobody in the house has ever used it in nineteen years. And the practice's clerk, asked what happens to a concession when the half that is refused is the half the concession is for, says it goes nowhere — not to a court, not to a House — **and goes in the book of concessions, in the entry for the reach, with the name of the person who gave it up, and that is the practice's rule and not the law and has been since before the clerk was born.** The column is filled in nine days later, in the fourth week of the sixth, and one half of the sheet carries a seal and the other half carries a name at the foot of a page in a book about a hundred and forty years old.

### Chapter 0135 — A Form With a Reason and No Why

Halla Wray's POV, the tool shed at the Slade end and the north bay, **the first and second weeks of the sixth month, from about the seventh hour of the first day to about the fifth hour of the last.** The shed is about nine feet by seven with a bench and a vice and about eleven tools on nails that all used to have names painted under them, and the doors are off their top hinges and it is not a room anybody sits in. She is fifty-one and has been foreman for nine years and has been asked about a bank five times this year and has said no five times and every one of the five was right, and her condition was that a crew be paid out of something with a figure on it and not out of a man's wage in a city, and there was no such thing, and the ford work lapsed on the last day of the second week of the fourth month with two figures on a lapsed list and her name under both. **A paper comes up the road on the ninth of the sixth month with a seal on it and her name at the top of it.** Wendt Sayer, the wrong side of sixty, a foreman of the district works and not a lawyer, brings it, reads the outside of it out, and says he does not know what to do with it and is not going to pretend he does. The notice is one side of good paper, not printed, in six numbered lines: the seat gives notice of the instrument and the offer of it; the seat states that the instrument requires the answer of a person and not of a body, and is aware the body has not answered, and that the instrument stands offered and unanswered; the seat asks the person named below to acknowledge receipt; the seat states that the answer of the person is not a consent and does not bind any person who has not answered, and that the instrument is not affected by an acknowledgement either way; **the seat will pay the carriage of any person whom the district court thinks should be heard about the instrument**; the person is this one. Asked about the fifth line, Sayer says: **it pays carriage, and it offers to pay the carriage of anybody the district court thinks should be heard, and I have been in this matter for about eleven weeks and I have read four hundred and thirty miles of paper about people not being heard, and I have never once seen a court asked which people it thinks should be heard. That is what the fifth line is for. It is the only one of the six that is not about the guarantee.** Halla Wray can read, and it is not in dispute, and in nine years of a shed nobody's business; **it is that nobody at the district works has ever asked her to read anything, and the ordinary consequence of her name being at the top of a paper is that she has to, and there are about nine men in and out of that shed in an afternoon and she reads it to every one of them who has not heard it and makes the second reading for the two men who cannot read a paragraph, both about fifty-five and both about thirty years on that cut.** The form is a House form about a seat, one side, not printed: **I acknowledge receipt, and I am the person named; my answer is I do not acknowledge, or I acknowledge and wish to say; Reason, if any** — two boxes and a line about two inches long that takes about nine words — **and nothing on it at all about why.** Her reason is nine words: *I am not the head of a body and cannot be and there is none.* She reads it back twice, makes Sayer read it back, and makes him write it into the book at the Slade end in his own hand because hers would be thrown away by whoever receives it, and tells him **not to put her name at the foot of it, because she does not want to be the person whose name is at the foot of a seat's paper and she is not going to be findable twice in one month** — and Sayer says that is the first sensible thing anybody has said in this matter for about four months and is also the exact thing she said in a bay about a man from a city, *you told him you would rather be able to be found; you have changed your mind in six weeks*, and she says **I have not changed my mind; he did not have a piece of paper with my name on it and I have.** Then the chapter's real scene: **she asks the shed, in the ordinary way, over about an hour and a half, has anybody here ever had a thing they did not want and could not put down.** Four say yes. Two cannot say what. One of about forty says **I wanted to go to the coast.** And Wendt Sayer, still on the mill wall, says: **I signed a return once in the ninth year I did not agree with and I have been carrying it for about nine years and there is nobody in that office I could hand it to and there is no form.** Halla Wray writes that down on the back of the form she has just filled in and then does not give it to him, and puts it in her own pocket, and gives the reason: **you have not asked me to, and if I enter it in a book in that office it is a return about a person and I do not write returns about people and I have not written one in nine years and I am not starting in the sixth month with a piece of paper in my hand.** And then the sentence the chapter ends on, out loud, to about nine men: **a person can be asked and can say no and can put it down; a body cannot be asked, and cannot say no, and cannot put it down, and if a thing is put on it, it is put on it. That is the whole of what I have learned this year and I have learned it in a shed and not in a room with a table in it, and I have said no five times and every one of the five was about a bank, and I would like it on the record that I have never once been asked about a guarantee and I have been put in one.** The two men who cannot read say that is six lines, and one of them says **my father had a thing like that on him once and he could not read it either and he never found out what it was**, and is told that he is the only person on that cut who came out of a thing like that all right and it was not because anybody did anything for him, and nobody laughed. She sends the form back down the road in a hand and not in an envelope on the eleventh, carried by Sayer himself, and at the gate of the district works yard she says the thing about the price of being findable: **take it yourself, not a boy; if a boy takes it then a boy is what a House sees at the end of it, and in about four years there will be a record somewhere that says a boy was involved in a thing a seat did and he will be twenty-two and he will not be able to say no. And read the sixth line out again before you go in; it is your own name and it is the first time in nine years your name has been at the top of anything a building will keep. And come back up the hill and tell me what you saw, and whatever it is I am not going to argue with it. A gate a person can shut is the only thing worth having and there are about sixty of them shut on purpose at the foot of that bank at this moment, and the price of shutting one is that the person who shut it can be found. That is the arrangement. That is the only one on offer. I have been the price for about nine weeks now and I would rather be the price than be a room with nobody in it and four hundred feet of bank in front of it.** He takes it himself. The carriage offer is still live and inoperable and says a seat will pay the carriage of any person whom the district court thinks should be heard, and its own terms require the district court to be asked, and nobody has asked it, and there is no form in this empire for one court to ask another court what it has found.
