On the thirteenth of the sixth month, at about the tenth hour, a clerk of thirty-eight hands nine lines of the notice to a magistrate in her own chamber, says **it is a House paper and it is the second-best piece of drafting anybody has ever handed me at this window, and the first one was the other half of the same office in the sixth month of last year**, and that the fifth line pays carriage. Leda Rell reads the fifth line twice and then goes and stands at her own window and looks out at the embankment, where the water mark from the fourth month is still about four inches above the paving, and where about a hundred and forty files are in a press in a corridor behind her, in her own hand, and which she has never read as a set.

### Chapter 0136 — The Names in the Press

Leda Rell's POV, the Mosswake Roll Court on the flood terrace — her chamber, the clerk's window, the press in the embankment corridor — **the second and third weeks of the sixth month, from about the seventh hour to about the ninth hour of the last day.** She is fifty-one and has been a magistrate in this district for nine years. She entered, on the ninth of the second month, on her own motion and asked by nobody, that a body of persons may not be made a party by her court or any other, and that a balance has stood in about a hundred and forty files for nineteen years and has never been added up and is owed by nobody and the figure stays out of the minute on purpose, **because a clerk who thinks somebody owes something is the beginning of everything that has gone wrong in that district since the sixth year.** She said out loud in the second month of this year, in a room, that she does not know whether she was right, and nothing has happened in between that has told her anything, and she is not going to manufacture a feeling about it in front of people from a lane. The notice is handed to her at about the tenth hour on the thirteenth of the sixth and she reads all six lines twice, standing, and says **I have been asked in writing. Write that down, in my own hand, and put the day on it, because in about four years somebody will read that this court had never been asked and the person who says so will not be me** — and then: **a seat's notice asks me who I think should be heard about a water in my district. That is a proper question and it is a question this court is competent to answer and has never once been asked. I am not going to treat it as an insult and I am not going to treat it as an honour. It is a piece of administration and I am an administrator and I have nine years of a reputation for being the second one.** She goes into the corridor alone, into the cold room, where the flue was cut on the fourteenth of the second month and is not lit for anything but paper. About a hundred and forty files, nineteen years, bundles of ten, and on the inside front of each the same four lines in her own hand, written by her in the first year because nobody showed her a form: **By whose authority this is enforced. The instrument. The person. The person heard.** The fourth line is the same eleven words in most and different in about nine. **She had not opened the corridor to answer a seat's notice. She had opened it because a man from the district works had said nine words to a woman of sixty-four in a bay four miles away and she had heard them from a window, and she wrote that reason on the top of the first sheet she used, and it is the most honest thing in the chapter and the least useful.** What is in the files: **in nineteen years she has enforced continuation clauses against about nine hundred and forty people in this district, in about a hundred and forty matters, taking an instrument, finding a person, and holding that person to a duty the person had inherited.** And in the same nineteen years she has not enforced them against about four hundred, in three categories. **First: about two hundred and ten people who cannot be found** — the service is on the file and no court in this empire has ever gone back and said *this person was not served, they were not there*, so they stand served for ever and were never heard. **Second: about a hundred and forty people who are of a household**, where the duty falls on the household and the household is not a person, and she has entered *household* in the third line of about a hundred and forty files in nineteen years, and a household cannot be put in a room. **Third, and this is the one she has never written down and is writing down now: people she chose not to** — not through negligence, **but on purpose, twice, both times in the same fortnight of a flood season, and both times she can name the year and cannot name the file.** In the first she had about a hundred and thirty in front of her and a river coming, and she held about forty, and chose which forty, because a predictable claim in the right place stops an eviction in the wrong place, and wrote that reasoning down in a minute in her own hand and it reads like sense. In the second she had about two hundred and forty and held about nine, and chose those nine because they were the nine who had water standing in their ground. **She chose. Both times, by hand, on a day, in this district.** And the finding, in about a hundred and sixty words in her own hand: **nine hundred and forty people made answerable in nineteen years, by choice, and about four hundred not made answerable, by choice, and the second four hundred are the ones that make the first nine hundred and forty indefensible as a rule, because a rule is a thing that is applied the same way each time, and this was not the same way each time, and she chose, and the reason she chose is good, and a good reason applied unevenly by one person in a district for nineteen years is not a rule and cannot be defended as one.** And under it, the part that matters: **the three categories above are not three wrongs; two hundred and ten could not be found and about a hundred and forty are households and both of those are forms failing and I could have written a heading and did not. The third is not a form failing. The third is me.** And the reason she will not name them: **if I put the names in then a person in about four hundred and thirty miles who is entitled to be heard about it will be able to find me, and I have a warrant on a different matter, in a different city, that says a person from an office may read anything I write, and I am not going to hand them a list of about four hundred people I decided not to hold. So it goes in as this. A finding of fact about my own practice, made by me, on my own motion, in my own court, naming no person, against myself, and not appealable by anybody, and not a finding about anybody else, and not a discovery, and not a seam, and I am aware that the word I want to use is *arbitrary* and I am not going to use it, because I am not arbitrary and I was consistent to a rule, and the rule was mine and I made it and I applied it and it is the rule that is not defensible and not me, and I do not believe that and I am writing it down anyway because it is the version of it that is true in about four years.** She answers the notice at the bench outside, in a form, with a reason and no why, naming **four persons described or named on the form filed on the last day of the third month — two named, two described, and a person of about seventy-one at the fourth house at the north of the ford who cannot read a paragraph — and the foreman of the Slade Cut, whose name is at the foot of a list of works held in a hired store, and the person who holds the book of that Company:** five in all, and the court cannot identify any other and says so, and anybody who wishes to be heard may come to the window and ask. Four entries go in the minute: that she was asked in writing and answered in writing, and this is the first answer this court has ever given to a seat and the first time in nineteen years anybody has asked it; that the carriage offer is live and remains inoperable, and this court has now said who it thinks should be heard, and **the offer has still not operated, and the reason is that the offer pays carriage and not attendance and there is no form in this Empire for compelling a person to be brought to a room, and this court is not going to write to the seat about it again**; that the finding above names no person, is made on the magistrate's own motion, is not a finding about any other court and is not a discovery and is not a seam, **and that the undertaking this court entered on the ninth of the second month was used in this matter for the first time with the magistrate in the building and having been asked, and that the first three uses of it were without her and she was told about them and was not asked**; and that **nothing is ordered against anybody, nobody is liable, no fund is found, five persons are named or described and none of them is served, because this court cannot compel a person to attend, and any of the five may come to the window and ask, and two of the five have already come once.** And then: **I am not going to say anything about whether I was right. I said in the second month of this year that I do not know, and nothing has happened in between that has told me anything, and I am not going to manufacture a feeling about it in front of five people from a lane. I have read a hundred and forty files in a cold room this afternoon and I have found out one thing about myself that I did not know at the seventh hour this morning, and I am entering it, and that is all I am going to do with it today.** She does not open the file of the sixth year and nobody mentions it. A sheet goes four hundred miles up a river in a hand on the next day at four pence, to a person who reads the second entry of the minute, **with the word *inoperable* in it, and laughs out loud once, in a room with two people in it, and would not say why, and it was not a funny thing.**

### Chapter 0137 — A Figure, and a List With It

Aldis Kern's POV, the landing a hundred and forty yards below the ford, a wagon, the weigh-house and a lock-up, **the third and fourth weeks of the sixth month, from about the sixth hour.** He is forty-six and is the factor of the Kingsway Road Guild for the western reach, and he has a contract with nine months on it, about four hundred yards of road a flood has turned into a season's work, about nine men in three gangs, a wage bill, a toll, and a road a man in a good coat in a city will come and look at in the eighth month. **He came up the road not in a hurry, and the not being in a hurry was the first thing anybody noticed, and it was because he had decided what he was going to say in a wet week and what he had decided was not the words: it was that he was going to have to stand in a room and be told no by people who had nothing, and that a man in his position who has never been told no by anybody who cannot afford to is a man who has not been in a room.** He puts a slate on the bench in the north bay which is not a printed thing — *a barrow of stone, a man and a boy, one day: one shilling and sixpence, paid at the landing; ninety days, weather permitting, on the four hundred yards from the ford to the mill wall* — and it is read out, and about nine people in the room cannot read it and are spoken to by a person sitting next to them. Then **he says the price out loud, in the ordinary way, before he offers anything, and it is the only time in the chapter that he does anything right that is not usual:** that is the guild's rate and not the cut's rate, that the cut's rate is five men at eight pence and a boy at five, forty-five pence a day on the five and one, written on a lapsed list about a hundred yards up the road which he has read because he came up here to be told things and not to tell them; **that the guild pays a man with a barrow more than the Company pays five men, and he is not going to pretend that is generosity and he will not let anybody in that room say it is; and that the guild is in business, he is a factor, he has nine months and a road, and a road with a ford in it that cannot be crossed in the eighth month is a road he is told about in the eighth month.** Then the price of the price, in about four minutes, and nobody interrupts him: **the guild cannot pay a man it cannot prove did the work. Not a rule of the guild — a fact about a man with a wage bill and a guild with about nine hundred years of accounts. If I pay ninety days at one shilling and sixpence and nobody can produce a record that the work was done, then in about four years somebody at the counting end of this road is going to ask me for a list, and there is no list, and I will have been paid out and they will not, and I will be a man who took money and did not deliver, and there is no form anywhere for defending that. So the guild keeps a list of the persons who did the work. Not of the households. Of the persons. A name, a day, and a day rate against it. And I am telling you that before I show it to you, and I am telling you what it does, and then you can decide, and I want it understood that I am not able to take the money back off the table and I have known that since the third week of the fifth month and I came anyway.** The second slate is turned round by him: *the person who did the work; a day; a day rate; nothing else* — with a line that a person may be entered who is not of the Company and is under no duty of the Company, because a road is not a bank — and **at the foot of the list a line where any person may ask to be left off it, with no figure against that line and no reason asked for and nothing following from it.** **Nobody has been entered on it. It is blank. It is blank because I have not had it filled in and I could fill it in this afternoon in about nine minutes, and I have had it in a wagon since the third week of the fifth month and I have not filled it in, and I would like somebody to ask me why.** Then his answer to his own question, and the thing he is not able to do: **I cannot offer you the money without the list. I cannot offer you the list without telling you that I cannot refuse it. The guild is a body. I am a person and I am instructed. If the treasury at nine hundred miles says the western reach is to have a list of persons working the Slade ford for the term, then there is a list, and I have been the man who made it, and the fact that I stood in a bay and told you I could not refuse it will be in my own letter of instruction, which is a thing I write every week and which I have to write in the last four days of it. So I am telling you, and I would be a poor factor if I dressed it up: I am putting you in a position where you can say no to me. And I am aware that about nine people in this room have learned in the last two months that a person who is instructed is not a person who is free, and that I am the first one of those who has come up here and said so about himself before anybody asked. And I am not able to tell you what the answer is.** Asked who told him to do it — the fourth question on a board in a lane in another district, which nobody on that bank has ever thought to ask a stranger — he starts to say a name and has to stop, and then: **his name is on a letterhead and I have said it nine hundred times and I cannot tell you what it is. He is a person. He writes at the end of every week. That is the whole of what I know about who I am and I have not met him and I have never been in his room and I have never seen a face and I have had about four hundred letters from him and I could not tell you one thing about him, and if he were to die this morning the guild would be a different shape in about nine months and I would be told about it in a letter. When I came up this road I was carrying two things from two places that are four hundred miles apart and neither of them is a person, and both of them are bodies, and both of them have sent me here. A seat sent me a form with a seal on it. A guild sent me a list with a column for a person. They are the same form. Somebody in one of the two buildings decided that there must be a person at this end of it, and the two of them have not met, and I have met both of them, and I am the only place in this Empire where the two of these are in the same pair of hands.** Then the difference he is the only person in the chapter to see, said to a foreman and a book-keeper: **your book has nine hundred and forty names in it and no heading and it is sixty years old and nobody has ever been chosen from it. That is not a list and it is not a register and it is the best thing anybody on this cut has. It was not made in order to find anybody. It was made because work got done and somebody wrote it down, and the difference between that and a list is the whole of the difference between a record and a register, and I have been keeping registers for nineteen years and I know which one I am holding. A list of persons is a thing that exists so that somebody can be found. And that is not a bad thing to make. If the list exists, then in about four years a person can go to a guild strongbox about nine hundred miles up the road and ask a question about the seventh of the seventh and get the name of whoever was on the bank that day. And they can do that about ninety times a term. And it is not ninety names in one room, it is a name a day, for about nine hundred days, in a book, that anybody with four pence can come and read.** And to the book-keeper: **it is like the miller's name in our book, and I know he has not slept properly since the sixth of the third month and I am not going to pretend to him that a name in a margin is a good thing; I did it in about four seconds and I did not know what it was; you have had nine weeks to find out what it is and I have had nine days, and I have found out less, and I have brought money.** And then the ninety days: **ninety days at one shilling and sixpence for a man and a boy is a hundred and thirty-five shillings, which is six pounds fifteen shillings, which is more than the Company's whole money for about three years, and more than the spring bank cost**, and the bay goes quiet for about eleven seconds, and a woman of about forty-four at the third house says **it is a great deal of money**, and is told it is, and says **and there are about sixty of us under sixteen**, and is told he knew when he got off the wagon, because he stopped at the gate on the eleventh and read the list of works off a bench through a door, **because a thing on a bench is findable and I have heard that sentence three times this month and I have begun to believe it.** And then the last of it, in three sentences and no more: **the same form went to the Sorn border households on the northern marches in the second month of this year; House Sorn has the most restrictive border law of the nine and it requires that a thing offered to a border household at no charge be answered; the Sorn households answered it, refused it, and the refusal is entered at a waystation. Nobody on this cut has seen that entry. I have seen the title of it at a trade counter in a city about nine hundred miles up the road and I have not read it and I cannot get to it and it is not mine. And it is the only document anybody has mentioned to me in four months in which a House was made to put something in a book about itself and could not leave it out. I am telling you that not because it is any use to you. I am telling you because I am about to hand you a list of persons, and I would like it to be on the record that I know of one list that went the other way.** Nobody asks him what the other way was. He says a refusal is a thing that has a person in it. Then the two slates go on the bench together, and he asks for the list to be read out twice, and asks that the second reading be the slower one, and says he will wait while it is done and will not go out to the wagon.

### Chapter 0138 — The Fourth Line, and Who Can Fill It In

Marek Kest's POV, the Received Room and a room in the Roll Hall corridor, **the fourth week of the sixth month, from about the ninth hour to about the fourth hour of the afternoon, and the courtyard at the end.** He came back down the road on the nineteenth, having been eleven days in a bay four hundred and thirty miles from this desk, and there is a single side of good paper, not printed, on the shelf, that nobody sent him: **To the clerk of the Inheritance Desk of this city, being a person who has been named in a document touching a water guarantee sealed under the fire-and-water device. The instrument touches you. It touches you as follows, and the table asks the same question of every person it reaches, and it asks it in the fourth line of a form, and the fourth line is not long. In what way this instrument touches you.** And under that, in a clerk's hand at the foot: **the answer is free and the answer is not required for the instrument to be true, and a person may answer or not answer and nothing follows for them either way; a person should not answer without knowing what they are agreeing to, and there is no person in this matter who has been told what they are agreeing to; no person has been named in the instrument; it has been offered to a body.** He puts it down and picks it up up again, because he has been carrying it about eleven minutes. **It was addressed to him by name.** Then the working-out, in about four minutes standing up at about the ninth hour: **in the fourth month of this year nobody on the Slade could have answered that**, because a party has to be a person, and for eleven weeks there was nobody on that cut who was a person an instrument could touch, and the Company is a body and cannot be made a party and has no head and there is no form for a head — and that was true on the eleventh of the fourth month and true on the thirtieth of the last month, **and it is not true now**: four households at the north of the ford are parties and cannot be un-named, two of them described and a described person is a person; a foreman of fifty-one has her own name at the foot of a list of works, and a clerk of a court four hundred and thirty miles from that bay is in the same store in a book with no heading with a date against his name; and a man of about fifty opened a letter addressed to a body in a bay in front of about thirty people and became the person it was about, on purpose, having worked out in six years that he should not. **So the fourth line can be filled in. Somebody on that cut could put a name to it. About five of them could, this morning, in about nine seconds each, and two of the five cannot read a paragraph and one of the five is a woman of about thirty-one who has refused a name once already and would have to be asked a second time. And in four months this matter has done nothing whatever except make those five.** He sits on the edge of the table about nine minutes without writing anything, the second time in a year, the first being on the eleventh of the fourth month with nine pages in a weigh-house four hundred and thirty miles from this desk. Tamsin Rook comes in at about the half hour, makes him tell it in order, and when he gets to the end of the working-out she says one thing and he has to get to the rest of it in nine seconds: **a person who speaks for ninety-one other people is not hearing them, he is speaking for them; hearing is one person at a time; the form has one line and the line is for one person; that is not a defect in the form, that is the whole of what a form is — it is a line with one name's worth of room on it, built that way by about a hundred and forty years of people who had watched what happens when you put a body in one. And the honest answer to that fourth line, for anybody on that bank, is a sentence about about ninety-one households; and the day anybody writes it, that person is a party; and the reason nobody could write it in the fourth month is that there was nobody to be a party; and now there is, and that is the only reason the door is open, and the door being open is not a thing to be pleased about, it is the same finding as everything else in this matter wearing a different hat.** And then the answer to *in what way does the instrument touch you* is **no**, and the reason is the volume's: **if one person is to fill in that line, then the person who fills it in is the answer for the Company, and a person who answers for a body is its head, and there is no head of that Company and a magistrate has entered that one cannot be had, and I would rather say that in a room than have a person appointed to it in a city by a person who cannot be named. So the answer is that there is nobody to put in that box. Not because nobody will. Because anybody who fills it in is doing the thing the instrument wants done, in the box, in the box, where it is a line with a name on it.** He uses the rule, for the fifth time in this matter, on the back of a proof sheet: **the choice is one of two — one, he answers the fourth line in whole or in part in any form to any table on any matter touching this instrument; two, he answers nobody and nothing, and a copy of the fact that he has refused goes to the two people on that bank who can read it. Answer in a room, in words, with a date.** She reads it twice, comes round the table, and does not take nine minutes about it, which is the first time in four months that she has not needed nine minutes. **Two. And the date is the twentieth, and the reason is not a legal one and it is the same reason as in the fourth month: the moment a paper is in a bundle with a name at the top of it, somebody is going to come and find you, and you cannot be found and serve a person and also be the reason ninety-one people are inside something. That is not a principle. That is what it has taken a man in a city four hundred and thirty miles from here over about fourteen months, and a bar discharged on a page of his own, and a line on a wrist. Three. The copy goes to two people and to no body; a copy sent to a body is a copy about ninety-one households and the difference is about four hundred and thirty miles of carriage and a clerk's decision, and I made that decision in a bay in the fourth week of the fourth month and I have not changed it. Four. I am not going to be thanked for it and if you thank me I will write it down.** He does not thank her. He says: **I am not giving you the sheet, and I am not acting on it tonight, and I will not give it to you again, and the reason is not that you are wrong** — and she says that is permitted and she has thought about it for about nine minutes and is not going to make it a condition, and has never once made one. Then, in the ordinary way, at about the second hour, four minutes: **two things and they are about you and not about the paper. One. I asked you in a bay in the first week of this month, in front of about thirty people, four things about a line on your wrist, and you have not once mentioned it since, and it has been six weeks, and the review is in the first week of the fifth month of next year and nobody has promised to come to it. A mark is a symptom; it decides nothing; it reads nothing; it does not know where the ford is; and you have stopped mentioning it, and I want to be plain about why that is not the same as it having gone quiet, because a thing that has gone quiet is a thing that will come back louder. Two. You are treating your body as a place to store public cases.** And then, unsoftened, because she is not going to be tender about it, **because the moment anybody in this matter is tender about it, it becomes a virtue, and it is not a virtue, it is a system of storage. You have carried a fund, a rail, a bank, a child of sixteen and about sixty children you have never met, and you are the shelving in it. And the reason you do it is that it is faster than asking, and because a person who asks can be refused and a person who carries cannot. Stop. Not for me; I have no standing and I have said so in this room before and I will say it again. For the eleven people on a towpath who are going to be asked to carry something in the seventh month, because there is not going to be anybody left who can.** And: **I do not know how to stop. Neither do I, and I have been doing it for fourteen months and I have got a drawer with four things in it and one of them has been there since the first month of last year and it is not going out. So we are two people who cannot stop. That is not a reason to carry more.** Then the sixth door, which he does not let go: the letter's own warning, **a person should not answer without knowing what they are agreeing to**, is the most careful sentence anybody in this matter has produced in four months and it is free, and **a free warning is the most expensive object in this entire matter and it has been sitting on my shelf since the nineteenth; every other form in this matter has lied in the direction of being easy to fill in — a box that wants a person and will take a body, a line at the top for a head no cooperative has, a fourth line that is a question about a person printed underneath a letter about a company — and every one of them has been drafted to make it possible for somebody to say yes. And a warning is free, and a free warning is a warning that has been read by nobody, and the most honest sentence in the matter reached ninety-one households through a person who is not paid and is not allowed to explain it, and that is exactly how it was designed to arrive, and I do not think anybody designed it. I think that is just what happens.** And the five, written on the back of a second proof sheet and not shown to anybody for a day and a half: **the foreman of fifty-one, the book-keeper of thirty-eight, the miller of about fifty, a woman of about seventy-one at the north of the ford who cannot read a paragraph and has said in a room that she is not going to have anything to say and would like the asking, and a woman of about thirty-one at the first house with two children, who was asked her name once in a doorway and refused it and let herself be described, and who came two hours down a towpath in the mud to be told the water had not come into her house, and who is coming back on the twenty-third and did not, and who has not been to that bay once.** And under the list, the sentence he shows her: **if any of these five fills in that line, the guarantee is answered and the answer is against the person who wrote it, and each of the five is answering it alone and about ninety-one people, and none of the five has been asked by anybody whether they understand that those are the same act, and two of them cannot read what is being answered, and one of them has already been asked once in a doorway and said no to a different question about the same paper. I am not going to send this list. I am going to keep it in my own book, which has no office on it, because that is where I keep the things I am not able to do anything with. And in about four years somebody is going to find it, because that is what happens, and they are going to find five names and no answers, and I would rather they found that than found nothing at all.** A copy of his own refusal goes four hundred and thirty miles in a hand and not in an envelope on the twentieth, at four pence, to a foreman of fifty-one and a book-keeper of thirty-eight and to nobody else. **There is a line on the inside of his left wrist about two inches long and it answers nothing and it has been asked about twice in a day by two people who were not talking about it.**

### Chapter 0139 — What a Seal Is For

Vaunt Oris's POV, his chamber, a room with a table and a window and about nine things in it, and the register of concessions behind it, **the fourth and last weeks of the sixth month.** He is fifty-one or two, a notary of House Oris in his own right, nineteen years in that chair, and **he has certified about four hundred instruments a year in that time and has never certified one he had not seen, which is not in any rule of the house and is in him, and it is the thing that is going to cost this House something.** There are two people in the room: the practice's clerk, about thirty-four, who drafts, and the trade assessor, who stands between a house and a guild and is nobody's servant and has been writing things down for twenty-six years. **The request has been on that table for thirty-two days.** He answers it in two parts on the twenty-sixth and it takes about nine minutes, with **a witness at each end of the room** — the assessor here, and at the other end a person in a building four hundred and thirty miles away, because a notary's table and a House notary do not sign the same sheet and somebody has to be at both ends of it, and the somebody was a foreman of fifty-one who came down the road in the fourth week of the sixth month, stood in a passage in a House about two hundred miles from a notary's table, **did not sign anything, and said afterwards that she was glad of it.** First the second limb, certified in about four minutes: **that the Company is an unincorporated body upon the register of bodies in that district; that it has no head; that no person is its head; that a body may not be made a party by any court in this Empire; and that a body which has been given a duty and cannot refuse it has accepted it. The last of those is not an opinion, it is a consequence. A body cannot say no. Nine words on a piece of paper in a hired bay four hundred and thirty miles from this room are the whole of the acceptance, and nobody in ninety-one households said them, and the paper says on its face that the acceptance does not evidence a consent. That sentence is why this certificate can be given and it is the reason the certificate is worth nothing.** And he says: **I have just sealed a true statement that a body agreed to something it was not asked about, and I want it noticed that I have done it deliberately and that I would do it again, and that the reason is the last sentence, and that if that paper had not said it, this certificate would not exist.** Then the first limb, refused, in four sentences, copied into the book of concessions in his own hand: **the practice is not satisfied that the persons named in the said book are the persons of the said Company, and is not able to be satisfied of it, and is not asked to be satisfied of anything else in place of it. A book with names in it is not a list of persons. The said book has nine hands in it and about nine hundred and forty names in it over about sixty years, and no heading at the top of it, and no list of persons in it, and about four hundred of those names are of persons who are dead, and the figure of about ninety-one households is not in the book at all and is a count taken off it and written on the back of a person's hand by a person who cannot read a paragraph. The practice is not able to certify that the ground and the paper are the same thing, because they are not the same thing, and the difference is about four hundred dead names and sixty years of writing down work instead of making a list. A seal is a promise that the paper and the ground are the same thing. I will not put one on a statement that is true of a book and false of a cut.** Nobody says anything for about nine seconds, which is about how long Oswin Marrow waited for a different man in a different room in a different city, and Vaunt Oris has never been told that and would not have minded. Then the clerk asks what goes in the book: **that is the whole of what is left of the job, and it took me thirty-two days and about nine of them were spent being asked nicely by people I work for. What goes in the book is the refusal and the reason, in my own hand, with the day, and the name at the foot, because the practice's rule is that a concession is only ever entered with the name of the person who gave it up, and the reason the rule exists is that a House which can lose one quietly will lose the next one quietly and the next.** And: **it goes in a book in a room, and in about four years a person who wants to know whether this House can be held to anything will come and read it, and there will be a name at the foot of it, and the name will be mine, and I will not be able to say that I had no choice in the matter, because I did have a choice and I took the other one.** Then the sentence he asked to have put in, before he signs, having been asked in the ordinary way by the trade assessor whether he wanted anything put in that he was not being asked for: **I want one sentence on the page in my own hand, and it is not a reason and it is not a defence, and I am aware of that, and I am going to write it anyway because I have been in this chair for nineteen years and I have never once put a sentence of my own into a document and I am about to lose a hundred and forty years of somebody else's work and there is no reason on this earth why a man should do that without leaving a mark of his own. I would rather it held. The rotation. The list of works with a name at the foot of it and a date and a review. A foreman who says no before she says yes. A book-keeper who will not start a column called *why not*. I would rather ninety-one households were on a schedule than under a guarantee that costs them nothing and takes their children, and I have wanted that since the twelfth of the fourth month and I have not said it to one living soul until this afternoon, and I am saying it in a room with two people in it because I am about to do the other thing and I would like there to be one line on this page that is mine and is not a reason. And I am aware that a dragon saying *I would rather* is worth about what a person saying *I would rather* is worth, and I am aware of why the first is worth more, and I am aware that in about four years somebody is going to read this book and see that a House said *I would rather* and use it as a thing about Houses.** He writes it at the foot, in his own hand, and dates it, **and it is the only sentence in the book of concessions in a hundred and forty years that is in the first person, and it is the reason the rest of the entry is believed, and he knew that when he wrote it and wrote it anyway.** Then his own House's rule of about four hundred years — **no instrument takes effect in a room before the persons it touches have had the day**, not a law, which he told a human court in another city in the sixth month of last year before he was asked, and said he had never once been able to persuade a court to adopt it and had stopped trying, and said *you are about to see me use it* — and **he uses it on the certificate: he writes the second part and seals it and leaves the sheet on the table in the open for a day, unsealed, and the day was for the persons, and the persons were about ninety-one households four hundred and thirty miles away, and the day did them no good whatever and that was not the point.** And he says: **this is the first time in nineteen years I have used my own House's rule against my own House's request. I am aware of what that is. It is not defection and it is not a conversion and it is not a man discovering that human law is better. It is a notary refusing to put a seal on a sentence that is true of a book. And I am not going to leave the chamber and I am not going to stop certifying and I am not going to be a bridge to anything, and I would like it entered that I have said the word myself and I have not been asked for it by anybody.** The assessor writes that down, **and it is the only sentence in about four hundred and thirty miles of this matter that is in the record of a House and not in a court**, and it goes down the road to a bay on a towpath on the twenty-ninth in a hand and not in an envelope at four pence. Then the entry itself: **a split instrument, and the column in the register of concessions had never been used in this house in nineteen years and now has one line in it, and one half of the sheet carries a seal and the other half carries a name, and the name is at the foot of a page in a book about a hundred and forty years old with a crack in the spine.** In the clerk's hand: the concession with which the practice certifies on that reach is **lapsed**, in respect of the certification of the Slade Cut guarantee, the person who gave it up is named at the foot, and his reason is copied in above in his own hand. **What that means, in a room, in about nine words: this House can no longer certify a scale instrument on that reach. Not this practice. This House. From the day the entry is made, and there is no appeal, and there is no form in this Empire for anybody to ask whether it is right.** And the sentence he writes under the entry, in his own hand, which is the one that will be read: **an uncertified guarantee may be offered again, by a person, with a different question. I am not able to say that nobody will. I am the person who refused this one and I have written my name at the foot of the refusal, and in about four years the person who reads this will have both, and the person who comes then will not be a boy of fourteen and will not be a man from a district works and will not be a woman of sixty-four who cannot read a paragraph, and will be somebody who has been looking for this for a long time. I would like it recorded that I thought about that for about nine days and I did it anyway, and that the reason is that the practice's rule is a name at the foot of a page and a name at the foot of a page is worth exactly what it is worth against the person who will be looked up next, and I have been the person who will be looked up, and I would rather be looked up by somebody with a reason.** And then the last of the chapter, and the last of him: **he did not go down the road. A notary of a House does not go and tell a district what his House has done. There is no form for it and there is no expectation of it and the whole of the practice's arrangement is that a House does not go to a place.** A copy goes, at four pence, in a hand, carried by a person who is not paid for carrying it, and the person who carries it is a clerk of the district works who was the wrong side of sixty and had four men at a gate for four days with a list that said nothing on it, and he carried it up a towpath in three days and did not open it. It went into a bay where about nine people could not read a word of it, and it was read out twice, and the second reading was the slower one, and both readings were by a person who was not paid and neither of them would say what it meant. **Nobody on that bank knows what a concession is, and that was said out loud in that room on the twenty-ninth by a book-keeper of thirty-eight, and it is the first sentence in about four months that anybody in ninety-one households has said which is worse than not knowing. The guarantee did not go away. It stands, offered and unanswered, under a device that no longer has a notary behind it on that reach, and it will stand there in exactly the words it stood in on the eighth of the fifth month, and about sixty children under sixteen are inside it, and the instrument that would take them out of it is on a bench in a hired bay with a foreman's name at the foot of it and a date and no figure on it. So the cost of a seal that was not put on a sentence about a book is a hundred and forty years of a House's licence on one reach of one river, paid by a man who is not a villain and is not a friend and has not been thanked and did not ask to be, and what it bought is about four hundred and thirty miles of road and one sentence in a book in the first person, and neither of those is a wall. A body cannot refuse. A person can. And the whole of the difference is a name at the foot of a page, and it took a dragon nineteen years and a hundred and forty years of his House's work to be able to put one there, and it took a woman of sixty-four who cannot read a paragraph to say out loud, in a bay, in nine seconds, what it is worth.**

### Chapter 0140 — A Name at the Foot of It

Doreth Ferrow's POV, the north bay and the south end of the same hired grain store, **the last week of the sixth month, from about the seventh hour to about the ninth.** She is sixty-four, has kept this meeting for nineteen years, cannot read a paragraph, can add, can count a room, and has about sixty children under sixteen in that cut and has met the two youngest of them. The bay is full at the seventh hour because the weather has turned. There is a sheet on the bench in the middle with a seal on it and about nine words at the foot, and it has been read out twice already and is going to be read out twice again: **the first reading by a book-keeper of thirty-eight in about eleven minutes, the second by a girl of seventeen who is not paid and will not say what it means in about nine minutes, and the second one was the slower one.** What it says is this: a notary's table of a House was asked to be satisfied of two things; **it certified that the Company is an unincorporated body upon a register, that it has no head, that no court may make it a party, and that a body which has been given a duty and cannot refuse it has accepted it, and that certificate is true and it is worth nothing**; it refused the other, in these words copied from the original — **a book with names in it is not a list of persons**; the book has about nine hundred and forty names in it over about sixty years, no heading, about four hundred of them dead, and the number of households is not in the book and is a count on the back of a person's hand; **a seal is a promise that the paper and the ground are the same thing, and the notary refused to put one on a statement that is true of a book and false of a cut**; the concession with which that House certifies on this reach is entered as lapsped, **and the name of the person who gave it up is at the foot of the entry and it is not the name of a House**; and **nothing in this instrument affects any person in ninety-one households, no person has been ordered to do anything, no household owes any sum, no fund has been found, the guarantee stands offered and unanswered and may be taken up at any time afterwards by a person answering in its terms.** And at the foot, in a hand about five hundred years old, is a dragon's name. Nobody says anything for about nine seconds, and then she says, **read the last line again**, and the book-keeper reads it again and she makes him read it a third time. Nobody in that bay knows what it is, so they do it the way they do it: **what is a concession** — a licence, without which that practice cannot put a seal on a water paper on this reach and nothing else; **what is a seal** — a promise that the paper and the ground are the same thing, not a decoration and not a signature, and a seal on a thing that is not true is not a mistake, it is a lie with a House's name on it; **what has just happened** — a House has lost the right to say that kind of thing about water on this reach, a man has written his own name at the foot of the page that says so, about four hundred miles away in a book about nine hundred years old there is a line with his name on it, and in about four years anybody can pay four pence and read it. And then her sentence, in about four minutes, to about nine people hearing all of it and four of them understanding it, which she had worked out in a cold corridor four miles away about a fortnight earlier and has been carrying in her own head ever since: **a person who is not a person in the world can be made to pay for a thing by having his name written down. That is the whole of it. He is a dragon. Nobody in this bay has ever been asked to think of a dragon as a person who pays anything, and a dragon cannot be sued, cannot be fined, cannot be sent to a district house, cannot be made to carry a stone up a bank, and cannot be asked. And a name is a road. That is the only thing a name is. It is not a punishment and it is not a debt and it is not a party and it is not a vote. It is a road, and about four hundred yards down that road there is a house with four pence in a fund and about a hundred and forty years in it, and a person can walk up the road and pay the four pence and stand in the room and read the name and say, that is the man who lost it, that is the man who did it on purpose, and now I know whose it was. And an institution cannot do that. An institution cannot be sued and a person can be. That is the whole of the difference and it is not a fair one and I am not going to stand in this bay and pretend it is. So a dragon can pay. A dragon can pay by having his name written down, and the price of the payment is that he is findable for ever, and that is the only way anybody in the world has ever been able to make one of them pay for anything, and it is going to cost a person about four hundred miles of his life and he has to agree to it before it happens.** And then the second half of it, which nobody is going to like: **that is a name at the foot of a page. I have spent a month in this room telling about thirty people that a name in a book is a chain round about sixty necks, and I meant it and I was right. And on the sixth day of the third month a book-keeper wrote the miller's name against an entry in that book on purpose, so that the Company would be easy to compel itself, and I have watched what that did to a man of about fifty for eleven weeks, and on the eleventh day of the fifth month a man of about fifty opened a letter that had a body's name on it, in this room, in front of all of you, and he told us that he was the door to it and had been since the ninth. So the same thing has happened twice in about four months, about four hundred yards apart. Once to a man in a cooperative, who chose it, and once to a man in a House, who had it chosen for him, and the difference between those two is about a hundred and forty years of law and about four hundred and thirty miles of road, and the result on this bank is identical: there is a name in a book and a person who can be found. I am not saying the two are the same. I am saying a name is a road and that I have spent a month teaching this room that and I have just been shown what a road is for, and it is for the person who walks up it, and about four years from now somebody is going to walk up one about a man of fifty who opened a letter, and he will not have a dragon behind him, and he will not have a hundred and forty years, and he will have a mill.** And then the question she has never asked in nineteen years: **who came to this bay in the fourth month, and why.** It takes about half an hour and it is not comfortable. Four cannot say. Two say *to see*. One says *to help* and nobody believes him and he knows it and stays in the room. And a man of about thirty says, in nine words, to the middle of the floor: **I wanted to see if it worked.** A woman of about forty-four says that is not a bad thing to want, and is told: **that is the most frightening sentence anybody has said in this bay, and I have had a dragon lose a hundred and forty years in this room tonight and that did not frighten me as much as that did. Because a dragon losing a licence is a thing that happened in another building and it cost a person who could see it happening. And a man standing in a yard watching a household to see whether being helped works is a thing that happens in about four years, in about nine yards, on this cut, and nobody will have been asked, and I do not know what I am supposed to do about it and I am sixty-four and I have one question left in me and I have just spent it. Are you going to ask him why. No. He told me why. I asked and he told me and that is what asking is. What I am not going to do is stand in nine yards over the next four years making sure that is what a person came for.** It goes in the book with the day on it, in a book-keeper's hand, and not on the back of her hand. **The book has four things in it in a month with a name at the foot of them, and the last of the four is a sentence about a dragon's name on a page, and it is the first thing anybody in ninety-one households has ever entered in that book about anything that is not work.** A copy of the sheet goes up a towpath on the thirtieth in a hand and not in an envelope at four pence, carried by a person who is not paid and was not asked to and is going anyway, **and it is going north to a waystation on the northern marches about four hundred and thirty miles the other way, where House Sorn's border law requires that a thing offered to a border household at no charge be answered, and where the Sorn border households answered the same form in the second month of this year and refused it and the refusal is entered** — and nobody on the Slade has seen that entry, and a factor of forty-six has seen the title at a trade counter about nine hundred miles up the road and has not read it, **and it is the only document anybody has mentioned in four months in which a House was made to put something in a book about itself and could not leave it out, and it is the only one anybody on that bank can point at, and it is four hundred and thirty miles away, and there is a man walking.** Then the five live things, and nobody has done anything about any of them: **a clerk of a court four hundred and thirty miles from that bank holds a ford for a year and the review of his term is in the first week of the fifth month of next year and nobody has promised to come to it, and the paper says that if nobody comes then it stops on the day and there is no successor and nothing carries; a seat's notice has been declined by a foreman of fifty-one in nine words and a district court has been asked, in writing, for the first time in nineteen years, and has answered with five names, and the seat's carriage of anybody should be heard is live and inoperable and will stay inoperable, because it pays carriage and not attendance; a list of persons is on a bench in a hired bay with a line at the foot of it and no figure against that line, and it is blank, and a factor of forty-six has said out loud in that bay that he is not able to refuse it; a water guarantee under the fire-and-water device stands offered and unanswered on a bank about four hundred and forty foot long with about sixty children under sixteen inside it, and it costs nothing, and it can be taken up at any time afterwards by a person answering in its terms, and there is no such person and there is not going to be one; and a book in a stone store has a rule in it written on the twenty-eighth day of the third month, entered a second time in the second week of the fifth month, that binds about thirty people and says nothing whatever about the other about sixty, and both figures are the same figures as they are on the back of a woman's hand in two other places, and nobody has joined them and nobody is going to.** And a day has been fixed for the third week of the seventh month, by a chair, in a bay, for the purpose of asking a body whether it will accept a duty that stops. **A body cannot answer that, and a body cannot refuse it, and a body cannot be made to stop. And the whole of the arrangement that would work is a person, and there are about sixty of them needed, and there is no list.** Then the two small live things that close the batch, and they are the only ones anybody has asked for. **The woman of about thirty-one came back up that towpath on the twenty-third of the fourth month and did not come again, nobody sent for her, nobody is going to, and that is the fourth thing in this matter left alone on purpose** — and Rennick Adley writes it down anyway, on the thirtieth, in his own hand, in a book with no heading, at the bottom of a page, and reads it back twice: *I am not going to go up that lane a second time on my own account and I have written that down so that in about four years there is one sentence in a book that says a person was not asked twice, in a thing where everybody else in this matter has been asked two or three times and did not know it.* And **the woman of about seventy-one, who cannot read a paragraph, sent a message down the lane in the third week of the sixth month, and it was nine words and a person carried it and the person was not paid: *tell them I have read it twice and I am one of the five; tell them I would like the asking again.*** **A person of seventy-one who cannot read a paragraph asked, in writing, to be asked again, and nobody on the Slade Cut has been able to work out, in about nine days, whether that is a request or a warning. It is the fifth thing that is live and it is the smallest one and it is the only one anybody has asked for.** And the carrier of that message had the copy with a dragon's name on it in his coat, and asked the man of seventy-one at the door, in four words, whether he would want to come, and was told he would not, with a reason, and the reason was good: **I cannot stand in a room with about thirty people in it for nine minutes, and I would rather be asked at my own door four times a year than be in that bay once, and both of those are my choice and I have been able to make one of them and not the other and I do not know which one that is.** It went in the book the same night with the day on it.

## Volume 03, Batch 0005 (Chapters 141–150) — A Duty That Stops, and the Name at the Foot of the Fourth List

**Volume 03 is closed at Chapter 150.** What follows is the per-chapter record of its fifth and last movement and a closing statement of what the volume resolved, what it did not, and what it hands on.

### Chapter 0141 — A Duty That Ends

Rennick Adley's POV, the north bay, the tool shed, and the lane at the north of the ford — four houses and about a mile and a half of towpath — **the first week of the seventh month, the seventh of it and the morning after.** He is thirty-eight, has kept the Company's book for six years, reads a paragraph perfectly, cannot read a chapter of law, and has made it his business in four months to know which about nine people in ninety-one households cannot read a word. He took a slate and no form. On the thirtieth of the sixth month he wrote at the bottom of a page that he was not going up that lane a second time on his own account, and read it back twice, and that sentence is the chapter: he goes, and going is the only reason he can do anything, and he says so. The arithmetic is done out loud on the way — **forty-five pence a day, about ninety days, three pounds fifteen and threepence for a season, the Company's whole money four shillings a year and that is the rent of a store** — and the finding of the volume is in it: **a season on that bank costs the Company nothing, and the only thing that costs anything is a person's wage, and a wage is the price, and the price is the point, and everything else in this matter for four months has been free.** At the first house a woman of about thirty-one with two children hears the question twice and says yes and says she did not want to, refuses her name a second time and lets herself be a description again, makes him change *household* to *person at the first house*, and gives the chapter's sentence: **you are asking me to hold a thing for a season, and in three months there will be a day, and on that day it is not mine any more, and I will still be the one they come to — the duty does not stop, the person stops being asked.** At the third house a woman of about forty-four says no with a reason. At the fourth house, a mile and a half and forty feet up, a person of about seventy-one opens the door before he knocks, asks to be asked at that door four times a year, refuses the rotation, gives the same reason as in June and says she is not going to say it a third time, and asks him to write down that she is one of the five. And then she asks him what happens to the thirty-one, and he tells her the truth: they are on a board in chalk four miles away, he has never tried, and he has read it in a trade sheet. His own entry closes the chapter: **a rotation is not a list; it is one question asked sixty times, in sixty doorways, and the question has to be one a person can say no to, and the sixth line is what makes it one** — and eleven wanted, three answers, two of them no, and eight days to the day a chair fixed.

### Chapter 0142 — Four Lines and Two Ends

Marek Kest's POV, the Received Room and a room in the Roll Hall corridor, Auremar, **the second week of the seventh month, from about the ninth hour to about the sixth.** Tamsin Rook is in the room because a court order requires it, and he is in the room because she is, and neither has said so and it has been four months. He has been down the road four times this year and keeps a list of the dates. The first version of the four lines, ending **if no person is then found the duty ceases and nothing carries it forward**, and she makes him read the notice clause back: **that is the only line in it that is worth anything, and you have written a duty that a person can stop and have not written anywhere what stops it, and the water does not stop.** He gets up and opens the door nine inches, which he does not do, and then says the two ends: **an end where somebody comes on the day, which is a duty that stops and there is something else standing behind it when it does, and an end where nobody has to come, which is a hole in a wall** — and that he built the wrong one in the second week of the fourth month, in about eleven minutes, and has carried it eleven weeks, **and the reason is that I could not stand on a towpath for eleven weeks, and the end that works is the end with about sixty people in it.** She writes it down and makes him change *carried* to *carried by me*, and then says the second half: **you have just described a man building a paper around the size of himself.** The cost is priced out loud and is the arithmetic of the whole volume: forty-five pence a day, about ninety days, three pounds fifteen and threepence, a spring bank that cost three pounds eleven and threepence out of seventeen shillings and a penny the week entered under the word *held*, four shillings a year and that is the roof — **a season costs the Company no money at all, and the whole of the cost is the wages, and the price of this paper is that about sixty people are going to be findable, and a person who can be found is a person who can be asked about the bank, and there is no version of this arrangement in which they can be found and the bank cannot.** The second version has five lines and puts the reader on the schedule at the same rate as everybody else. It is put in his own book with the day on it and not sent. She says write the box with a nothing in it; he says that is a promise about ninety-one households in his own hand. Then she reaches for the rule, and he says **no, and the reason is that the choice is not mine, because I am not the person who is asked either way** — and she answers that the rule does not apply, and that this is the first correct use of it anybody has made in a year, and it is not using it, and she writes the date on a page with nothing under it anyway because she wants a record that he thought about it. At about the sixth hour he asks her what a rotation is for and she tells him a fact and not an advice: about eleven people have said yes, about forty-nine have not been asked, somebody else is going to ask them in about nine weeks, and it is not going to be him. The undertaking of the fourth of the first month is live and is not exercised. The rule is not used. Nothing is sent, no instrument is written, no reading is performed, and the fourth envelope in a drawer has been there since the first month of last year.

### Chapter 0143 — The Day a Chair Fixed

Doreth Ferrow's POV, the north bay, **the third week of the seventh month, the fifteenth, from about the seventh hour to about the ninth.** She fixed the day on the twenty-ninth of the sixth month in nine words and did not consult the room, and says so first. Then she says the finding before anybody has spoken, so that about nine people do not sit for an hour waiting to be told it: **a company cannot say no; it can be written to and it is being written to; it is on a schedule in a city and every future flood on that cut is guaranteed by nobody asking twice; and a body that is asked a thing and cannot say no has accepted it.** Somebody asks what the point of the day was. **There was no point of the day. Because I wanted to hear the word no out of a mouth in my own room.** She reads the question off the back of her own hand twice, the second slower: one person, forty-five pence a day, one season of about ninety days, a day to give it up in your own hand in a book that has no heading, your name at the foot of a list and a day against the name — and then adds a sixth line of her own, **a person who says no is not asked again by this meeting**, and offers to take it off and does not, and is told by a woman of about forty-four that she has never once said that in nineteen years. And answers the challenge to her own authority with it: **a chair cannot give anybody anything; the sixth line is not a gift, it is a chair saying out loud that she will not come back, and that is not power, it is the opposite of power, and it is all I have.** Then it is asked, one question at a time, about thirty people, and the count is done in chalk and read back and wiped off and put in a book. **Eleven said yes.** Nine had to be told a second time what a season is. **Four said no**, two of them in the room and two not, and the two who are not in the room are read in twice and the second reading is slower — the woman of about seventy-one at the fourth house, whose reason she has said twice and will not say a third time, and who asks that it be read to the room twice. Nine asked what happens after and were told there is no answer to that yet. The rest said nothing and the chair counted the nothing, which nobody in that bay had ever thought to do. A man of about thirty-one in a bay coat asks the question nobody had thought of — **what is in it for the next one** — and when told there is no answer, says **then that is the question**, and is asked who he is, and says **I am the man and the boy.** Then the chair enters, and it is read out twice: no household is bound by anything said in that room; the first exception is in force; the guarantee stands offered and unanswered at no charge; about sixty of them under sixteen are inside both exactly as they were on the eighth of the fifth month, and nobody in that room has made that better and nobody has made it worse. And then the sentence this volume turns on: **in nineteen years I asked this room forty times for a resolution and I got a no out of it about nine times, and the noes are the only things in that book I would put my name to — and a count is a thing you can act on and a yes is not.** She sits down, which she does not do. She refuses to have *failed* written in the book and says the chair has the only vote in the room and has never used it and is not starting at sixty-four. Last of all, the man of thirty-one comes back on his way out and says nine words nobody asked for: **I will take a season, and I am not the first one you should ask, and put me down as the last** — and it goes in with the day on it, untidied. Forty-nine.

### Chapter 0144 — The Second Book

Aldis Kern's POV, the landing, the weigh-house, the lock-up, the wagon, and the north bay, **the fourth week of the seventh and the first week of the eighth.** He answers his own question — nobody asked him why the list is blank and he answers it to four men on a landing at about the sixth hour: **not writing it in is letting a body decide later who was on it, and the blankness was his and the decision was not, and he has been carrying a decision he did not make for six weeks and calling it honesty.** Then the institutional finding, eleven words long, finished by a bay in the seventh month: **a person on a list of works is on it for a season and comes off, and the day they come off is a public day, and a person on a list of persons is on it until a man with a tray nine hundred miles up the road changes his mind in a city in the wet and tells nobody. The list of works has an end in it. The list of persons does not.** Then what came north. It went on the thirtieth of the sixth in a hand and not in an envelope at four pence, carried by a clerk of the district works who is not paid for carrying it, and came back on that man's return: **an enrolment and not a judgment**, in the form House Sorn's border law provides, with a number in the corner and no name at the foot — offered, offered at no charge, answer of the household **none, there being no household to answer, the thing having been offered to a body**, the same form refused by the Sorn border households in the second month and entered at a number above this one, and theirs answered by persons and this not. Read out in eleven minutes and then again by a girl of seventeen, slower. The finding: **that is the second book in two months in which a House has had to put something about itself in writing, and the first one had a man's name at the foot of it and this one has a House's, and the Sorn law does not need a person and does not care whether there is one, and that is why that form got an answer in three weeks when a House's own table took about eleven and then said no and lost a hundred and forty years about it.** Then he does the thing he has not done since the third week of the sixth month: he cannot name the man who instructs him — a name on a letterhead, about four hundred letters, never met, never a face, could not tell you where he lives or his mother's name — and **on the second of the seventh he asked for the name in writing**, in his own hand, with the day on it, and he reads it out in a bay and says out loud that **it may cost him the contract**, and that nine men in three gangs would be stood off one after another over about a fortnight and would all work out why inside a month. He asks the room what a person would have to be able to do to refuse this guild, and Doreth Ferrow answers in nine words and in the wrong order — nothing, there is nothing they can do, a refusal costs you the thing you refused it with, a no is real and in a book and the person is still standing there the next morning. He asks for it written down and read back twice and offers to pay for the second reading out of his own wage. Then Halla Wray asks him, standing, the same question that has been asked at sixty doors, and he says **no**, in about nine seconds, and gives a reason: **he has been the man and the boy in a rate for six weeks and has found out what that is, which is that a person can be paid for work and does not have to be found for anything, and his name at the foot of a bank in a book in a hired bay means whoever is holding the list already has him.** It goes in with the day on it. The answer did not come on the next weekly, or the one after.

### Chapter 0145 — A Column She Is Not Going to Write

Nell Kest's POV, a counting room off a dyers' lane in Greyfen, a lane of eleven houses, and a cart road, **the second and third weeks of the eighth.** She is twenty, a dye-house worker, and she keeps the books of eleven houses on this lane and of four other houses in three other districts, which is what she is not paid for. The fourth book of the association is about ninety years old and its fourth column has been empty since it was opened, with a heading struck through about sixty years ago and one entry under a rule from six years back: an assessor who refused to put names in it, in writing, and asked for the refusal to be entered and for whoever came after her to be told it had been asked twice. Under that, in an unknown hand, somebody wrote *Noted*, and that word is the last thing that has happened to that book in six years. The book reached her on the ninth of the seventh after about eleven months of letters between two clerks in two districts, and the sentence that took the eleven months is **no objection had been received** — about forty people, eleven months, not one. She looks for half an hour at whether anyone has ever put anything in the fourth column of any of the four books. Nobody has. She does not write for two days; she works the indigo and has blue in the second knuckle of the third finger of her right hand on the eleventh and it is still there on the nineteenth. What she writes, at the bottom of a page, with the day against it, is **a count and not a name**: houses asked of, days asked for, days agreed to, and a line that the rest is a rest day and there is no column for a rest day and there has never been one. Above it, four more lines, which is the chapter: the last person's declining is entered, her name is not entered because she did not give it, **this is the second time somebody has been asked for a name for this column and I am the second person and I am saying no to it in the same book**, with her own name, her age, and the entry that the book does not belong to her and she has done this on her own account and expects to be asked about it. Then the finding: **a column with a heading over it is a machine for turning a house's problem into a family's problem, and a column with no heading over it is the same book with the machine taken out** — and then that a woman of sixty-four in a bay four hundred and thirty miles down a river said something in the sixth month about a foreman throwing a paper with a heading on it away twice, which she had read in a trade sheet at a shilling a copy and thought was a nice story about a district works, and it was not a story. She asks three of the about forty why nobody objected and gets the same sentence three times: **it is in a book and I have never been in it and if you asked me who holds it I could not tell you, and a thing you cannot name is a thing you cannot complain about.** A woman of about sixty at the eleventh door, who has cut plates out of a bench under a window on that lane for twenty-two years, gives the other half: **a count is the same as having put nothing in, and better, because a count is a thing a person can check.** Then six hours in a cart to a lane where the assessor who refused it six years ago is still doing the round, and a sentence on a stand-up that has been owed on that lane for six years — **a person has to be able to say no in a room, not only in a book; a book is a thing somebody else reads at a time of their choosing, and a room is a thing where somebody has to stand there while you say it; I watched two of these books get filled in by a man in an office about forty miles from where the cloth was dyed and I have never once been in the office** — and the answer to *why a room* is **not a reason to say no in a room instead; it is a reason to say no in both.** And then the honest sentence about her own family, once, in nine words: **we got the levy off the mill, the shop lost its credit in the same month, both of those are true, and one of them is why there is one job in this town and it is not mine.** And the assessor's last two things, which are not bookkeeping: there is no way to be in a book and not be findable and she has been looking nineteen years and has not found one; and her brother has not written since the second month, she has not written, and that is not a matter for an assessor and she is not going to start.

### Chapter 0146 — The Column Nobody Could Give Them

Ivet Sarn's POV, the clerk's window of the Mosswake Roll Court on a flood terrace, her back room, and the embankment corridor with the press, **the fourth week of the eighth and the first week of the ninth.** She is nine years in that post and is the fifth instance of a thing two different courts have now said four times, which she entered herself. A form comes up the road on the twelfth of the eighth at four pence, carried by a woman of about twenty who walked six hours and would take nothing, with two questions on the back of it, the first being the fourth line every person is asked and the second being **and what is the paper for** — no: **and if there is not, what is the reason, in a form, with a day on it.** She is gone for an hour and forty minutes and comes back and writes the answer in about eleven minutes and then again in a form whose box for a reason is two lines deep. The answer: **there is no column this court could add that would be of use to them, and the reason is that a column with a date in it and a person against the date is a hearing, and a hearing is the only thing in this empire that makes anybody answerable to anything, and this court cannot make one; a person who has not been heard has not been given a duty, and a duty given to a person who has not been heard is the thing this court has spent nineteen years declining to do in about nine hundred rooms with about four people in them on an ordinary afternoon; and there is a standing offer from a seat, open since the sixth month of last year, to pay the carriage of any person this court thinks should be heard, and it has been inoperable for about sixteen months because it pays carriage and not attendance.** And then her own sentence, which she flags as hers and not part of the reason: **if this court had a seventh column, about nine hundred and forty people would be summoned about four at a time to rooms about four hundred miles from where they live, and this court would be doing to them the exact thing the district of this court did to them in the sixth year, with a better motive and a worse timetable.** Asked whether she is refusing or declining to answer, she says refusing, and why: a declination would leave it open for somebody to ask the same question in four years in a different room and get a different answer. The girl of twenty reads the board above the sill — thirty-one numbers in chalk in letters about four inches high, four of them gone, a second figure, and a column head reading ***next review: none named*** thirty-one times — and the arithmetic is done out loud and written in a book with no office: **fifty-four filed copies for three cycles; thirty-one with a name and a door, of which twenty-two seen and nine told; eleven with a word and no name; twelve back from the post with a stamp and nothing else; the fourth cycle of the sixth year was never made and there were six copies for it; so fifty-four and six is sixty; and there are eighteen returns that did not come back at all, which is a different number, and the two are not added together and I am not going to let anybody add them together.** And the fifth heading is *Copy sold*, the only honest thing anybody ever put in that register. Then the corridor, and the file of the sixth year: **a man of thirty-eight put his hand flat on the spine in the fourth month and did not open it, and the reason on its face, in her hand, is that it is not that the file is a secret, it is that nobody has ever asked her.** She stands in front of it for about nine seconds and walks out without touching it, says so at the top of the steps, and a person from a bay writes down the not-opening, which is now the only record of it witnessed by anybody but the person who did it.
