Then what the court *can* give: a sixth ruled space at the bottom of every page of that register, which has never had a heading and about two hundred and forty people in that district have never known it is there, and one line in her own hand with a day on it — houses on this register: 31; days held open without a hearing and without a date, and no return is kept and that is entered as a fact and not a figure; person to be summoned: none named. And she says out loud that a foreman of a district works four miles away arrived at the same thing nine months earlier by accident and threw a heading away twice, and nobody told her, and **that is the whole of what is wrong with the last two years of my life.** And then Lowcross's quarter, in plain numbers: nineteen pounds three and fourpence; six pounds and eightpence committed for the quarter and about six pounds on the average of the four quarters; no line for a bridge in that fund and there has not been one in nineteen years and that is not an oversight; about a penny a day real cost; four hundred and eleven on the far side and three hundred and eighty-eight crossings in the district's return for four weeks, which are two figures and are never the same, because a count of a bar omits everybody who crosses without the bar moving; nobody is liable; it is open; and it is not paid at the end of this month or of any month she has heard of. And last: **I am a clerk of the court that wrote the letters in the sixth year, and I do not know which hands wrote them, and I entered that I do not know, and nobody has ever asked me about it, and I am the person in this building who could answer it and I cannot.**

### Chapter 0147 — The Question That Is Not Hers

Halla Wray's POV, the tool shed, the north bay, and a gate about two hundred yards from the mill, **the second and third weeks of the ninth, from about the sixth hour of one day to about the seventh hour of the next.** She is fifty-one and has been foreman for nine years, said no five times about a bank and every one was right, said yes twice, **and has never once been asked to take anything, and in nine years there has not been a form anywhere that wanted her to.** The finding, written on the back of a return and then not entered in the returns, because a return is not a book that has no heading in it: **a duty that stops is not the same as a duty that survives stopping; a season's duty with nobody at the day stops; the water does not end; so the arrangement has to have a name entered before the term ends, and a name cannot be entered before a person has been asked, and asking is about nine weeks of doorways, and about nineteen of about sixty will say no, and the nineteen are still behind this bank.** And then the half that undid four months of her own work: she has spent four months saying no lists and was right every time, **and a rotation is a list, and the difference is that every name on it is on it for ninety days and then comes off, in front of about thirty people, on a day anybody can point at — that is what a term is, a chain with a door in it, and nobody can close that door and nobody has claimed to.** Then she goes to a gate, and **Kem Ostry, thirty-one, a carter with no employer of record, who has been *the man and a boy* in a guild's phrase for about six weeks with his name in no column anywhere**, is sitting on the end post under Hob Bray's slate, which he has read about nine times and has not asked Bray what it is for. She asks him standing, in four minutes, with no slate, and he says **no** in about eleven seconds, and she says **that is that, thank you** and does not ask why and is not going to. He tells her anyway, in about four minutes, to about nine people who do not move: he came up in the fourth month and stopped at the gate and watched, and it took him a fortnight to understand he was staying; he stayed because he did not want to find out whether it would keep working; he has been in about four of those bay meetings since and has said one word in any of them; and when a chair of sixty-four asked the room in the last week of the sixth who came in the fourth and why, he said **I wanted to see if it worked** — **that is the truest thing I have said in nine years and I would like somebody to have put it there, and I would also like it not to have been frightening.** And the reason for the no: **a name at the foot of a bank means somebody comes and finds out whether it is working every fortnight for a season, and I have been the one watching since the fourth month and I would like it stopped, and I would rather be the man and the boy in a rate with no column in it, and that is the truth and I have never said it out loud because nobody has asked.** She accepts it, does not argue, does not come back, and has it written down with the reason in the place where the no is, which is what a person is entitled to. Then, on her way to the gate, she asks him the second question — *if you were putting a thing on about forty-one people, what would you have asked the next person* — and he gives it in eleven words: **who is going to be standing there when I want to know what this paper says.** She gets a book and makes him say it slower and change a word. Tavi Venn, sitting on the bench, says that a girl of eleven asked a person of seventeen that question on a bank at the Reed Cut gate on the twentieth of the twelfth month of last year, that she has been carrying it since, that she did not answer it then and has not since, and that a carter of thirty-one has said it at a gate and does not know where he got it, **and he got it from standing in a yard in the cold, and so did she, and I think that is the same place.** Then the finding, in a yard, to about nine people: **about nine of the sixty cannot read a paragraph, so a rotation of about forty-one people of whom nine cannot read a paragraph is not a duty, it is a notice** — and the answer, which Rennick Adley gives and she takes: **the reader goes in the rotation.** Not paid extra, not paid by the bay, forty-five pence a day like everybody else, for a season, with a day against the name, **because a person who reads and is not in the rotation is a person who cannot be told to stop.** And she names the four people who have arrived at that independently, about four hundred and thirty miles apart, and says she would put money on there being no way to get them in one room. And then she does the thing nobody asked her to: she writes her own name at the bottom of a slate with a date against it, reads it out twice, and refuses to state the day herself — **forty-five pence a day, about ninety days, one day's notice in my own hand, and my name at the foot of it with a day against it, which means about forty people in ninety-one households now know where I am on any given day without having to send to a house, and they have known it since about the ninth of the first month anyway, and the difference is that this is on a page and that was on a habit.** And: **I have been a foreman for nine years and I have never once been on a piece of paper, and I have spent four months telling this bay that a name at the foot of a page is a road, and I am not going to stand in a yard and say that and then not be on one.** The count at the end is seventeen yes and twelve no, and she refuses to have the sum of the unasked written down at all, on the grounds that a moving number in a book stops being a record and becomes an opinion.

### Chapter 0148 — The Choice About Forty-Nine

Marek Kest's POV, a room in the Roll Hall corridor and the courtyard in Auremar, and the north bay at the end, **the fourth week of the ninth and the first week of the tenth, out of about six days of travel each way in the writing and about nine days in the room.** Tamsin Rook is in the corridor outside the door with a satchel, having come down the same road in the same cart because a court order requires it. The thing that came up the road before he did is a form with a second question on the back of it that nobody on that bank had thought to ask, carried four hundred and thirty miles in a hand and not in an envelope at four pence by a woman of about twenty who walked six hours: **and what is the paper for.** He answers it in a bay in about nine minutes, standing, to about thirty people: **it is for a person to be found by it, and nothing else; it does not do the work, it does not stop the water, it does not make anybody safer, it does not make a child not a debtor; it makes a person findable; and every single thing anybody in this matter has found in fourteen months has been a person being found by a piece of paper, and not one of them has been a fact about the water.** And: **then why write any of it — because the alternative is a man with a tray and a good coat, and he has never once been worse than the paper.** Then the sentence the volume is built on: **an exception is a piece of paper; a speech can be made about people who are not in the room, and I made one, and it was true, and I was a party to the whole of it, and I could have made it with an empty room; a paper cannot; a paper with a name at the foot of it requires that somebody has been asked — not spoken to, not written down, asked — and if the person was not asked then the paper is a speech somebody has written down and got notarised, and the notarising is the only difference, and in this matter a notary's table is a machine for putting a seal on a speech and calling it a document. And that is the only law in this matter that has never once been broken, by anybody, in fourteen months, and I do not believe it is anybody's doing. I think it is just what happens.** The count is read out: about twenty-eight of the sixty asked, seventeen yes, twelve no, and the foreman's refusal to have the third sum written down. Then the rule, for the sixth time, on the back of a proof sheet, two options — write it on seventeen answers out of about sixty and about sixty children inside it, or write nothing until every person the consequence can land on has been asked and hold the bank alone for about eleven more weeks — and he reads it out and gives it to her. Her answer is not legal and she says so twice. What she says first is that both halves of his choice are wrong because they are two ends of a question he has not asked, and that the real question is what happens to a child, **and that nobody in this matter has ever put that on a piece of paper and offered it to anybody, and you have just put it on a piece of paper and used it as scenery.** She is forbidden to advise and says so, and says a fact is not advice. Then the answer, in words, in a bay, with a date: **one is not available to you and I am not saying that because I have standing. You cannot write it on forty-one people who have not been asked, because the instrument says in its own second line that no person is to be entered on the schedule who has not been asked in that person's own doorway — and you wrote that line yourself in the second week of the seventh month, and it is the best sentence anybody has written in this matter, and it is four months old. So the instrument forbids you. Not me. The instrument forbids you, and you are the person who wrote it, and that is the first time in fourteen months that anything anybody in this matter has written has stopped me doing anything, and I want you to notice what it was: it was not a court and it was not a House and it was not me. It was a line you put in yourself in a room with a stove in it because a man asked a woman a question in a doorway.** So the answer is two, and the date is the second of the fifth month of next year, and she says he knew that in the second week of the seventh month and put the choice in writing anyway, and wants that entered, and says the choice was not necessary and he made it because he has not slept properly since the fourth of the fourth month. Then he does the thing: **he withdraws the first exception, in nine sentences, in front of about thirty people, and a girl of seventeen who is not paid reads it out twice and the second reading is slower.** The nine sentences record what it was, that it is in force, that it guarantees every future flood by never asking anybody twice, that about sixty children under sixteen are inside it, that the clause that takes the children in is on the third page and the clause that says no child may be sued is on the fourth, and that both are honest and the second does not stop the first and does not need to; and they record the whole of the consequence, which is **that from this morning the bank is held by me alone, at seventeen shillings and a penny the week, until a person is named on a list of works**, and that **the district's flood authority in respect of those works is undispensed and has been since the first week of the first month and there is no office in this district with power to give it to anybody, because the keeper gave that authority away in writing in the first month to two people without asking either, and I was one of them, and she was right to, and it cannot be got back** — and then the sentence he says out loud in front of about thirty people rather than in a room with a stove in it: **the fourth month of next year is when it rains on this cut, and if I have not got somebody on a list of works by then, then I have put about sixty children on a bank on my own, by myself, in a city, and there is no form in this empire for asking them whether they would rather it was me.** And it takes effect on the second of the fifth month of next year and on that day the first season of a list of works begins, and the two are one document and there is no morning in between. Then she says the price out loud, before he can: **that is nine weeks of a man's wage and it is not the price; the price is that for nine weeks there is exactly one name against that bank and it is his, and if the bank goes in the fourth month of next year it goes onto one household in a city four hundred and thirty miles away, and nobody in ninety-one households will be able to find him and he will be able to find all of them — and that is the first time he has said it out loud in a room where about thirty people can hear it, and she did not have to make him.** And the second half, untender: **you are going to do this alone and you are going to tell yourself it is humility, and it is not; it is that you have not found out whether anybody else can, and in about nine weeks you are going to find out, because about forty-one people are going to be asked by somebody who is not you, at about nine doorways a week, by a woman who cannot read a paragraph and a foreman who has never volunteered for anything and a carter who is on the list last because he asked to be — and when that happens you are going to be a man with nothing on his wrist and a term and no bank, and it is going to be the first time in fourteen months that you have handed something to somebody, and I do not know what you are going to do with your hands.** He does not thank her. She says she has written down that he did not, that it is not a reward, that the two of them have not had a moment in four months and there is not going to be one, and that she is here because a paper says so **and that the paper is not why.** A book-keeper offers to enter that in a book with no heading; she says no; he says he will enter his own half of it.

### Chapter 0149 — The Engagement, and the Answer With No Why

Doreth Ferrow's POV, the north bay and the south end of the same store, and the Reed Cut district water office at the end, **the second and third weeks of the eleventh, from about the seventh hour to about the eighth.** A stove is lit in the south end and about nine people are standing because it is warmer standing up, and a single sheet of paper is on the bench with a keeper's signature at the foot of it and a day on it, and nobody in that room has read it and she cannot. She starts by telling the room four things about the cost before anybody asks a question. **A reader's day is between four pence and a shilling; four pence is what a copy of a thing costs and a shilling is a question at a notary's table and about four days of a bay of thirty people's breakfast; this bay has four shillings a year and that is the rent of a stone store and it is the whole of the Company's money and it has been for nineteen years. So there is no money. There has never been any money. Ninety-one households have never had four pence in a pot to give a person for turning up, and the reason nobody has ever paid a reader is not that the reader is not worth paying. It is that there is no such thing as four pence.** And the reason a person who turns up cannot be paid: **a person who is not paid is doing you a favour, and a favour has no end and no terms and no notice, and you cannot tell a favour to stop, and a duty you cannot stop is the whole of what this volume has been about since the ninth of the second month.** So it is forty-five pence, because a reader goes in the rotation with everybody else, for a season, with a day at the foot of her name, at the same rate as the men. Then she tells the room why a girl of seventeen is standing up: **in the third week of the ninth about nine people thanked her in that bay, four or five of them said thank you and one said it twice, and it was the first time in four months that anybody had said it, and she said no and asked for something to be entered — enter that I have been thanked, and enter that I have asked for the thanks to be withdrawn, and enter why — and it is in that book and about nine people in the room on the twenty-first of the ninth have not once said anything about it since, and I think some of you noticed and thought it was modesty. It was not modesty. It was a girl of seventeen doing arithmetic: a person who is thanked is a person who reads for thanks, and in about four years somebody is going to come and ask her to do it for nothing and say it is her mother's river, and she will have to say no to somebody who has already said thank you to her, and that is a harder no than this one.** And she asks nine people in the room not to thank her, so that it is a thing they are not doing rather than a thing they are managing. The girl of seventeen then says the four words, and the chair tells her she is going to say them today because she is going to be paid for the second reading from the first of the fourth month of next year, **and the first one is the one you have been doing for nothing since the twentieth of the twelfth month of last year, and I am not going to have that put in an engagement in that order; you are going to be paid for both.** Then the keeper, who came four miles on the second of the eleventh and was in the yard before the bay was full: **Sivra Oris, who put two human names into a box headed *who is to answer* in the first month without asking either, left a boy of sixteen out on purpose, entered the leaving-out as the only protection the office was able to give anybody, and disclaimed her own office's flood authority in writing to do it, and it cannot be got back.** The engagement, read out twice and the second reading slower, with about nine people in the room who cannot read a word: four days a week, forty-five pence a day, the same rate as every other person on the list, to begin on the first of the fourth month of next year, for a term to be stated at the foot of the list of works, on the same notice as every other person, **on a written engagement and not a verbal one, and the person engaged may have a copy of it, and there is a copy.** And the reader says out loud, unasked, that this is the first time in five months a sheet has been read out in that room with her name at the top of it, that she asked for the written one and asked twice and a keeper said no the first time, **and I am not thanking anybody and I want the asking entered with the day on it** — and the reason she asked for it in writing is that a verbal engagement is a thing a master can end at a gate in about nine seconds, and a written one has a day on it and a copy, and she has watched what a day and a copy are worth in this matter, **and I would like a road.** Then the chair asks the one question she has left — is this because it is right, or because you cannot stop — and is told that it is the fourth question on a board in a lane four hundred and thirty miles down this river, and is not answered, and then gets the real thing: **I gave away my office's flood authority in the first month on purpose, to two people, without asking either, and I have been right and everybody says so, and I have not had to do a thing since the first week of the fourth. And I can still sign a wage. That is the whole of what is left of nine years. And the size of what I can still do is one girl's reading, four days a week, from the first of the fourth month of next year. That is it. That is the whole of me as an instrument of anything.** And then: **a thing that can be refused is worth more than a thing that cannot; I have had four months to work that out after the first week of the first month, and the working out took me a keeper's authority with a seal on it to get, and I would have given that back at the start of the second month if somebody had asked me to.** Nobody asked her, and the girl wrote it down and read it back twice. Then the second half of the week, in a form with a reason and no why: the district court of Mosswake is asked whether it will treat the second of the fifth month of next year as a date of its own, and answers **no**, and refuses, and enters that it cannot hold a term in another person's paper, and that the order of the court sitting in room four of the eighteenth of the seventh last year gives a hearing and does not say when, and the finding of this court of the ninth of the eighth says when, and the two are nine days out of order with each other, and this court has not read the first and that court has not read this, **and there is no form in this empire for one court to ask another court what it has found, and this court has tried to write one four times in nine years and has not sent it and has written *why not* against it three times** — and a last line in a magistrate's own hand that is not part of the form: if the two halves are ever put on the same table in a room with a witness in it, this court will read both and will not say which is wrong, **because this court does not know, and a court that does not know should say so in a form with a day on it, and that is what I have done.** And the finding that follows: **nobody in that bay is going to get a court to fix that day; what they are going to get is a person to turn up on it, and there is no form in this empire for compelling a person to be brought to a room, and a seat has had a standing offer open for about sixteen months to pay the carriage of anybody this court thinks should be heard and has never worked once, because it pays carriage and not attendance; so the date is going to be kept by somebody walking, and it has to be a person who is not paid, or it is a favour, and a favour has no notice.** Tavi Venn says she is on it. And the count at the end: about forty-one yes with days against them, about nineteen no with reasons, about four people who cannot read a paragraph never asked and never going to be, the seventy-one-year-old being asked at her own door, the reader engaged in writing with a copy, a court that will not fix the day, a seat that will not pay the attendance, and **the day is going to be kept by somebody walking, and there are about forty-one of them, and the first of them has asked to be last, and a chair of sixty-four has entered that untidied.**

### Chapter 0150 — A Name at the Foot of the Fourth List

Marek Kest's POV, the north bay, the bank at the third-of-the-way mark, and the Reed Cut gate with its rail, **the first week of the fifth month of next year, the first and the second, from about the sixth hour of one day to about the ninth hour of the next.** He got there on the first with Tamsin Rook, and the carriage of both of them came out of seventeen shillings and a penny the week and nobody paid it, and the office that requires her to be in the room cannot be named. About thirty people are in the bay and about nine cannot read a word and are spoken to by a person sitting next to them. At about the seventh hour of the second, a foreman of fifty-one reads out what happened: **a clerk of a court four hundred and thirty miles up this river held the seventh work of eleven on a ninety-one-year page for one year, at forty-five pence a day on five men and a boy, out of his own wage, and the review of the term was this morning, and nobody came, and the ford is now on nobody's list again** — and then adds that nobody in that bay was under any obligation to get on a cart for four hundred and thirty miles to keep it alive, that the person it was written for did not expect anybody to, that he came anyway, and that he is standing at the west end of her bay and somebody should say something to him that is not about paper. **The term stopped on the day. That is what the paper says it does and it did it.** Then the instrument, four lines, read out twice with the second reading slower, and it is a human clerk's writing on the back of a sheet and a record and findable and in about four years anybody can pay four pence and read it. The consequence specified is the flood duty of the bank at the third-of-the-way mark and about one hundred and forty feet of ditch on the south side and the scour at that place, **which are on nobody's list and are on this one from today**; it is moved from him, a named party, to the persons on the schedule in rotation, one season each, forty-five pence a day, the same rate as every other person, a day stated against every name, a right in each of them to give it up on one day's notice in his own hand into a book that has no heading, a review at the end of each term; **no person is entered who has not been asked in that person's own doorway, and no person is entered who cannot say no, and the schedule is to be read to any person on it and to any person in any house behind the bank by a person entered on the schedule for that purpose and at the same rate as every other person on it, being about nine persons who cannot read a paragraph**; and it takes effect today, and **the cost named is his own labour for the length of the term and his own liability for it, and there is no other cost, and nothing in it is a debt of any household and no child is named in it or is party to it** — and a copy in a book with no heading and a copy on the bench, and he is not sealing it because he has no seal and there is no notary behind it on this reach. It is then named for what it is: **the third instrument of this kind he has written in fourteen months, and the other two are both on a page in the same book with his name on them and one of them was withdrawn this morning**, and the word for a thing like this is *exception* and he has used it twice in fourteen months and both times about a river, and it is not the word of a House and not the word of a guild and not the word on the rail at a gate. **There is no seal and no magic and it decides nothing on its own and it will be produced in about four years by anybody who wants it and there is no way in this empire to give a name back.** The count is read out: about forty-one yes with days against them and one of them entered as a description and not by name at her own insistence twice, and a described person is a person and is harder to serve and easier to lose; about nineteen no with reasons, not argued with and not asked a second time; and **not asked, and never going to be asked: about four people who cannot read a paragraph, and a woman of about seventy-one at the fourth house at the north of the ford who walked four miles of towpath in about two hours each way on the advice of nobody, and who cannot stand in a room with about thirty people in it for nine minutes, and who has said the same reason twice and will not say it a third time, and who has asked in writing to be asked at her own door four times a year, and who is being.** The first name at the foot of the season is written by the chair, in her own hand, in front of about thirty people, and it is **Kem Ostry, thirty-one, carter**, who had asked on the fifteenth of the seventh to be asked last. He refuses to stand where a person reads a name, and gives the honest account: **I said in the ninth month of last year that I would rather be the man and the boy in a rate with no column in it, and I meant it, and I have had five months of meaning it, and I am not going to pretend I have changed my mind. I have changed my mind about being watched. That is a different thing and I would like a person in this room to write down that it is a different thing and not the same thing.** And then the reason, unchanged, and the change: **it has not stopped; it has got worse; I am still the one watching; there is a list now and I am at the top of it and the first fortnight I am somebody's business. So I am taking it, and I am first and not last, and I am not asking to be last again, and I have taken a fortnight to work out how to say that and I have said it badly and it is in the book.** Somebody starts to thank him and about nine people look at the chair and it stops and nothing about it is on the page. The second name is the foreman's and the day is not stated by her. **A line about two inches long is on the inside of his left wrist and at about the fourth hour it did something for about nine seconds and the room saw it and about four people said so afterwards and the two people in the matter said nothing: it is a symptom, it reads nothing, it decides nothing, it imposes no geographic tether, it does not know where the ford is, and it did not tell anybody anything when it did it. The mark is not the price. The wage is the price** — a wage of seventeen shillings and a penny the week, three pounds eleven and threepence of it for nineteen days on a bank in the second month of last year, entered in a weigh-house ledger under the word *held*. The book in the stone store got a new hand in it, a woman's, the fifth hand in about sixty years, on a page with no heading at the top of it on purpose, and the page from ninety-one years back that carries eleven works and eleven names and says the terms are in the instrument and not in the book **is a record of a duty nobody holds, because the instrument it referred to was withdrawn that morning**. And the counting: **it is the fourth list of works on that cut in ninety-one years, and the first one anybody on that bank will be able to point at.** The last scene is the Reed Cut gate, about four miles down the water, at about the seventh hour. There is a rail with a curve in it that somebody's grandfather set into the platform, and a schedule nailed to it on good paper folded once, held by four nails and the holes of an earlier set, tied on once with a length of oiled cord that is long gone, **carrying the water office's device, which is not a name, and a memorandum number, which is a number of a book, and a date, and a foreman's name at the foot, which is the only person's name on the whole sheet** — and nobody came to read it for longer than anybody at that gate is alive to remember. **A second sheet goes up beside it on the second of the fifth month of next year, on a length of oiled cord tied on the evening it went up, on four new nails, and the four old nails were left in the rail, and nobody pulled anything down.** It is a list of works with a name at the foot of it and a day against that name, the fourth such sheet on that gate and the first anybody can point at. A girl of seventeen stands on the platform and reads it out in plain words twice, the second slower. **There is a girl of eleven on that platform with a pail, because there is a pail on that platform every evening of the fourth and fifth months and there has been for years, and she cannot read a paragraph, and nobody has ever given her a reason for it, and she is not a device and not a symbol and is owed nothing at all, and the only thing that happened to her on the second of the fifth month of next year is that somebody stood there and read it to her.** She asks four questions, three of them about the work and answered, and the fourth is the same question, near enough, that a carter of thirty-one asked a foreman at a gate in the seventh month of last year: *and who is it that reads it to the ones who cannot read it.* And the answer, in about nine words, from the person standing there: **me, and I am on it, and I am at the same rate as everybody else, and I have a term, and I can be told to stop, and I have never been able to be told to stop in five months of turning up.** Then four things said standing on a bank in the cold, unasked, and about nine people at a gate and about four in a bay and about thirty at a landing have all found out afterwards from somebody who was not paid to tell them: **one, I have read that list and every other paper in this matter out loud about nine hundred times since the fifth month of last year and I have not been paid for one hour of it, and nobody paid my carriage, and I came because there was nobody else. Two, I have been paid since the first of the fourth month of this year, and I am on that list at forty-five pence a day like everybody else, and the reason I asked for a written engagement and asked twice is that a favour has no notice and a duty has a notice, and about four years ago a girl of eleven stood on this platform and said that to me and I have been carrying it since. Three, I have not been thanked and I am not going to start, and a book-keeper wrote down in the ninth month of last year that I asked in writing for the thanks to be withdrawn and why, and the reason is that a person who is thanked is a person who reads for thanks, and in about four years somebody is going to come to this gate and ask me to do it for nothing and say it is my mother's river, and I will have to say no to somebody who has already said thank you to me. Four, a reader who is paid can be told to stop. That is the whole of what this rotation bought and it is not a wall and it is not justice and it is a list with a name at the foot of it and a day against the name. About nineteen people behind that bank said no and are behind it now, and one of them is a woman of about seventy-one who cannot stand in a room with about thirty people for nine minutes, and I am not going to stand on this bank and pretend the list is the river.** Then the standing account, in plain numbers and with no question asked: the House a hundred and forty years of licence gone with a name at the foot of the entry in a book with a crack in the spine, and a copy of it enrolled in a second book four hundred and thirty miles up a towpath with a number against it and no name at the foot, and the man not told and not going to be; the guarantee standing offered and unanswered at no charge with about sixty under sixteen inside it, and inside a guild's blank list, and inside a rotation they have not been asked about, told about none of it; a district court nine days out of order with a high court still not joined and no form in this empire for one court to ask another what it has found; the thirty-one of Mosswake still thirty-one with the arithmetic intact and *Copy sold* and a fourth column reading *next review: none named* thirty-one times, and a sixth space with one line in it and no heading over it worth nothing at law; the Lowcross bill at nineteen pounds three and fourpence unpaid, nobody liable, no line for a bridge in nineteen years, four hundred and eleven on the far side, three hundred and eighty-eight crossings, two figures never the same, a penny a day, about fifty-seven pounds of a tally never claimed; a factor of forty-six with a blank list and an unanswered written request for the name of the man who instructs him; a magistrate of fifty-one with a finding against her own practice and about four hundred unnamed; **a run number in a notary's table and a category of person who may be made a party, which is the road to a document in an archive that names a blank heir, and it is a road and not an answer and nobody has walked to the end of it**; and in a drawer in a city an envelope that has been there since the first month of the year before last, unsent and unopened, with three others, and a woman who keeps them holding an office that does not exist with a limit that does, who does not advise, and who has been in four bays this year because a paper said so, **and the paper is not the reason and both of them know it and neither of them has said it and it has been sixteen months.** And the last page: a list of works on a bank with one person's name at the foot of it and a date against that name, and a book in a stone store with a new hand in it, and a girl of eleven who cannot read the list being read to by somebody who stood there.

## What Volume 03 Resolved, What It Did Not, And What It Hands On

**Resolved on the page.** The volume's central question — whether a cooperative can be made to hold a duty that is public without making every child in it a debtor — is answered, and the answer is that it can be done in two parts and only both. **A body is made able to stop by being given nothing at all and by about forty-one people being given a season each, in rotation, at forty-five pence a day, on a list of works, with a date against every name, a right to give it up on one day's notice in the person's own hand, a review at the end of each term, and a reader on the same list at the same rate** — and the Company is not made a guarantor of anything. **The first exception is withdrawn** on the page, in nine sentences, in a room of about thirty people, and the withdrawal is dated so that the rotation's first season begins the same morning, so that there is no interval in which about sixty children under sixteen are on a bank held by one man in a city. **The second exception ends on the day because nobody came**, and that is what its paper says and that is the correct outcome. **The first person in this matter who turns up is paid**, on a written engagement, from the first of the fourth month of next year, with a copy, a term, and the same notice as everybody else, and the reason she is not thanked is entered in a book. **A guild list with no end in it and a list of works with a term in it are shown to be different kinds of paper**, and the difference is eleven words long. **A border law enrols a House's form four hundred and thirty miles north with a number and no name**, and that is the second book in two months in which a House has had to put something about itself in writing. **A refused column and a heading-less column are shown to be the same protection arriving from two trades about four hundred and thirty miles apart, independently, in about four months.** **The seal is a promise that the paper and the ground are the same thing and a name at the foot of a page is a road, and both were paid for by a person and neither was paid for by an institution.**

**Not resolved, and said plainly on the last page.** About nineteen of about sixty said no and are still behind that bank. About four people who cannot read a paragraph were never asked and never will be. The guarantee stands offered and unanswered, at no charge, answerable at any time afterwards by a person answering in its terms, and there is no such person. The thirty-one of Mosswake are still thirty-one and there is no seventh column. The Lowcross bill is unpaid and nobody is liable and there is no line for a bridge in that fund. A district court and a high court are nine days out of order with each other and neither has read the other and there is no form for asking. A file of the sixth year is shut between two others. A factor's request for the name of the man who instructs him has not been answered. About four hundred people a magistrate chose not to hold are not named and will not be. The seat's carriage offer is still inoperable after about sixteen months and the substitute is a person walking. Two courts will not fix a date, so a date is kept by a person who is not paid.

**Handed to Volume 04.** A run number in a notary's table in Auremar and a category of person who may be made a party, which is the road to a document in an archive that names a blank heir, and it is a route and not an answer. The first instrument of this kind this matter produced was written on a page about a river, and the second and third are on a page in a book with no heading, and about four years hence a person with four pence can read all three. **Nell Kest, twenty, a dye-house worker four hundred and thirty miles away, with a practice in four buildings in three districts that does not belong to her, has a lane and a shop and one honest sentence about what the work has cost her family, and her brother's letter of the sixteenth of the eighth of last year is still unanswered and she is still entitled to that.** Tamsin Rook holds an office that does not exist with a limit that does, has a drawer with four things in it, one of which has been there since the first month of the year before last, and has been in four bays because a paper says so and the paper is not the reason. Marek Kest has no certification, no register, no office and no fund, a discharged bar, a watch, a symptom on his wrist, and a term that ended on the day nobody came. And no romance has begun, and the V3–V4 milestone is not paid and is not to be paid in Volume 04's first batch without a scene of its own.

### Chapter 0151 — The Number of a Run

A clerk of thirty-four at the fourth counter of the Notaries' Table in Auremar is given a four-figure number by a man who has copied it off a covering slip four hundred and thirty miles away, and tells him at the counter that she is not going to be a door for him and that a register on a high shelf is the only place in the city the numbers exist. She looks the run up on her own time over two days and reports that about thirty of the rows in that run are not scale instruments at all but a striking-out of a name out of a roll, because an impression takes a number like anything else, and that the column a name goes in is headed *by whose authority*, which is a heading and not a charge, and that a name under it is an office discharged and not an admission. She refuses to read the day aloud in a public room and gives the two prices of not having it, one of which is free and has been paid. The chapter ends on the fee board, which has four things on it and none of them is what is in the book, and that this is not a fault in the board.

### Chapter 0152 — What a Table May Hold

The second thing a clerk can say, said because nobody in a hundred and forty years has asked her for it: a table may hold a thing for a hundred and forty years, may take fourpence to give it up, in person, and only a person may take it, being the person the thing is about or a person holding a written authority from that person, and there is no provision for anybody else and there never has been and she looked once in her second year and told nobody she had looked. She says the answer to a question is given to the person who asked and that the person who asked is a party from the day the day goes at the top of the form, so the door upstairs costs findability and the eleven weeks is not the price. She refuses to repeat the three lines she wrote on a blank in the sixth month of last year and says so, and sells him the question he can afford instead of the one he wants, and tells him she has let him. The chapter's finding is that the fourth line is free — the only line on that form with no charge against it anywhere in the building, and the one that changes what a person is — and that a thing nobody is charged for is a thing nobody thinks about, which is the second time she has wanted to argue it and the first was about a duty in a bay.

### Chapter 0153 — A Form With Three Boxes

The written application is written on the back of a blank because the Table stocks no form for asking a table a question, in three ruled boxes, and the third one wants a person the instrument touches and there is no such person alive. The clerk goes back to the box under her counter and to a finding she made on her own time in the sixth month of last year and told nobody, and finds the mechanism: **the fourth line is on a strip about two inches by one gummed to the foot of the form with a printed corner number so the two can be matched, there is no knife on that counter and there never has been, to lift the strip you damage the sheet, and there are exactly two things a person can do with the form afterwards — hand the sheet in with the strip still on it and a corner torn off, which is what a hundred and forty of the forms in the box have had done, or take the strip off and keep it, which tears the office's copy of the form in half and leaves the office with a sheet with a name on it and a hole where the answer was.** **What the box is, and the batch's arithmetic, are both on the page now: every one of the four hundred forms in it was bought at that counter and every one of them came back to that counter rather than leaving the building in the hand of the person who filled it in, and it is not a rule and not anybody's job and she cannot find out who does it.** About nine hundred forms a year go out of the rack, about four hundred and forty are written on, about forty a year write the fourth line badly and about four write it well, about four hundred a year do not get to it at all, and **so about forty-four people a year answer the only question on that form and the building can show a hundred and forty of them in a hundred and forty years and the other six thousand are in six thousand houses, because a strip of paper is a record of a question for exactly as long as somebody is carrying it and nobody is paid to carry it back.** The eleven are the only names in the hundred and forty, and the reason is on the page: **a body is the only thing on that form that cannot answer for itself, so the form does the only thing this building knows how to do and asks for a person, and it did not put a question to that person and did not print a line for one, and it took a hundred and forty years to produce eleven names and has never once asked one of the eleven whether they were there.** The nine minutes at the counter are the finding: a form with a reason in it and no box for a no is a form in which nobody is offered a no, and the no on that form is under the gum. It is not a conspiracy because there is nobody to conspire, and not a coincidence because it is the same in all eleven, and not a small number because of the gum, and about four hundred a year do not get to the strip at all because most of them never find out it is there. She writes nine lines out, dates and signs them, refuses to put them in a file, and puts them in a press in the back room with the day on the outside and no address on the outside.

### Chapter 0154 — The Refusal, and What It Cost

Marek Kest fills the third box with his own name and is refused, correctly, in a form with a reason written in a clerk's own hand in more words than the box holds, a day on it, a name at the foot of it, appealable by nobody, and no box of any kind for a why. The reason is on the fee board: the first, third and fourth of its four items are the same question asked three ways, and a person who says out loud and in writing that nothing touches him is not a party, and there is no third box and there is no application. The cost is named and it is findability: a refusal is a document, it goes in the day-book, and in about four years anybody with fourpence can read that he came here and asked, and there is no way in this empire to un-ask. He does not argue and does not put the same question in a different form, and says out loud that he has been looking for a written no since the fourth month of last year and that wanting to be refused is preparation and the only person it protects is him. She enters the question and the day and his name and does not enter the reason, because a reason entered in that day-book becomes a rule of that office. The chapter closes on a refused application, which is not an application, with the third box in his coat in a third of a sheet of good paper that no table in this empire may certify.

### Chapter 0155 — The Third Box

Tamsin Rook fills the third box at about a quarter past the second hour of the fourth week of the fifth month while he is four feet away at the estates counter, in a copyist's hand that slopes about nine degrees to the right, and does not sign the first box, and tells him about four minutes afterwards. She uses the rule of the twelfth month of last year once — a choice in writing with a day on it, either do not put yourself in the third box or put yourself in the third box — and answers it in the room in words with a date, and the answer is that she has been in it since that morning and the rule does not apply, which is entered as a correct non-use. She says the price out loud twice and does not soften it: she can be served anything about this instrument for the rest of her life, a party is a person a book can be true about, and there is no form in this empire for taking a party back out of a box, and she looked for about four minutes standing at a counter and did not look in every place. She says that you are cheaper than she is, and that she knew it, and that this is not a rescue, and that the reason she will not give is that it is a want and not a reason and a want entered in a form at that table would be in a book for a hundred and forty years. He does not thank her, she says in advance that nobody is to, and both of them make a second correct non-use of the rule. She is not thanked, does not want to be, and says what she wants out loud: to know what it is like for about eleven weeks.

### Chapter 0156 — Four Hundred and Thirty Miles

The road, the cost, and the arithmetic done out loud on a bench at a lock. A day of a wage of seventeen shillings and a penny the week is a little over two shillings and fivepence, six days is a little over fourteen shillings and leaves about one whole day, three journeys are twenty-one days and a fourth will be about twenty-four, and twenty-four days is about three pounds out of about forty-four pounds a year, and there is no fund and no form for a fund. Three pounds is very nearly what nineteen days on a bank cost him in the second month of last year, entered in a weigh-house ledger under the word *held*. The reader on that bank is paid forty-five pence a day and has a copy of an engagement in a satchel with a date on it of the first of the fourth month of next year; the two people walking the road are paid nothing, and the difference is a sheet of paper with a date on it. He pays a carrier ninepence at a lock and does not enter it in the book with no heading, because that book is for work. They come down onto the Slade in the first week of the sixth month, a girl of seventeen reads a list of works twice at a Reed Cut gate with the second reading slower while a girl of eleven stands on the platform with a pail and is read to and given no reason and owed nothing, and in the north bay he looks at nine hands in a book with no heading and says he has been told to look at a hand and has not got to it. She tells him there is no room four hundred yards from that bay with nobody in it, and that a party cannot speak for a body, and that the reason she could stand up in Auremar and put her name in a box is that she had never been one.

### Chapter 0157 — A Name in a Hand

Tamsin Rook recognises a hand in about four seconds and then spends about nine minutes of work on it, standing at a bench in the north bay in front of about nine people, and says the finding is a fact and not advice and may be checked word for word against the column of foolscap. The line is a run number, a device described by a mark that is a mark, and a name in a column headed *by whose authority*, and no day, because a clerk would not read a day aloud in a public room and said out loud why. She will not read the name twice, because a name read twice in a room is a name the room has. Her three findings are that the column is a heading and not a charge and is the only reason that register may hold a name at all; that about thirty rows in one run are not instruments and no column in the book says which is which, which two trades about four hundred and thirty miles apart have now worked out by accident without knowing the other exists; and that a heading over a column is a machine for putting a name in a book so that the name can never be asked anything. Then she says the batch's engine — a person with nothing enforceable behind them can be made to answer for nothing at all, because there is nothing to be liable for and nobody can serve a person who has nothing to serve, and therefore the cheapest party in this matter is the man standing in the bay, and the machine that has been arranging that is a strip of paper gummed to the foot of a form. She is asked why she wanted the room to know and says she was paid a copyist's wage for four years to tell whose hand a thing is in, that she has never once been on the other end of a line of paper of her own, and that she cannot say why she wanted them told and is not going to pretend to a room that she can.

### Chapter 0158 — A Door That Is a Door

Sefa Ild, twenty-eight, who keeps the north bay and is paid for standing in a room and is not a foreman and does not want to be one, explains why the lintel of the one door in the west end, dropped about half an inch in about forty years, has never been packed: because packing it would make it a thing somebody had done, and in a room where about nine people cannot read a paragraph and about nineteen have said no in a doorway, a thing somebody had done is a thing that can be asked about. She packs it herself in about nine minutes with a wedge off a sleeper, on her own account, with nobody instructed her and nobody helping, and enters one day in the bay's own book and does not read it out, which is the point. Her three statements are that a room with one door is a room a person can be put into and a door that does not shut is a door anybody can be put through; that a door needs a person to shut it and a duty needs a holder that can stop and neither of them is a work and there is no column anywhere for either; and that the room is for the asking, and that a room with a door that shuts is a room a person can leave, and that there is one name on the list of works which is a description instead of a name and a described person is harder to serve and easier to lose. Three doors four hundred and thirty miles apart and no form that joins them. Nobody thanks her, she says so first, and gives the reason that is not for the room.

### Chapter 0159 — The Second Question on the Back of a Form

At the Reed Cut gate in the second week of the seventh month Tavi Venn, who is seventeen, reads a schedule and a list of works twice, the second reading slower, on four new nails with the four old nails still in the rail, while a girl of eleven stands on the platform with a pail and is read to and is given no reason. Then the girl of seventeen asks, out loud, once, with a platform full of people on it: **and who is the cheapest person in this matter to put in a box.** She asks it again in a bay because somebody did not hear it. The answer, worked out in about nine minutes, is that a body cannot be made a party, cannot be instructed and cannot stop; that a person with a wage can be made to answer for a thing; and that a person with nothing enforceable behind them can be made to answer for nothing at all. And the second half, entered here for the first time and in her own words: **a person made blank of enforceable rights is not made safe; the blankness is a door with a person in it; the person is nearly always the one who did the blanking, because she is the one who could; a family with nothing enforceable can be put to by nobody and can also be named as the road by which somebody else is put to, and both are true of the same piece of paper; and the person who arranged the first is the only person who can say which of the two was meant.** Nobody says the word *eligibility*. He says *That is a category* and she says *That is a category* and she does not follow it, and gives the reason: she is paid to read things out loud and a person who is paid to read is a person who can be told to stop, and she was not going to turn it into the other thing. A chair of sixty-four who cannot read a paragraph has it read to her three times and asks for the words *cheapest* and *party* to be left out of the book, because a book is a place a thing goes to be looked up and she is not putting a judgement in one. The person who objects to being told she is the cheapest party is **the woman with the shop, and she is not named, and the chapter reaches her as a description and not as a name, and that is the chapter's own subject rather than a device.**

### Chapter 0160 — Two Names That Are Not the Clerk

They come up the road in the third week of the seventh month, the third journey and about six days, and the application goes back onto a slab of oak at the end of a passage at the end of the seventh month. The clerk reads it back twice, the second reading slower, and neither party signs the first box and neither signs the third. She enters the question, the day, the fee, and the two names in the day-book of that counter, and says it is the first name she has entered in that book in eleven years and that the one above it is nineteen years old and is a refusal put there by a man who is dead. She says the price out loud before either of them can: the two of them are now findable about this instrument and there is no form for un-writing a name out of a box, and the price of a door is the door, and she has been the door in that room for eleven years and has never once asked what it was for, and it was for this, and it cost her fourpence out of her own column that she did not enter in the fee book. She says do not thank her before anybody starts. **She says she is not going to fill the third box and she is also not going to un-fill it, and gives the reason in both directions: a box at that table gets filled with a person who has been asked, and where nobody has been asked the box stays empty, and an empty box on a form at that counter is the only honest thing that has ever been on one — and then she says the other half out loud too, that the woman who filled that box was not asked by the office, she asked herself, and there is no box on that sheet for a person who was not asked, and the reason there is no line for it is that this table has never once had a person walk up to it and ask to be a party. She says every one of the eleven had no more right to than she has and not one of them was asked, and that she is the twelfth, and that she entered her and did not find her.** She names the day the answer comes — the first week of the eighth month of next year — and says it is the same week that about forty-one people on a bank four hundred and thirty miles down have to decide something, and that there is no form in this empire that puts two dates in one box. Then she stands at the counter alone and does not go and do anything else. The form closes the batch with two of its three ruled boxes filled with the names of two people who are not the clerk, a copy at fourpence in a bag in a city four hundred and thirty miles down a river, and the answer-space empty and worth more than a filled one.
