### Chapter 0161 — The Name Against the Date

Halla Wray, fifty-one, the foreman, second on the list of about forty-one, finds out in the first week of the eighth month of next year that the review day is written at the foot of the list of works against her own name, and that a name at the foot of a list of works is a signature and a signature is a promise and a promise has a day. **Nobody wrote the word *review* on anything that asks anybody to come.** A court will not fix the date, because the sheet is not an instrument, and a form came back with a reason in it and no box for a why. A seat will not pay it, because the district's flood authority was given to two persons and the second of them refused in nine words in front of about forty people, and an office that has disclaimed the power to compel work has no fund, and the Company's whole money is four shillings a year and all of it is the rent of the building. The date has to be walked to about nine doors that cannot read a paragraph by a reader of seventeen who is paid forty-five pence a day and is not paid to walk. **A review is a day a person comes to and it is not a hearing: nobody to hear anything, no assessor, no chair, no minute, no form, and the only question on the sheet is whether a person is in the room, and the only evidence is a mark in a column in a book made by somebody who was not the person who came.** About thirty-one people came; about nine of the forty-one did not, and nobody knows which nine and there is no column for it. A book-keeper refuses to rule a fourth column in the bay's own ledger and the attendance goes on a slate, because a list of works is a list of jobs and a list of people who came is not that, and a slate is worse on purpose. **A reader who is paid can be told to stop.** A chair of sixty-four has it read three times and asks for the words *cheapest* and *party* to be left out of the book because a book is a place a thing goes to be looked up. And at about the seventh hour a girl of seventeen asks, once, out loud: *and who is going to ask us again.*

### Chapter 0162 — What It Costs to Get Here

Sefa Ild, twenty-eight, who keeps the north bay and is the only person in ninety-one households paid for standing in a room, works out in the first week into the second week of the eighth month what it costs to get about forty-one people to a place on a day nobody is paid to bring them to, and reads it out in a bay, and refuses the second season — **not the title, which has never changed, but the arithmetic.** The price is not money: four shillings a year of which all of it is the rent of a building and the smaller half of it reaches her; one reader's wage for two days and the walking that is not that wage; about four people who cannot read a paragraph and were never asked; about nine who cannot read one; about nine who said yes and did not come; one bench about two feet higher than the bench in the middle that will not take a woman of sixty-four. **And one cart, which belongs to a man of thirty-one who is on the list first, and a man on the list has to come as a person coming and not as the man bringing the cart, and if he brings the cart he is working and not attending, and if he does not about eleven people do not come.** She refuses to name the four, because naming them is asking them. Three objects end up on a bench and none of them is a record: a slate that can be washed, a leaf in a satchel that can go into a house, and a book in a hired store that can lose its building. She gives the price to the room rather than holding it, and the book-keeper writes it in the third column of the bay's own ledger, which has had no heading over it since the sixth year, and she does not read it out.

### Chapter 0163 — A Row and Not a Leaf

Marn Ottery, thirty-four, eleven years at the fourth counter and not a lawyer, reads out the answer at about the second hour of the fourth day of the first week of the eighth month of next year, twice, the second reading slower, to a man who is in the room because he is not on the list and the review on a bank four hundred and thirty miles down the river is happening the same week and costs him nothing at all. The answer is four lines and it is correct: there is a row, the row records a striking-out, the striking-out was made with an impression because an impression is the only thing in that building that will take a number, the column is headed *by whose authority* and a name under it is the discharge of an office, and **no document corresponding to the run number is held at that table.** Then she says the four things that are not on it. **What was struck out was a leaf of a bound schedule, and a bound schedule does not travel, and the reason is not law: the binding is what holds the schedule whole, and a leaf out of one is a removal.** It is in the fourth press, in a wall, in a corridor behind a court on a flood terrace, and it has been in that corridor longer than anybody in that building has been alive. Three things are required and one is had; a court direction is a direction of a court and this table is not a court and she looked for a form for about nine minutes and is telling the size of the looking. **A copy of a thing is a fact about the copying.** And she says the price out loud before either of them can, and it is the second door, and the price of a second door is the second door, and it is worse this time: **a door that does not know which way it is facing is not a door anybody can shut.** Tamsin Rook says the price of a party out loud for the third time and then notices that it is not about the party, and asks for that to be noticed.

### Chapter 0164 — The First Fortnight of a Season Nobody Agreed To

Tavi Venn, seventeen, forty-five pence a day, four days a week, on a written engagement, not thanked, not going to start, spends the second week into the third week of the eighth month of next year on a fortnight of work nobody agreed to. The first season began on the second of the fifth month of next year and is about ninety days and **the season ended by arithmetic, and arithmetic is not a notice.** She asks out loud on the platform at the Reed Cut gate, once, and again in a bay because a foreman at the back did not hear it, and the answer takes four days and half a bay. **A duty is found to have stopped when somebody stands in a room and does not do it, and there is no column and no form and no notice that ends a duty, and the only instrument in this empire that can stop one is a person standing in a room not doing it** — and about four people who cannot read a paragraph have never been in that room, so about four duties have not been shown to have stopped and have not been shown to have started. **A no with a reason in it is in a book and a no with no reason in it is in a doorway.** The schedule on the rail is referred to without a date and without a figure of an age, on four new nails with the four old ones still in the rail. The notice on her own engagement does not contain a season and does not contain a date at the end of anything, and **a date at the beginning of a thing is not a date at the end of it.** And on the seventh day she takes a slate up the lane at the north of the ford to ask four people a question, gets to the second door, and does not knock, and gives the reason: **they have not been asked once, and if I put a day against a name now then in about four years somebody will think somebody asked them, and the whole of this matter is a machine that puts names in books so the names can never be asked anything, and I am seventeen and I am about to do it with a piece of chalk.**

### Chapter 0165 — Three Pounds and Twelve Shillings

Marek Kest, thirty-eight, walks about four hundred and thirty miles down the old river road from the third week of the eighth month of next year into the first week of the ninth, in six days, with a row in his coat and no money, and the fifth journey is the one the batch is about. He does the arithmetic out loud on a bench at a lock to a carrier of about fifty who did not ask: **a day of a wage of seventeen shillings and a penny the week is a little over two shillings and fivepence, twenty-one days is two pounds and eleven shillings and threepence, twenty-four days is about three pounds, and thirty days is three pounds and thirteen shillings and threepence out of about forty-four pounds a year, and there is no fund and there is no form for a fund.** He does not take a cart, because a cart is a day of somebody else's wage and a bench and a towpath and a lock are free. The carrier reads the heading and stops there and says he would rather be a man who has read the heading than a man who has had a clerk explain it to him on a towpath. A factor of forty-six has a contract at one shilling and sixpence a day and ninety days of it is a hundred and thirty-five shillings, which is six pounds fifteen shillings, and about nine men in three gangs have asked in writing for the name of the man who instructs them and one of them has said out loud that asking may have cost him the contract. **The collision of the two dates cost him nothing, because he is not on the list and the list has never had him on it and he refused the seat in nine words**, and the only person the week cost anything to is the girl of seventeen. A district court and a high court nine days out of order with each other are not joined, because there was no witness on a landing a hundred and forty yards below a ford, and the form that came back has a reason in it and no box for a why. He comes down onto the Slade on the second day of the first week of the ninth, about a week late, and reads two lines in the third column of a bay's own ledger written about a week apart in two different hands, and the woman who keeps the bay tells him she washed the slate on purpose and that the door shut on his way in because she packed its lintel with a wedge off a sleeper.

### Chapter 0166 — A Document Given at a Lock

Oren Tal, sixty-one or two, a retired scale notary with a burn-scarred hand, walks about nine miles to a lock on the old river road in the second week of the ninth month of next year, because **a carrier would have written it down and a document that a carrier has carried has an entry against it and there is nowhere at a lock to keep an entry.** He gives a man of thirty-eight and a woman of twenty-four a form for a recorded custody entry, written out in his own hand off a memory of about sixty years, on the back of a sheet, with his own day on it and not the day of the thing. It has four lines: the thing, the person in charge of it, the day it was taken, the signature of the person who took it. **There is no fifth line, and there is no line for a body, and that is not a decision anybody made — a body cannot answer for itself, cannot be instructed and cannot stop, and the reason nobody has printed the reason is that everybody who has ever used the form has known it without being told.** The hard half, in his own mouth: **a person in charge of a bound schedule in a press is a person, a press is not, a person cannot be appointed to it by anybody who is not standing in the corridor, and if a thing has been in a press for a hundred and forty years and nobody was ever put in charge of it then there is no entry and there never has been one, and the absence is not a gap — the absence is the record.** He asks for nothing and says the price of a door is the door, and that it is his own method, and that a thing given for nothing and thanked for is bought on the way out. **And he refuses one word, a first name, and he refuses it before anybody asks, and says out loud that a refusal is not a denial and is not a confirmation, and that a document is a document and there is no way in this empire to un-ask, and that everybody is entitled to read into it whatever they had already decided to read into it.** A woman of twenty-four does not ask him a question for about nine minutes and he notices, and says one sentence: that a person who has been refused once knows what a refusal costs and that is not the same as being loyal and he does not know the difference either. He will leave if anybody thanks him. He walks back up the towpath and neither of them follows him.

### Chapter 0167 — A Correction on a Table

Aurelia Pell, human, a provincial archivist, on the ordinary round of her office with a box in a cart and eleven stops on a card, puts a back-room correction on a table in the third week into the fourth week of the ninth month of next year. She is Pell Vey's estranged daughter and that is a fact about her and not about this room, and the two people who come in do not know it and do not ask. The disagreement is nine years old: her office's copy of a district roll carries a body in a column headed for the person who did a west ditching, and the roll carries a man, Tarn Belwick, forty-one then and forty-four now, alive, who does not want to be in a roll and says so in front of his wife. She corrects the copy in the margin, in her own hand, with the day and her name at the foot of it, and the correction says that a body is not a party, cannot be instructed, cannot stop and cannot be paid. **A roll is a copy of a copy of a thing and every copy in it is a person copying, and a person can be put into a column of a roll by hand and a person can be taken out by hand, and there is nothing in this empire that stops anybody doing it, and that is not a flaw in the record — that is what a record is, and it is the reason nobody has ever found out who did a particular one: not because it was well done, because it was ordinary.** She answers one question a stranger asks her and declines a second, and gives the reason in one sentence: **a provincial archivist who answers a stranger's question about a roll has put a stranger's question into a roll, and there is no form anywhere for taking a question back out of a record.** She gives her name once, refuses to be thanked in advance, and shows them the card, on which ten of the eleven stops are buildings and one is a man in a house, and a man in a house is the only stop anybody can turn her away from. **She is not an ally. She is a cart. She does not know she is in anything.**

### Chapter 0168 — What a Clerk Is In Charge Of

Ivet Sarn, thirty-eight, nine years at a clerk's window about four feet off the ground on a flood terrace, with a board above the sill, a back room and four presses in an embankment corridor behind the building, finds out in the first week of the tenth month of next year what she is for, in a corridor, with a witness in it. **Nobody had ever asked her what the presses were for. She has been in charge of four of them for nine years.** There is no form that makes a person be in charge of a thing and there never has been, and the way she came by it is that she was the only one left holding a key, on a string, shortened twice by herself. **A person in charge of a thing is not a party to it, and it is the only position in this matter that is not a party: a party is served, a witness is asked, and this is served and asked and holds no box and no sheet and no fee and no term and no day at the end of it, and it is the only one of the four positions anybody can stop.** A man of about fifty comes to the sill and asks for his name to be taken off the board, and is refused, and says the sentence that is the reason she can say her own in one line: *I did not think of it like that.* The form from a lock is read out and the finding is that **a custody entry is a statement that somebody was in the room, and the whole of what it requires is a person who can be instructed and who can stop, and that is why a door has to be opened by a person and not by a body, and it is not strength and it is not law** — and that a person who is going to be asked to stand in a room ought to be told how long she has, and nobody in this matter has told anybody. Neither of the two who have asked a question can witness what is done in answer to it, so the witness is a woman at the sill with a paper who is told in one sentence that being a witness does not mean being to blame. Then the entry is made in four lines with the day on it, **not taken, only opened, with the person in charge standing in the corridor while it was open**, and it is not read out, and the reason is not the reason the lintel was not read out: *a lintel was a thing I did to a building; this is a thing I am going to be asked about for the rest of my working life and I am not writing it twice.*

### Chapter 0169 — A Door That Has to Be Opened by a Person

Marek Kest opens a press in a wall at the end of a corridor in the second week into the third week of the tenth month of next year, and a clerk of thirty-eight opens it because she was asked to and not on her own account, and a woman of twenty-four stands at the end of the corridor for about forty minutes because a party is served and a witness is asked and a person cannot be both about the same thing. The schedule is bound in a vellum gone the colour of a thumb and it is one volume and it is about the size of a hymn book and it is heavier than a hymn book. **Nine completed terms, nine holders, nine days at the end of nine terms. The tenth entry has a holder and no day, and the holder is the office, in its own name, as its own holder, and the line where the day would go is empty, and there is no column in that book for the person who emptied it, because a schedule of a term is not a register of duties.** He copies the ten entries onto four sheets in his own hand and does not take the leaf out and says why out loud twice: **a gap in a copy is a fact about the copying, and a gap in an original is a different sentence, a gap in an original is a fact about a removal, and I am not going to be the man who writes that sentence about this.** He has five journeys and three pounds and thirteen shillings and threepence and he has got a copy, and the copy is worth exactly what a copy is worth: it can be read and it cannot be leaned on. Then he applies at a district court for a direction and is **refused in a form, in four days, with a name at the foot of it and a day on it and no box for a why**, and the reason uses the word *bound* and does not say what it means, and there are about nine words on either side of it, and the clerk of fifty-five has been there nineteen years and has never had a form come back with a word like that in it. **A refusal that is anticipated is a threat and a refusal that is applied for is a document, and being right is what makes it a document, and there is no way in this empire to un-ask.** And he cannot post any of it to her, because there is no notice in this empire that will go to a person in charge of a press.

### Chapter 0170 — The Notice on a Nail

Tamsin Rook, twenty-four, works out in the last week of the tenth month of next year that there is no notice in this empire that will go to a person in charge of a press — **a notice is a list of works, a list of works has a name at the foot of it, and a foreman nails one to a rail at a gate and anybody may come and read it, and you cannot send one to a clerk or to a woman with a key on a string or to a person at a window who has never been appointed to anything, and a form that appointed her would take away the only thing about her that makes the four lines work.** So she stands in the corridor and says it out loud in person. The refusal is read once and put face down on the sill, and the clerk says the court is right in about four seconds and refuses to speculate about what the word *bound* means in the middle of a sentence on a day when a room is about to be cleared. **The notice on the nail is a list of works with jobs against days and a name at the foot of it and no day at the end of it, and the name is a district, and that is the same shape as the tenth term of a schedule in a press in that building, and a list of works with no day at the end of it does not stop — it goes on.** She uses the rule of the twelfth month of last year once, in writing, with a date, offering him a choice, and the answer in a corridor in words with a date is that **the rule does not apply**, because a day is not a choice, and because a rule that only ever works in one direction is not a rule but a preference. She writes the finding in a book with no office on it with the day on it, reads it back twice the second reading slower, and says out loud where she got that, for the first time: **a girl of seventeen, who is the best reader in the district, whom she has never met.** The clearing begins on the fourth. Ivet Sarn says she has been in charge of a press for nine years and will not be after the fourth, that nobody has told her she is going to be asked, that she has not asked whether she will be, that a person cannot be compelled to be in a room and there is no form for it, and that if somebody in an office four hundred and thirty miles up a river writes her name on a list of works and nails it to a rail she will not see it, because she is not on a bank and there is no rail. And then the batch closes in the plainest words there are: the guarantee standing offered and unanswered with about sixty children under sixteen inside it, the Lowcross bill at nineteen pounds three and fourpence unpaid with nobody liable and no line for a bridge in that fund in nineteen years, a reader of seventeen who is not thanked and is not going to start, and about four hundred and thirty miles of road in each direction with no fund and no form for a fund, and a river that is running and does not know any of this.

**Repaired after review, and this entry is unchanged otherwise.** The corridor exchange this summary compresses has been re-cut into seven turns with one instruction and one refusal: **the one-direction reason is Marek's, with the rule's own figures — sixteen months old, used six times in fourteen months, never once about a person — and *a fact is not advice* and *about four months* and *I have never once had it tested like this* are Tamsin's, and Marek keeps the Nevin Trost material and the words *I have never written to him*.** The non-use is still a correct non-use and is still the third anybody has made with it. The closing roll-call is nine paragraphs and not fifteen. No plot beat in this entry moved.
